HIGH SCHOOL SCIENCE TERMINOLOGY PART I The Most Useful Terms of Chemistry and Physics DEFINED AND EXPLAINED BY J. O. FRANK, A. M. (INDIANA) PROFESSOR OF SCIENCE EDUCATION AND HEAD OF THE DEPARTMENT OF CHEMISTRY, WISCONSIN STATE TEACHERS COLLEGE AT OSHKOSH. 

ASSISTED BY H. K. WHITE, A. M. (HARVARD) FOR SEVERAL YEARS TEACHER OF SCIENCE IN THE MIS- SOULA COUNTY HIGH SCHOOL, MISSOULA, MONT.

SCIENCE PREFACE 

CIENCE teaching, which is the foundation of all scientific work, calls for the reproduction of the teacher's thought in the mind of the pupil. 

This transfer of thought from teacher to learner is brought about by the use of words, whether printed or spoken, and many of these words must be used in a highly technical sense. 

If the teacher and pupil fail to use technical terms to express identical thoughts, the teacher's thought is reproduced in the mind of the pupil in distorted form, or not reproduced at all. 

Therefore, in beginning a new science, or phase of science, it is necessary for the pupil to learn at the outset the correct meanings of the technical terms it introduces as these are the tools with which teacher and pupil work together.

There are a number of reasons why pupils in high school science in the past have not built an adequate science vocabulary. A few of these. 

1. It has been shown that the most widely used texts in the various high school sciences contain a large number of unnecessary new terms. In some cases as many as ten different terms have been used to indicate one single idea, while in other cases a number of different meanings (even in the field of one science) are given to a single term. In many cases pupils are required to attempt to learn the meanings of a long list of terms which they will never use again in school or everyday life. It is obvious that much effort is wasted by teachers and pupils in teaching and learning the meanings of useless scientific terms. 

The need for a reduction in number and a standardization of the terms of each of the high school sciences is equally obvious. 

2. Many terms have one meaning in a given field of science and an entirely different meaning in another field. Some words have as many as four distinct meanings in as many branches of science. When a pupil begins a new high school science the teacher rarely realizes that many terms of the new science have other meanings in other fields from which the pupil has come. Often teacher and pupil are slow to learn the true cause of their failure to understand each other and much time and energy is lost to both. 

3. High school science texts often fail to define important terms-frequently the definitions which are given are faulty, inaccurate, or wholly wrong. Texts do not agree with each other in giving definitions of terms fundamental to a given science. In nineteen widely used modern high school chemistry texts, sixteen different definitions of the term valence were found. Definitions of many other equally important terms are just as unsatisfacory.

There is but one way to acquire an accurate, practical, working vocabulary of the sciences-by dictionary work. Pupils are easily convinced of this, and show a laudable desire to do this work. They buy all sorts of small dictionaries, only to find out very soon that no abridged dictionary which is now on the market is of any great value to the beginning student of science. The definitions of scientific terms given in such books are wholly inadequate. Often they are misleading or erroneous.

The unabridged dictionaries, however, contain most of the information. which the pupil needs. Unfortunately there are two reasons why their use by beginners is not effective: (1) They bewilder the young student and waste his time by presenting not only the scientific meanings, but also the colloquial, literary, and other meanings of the terms. From among these the pupil must first choose if he can the definition which he wants. 

For example, one standard dictionary gives seventeen definitions of the word light. Having chosen the definition he wants (2) he must interpret a definition written by a specialist for specialists-incomprehensible to the average high school pupil.

There is need, then, for a dictionary (1) which gives scientific terms only; (2) contains only the scientific meanings of those terms; (3) distinguishes clearly between the meanings of the same term in the different special sciences; and (4) is written in language which can be comprehended by the high school pupil at the time when he first meets the term in a high school science course.

This book is the result of the authors' attempt to meet the needs of teachers and pupils in the field of chemistry and physics. Similar books dealing with the terminology of Biology and General Science are in preparation.

ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

It would be impossible to mention all those who have contributed something toward the making of this book, but in a few cases the indebtedness is so great that it deserves special mention. Three articles were of great aid in making the list of terms to be defined. These were: “The 

Vocabularies of High School Science Texts" by S. R. Powers, Teachers College Record, Vol. XXVIII (Nov. 1926); "The Technical Vocabularies of The Public School Subjects" by Luella C. Pressey, Ohio State University; 

and "Some Values Derived From Extensive Reading in General Science" by F. D. Curtis, Contributions to Education No. 163, Teachers College, Columbia University. 

One article was of the greatest assistance in bringing out the origins of the various terms. This was "Classical Origins of Scientific Terms" by 

Gonzalez Lodge published in Number 26, Latin Notes Supplement Nov. 

1927, at Columbia University.

Questions of fact in the field of chemistry were usually settled by reference to the texts of Bloxam, Molinari, or Roscoe and Schorlemmer.

Figures and tables were usually corrected by reference to the International 

Critical Tables.

abrasive, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to scrape off.] A substance used for grinding or polishing. The principal commercial abrasives are carborundum, corundum, emery, quartz (silex), pumice, rouge, and sand. absolute, adj. [From the Latin verb meaning to set free.] 1. Without any restriction. or condition: as, an absolute necessity. 2. Perfect: complete: as absolute freedom. Absolute alcohol is alcohol from which all water has been removed. Absolute humidity is the actual amount of water, in the form of vapor, which is present in the atmosphere. This is usually expressed in grams per cubic meter or in grains per cubic foot. Absolute temperature is temperature expressed in degrees above the absolute zero; as, the freezing point of pure water is at 273° absolute and its boiling point is at 373° absolute temperature. Absolute zero is the temperature at which no heat exists and it represents the condition in which there is no molecular motion. It is the lowest point or beginning of the absolute scale of temperature, and is equivalent to-273° C. and -458.4° F. The degree absolute has the same magnitude as the degree centigrade, so the freezing point of water is 0° C. or 273° absolute. While attempting to solidify liquid helium, Onnes of Leyden, in 1924, secured a temperature of 272.08° Centigrade, or 0.92° absolute. This is the nearest approach to absolute zero that man has attained in the laboratory. absorb, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to suck in.] 1. To draw in; to soak up; to merge a smaller into a larger thing; to take in, as water is taken into the openings in a sponge. 2. In physics, to take in because of molecular attraction, as when ink is taken into a blotter—an example of capillarity. The term is also used to cover the disappearance of light which falls upon the non-reflecting surface of an opaque object, such as a piece of black cloth; and less appropriately to the transfer of heat from a hot object to a cooler one. absorption spectrum.

The dark line spectrum produced when the white light from an incandescent solid is passed through a glowing gas which is itself capable of producing a bright line spectrum. When sodium vapor is interposed between an incandescent solid and the spectroscope, two dark lines corresponding to the bright yellow lines of the sodium spectrum, appear in the continuous spectrum of the incandescent solid. This is due to the fact that substances which can emit light of a given wave length can also absorb light of the same wave length so that each element, when in the form of a glowing gas, will produce distinguishing dark lines (called Fraunhofer lines) when the continuous spectrum of an incandescent 1

ACCELERATION 

solid is passed through it. These dark line spectra are called absorption spectra. acceleration, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to hasten.] The time rate of change in velocity or direction of a body, or both. Acceleration is said to be positive or negative according as the velocity increases or decreases. 

Acceleration is said to be linear if the change in velocity is one of magnitude, and angular if the change is one of direction. See velocity. acetanilid, n.

A white crystalline compound (C6H5NHOCCH3) prepared by boiling a mixture of aniline and glacial acetic acid together for several hours. 

Acetanilid is used in medicine in the treatment of fevers under the name of antifebrin, in the industries as a preservative of hydrogen perioxide, in the manufacture of lacquers and cellulose nitrate and cellulose acetate substitutes for varnishes. acetone n. [Acetone, so named because it is a ketone obtained by heating an acetate. The first half of the word is taken from acetate and the second half from ketone.] A colorless, inflammable liquid (CH3COCH3), the simplest ketone. It has a fragrant mint-like odor and is usually obtained from pyroligneous acid (one of the liquids resulting from the distillation of wood), by heating calcium acetate, or by a fermentation process. Acetone is one of the most important solvents for resins and gums, and is used extensively in the paint and varnish industry. It is also used in the preparation of chloroform, iodoform, sulphonal, and other important organic compounds; and in the manufacture of high explosives. acetylene n.

A colorless gas (C2H2) formed by direct combination of carbon and hydrogen at very high temperatures, or by the action of water on calcium carbide. It is 0.92 times as heavy as air. At 0° C. it is condensed to a colorless liquid by 26.05 atmospheres pressure. Under ordinary conditions it burns with a very smoky flame owing to insufficient supply of oxygen, therefore a special burner is usually employed. The gas is sometimes stored in cylinders containing acetone and asbestos. 

It is used as fuel in a blowpipe designed for the cutting and welding of metals. acid, n. [From the Latin adjective meaning sour or sharp.] 1. In inorganic chemistry, any compound containing hydrogen as the only positive element and whose aqueous solutions show properties characteristic of the hydrogen ion. An acid may be a liquid, a solid, or a gas soluble in water. Its water solution is usually sour; is capable of changing the colors of certain dyes called indicators (for example, litmus), and capable of reacting with (neutralizing) a base to produce a salt and water. All acids are electrolytes (i. e. produce conducting solutions 2

ACIDIFY 

when dissolved in water.) All acids dissociate on going into solution, producing positively charged hydrogen ions, and negatively charged acid ions. A strong acid is one which on going into solution produces a relatively large number of hydrogen ions and whose solutions, therefore, show strongly the characteristics of the hydrogen ion. A weak acid is one which, on going into solution, produces relatively few hydrogen ions, and whose solutions, therefore, show but feebly the characteristics of the hydrogen ion. A part or all of the hydrogen of an acid may be replaced by various metals to form salts and by various radicals to form esters. Acids are termed monobasic, dibasic, tribasic, and tetrabasic, according as each molecule has one, two, three, or four hydrogen atoms which may be replaced by monovalent atoms or radicals. Thus, hydrochloric acid (HCl) is monobasic, sulphuric acid (H2SO4) is dibasic and phosphoric acid (H3PO4) is tribasic. 2. In organic chemistry, all those compounds which show the characteristics. of acids as defined in (1) above, and also all those compounds which contain the carboxyl radical (-COOH), though some of these are insoluble in water. There are several series of organic acids, the most important of which is the fatty acid series of which formic acid (HCOOH), is the first member. Many of the common vegetable and animal fats and oils such as olive oil, lard, tallow and butter, are chiefly composed of the glycerine esters of some of the higher members of this series of acids. acidify v.t. 

To cause to become acid; to convert to an acid. acidity, n. 

1. The degree to which a substance or a solution contains acid: as, vinegar has an acidity of four percent. 2. In chemistry, the power of a base to combine with acid radicals, producing salts. Bases are classified as monoacid, diacid, triacid, etc., according as .they combine with one, two, or three monobasic acid radicals. Bases may also be classified as mono-, di-, or tri- acid bases according as they produce one, two or three hydroxyl ions per molecule on going into solution, though it must be understood that all possible hydroxyl ions are certain to be produced only in case of neutralization of the base by an acid. Thus sodium hydroxide is a monoacid base because one molecule of sodium hydroxide (NaOH) will yield but one hydroxyl ion on going into solution and it can therefore neutralize but one equivalent of acid. Calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2), is a diacid base because one molecule yields two hydroxyl ions on going into solution. acidulate, v.t. [From a Latin verb meaning to make somewhat sour.] To give an acid reaction to; to cause to become to a moderate degree sour or acid in character. acoustics, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning related to hearing.] 1. In physics 3

ACRID 

the science of the nature of, the production, transmission, and effects of those vibrations of matter which are capable of being perceived by the sense of hearing as sounds. 2. In architecture, all those features of a building or a room which affect the transmission of sound within it; the science of designing rooms so that sounds will be distinctly heard in them. 3. In psychology, that branch of the science which deals with sound. It includes the physiology of the organs of hearing, the analysis of the sensations of hearing, and the effect of musical sounds on the hearer. acrid, adj. [From the Latin acer meaning sharp.] Tart, sharp, acid, pungent, burning. adhesion, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to stick to.] The attraction which holds together molecules of different kinds. Examples: paint or varnish to wood; nickle plating to brass or iron; plaster to laths; glue to wood. It is a manifestation of the attraction which exists between all particles of matter in the universe but shows itself only when the bodies attracting each other are minute (molecules) and at minute distances from one another (in contact). adsorb, v.t. [ad-+ the Latin verb meaning to draw in.] To condense or concentrate the molecules of a gas or a dissolved substance by adsorbtion. adsorption, n.

The process by which adhesion causes the particles of a gas or of a dissolved substance to gather on and cling to the surface of a solid. 

It results in the concentration of the gas or of the solute at the surface of the solid. It is by adsorption that the pores of a piece of charcoal become filled with the offensive gaseous products of decay, rendering charcoal an efficient material for purifying the air in a refrigerator. 

It is a physical, not a chemical, process. adulterant, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to adulterate or to counterfeit.] Any foreign substance which renders impure or less valuable by its presence; a substance added to cause deception as to the value, or to change the appearance of a commodity. An impurity naturally present is not an adulterant unless it has purposely been allowed to remain to give increased weight or to render the commodity of greater apparent value. aerate, v.t. [From a Latin verb meaning to air.] To charge a liquid or other substance with air, or especially with carbon dioxide, which was formerly called fixed air. The oxygenation of the blood in the lungs and the charging of drinking water with air by agitating it in the presence of air are both typical cases of aeration in the modern sense of the word. The charging of water to make "soda water" and the charging of dough with carbon dioxide are both cases of aeration in the older sense of the word. 4

AFFINITY 

affinity, n. [From the Latin adjective meaning related to.] 1. In chemistry, chemical attraction; a property possessed by elements which causes them to enter into chemical union if conditions are favorable. This tendency to react chemically is exhibited by the elements in widely different degrees. Oxygen, which has a strong affinity for a wide range of other elements, is, therefore, called active, and is a constituent of many compounds; while helium, showing no affinity for any other element and having no compounds is called inert. The degree of affinity which exists between elements governs the ease with which their union can be brought about and the difficulty with which the molecule of their compound can be broken up after it is once formed. 

2. In biology, similarity between organisms so strong that it is taken as evidence of descent from a common ancestor; the relationship inferred from this similarity. agar or agar-agar, n. [The Ceylonese name of the seaweed which yields the substance.] 

A gelatinous substance extracted from Japanese and East Indian seaweeds, consisting largely of several of the higher carbohydrates and almost completely soluble in hot water. It is widely used as a culture medium in bacteriological work, where it is superior to gelatine because it melts at a considerably higher temperature. Agar is also used to give bulk to foods and as a water carrier in certain intestinal disturbances. It has no food value. air, n. -al, [From the Greek name of the substance.] The mixture of gases which surrounds the earth and forms its atmosphere. It is colorless (hence invisible), tasteless, odorless, perfectly elastic (hence easily compressed or rarefied), easily set in motion, essential to combustion and to animal life (because it contains free oxygen), and to plant life (because it contains carbon dioxide). It is the medium by which sound is usually transmitted. 

Since air is matter, it has weight and is attracted to the earth by gravity. Having weight, it consequently exerts pressure, this pressure being at sea level, 14.7 pounds to the square inch. Since all gases are elastic, its density is greatest at sea level, and decreases rapidly with an increase in altitude. In consequence of this, half of the atmosphere is found within four miles of sea level, as is shown by the height of the barometer column. Ancient philosophers named air as one of the four elements (together with fire, water and earth), and this belief was held until modern chemists identified oxygen as a constituent of air. For the composition of air, see atmosphere. [From the Latin suffix -alis meaning belonging to, or derived from.] 

A suffix used in forming adjectives from nouns. It means pertaining to, derived from or belonging to; as thermal, regal, tidal, etc.

ALBUMEN 

albumen, n. 

[From the Latin adjective meaning white.] 1. The transparent protein 

(white) of the egg. 2. In botany, endosperm. 3. Albumin (q.v.). albumin, n.

In biology, a gelatinous substance, a simple protein, which is found in nearly all animal and in many vegetable tissues. It is the chief constituent of the white of an egg and of blood serum, and is abundant in many seeds. It is soluble in water, and is coagulated by heat, by alcohol, and by other chemical reagents. alchemist, n.

A person who studies or practices the art of alchemy. The name alchemy is said to have come from Al Kamia, an ancient Arabic name for Egypt. The alchemists of the middle ages believed that their art had originated in Egypt. They were searchers for the "Philosopher's 

Stone," which, it was supposed, would change base metals into gold; for the "Elixir of Life"-the mythical universal cure for disease; and for many other fabled secrets. Out of the pseudo-science of alchemy grew the legitimate science of chemistry. alcohol, n. 

1. Commonly grain or ethyl alcohol (C2H5OH), a colorless liquid obtained by distilling fermented fruit juices or liquids containing fermented grain mash or fermented carbohydrates from other sources. 

Ethyl alcohol is present in amounts of less than twelve per cent in all liquors produced by fermentation, and in greater amounts in distilled liquors. Alcohol is made commercially in the United States by the fermentation of glucose obtained by the hydrolysis of corn starch. 

Commercial alcohol is usually a 93 to 96% solution of alcohol in water. 

Absolute alcohol is alcohol from which all but traces of water have been removed. Denatured alcohol is alcohol to which some substance has been added to render it poisonous or unfit for beverage purposes. 

The most common denaturing agents are wood alcohol or methylated spirit (CH3OH), and benzene (CH). Alcohol is widely used in medicine; as a solvent in the industries; and as a base material for the manufacture of many important chemical products. It has a high fuel value and can be used in internal combustion engines in the place of gasolene. 

2. In chemistry, a hydroxyl substituted hydrocarbon; an organic hydroxide; any one of a series of compounds having the general formula (R-OH), where R stands for an alkyl or aryl radical. Alcohols are usually divided into three classes on basis of the type of radical with which the hydroxyl (OH) radical is united: (a) Aliphatic alcohols, in which the -OH radical is united to an alkyl radical, as methyl alcohol (CH3OH) and ethyl alcohol (C2H5OH); (b) The phenols and naphthols, in which the (-OH) radical is united directly to the ring of carbon atoms which forms the nucleus of all aromatic hydrocarbons, as, phenol (C6H5OH); and (c) the aromatic alcohols, in which the (-OH) is united to some part

ALDEHYDE 

of an aromatic hydrocarbon other than the nuclear ring of carbon atoms, as benzyl alcohol, (CH¿CH₂OH). aldehyde, n. [From alcohol dehydrogenatus or dehydrated alcohol.] 1. Any one of a series of compounds having a - CHO group and obtained by the partial oxidation of primary alcohols. The complete oxidation of the alcohol produces an acid. The aldehyde is thus a product intermediate between the alcohol and the acid. Aldehydes are named from the acids produced when they are oxidized. 

2. Commonly acetaldehyde, obtained by the partial oxidation of ethyl alcohol, unless a specific aldehyde is named. alizarin, n.

A red crystalline substance used in the dye industry; a dyestuff giving various shades of red according to the fabric and the mordant used. 

It was formerly obtained from madder, but is now made from anthracene which is obtained from coal tar. alkali, n. 

[From the Arabic alquali meaning ashes of the saltwort plant.] 1. Origin ally a product prepared by treating the ashes of plants with water, and evaporating the liquid thus obtained to dryness; potash, and soda ash. 

2. In the western United States, any soluble mineral matter other than common salt contained in soil or natural waters. 

In chemistry. 3. Any soluble base; as, sodium hydroxide (NaOH), and potassium hydroxide (KOH), called the fixed alkalis; and ammonium hydroxide (NH4OH), called volatile alkali; but including also other hydroxides and carbonates which dissolve in water, producing hydroxyl (-OH) ions. 

4. In a more general sense, any substance which is soluble in water, giving a solution: (a) which will conduct a current of electricity and liberate oxygen at the anode; (b) which has a taste suggesting soap or borax; (c) which will change the color of red litmus to blue; (d) which has the power to neutralize an acid, forming a salt; and (e) which will saponify a fat, forming a soap. Caustic alkalis are those whose solutions. readily attack and decompose organic matter, (e.g.) lime, soda ash, potash, sodium and potassium hydroxides. alkaloid, n.

An organic nitrogenous base occurring in vegetable or animal tissues, in many cases combined with organic acids to form salts. Morphine, found in opium, is a typical alkaloid. allotrope, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning another and noun meaning turn (i.e., form).] In chemistry, one of two or more substances which differ in physical properties but have the same chemical composition. This term is usually applied only to elementary substances, but by extension it is sometimes applied to compounds. Thus carbon in hard, transparent octahedral crystals—the diamond; in soft, opaque hexagonal

ALLOTROPIC 

crystals-graphite; and in soft, opaque amorphous form-soot or lampblack,—are allotropes. The minerals calcite and aragonite are sometimes referred to as allotropes. allotropic, adj. 

In chemistry, of or pertaining to allotropes; exhibiting allotropism as, oxygen and ozone are allotropic. allotropism, n. 

In chemistry, the ability to assume allotropic forms or to exist as allotropes; as, because sulphur possesses allotropism, it takes three formsneedle-shaped monoclinic crystals, rhombic crystals, and an amorphous, plastic form. alloy, n. [From the Latin verb alligare meaning to combine.] A solidified solution of two or more metallic substances. The constituents of a solid alloy may be pure metals, solutions of one or more metals in another, metallic oxides, eutectic mixtures, or definite intermetallic compounds. 

In some cases carbon, sulphur, or phosphorus may also be present but the solid solution is an alloy as long as it retains its metallic character. 

An alloy is described as binary, tertiary, etc., according as it contains two, three, or more main metallic constituents. Alloys of mercury with other metals are called amalgams. (q.v.) alloy, v.t. 

To form or make an alloy. alpha rays.

In physics, streams of positively charged helium atoms which are thrown out in all directions by masses of certain radioactive substances. 

Although the atoms are moving at high velocities, these rays have only a moderate penetrating power, and they may be deflected from their course by a strong electrical or magnetic field. About seven-eighths of the heat which is developed by radioactive substances is due to the alpha rays. alternating current.

In physics, a current of electricity which reverses the direction of its flow at regular intervals. In electrical machinery now in common use these reversals occur from 50 to 100 times per second, the number usually being 60, in city power systems. See dynamo. alternations, n. pl. 

The number of reversals in the direction of flow of an electrical current in a unit of time; as 60 alternations per second. alternator, n.

A type of dynamo that produces electricity which reverses its polarity very rapidly. altitude, n. [From the Latin noun meaning height.] 1. The vertical distance which a body extends upward from some given level used as the starting point,

ALUM 

as from the foundation of a building, ground-level, sea-level, etc. 

The altitude of a pass through a mountain range, of a mountain top, or of a city is measured from sea-level. 

2. In geometry, the perpendicular distance between the base (or the base extended) of a plane figure and its apex, or the perpendicular distance from the base to a parallel line or face. 

3. In astronomy, the elevation of a point or of some celestial body, measured in degrees on a vertical circle, from the horizon toward the zenith. alum, n. 

[From the Latin name of the substance.] 1. The colorless, glassy crystalline hydrated double sulphate of potassium and aluminum (K2SO4 · Al2(SO4)3 24H2O). The compound is also called potash alum, potassium alum and hydrated potassium aluminum sulphate. It has a sweetish sour taste and is readily soluble in water, producing a solution which reddens litmus. Upon exposure to the air the crystals lose part of their water of crystallization (effloresce). In medicine it is used. externally as a styptic (to stanch the flow of blood from wounds) and internally as an astringent (to cause the contraction of tissues). 

While the name alum belongs especially to the substance defined above, it is sometimes applied in medicine and in commerce to another compound, ammonium alum (NH4)2SO4 · Al2(SO4) 3 · 24H2O). These two compounds are widely used in dyeing, calico-printing, tanning, fire proofing, the purification of water and of sewage, paper making, etc. 

The corresponding sodium compound is known, but owing to the difficulty of its preparation and its chemical instability it has no commercial use. Alum is generally understood to be a constituent of one type of baking powder, but the substance used is not an alum. 2. In chemistry, the name of a class of hydrated double sulphates whose crystals have the same form as those of potassium alum, and in which the potassium may be replaced by ammonium or some univalent metal, and the aluminum by chromium, iron, or some other trivalent metal. All alums crystallize with 24 molecules of water of crystallization. aluminum, n. [From alumen the Latin noun for alum, a compound of aluminum.] 

Symbol Al; atomic weight 26.97; specific gravity 2.70; valence 3; melting point 658.7 °C. A silvery white, rather soft metal having a bluish tint. It has a brilliant metallic luster when freshly cut and takes a high polish, but quickly becomes dull in appearance owing to the formation of a coating of the oxide. The metal is very malleable, fairly ductile, and an excellent conductor of both heat and electricity. It is strongly electro-positive, therefore, in contact with most other metals it causes excessive corrosion. Only iron and copper have greater tensile strength. 

Near its melting point aluminum is brittle and can be ground to a powder. Chemically the metal is very active. It dissolves readily in both hydrochloric acid and sodium hydroxide and is attacked slowly by dilute

ALUNDUM 

nitric acid. Because it is light, strong, not easily corroded, and fairly cheap, aluminum is widely used in the industries. Cooking utensils of all types are now made of aluminum and the powdered metal is one of the ingredients of the welding mixture, thermit (q.v.). Alloys of the metal are used in the manufacture of automobile parts, small tools, aluminum paint, and hundreds of similar products. Magnalium, an alloy containing aluminum and magnesium is stronger than aluminum and more easily machined. It also has a very low coefficient of expansion. It is used in the manufacture of air ship girders, scale beams for analytical balances, telescope parts, and parts for other precision instruments. Aluminum bronze, which contains from 92 to 95 % copper and 5 to 8 % of aluminum is a fairly hard alloy, very resistant to wear yet unlike steel, which it greatly resembles in most properties, can be readily cast. It is used in the manufacture of worm gears and other machine parts where resistance to wear and corrosion, and toughness are desirable. This alloy will become of much greater importance in the industries if its present high cost of manufacture can be sufficiently lowered. Duralumin contains about 95 % aluminum. Varying amounts of copper, manganese and magnesium make up the other 5 %. This alloy is white and hard and is used in the manufacture of most of the so called aluminum objects in common use. The most important ore of aluminum is bauxite (Al2O3·3H2O) (q.v.) from which the metal is obtained by electrolysis. Aluminum oxide also occurs in the crystalline form as corundum, varieties of which are emery, ruby, and sapphire, all of which rank near the diamond in hardness. The colors of these crystals are caused by the presence of small amounts of metallic oxides. 

Artificial rubies and sapphires are now readily produced in the laboratory. 

They are equal in every way to the natural gems. Aluminum compounds are numerous and very commonly used as chemical reagents and in many industrial processes. The most important salts of aluminum are the sulphates, and of these the alums are most common. (See alum). 

Potassium alum and aluminum sulphate are both used to produce aluminum hydroxide in the purification of water. Anhydrous sodium aluminum sulphate is extensively used as an ingredient of one type of baking powder. Since sulphates of aluminum are used in the purification of water for drinking purposes, cooking utensils of aluminum are widely used in preparing all kinds of foods, and baking powders as well as other materials used in preparing foods contain aluminum compounds, the possible toxic effect of aluminum compounds becomes a matter of considerable interest. There is no evidence to indicate that aluminum compounds remaining in water or in foods from the above mentioned sources are in any way deleterious to health. alundum, n. 

An artificial crystalline form of aluminum oxide prepared by barely melting powdered aluminum oxide of high purity in an electric furnace. 

The white crystalline mass thus produced is crushed and washed. 

Except for color it has properties practically identical with those of 10

AMALGAM 

emery and is used as an abrasive, as a refractory in the manufacture of crucibles, tubes, and other articles for use in the chemical laboratory, and in the industries. amalgam, n. 

1. An alloy in which one of the constituent metals is mercury. Amalgams of nearly all the metals except platinum and iron are known. 

They are usually made by bringing mercury and the other metals into contact, and are solid or liquid according to the amount of mercury present. Gold, silver, and zinc amalgams have important applications in dentistry. 2. Any soft alloy may be called an amalgam, but not properly. 

3. A mixture of different things which seem to fuse together to form a homogeneous mass. amalgamate, v.t. 

1. To form an amalgam. 2. To bring together into homogeneous form or appearance. amber, n. [From the French noun ambre.] A translucent, yellow fossil resin, found especially in brown coal beds, and on the shores of the Baltic sea. 

It acquires an electric charge by friction, takes a fine polish, and is used for beads and pipe mouthpieces. amino acid. [Amino from ammonia+acid.] In chemistry, an organic acid in which one or more of the non-acid hydrogen atoms have been replaced by the amino group (-NH2). This radical is strongly basic, so that some amino acids actually have a basic reaction. The amino acids are important products formed in the body from proteins which have been taken as food. ammeter, n. [From ampere and meter.] An instrument for measuring the amount of electric current passing through a circuit. The instrument usually registers in amperes, or fractions of an ampere. Some instruments are made to register in thousandths of an ampere (milliamperes). ammonium, n. [From ammonia and -ium, a suffix given to names of metals.] In chemistry, the radical (NH4), which exists only in solution or in combination with some other element or radical. Ammonium has never been isolated but it is believed to exist as an independent ion when amonium salts or ammonium hydroxide go into solution in water. In most of its properties ammonium resembles the monovalent metals sodium and potassium. It always has a valence of one, and its hydroxide is a strong base. Ammonium (NH4-) the radical should not be confused with ammonia (NH3) the gas, from which it is derived. When ammonia (NH3) goes into solution in water it is supposed that a large portion of the molecules dissolve as in the case of any other gas going into solution. 

In this case, however, another action takes place in which the molecule NH3 causes the H and OH ions formed from water to combine with it to form ammonium hydroxide in which the nitrogen atom has a val- 11

AMORPHOUS 

ence of five instead of three as in the gas ammonia. Thus a solution of 

NH3 gas in water contains NH3 molecules in solution as in the case of solutions of gases like N, and O, but it also contains NH₁- ions and -OH ions. amorphous, adj. [From the Greek adjective meaning having no form.] Having no particular form or shape; when applied to solids, noncrystalline; as, amorphous carbon (charcoal, coke, lampblack). amperage n.

The current strength measured in amperes. ampere, n. [After A.M. Ampere, the French electrician.] The unit of rate of flow of electric current (one coulomb per second); the amount of current that flows in a circuit having an electrical pressure of one volt and a resistance of one ohm; a current of such strength as would deposit .001118 gram of silver per second. amphoteric, adj. [From a Greek adjective meaning both of two.] Having the power to act as a base or an acid, said of elements whose hydroxides exhibit both basic and acid properties without undergoing oxidation or reduction in so doing. E.g., aluminum hydroxide will dissolve in hydrochloric acid to give aluminum chloride (AlCl3) and also in sodium hydroxide to give sodium aluminate (NaзAlO3). amplifier, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to enlarge.] 1. That which produces greater intensity or increased size. 2. A lens placed between the objective and the eyepiece of a compound microscope to enlarge the field of vision. amplify, v.t. 

1. To make larger or more intense; to expand. 2. In radio, to increase the intensity of electromagnetic waves, or to increase the loudness of the sound produced. amplitude, n. [From the Latin noun meaning width, size, bulk.] 1. The state or quality of being broad or large. In astronomy. 2. An arc which is measured along the horizon between the true east point and the center of a celestial body at its rising, or between the true west point and such a body at its setting. If measured at its rising the amplitude is eastern or ortive, and if measured at its setting it is western, occiduous, or occasive. It is either northern or southern. Because of the great distances to the fixed stars, the amplitudes of these bodies remain the same throughout the year. When observed from the equator on either of the equinoxes, the sun has an amplitude of 0°, and when observed from the equator on either of the solstices, it has an amplitude of 23-1/2°, its maximum for that locality. North and south of the equator the sun's amplitude varies with the latitude, up to 90° at the arctic 12

AMYL and antarctic circles. The amplitude is the complement of the azimuth, which is the similarly measured distance from the north or south point. 

3. An arc of the horizon measured between the true east or west point and the point where a circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body meets the horizon. 4. The magnetic amplitude is found by using the east or west point as indicated by the compass instead of the true east or west point. The difference between the amplitude thus obtained and the true amplitude as defined above is the variation of the compass needle for that locality. 5. In physics,-applied to the movement of a vibrating or oscillating body, such as a pendulum,the distance from the mean or average position of the body to one of its extreme positions. Less properly, but very commonly, the term is used to signify twice this distance, or the whole distance traveled in one oscillation. amyl, n. [From a Greek noun meaning starch.] In organic chemistry, a monovalent organic radical (С¿H₁₁—), occurring in fusel oil (which consists chiefly of several different isomeric forms of amyl alcohol), in amyl acetate (an ester with the odor of ripe bananas), and other organic compounds. amylopsin, n. [From Greek words meaning having the form of starch.] In physiological chemistry, a digestive ferment or enzyme found in the pancreatic juice which converts the starches into soluble carbohydrates such as dextrine and maltose. anabolism, n. See metabolism. anaesthetic, anesthetic, n. [From the Greek noun meaning stupor or lack of feeling.] In medicine, a substance capable of producing partial or complete tem orary insensibility, especially insensibility to pain. A local anesthetic affects only a limited portion of the organism, while a general anesthetic affects the whole organism. To produce a local effect, cocaine may be applied externally or injected, or the part to be deadened may be frozen by means of a spray of rhigolene or of ether. The commonest general anesthetics are laughing gas (nitrous oxide), chloroform, and ether. analysis, n. 

[From the Greek verb meaning to loosen up or back.] 1. The act or process of resolving a thing-such as an argument, a picture, a machine—into its constituent parts. 2. In biology, an examination of the structural characteristics of a plant or animal, and the determining of its place in a system of classification by the use of an analytic key. 

3. In chemistry, an act or process which identifies one or more of the ingredients of a mixture or a compound, either with or without the physical separation of the ingredients. A proximate analysis identifies the compounds in a mixture or the radicals in a compound, while an ultimate analysis shows how many and what elements are present. An analysis is qualitative if it determines the identity only of the ingredients, 13

ANALYTIC 

and quantitative if it determines also the relative amounts of the ingredients present. On the basis of the method used, a quantitative analysis is gravimetric if the process involves the separation and weighing of the ingredients, or volumetric if it involves the determination of the quantity of any of the constituents or ingredients by measurement of the volume of any liquid reagent used. The processes employed in making chemical analyses include oxidation, reduction, precipitation, electrolysis, blowpipe reactions, flame tests, and, most delicate of all, examinations by means of the spectroscope. 4. A tabulated statement of the results of an analysis as defined above. analytic, analytical, adj.

Of, referring to, or pertaining to analysis; as analytical chemistry, that branch of chemistry which is concerned with analysis, or an analytical table, a table of characteristics of animals, plants or other organisms, arranged so as to make possible the determination of their names, by a process of search and comparison. analyze, analyse, v.t. 

1. To carry out a process of analysis; to resolve into component or constituent parts, quantitatively or qualitatively. 2. To determine the relationship of constituent parts, or contributing factors. 3. In biology, to ascertain the family or species name of an organism by reference to a key. aneroid, adj. [From Greek words meaning not wet or moist.] Not containing a liquid, referring to instruments which do not depend upon any liquid for their operation: as, an aneroid barometer. aneroid barometer.

A form of barometer whose essential part is an air tight and partially exhausted metallic box with rigid bottom and sides, and a thin, flexible, corrugated top. Variations in atmospheric pressure cause slight movements of this top. By a system of mechanical devices these slight movements are amplified and made to turn a pointer which revolves above a face. This face is appropriately graduated so as to indicate atmospheric pressures in terms of millimeters of mercury, corresponding to the readings on a mercurial barometer. Another form of aneroid barometer uses a curved metallic tube instead of the box. Some forms of the aneroid barometer are made to read in terms of the approximate elevation above sea level. angle of incidence.

In physics, the angle which is made by two lines, one representing the path of a ray of light or heat or sound, or of a projectile, and the other being a perpendicular to a surface which the first line encounters, this perpendicular being erected at the point where the first line and the surface meet. The angle of incidence is equal to the angle of reflection. angle of reflection.

In physics, the angle made by two lines, one representing the path taken 14

ANHYDRIDE 

by anything which is reflected from a surface, and the other being a perpendicular to that surface, erected at the point where the first line leaves the reflecting surface. The angle of reflection is equal to the angle of incidence. anhydride, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning without water.] In chemistry, an oxide of an element or of an organic radical, which upon reacting with water will produce an acid or a base. Oxides which on combining with water produce bases are called basic anhydrides; thus, CaO is the anhydride of calcium hydroxide Ca(OH)2, a base. Oxides which on combining with water produce acids are called acid anhydrides; thus, SO, is the anhydride of sulphuric acid (H2SO4). anhydrous, adj. [From the Greek adjective meaning without water.] In chemistry, without water; free from water-especially water of crystallization. Anhydrous liquid ammonia is the pure liquid, absolutely free of all water. 

Anhydrous copper sulphate is the white powder CuSO4, obtained by heating the crystalline CuSO4.5H2O until all the water of crystallization has been driven off. anilin, aniline, n. Amino-benzene; 

[From a Sanskrit adjective meaning dark blue.] 

phenylamine (C6H5NH2). It is an oily, colorless liquid boiling at 

184° C., very slightly heavier than water; slightly soluble in water, but soluble in ether, and alcohol; poisonous in either the liquid form or as a vapor; and showing an alkaline reaction. Upon exposure to the air it first darkens, and then finally becomes a resin-like solid. It is easily made by reducing nitrobenzene (CH¿NO2) by means of nascent hydrogen produced by the action of hydrochloric acid on zinc. It is important in the dye manufacturing industry, since it is the starting material or base for the making of many dyestuffs. anion, n. [From the Greek verb meaning to go up.] The negatively charged ion, which appears at the anode in electrolysis. anneal, v.t. [From an Anglo-Saxon verb meaning to burn, to heat.] 1. To heat or bake an object, such as an earthenware or porcelain dish, to which coloring matter has been applied, for the purpose of making the colors permanent. 2. To heat intensely and then cool an object, in order to reduce the brittleness and hardness of the material and increase its toughness. The heating lessens cohesion and allows a slight rearrangement of the molecules, thus removing internal strains which developed during solidification. In annealing steel, white iron, and glass, the cooling must be prolonged and gradual, while the desired effect on copper and brass is attained by sudden cooling. anode, n. [From Greek words meaning upward path.] The positive pole in an 15

ANTHRACENE 

electric circuit; the terminal by which the current enters an electrolyte; the opposite of cathode. anthracene, n. [From a Greek noun meaning coal.] A pale yellow crystalline compound (C₁4H10), obtained from coal tar, and used as a base material for the manufacture of the important dye alizarin or turkey red. anthracite, n. [From the Greek noun meaning coal.] A kind of jet-black coal, with a luster almost metallic, and with density from 1.57 to 1.67. Because of its hardness, from 2 to 2.5, it is commonly called hard coal, to distinguish it from soft or bituminous coal. It burns with intense heat, but, because most of the substances which would produce gases were driven off while it was being changed from green vegetation to its present form (see coal), it produces only small flames, which are blue. in color. It is mined in large quantities in eastern Pennsylvania, where the mountain ranges were produced by the wrinkling of the earth's crust. The heat and pressure needed to change bituminous deposits to anthracite were furnished by the forces which formed these mountain ranges. The coal of western Pennsylvania, where the folding of the strata has not occurred, is still in the bituminous form. Small amounts of anthracite have been found in the Rocky Mountain region, where intrusions of igneous rock have cut across deposits of bituminous coal and furnished the heat needed for the change of bituminous coal into anthracite. antidote, n. [From Greek words meaning to give against.] A remedy which counteracts or corrects the effect of a poison; as, the antidote for bee-stings (formic acid poisoning) is an alkaline application, such as ammonia. antimony, n. [From a Latin name for the metal.] Symbol Sb (from the 

Latin noun stibium, which was the original name for the element); atomic weight 121.77; valence 3 or 5; specific gravity 6.62; melting point 

630° C. A silvery white, hard, crystalline, brittle solid, obtained by roasting stibnite (Sb2S3), in air and reducing the oxide thus formed with carbon. The metal expands on solidifying, and is therefore used in the manufacture of alloys, such as type metal, which must be cast with sharp detail. Antimony is also used as a constituent of other alloys such as Babbitt's metal, pewter, Britannia metal, hard lead, storage battery grids, and the low melting alloys used in automatic fire extinguishers. The most important salts of antimony are antimony sulphide (Sb2S3), used as a paint pigment, as a filler for rubber and in the manufacture of safety matches; the chloride (SbCl3), an important chemical reagent; and potassium antimony tartrate or tartar emetic (KSBOC4H4O6 H2O), used in the industries and in medicine. antiseptic, n. [From Greek words meaning against putrefaction.] A substance, which when applied to a living body, will destroy bacteria which may be pres- 16

AQUA FORTIS 

ent, with little or no harmful effect on the living tissues. Examples: weak solutions in water of corrosive sublimate and of carbolic acid. aqua fortis. 

An old name for nitric acid (q.v.). aqua regia. [Latin words meaning royal water.] In chemistry, a fuming red liquid made by adding three parts of concentrated hydrochloric acid to one part of concentrated nitric acid. The mixture evolves chlorine according to the equation: 3 HC1+HNO3=NOC1+2 H2O+Cl2. Because of the presence of free chlorine, gold, platinum, and other metals are readily attacked by aqua regia. Some authorities think the nitrosyl chloride (NOCI), causes the attack of the chlorine to take place more readily. aqueous, adj. [From the Latin noun aqua meaning water.] Pertaining to, containing, or made of water; watery; as, an aqueous solution. aqueous tension. 

The vapor pressure of water. See vapor pressure. aqueous pressure. 

The vapor pressure of water. See vapor pressure. arc, n. [From the Latin noun meaning a bow.] 1. Any curved object having the shape of an arch, or of a part of a circle or an ellipse. 2. In astronomy, a part of the apparent path which a heavenly body traces across the sky; as, the sun's diurnal arc-i.e., the apparent path of the sun between sunrise and sunset. 3. In electricity, the body of incandescent vapor which sometimes forms when an air-gap is made in an electric circuit. It bridges the gap and conducts the electricity across it so that the flow of the current is uninterrupted. This vapor is produced from the materials of the conductors at their point of separation, and the conductors are gradually consumed to keep up the supply of this vapor. At the point where the conductors are separated, intense heat is produced, owing to the resistence to the flow of the current offered by the air-gap. The temperature is at least as high as that at which the conducting material boils, and high enough to maintain the incandescence of the vapor. Thus, if the arc is formed between carbon electrodes, as in the ordinary arc lamp, and a direct current is used, the temperature at the positive electrode is about 3600° C., and considerably lower at the negative. In such a lamp the E.M.F. needed to overcome the resistance at the gap is about 40 volts. Of the light produced, about 85% comes from the positive electrode, 10% from the negative electrode, and but 5% from the arc itself. If an alternating current is used instead of a direct current, the temperature of the two electrodes is the same and they produce equal amounts of light. A metallic arc is produced between metal terminals, a voltaic arc between carbon terminals, and a flaming arc between carbon terminals into which have been introduced salts of various metals, such as potassium, calcium, 17

ARGON 

titanium, etc. A lamp which produces a flaming arc gives several times as much light as an ordinary voltaic arc lamp, and most of this light comes from the arc itself, instead of from the terminals. In addition to its use in the production of light, the electric arc is employed in electric furnaces to produce the intense heat needed for the fusing of highly refractory substances. argon, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning lazy.] Symbol A; atomic weight 39.94, valence 0; specific gravity 1.38 (Air=1); melting point -186.1° C. A colorless, inert gas making up about .94 of one percent of the atmosphere by volume. The gas is obtained by fractional distillation of liquid air. 

It is absolutely inactive and is extensively used to fill incandescent electric lamps. armature, n. 

[From the Latin verb meaning to arm.] 1. That part of a dynamo which carries loops of conducting wire through the field of a magnet in such a way that they will cut the lines of force, with the result that a current of electricity is induced in them; or the corresponding part of a motor, through which electrical current is passed in order that electrical energy may be transformed into motion. See dynamo. 2. A piece of soft iron or steel laid across the poles of a horse-shoe magnet when not in use, which prevents its loss of strength; a piece of soft iron, connecting the poles of an electromagnet, which serves as a place to attach loads which are to be carried. aroma, n. 

[From a Greek noun meaning spice.] The fragrance or agreeable odor, especially of spices or drugs, but also commonly applied to foods and chemicals; as the aroma of coffee. aromatic, adj. 

1. Having a spice-like or agreeable odor. 2. In organic chemistry, a descriptive term originally applied to a few sweet-smelling compounds derived from plants, such as oil of wintergreen and other volatile oils, but now extended to all derivatives of benzene (CH), naphthalene (C10H8), or anthracene (C14H10); any hydrocarbon of the closed-chain series; of the carbocyclic series of hydrocarbons. arsenic, n. [From the Greek noun arsenikon, which carried the meaning male, it being thought that the elements were of different sexes.] Symbol As; atomic weight 74.96; valence 3 or 5; specific gravity 4.7 to 5.7; when heated in air it volatilizes without melting. A gray crystalline metalloid obtained by roasting realgar (As2S2), or orpiment (As2S3), in air and reducing the oxide thus obtained with carbon. The most important compound of arsenic is the trioxide. It is the base material for the preparation of compounds of arsenic, which are used extensively in the manufacture of paints, insecticides, and chemicals, and in medicine. 

Practically all compounds of arsenic are poisonous. 18

ARTESIAN WELL 

artesian well. 

1. A well in which the water rises to the top and overflows. This overflow occurs because the bottom of the well extends into a water-bearing stratum of sand or gravel which is enclosed between two impervious layers and is filled with water to a level higher than the top of the well. 

The water enters this porous layer as rainfall or as seepage from streams which flow over its surface where this layer outcrops in a region higher than the one in which the well is bored. In some cases the water travels hundreds of miles underground before reaching its outlet at the well. 

The outflow from an artesian well illustrates the principle that water seeks its level. Such wells were bored in the French province of Artois (ancient name Artesium) as early as the middle of the 18th centuryhence the name. 2. In the United States the name is improperly applied to any deep well, whether its waters overflow or not. It is also improperly applied to deep wells drilled to obtain petroleum, which liquid is sometimes forced to the surface in an entirely different manner. asbestos, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning unquenched.] A name given to any one of a group of silicate minerals, of a fibrous texture and capable of being used in the manufacture of fire resisting materials; more strictly, a fibrous form of hornblende (a double silicate of calcium and magnesium). Practically all forms of asbestos are impure, often containing considerable quantities of iron and aluminum. atmosphere, n. [From the Greek nouns meaning vapor and sphere.] 1. The gaseous layer of matter which surrounds the earth and extends outward from it to a distance variously estimated as 50, 100, 300 miles. It is a mixture, and samples collected in the open fields, where the smoke from cities does not affect it, show the following composition: Oxygen. Nitrogen. 

Water vapor.. 

Carbon dioxide. Argon.. .21 volumes in 100 volumes of dry air 

78 volumes in 100 volumes of dry air varying widely at different times and at different places .3 to 4 volumes in 10,000 volumes of dry air .0. 937 volumes in 100 volumes of dry air 

Helium, krypton, neon, xenon, ammonia, ozone, organic matter.. traces The word atmosphere is generally used when the whole body of the gaseous envelope of the earth is referred to, while the term air is used when referring to the material of which the atmosphere is made up. 

2. A unit of pressure used in speaking of pressures due to causes other than the weight of the air. It is used in measuring the pressure of any vapor (such as steam), gas, or liquid. One atmosphere is the amount of pressure which the atmosphere exerts at sea level when the temperature is 0° C. and the barometer registers 760 mm. This is equivalent to 

14.7 pounds per sqare inch, or 1033 grams per square centimeter. Ex- 19

ATOM amples of its use: The steam boiler carried a pressure of four atmospheres (about 60 pounds to the square inch); the bottom of the tank resisted a water pressure of two atmospheres (about 30 pounds. per square inch). atom, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning indivisible.] In physics and chemistry, one of the still smaller units which make up a molecule; the smallest portion of an elementary substance which can exist either by itself, in combination with others like itself, or in combination with others not like itself. In the light of recent investigations the atom is assumed to be a more or less complex system, consisting of an equal number of protons (particles of positive electricity) and electrons (particles of negative electricity). The central part, or nucleus, contains all the protons and (except in the simplest atom, that of hydrogen, which consists of one proton and one electron) part of the electrons, while the other electrons revolve around the nucleus at various distances from it. 

The atomic weight of an element is approximately the weight of the protons in the nucleus-the weight of the electrons being negligible. 

The most complex atom, that of uranium, has 92 electrons outside the nucleus (planetary electrons), in addition to 146 within it. In chemical reactions only the electrons in the outer shells (planetary electrons) are involved. Those in the nucleus are not in any way affected. When a radio-active change takes place, some of the individual electrons or groups of electrons and protons, in the form of Helium nuclei, leave the the original group, and the remaining members rearrange themselves into a new stable system, thus forming an atom of another element which has a different atomic weight. atomic disintegration.

In physics, the act or process by which the atoms of an elementary substance spontaneously give off rays consisting of material particles or of electrical energy or of ether vibrations, with the result that when the emission of rays ceases, the disintegrated atoms have become atoms of another elementary substance. Thus iomum becomes uranium, then radium, and finally lead. The emission of these rays constitutes radioactivity (q.v.). Elements whose atoms are known to be undergoing disintegration are actinium, ionium, polonium, potassium, radium, rubidium, thorium, and uranium. It is probable that further investigation will lengthen this list. With the exception of potassium all of the elements listed above have high atomic weights. Forty or more products of atomic disintegration are substances which cannot be broken up into simpler substances by any known chemical process. They might therefore be considered elements. Because they disintegrate rapidly-some so rapidly that half the mass is gone within a fraction of a second-they are excluded from the list of elements. atomic number. A number representing the number of the free positive charges of electricity (protons) in the nucleus of the atom of each of the 20

ATOMIC THEORY 

elements and equal to the number of electrons (negative charges) in the shells about the nucleus; a series of values expressed in whole numbers and representing the numerical relation between the wave lengths of the most intense lines in the X-ray spectra of the different elements. The 

English physicist, Moseley, made discoveries in 1912 which led him to assign a number to each of the elements in the periodic system. These numbers, (atomic numbers) indicate the magnitude of the electric charge on the nucleus of each of the atoms. The numbers are significant because the fundamental nature of an element is more closely related to the charge on the nucleus than to its atomic weight. atomic theory.

The theory (q.v.) advanced by John Dalton in 1802, that the smallest particle of an elementary substance which the human senses can perceive is made up of a great number of still more minute bodies, called atoms; that each elementary substance has its own distinctive kind of atom; that all the atoms of one kind are exactly alike in size, weight, and other characteristics; that the atoms remain unchanged in mass when they combine to form the molecules of a compound substance; that atoms always combine so as to form compounds of definite composition (see law of definite proportions); that under varying conditions the same kinds of atom may combine in varying ratios (e.g., to form CO and CO2); and that the atomic weights of the atoms indicate the relative masses of the atoms. atomic weight.

A number which expresses the weight of the atom of a chemical element when 16 is assigned as the weight of the atom of oxygen. Each element has its own specific atomic weight. The absolute weights of the atoms of the elements are unknown, so that for convenience the weight of the atom of hydrogen (the lightest of the elements) was originally selected as a standard and given a value of one. The weights of the atoms of each of the other elements were then determined in terms of the weight of the hydrogen atom. Thus, oxygen was found to have an atomic weight of 15.88, which meant that the atom of oxygen weighed 15.88 times as much as the atom of hydrogen. It was later found that the atomic weights of a larger number of the elements could be directly determined, and that more of them would be whole numbers if the number 16 were assigned to the oxygen atom. The unit of measurement of both atomic and molecular weights is now the one-sixteenth part of the weight of the oxygen atom. Since atomic weights are determined from analytical data, all determinations are subject to error and are never known to be exactly correct. Each year a commission of chemists reviews the experimental work done during the year and publishes what seems to be the most reliable values for the atomic weights of the various elements.
