B barium, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning heavy.] Symbol Ba; atomic weight 

137.37; valence 2; specific gravity 3.78; melting point 850° C. A golden yellow metal slightly harder than lead, which oxidizes very rapidly in air, and decomposes both alcohol and water with ease. The most important source of barium compounds is barite or heavy spar (BaSO4). 

The metal is best prepared by electrolysis of barium chloride in the presence of ammonium chloride. Chemically, barium resembles calcium, except that all soluble barium salts are poisonous. Some of the insoluble salts of the metal such as the chromate and sulphate, are used as paint pigments. Barium nitrate is extensively used in pyrotechnics, as a constituent of green fire. Barium compounds are also widely used as chemical reagents. barometer, n. 

[From Greek nouns meaning weight and measure.] An instrument for measuring the pressure of the atmosphere. It was invented in 1643 at 

Florence by Torricelli. It is used to forecast weather and to determine altitudes. There are two types, the mercurial and the aneroid. The mercurial barometer consists essentially of a glass tube, closed at one end and over 760 mm. (30 inches) long, which is first filled with mercury and then inverted into a dish of mercury. The downward pressure of the atmosphere on the surface of the mercury in the dish, being transmitted equally in all directions through the mercury, becomes an upward pressure within the tube and holds up the column of mercury a distance which varies with the atmospheric pressure. At sea level the height of the column is about 760 mm. (30 inches). When the mercury column grows shorter, spoken of as a falling barometer, it is because atmospheric pressure has grown less, due to either a rise in temperature or an increase in the amount of water vapor present, or both. Since the rising of a column of warm moist air creates a condition likely to bring on a storm, a falling barometer indicates the coming of foul weather, and a rising barometer, fair weather. For moderate distances above sea-level the barometer falls one inch for every 960 feet of ascent, making it possible to measure the height of mountains with moderate accuracy by means of the barometer. The essential feature of the aneroid barometer is an air-tight rigid metallic box partially exhausted of air, having one side thin enough to be flexible, and corrugated so as to render it more flexible. 

The inside air pressure remains constant, and a change in the outside pressure moves the flexible top, and delicate levers multiply this movement and move an arrow, or hand, over a dial. This dial is so marked as to indicate what would be the corresponding change in the height of the column in a mercurial barometer. The aneroid barometer is convenient to carry about, but is less accurate than the mercurial barometer. 24

BAROMETRIC 

barometric, adj.

Pertaining to or indicated by the barometer; as, the barometric reading, usually given in millimeters of mercury but sometimes in inches, and not infrequently (though not properly) in atmospheres, pounds per square inch, or grams per square centimeter. See barometer. base, n. 

1. In chemistry, a compound which will react with an acid, neutralizing it and forming a salt, usually but not always with the elimination of water. Many bases are practically insoluble in water, but if soluble they change the color of certain dyes (called indicators), such as litmus (q.v.), which they change from red to blue. This serves as a test by which bases can be distinguished from acids. Soluble bases have a brackish or a bitter taste and a soapy feeling. The term covers substances whose effects are weak, like common baking soda; or strong, like the alkalies, caustic potash (KOH) and caustic soda (NaOH). See alkali.

Formerly, the oxide of a positive element or radical. 3. Now, the hydroxide of a positive element or radical, or a compound which yields hydroxyl ions (−OH) when dissolved in water. 4. In geometry, that part of a figure, whether a line or a surface, upon which it is supposed to stand. basic, adj. 2. 

Pertaining to, or having the properties of a base; having an alkaline reaction; capable of neutralizing acids. bauxite, n. [Named from Baux, a locality in France where the mineral is found.] 

The most important ore of aluminum, usually a mixture of the hydrated oxides Al2O3 H2O and Al2O3·3H2O, mixed with a small quantity of iron oxide. Bauxite is obtained in commercial quantities in France, British Guiana, Arkansas, and several of the southeastern states. 

Aluminum is obtained from it, by electrolysis, by the Hall process. 

See aluminum. benzene, n. (Also benzol or benzole.) A colorless, volatile, liquid hydrocarbon (C6H6), usually obtained as a byproduct in the manufacture of coal gas from soft coal. Benzene has a freezing point of 5.4° C. and a boiling point of 80.36° C. It has an ether-like odor and burns with a bright, sooty flame. It dissolves fats, resins, sulphur, phosphorus, iodine, and several alkaloids. It is used as an important starting material in the manufacture of many organic compounds, as a solvent, as a substitute for gasolene, and an anti-knock ingredient in motor fuels. benzene, adj.

The name benzol or benzene is used in describing aromatic compounds which are actually or theoretically derived from benzene by substitution. Thus aniline, phenol, salicylic acid, thymol, benzoic acid, and many other compounds which can be made from benzene, are described as benzene derivatives. 25

BENZINE 

benzine, n.

An old name for a high boiling gasolene, distilling between 90° and 

150°F.; not to be confused with benzene (C6H6), (q.v.). benzol, n. 

1. See benzene. 2. Liebig's name for benzene (CH), still retained in common use in the German. beta rays.

In physics, streams of particles of negative electricity (electrons), identical with the cathode rays, which are emitted by certain radioactive substances. They have great penetrating power, a velocity in a vacuum nearly equal to that of light, and they are not easily deflected by an electrical or magnetic field. bicarbonate, n. [The prefix bi which means two or double+carbonate.] A salt of carbonic acid in which only one-half of the hydrogen of the acid has been replaced by a positive element; an acid carbonate; as sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3), and calcium bicarbonate (Ca(HCO3)2). binary, adj. [From a Latin word meaning consisting of two things.] 1. Made up of two elements, or parts; that which consists of two things, figures, or parts. 

2. In chemistry, referring to a compound made up of only two elements; as, sodium chloride (NaCl), or hydrogen sulphide (H2S). The names of binary compounds usually have the ending -id or -ide. binocular, n. [From Latin words meaning having two eyes.] An instrument with which both eyes are used; an opera or field glass, a microscope or a telescope, which provides for vision with both eyes. binocular, adj.

Pertaining to or using both eyes; as, a binocular microscope, a binocular field glass, etc. bismuth, n. [From the German bismuth or Wismuth.] Symbol Bi; atomic weight 

209; valence 3 or 5; specific gravity 9.75; melting point 270°C. A silver gray metal having a peculiar reddish sheen. It is harder than lead and quite brittle when unalloyed. It occurs native, or is obtained by reduction of the ore bismite (Bi₂O3), with charcoal. The metal has both acid and basic properties but usually acts as a weak base. Bismuth is an important constituent of many of the low melting alloys. Its compounds are used in medicine and as chemical reagents. See low melting alloy. bituminous coal.

Commonly called soft coal. A kind of black mineral coal with a somewhat resinous luster, having a density from 1.2 to 1.4, and a hardness from 1.5 to 2. It contains from 50 to 80% of fixed carbon. Because it contains a large percentage of volatile hydrocarbons, it burns with a sooty flame, which is bright yellow in color. See coal. 26

BIVALENT 

bivalent, adj. [From the Latin word meaning twice+valeo, meaning to have value.] 

1. In chemistry, having a valence of two. 2. In biology, representing or composed of material (determiners) from two chromosomes, one from each parent. blowpipe, n.

A tapering tube through which air or another gas may be forced into a flame or fire so as to concentrate or increase the heat produced; a device for directing and controlling a stream of oxygen or air into a burning gas or other substance so as to increase combustion and concentrate the heat produced. The common jeweler's or chemist's blowpipe is usually a tapering brass tube about twelve inches long, through the large end of which air is forced from the mouth into the flame of an alcohol or gas lamp. Combustion is increased and the heat concentrated, so that a very hot blue cone of burning gas can be directed onto a very limited area. Metals may be soldered, ores fused and refractory chemiical compounds oxidized or reduced, by the proper manipulation of the flame from the blowpipe. The oxyhydrogen blowpipe is so constructed that a stream of oxygen under pressure can be directed against a flame of burning hydrogen. To produce complete combustion the ratio of the volumes of the two gases must be kept at two parts of hydrogen to one of oxygen. This is accomplished by forcing the oxygen through a small aperture, which is surrounded by a large tube which delivers the hydrogen. The oxyhydrogen blowpipe is capable of producing a temperature of 2000° to 2500° C. At these temperatures platinum can be melted, quartz fused and worked, and lime made to give the brilliant white "lime light" formerly used in theaters, and now utilized to some extent in the stereopticon lantern. The oxyacetylene blowpipe is constructed in much the same way as the oxyhydrogen blowpipe, except that provision is made for a much larger supply of oxygen. This blowpipe is capable of producing a temperature 500° C. higher than that of the oxyhydrogen blowpipe. It is used as a substitute for the oxyhydrogen blowpipe, and in addition as a tool for cutting iron, steel, and other metals. When used for the last named purpose the flame is directed upon the point where the cut is to be started, until the metal becomes red hot. The acetylene supply is then reduced or entirely cut off, and the jet of oxygen allowed to play against the hot metal. The heat developed by oxidation is sufficient to keep the metal red hot. 

As the oxide of the metal is produced the pressure of the blast forces it away, thus keeping the hot metal exposed to the oxidizing blast. The metal is quickly eaten away by this process, and a clean cut of any desired size or shape can be made. The method is extensively used in cutting steel girders and plates. body, n. 

1. A collection of molecules. 2. A more or less distinct portion of matter, with or without definite bounding surfaces; as, a flame is a 27

BOIL body of burning gas. 3. In geometry, a figure or solid which has length, breadth, and thickness. boil, v.i.

To stir up or agitate (a liquid) by causing bubbles filled with the vapor of that liquid to form within it, to rise to the surface through it, and to burst at its surface. This action is the result of vaporizing part of the liquid, that part usually being the layer touching the bottom of the containing vessel because heat is most effectively applied at that point. 

While the upper layers of the liquid are still comparateively cool, the bubbles may be reduced in size as they rise or may wholly disappear by having their contained vapor condensed. When the whole body of the liquid has been sufficiently heated by this process and through the action of convection currents, the bubbles will reach the surface and the liquid will begin to boil. Since the boiling point of a liquid rises with increased pressure and falls with decreased pressure, boiling may be brought about by either raising the temperature or reducing the pressure. This fact underlies the use of vacuum pans in the manufacture of sugar, condensed milk, salt, etc. This word is applied to the liquid, to a food cooked in boiling water, or to the vessel which contains it: as, the water boils; boiled potatoes; a watched pot never boils. boiling point.

In chemistry and physics, that temperature at which heat overcomes cohesion between the molecules so that the change occurs from the liquid to the gaseous state of matter; that temperature at which the pressure of vapor arising from a liquid just exceeds the opposing atmospheric pressure. As a liquid is heated, the energy it absorbs goes to raise its temperature and to convert a portion of it to vapor. This vapor exerts an upward pressure which increases as the temperature rises. Finally, this pressure just exceeds atmospheric pressure and the boiling point has been reached. At the boiling point the temperature ceases to rise and all the energy imparted goes to change the liquid into vapor and into the mechanical work of pushing back the atmosphere. 

It is, thus, impossible to raise the temperature of a liquid beyond its boiling point under ordinary conditions of pressure. bolometer, n. [From a Greek noun meaning a ray,+meter.] An instrument devised by Langley for the measurement of extremely minute quantities of radiant heat. The instrument is usually employed in connection with the spectroscope. Its action depends on the variation in electrical resistance of a blackened platinum strip when it is affected by radiant energy. boneblack, n.

The black mass of porous impure carbon into which bones are converted by heating strongly in a closed vessel; called also animal black or animal charcoal. It is used in purifying and decolorizing sirups, extracts, etc., and as a black pigment. In decolorizing raw sugar, 28

BORON 

the sirup is heated in the presence of the bone black and then filtered. 

Much of the coloring matter is thus removed. boron, n. [From borax from which many of its compounds are made.] Symbol B; atomic weight 10.82; valence 3; specific gravity 2.45; melting point well above 2000° C. An extremely hard gray metal prepared in an impure state by heating boric oxide (B2O3), with magnesium powder. 

The metal is used as a catalytic agent, in metallurgy, and in the preparation of its compounds. The most important compounds of boron are boric acid (H3BO3), used in medicine; and borax (Na2B4O7) an important cheap alkali used as a flux in welding and soldering, as an ingredient of enamels, as a chemical reagent, as a water softener, and in the manufacture of glass. brass, n. [Of Anglo-Saxon origin.] A very important alloy, consisting essentially of copper and zinc. By varying the proportions in which these ingredients are used and by adding lead, tin, and more rarely iron, widely different grades of brass, having various characteristics which suit them to different uses, are produced. In general it may be said that brass is harder than copper and consequently wears better than that metal; that it is highly tenacious, malleable and ductile, and hence suitable for use where these qualities are desired; far less subject to oxidation than iron; easily turned in a lathe; capable of taking a high polish; and of an attractive golden color. A full yellow variety contains about two parts of copper to one of zinc. A large proportion of zinc gives to the brass a light color, brittleness, and springiness. Increasing the proportion of copper renders the brass reddish in color, soft, and tough. The addition. of tin increases the hardness of the alloy. The addition of from two to four percent of iron renders it very hard and highly tenacious. Cast brass has a specific gravity of 8.3, hardness of 3 by the Mohs scale (see hardness) and a melting point of about 900° C. The ancients made brass before they had any knowledge of the metal zinc as such. Pliny records its use by the Romans. Moses described the Promised Land as "a land out of whose hills thou mayest dig brass." (Deuteronomy 

VIII, 9.) 

British thermal unit. [B.T.U. (American usage). B.Th. U. (English usage.)] A unit of heat, being the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of a pound of pure water one degree Fahrenheit (at temperatures near 39° F.). The 

British thermal unit is the equivalent of .252 Cal. or 252 cal. brittleness, n.

A specific property of some substances which causes objects composed of those substances to be easily broken. Diamond, the hardest known substance, is also exceedingly brittle, but these two properties are not always found in the same substance. 29

BROMIDE 

bromide, n.

A salt of hydrobromic acid (HBr); a compound of bromine with some more electropositive element or radical; as, calcium bromide CaBr2, or ethyl bromide (C2H5Bг). bromine, n. [From the Greek noun bromos meaning stench.] Symbol Br; atomic weight 79.92; valence 1, 3, or 5; specific gravity 3.188; melting point -7.3° C.; boiling point 58.7° C. The only nonmetallic element which is in a liquid state at ordinary temperature and pressure. A dark red fuming liquid which produces very painful and dangerous burns, when brought into contact with the skin. It is a very active element and for this reason is never found free in nature. It is prepared by the action of chlorine on the concentrated solution of sodium and magnesium bromides left after the crystallization of sodium chloride from the brine of salt wells. In its chemical properties bromine resembles both chlorine and iodine, two other members of the halogen family. It is less active than chlorine but more active than iodine. All of these elements are oxidizing agents. Bromine is used in the preparation of bromides and other chemicals, and as an oxidizing agent particularly in the dye industry. It is usually used in the form of bromine water, which is a 4% solution of bromine in water. Bromine is soluble in carbon bisulphide, carbon tetra chloride, and a number of other solvents. bronze, n. 

1. An alloy of copper and tin, to which are frequently added small amounts of other elements, such as zinc and lead. It is hard and durable, golden brown in color, and makes good castings, so that it is a highly suitable material for statuary. It gives out a loud sound when struck, so it is used as the material for bells. As in making brass, the proportions of the ingredients are varied to change the specific properties of the alloy and suit it to its various uses. 2. By extension, alloys similar to bronze; as, aluminum bronze, an alloy which contains from 92 to 95% copper and 5 to 8% of aluminum. This is a fairly hard alloy, very resistant to wear and yet unlike steel, which it greatly resembles in most properties, can be readily cast. It is used in the manufacture of worm gears and other machine parts where resistance to wear and corrosion, and toughness are desirable. This alloy will become of much greater importance in the industries if its present high cost of manufacture can be sufficiently lowered. 

Brownian movement.

The vibratory movement of microscopic particles suspended in a liquid. 

This movement was discovered by Robert Brown in 1827 and is believed to be caused by the blows struck the particles by rapidly moving molecules of the liquid. The erratic irregular movement of suspended particles never ceases and the smaller the particle, the more rapid is the motion observed. 30

BUCKLING 

buckling, n.

A bending or an irregularity in the shape of the plates of a storage cell, generally caused by the too rapid discharge of the battery. buoyancy, n. 

1. In physics, the upward force exerted by a fluid (a liquid or a gas) upon a body which is immersed or floating in it. This force is equal to the weight of the fluid displaced by the body. 2. The lifting power of a body, or of a unit quantity of a substance which is immersed or floating in a given liquid or gas. It is equal to the difference between the weight of the body and the weight of the fluid displaced. The buoyancy of cork just immersed in water may be calculated as follows:-A cubic foot of water weighs 62 pounds while a cubic foot of cork weighs only 15.5 pounds. The difference between the weight of water and the cork is thus 

46.5 pounds per cubic foot. This is the buoyancy of cork in water. In like manner, the buoyancy of hydrogen or helium in air may be computed by calculating the difference between the weight of a given volume of one of these gases and the weight of the same volume of air. burn, v.t. 

[Of Anglo-Saxon origin.] 1. To bring about the rapid oxidation of a substance, with the production of light as well as heat. 2. To bring about any chemical process which produces heat and light; as, to burn antimony in the gas chlorine. burn, v.i.

To undergo any reaction in which light and heat are evolved. by-product, n.

Something of value produced in the course of manufacture of some more important product; any commodity obtained incidently during the production of something of greater value. 31

cadmium, n.

C [From the Greek noun Cadmean.] Symbol Cd; atomic weight 112.41; valence 2; specific gravity 8.642; melting point 321°C. A grayish white metal resembling zinc in appearance but more malleable and softer than zinc. The metal is obtained in small amounts as a by-product in the reduction of zinc ores. Cadmium is used in making colored glass, in the preparation of cadmium salts, and as a constituent of low melting and dental alloys. All of its salts are poisonous. Some cadmium salts are used in medicine, especially in the preparation of antiseptics and lotions for glandular and skin diseases. Cadmium sulphide is an important yellow pigment. caffeine, n. [From the Latin caffea.] An alkaloid (C8H10N4O2), occurring in coffee tea, kola, and other plants. The pure substance may be obtained in the form of long white silky needle-like crystals by extracting a tea or coffee infusion with chloroform. It is soluble in water, fairly soluble in alcohol, insoluble in ether, but easily soluble in chloroform. It is a nerve and heart stimulant and a retarder of tissue waste. It is used in medicine in treatment of heart disease, dropsy, certain forms of headache, and as an antidote for opium poisoning. calcium, n. [From the Latin noun calx meaning limestone.] Symbol Ca; atomic weight 40.07; valence 2; specific gravity 1.544; melting point 805°C. 

A soft silvery white metal, which acquires a yellowish tinge when exposed to the air, due to the formation of a nitride. The metal may be obtained by electrolysis of the fused chloride. The pure metal decomposes water slowly, reacts with acids with great rapidity and burns in oxygen with the evolution of a dazzling white light. The metal has very limited uses. 

Calcium salts, such as the carbonate (CaCO3), the silicate (CaSiO3), and the sulphate (CaSO4) are found in great quantities in nature. The carbonate gives off carbon dioxide when sufficiently heated, and thus leaves the oxide, which is known as quicklime (q. v.). The sulphate occurs as gypsum from which plaster of Paris is obtained by careful heating. (See plaster of Paris). Other important calcium salts are fluorspar (CaF2), phosphorite (Ca3(PO4)2), and calcite, which is a crystallized calcium carbonate (CaCO3). calcium cyanamide. See cyanamide. calibrate, v.t.

To determine the capacity of a container or the quantitative accuracy of an instrument; to correct or standardize the graduation of an instrument, such as a thermometer. calorie, n. [From the Latin noun meaning heat.] 1. A unit of heat, being the amount 32

CALORIMETER 

of heat required to raise the temperature of one gram of pure water from 15° to 16° centigrade; commonly, the one hundredth part of the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of a gram of pure water from 0°C to 100°C. It is the unit in which quantity of heat is measured, just as the degree is the unit in which intensity of heat is measured:-called specifically the small calorie; symbol, cal. 2. Large, or great calorie. A unit used in expressing the heating value of foods, being the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of one kilogram of pure water 1° centigrade; 1000 small calories, or 3.968 B. T. U. Symbol, Cal. calorimeter, n. [From the Latin noun meaning heat+meter.] An instrument used for measuring quantities of heat. A common form of calorimeter is a beaker or cylindrical vessel of metal, which is surrounded by an insulating jacket of wood or other good nonconductor of heat. When a known quantity of water is placed in the metal vessel, and a thermometer is immersed in the water, many approximate thermal measurements can be made; such as the latent heat of fusion of ice, the specific heat of copper, etc. More accurate and elaborate instruments are used in fuel analysis. The commonest type of calorimeter in commercial use is known as the bomb calorimeter. It consists of (1) a closed metallic chamber, called a bomb, into which is put a definite amount of the fuel or other substance whose heat-producing qualities are under investigation; (2) a device, either electric or chemical, for igniting the substance put into the bomb; (3) a larger vessel, also metallic, containing a measured amount of water, in which the bomb is immersed; and (4) a thermometer, placed in the water, by which it is learned what rise in temperature occurs in the water and consequently in the metallic containers. 

The product of the rise in temperature in degrees, the weight of the metal containers, and the specific heat of the metal of which they are made gives the heat absorbed by the calorimeter. The product of the rise in temperature in degrees, the weight of water used, and the specific heat of water, gives the quantity of heat absorbed by the water. The sum of these two amounts, divided by the weight of the substance burned within the bomb, gives the quantity of heat produced by each unit weight of the substance burned. This calorific value is expressed either in calories or British thermal units. candle power.

In photometry, a unit used in measuring the amount of light produced by any source of light. It is the amount of light given out by one standard candle, i. e., a spermaceti candle which burns 120 grains of sperm per hour. caouchouc, n.

An elastic sticky substance (C5Hs), obtained by coagulating the juice (latex) of certain species of tropical plants, especially of the genus. 

Hevea. India rubber; gum elastic; the esential ingredient of the various mixtures which go by the name of rubber. See rubber. 33

CAPACITY 

capacity, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to hold, to contain.] 1. The ability to receive, hold, contain, or absorb; hence, the cubic contents or amount of space contained. 2. In physical geography, the ability of a stream to carry sand, silt and other suspended matter, in terms of unit weight carried unit distance in unit time. In physics. 3. The capacity of a condenser is a constant determined by its ability to permit induction to take place through its mass or body, as compared to a condenser of unit size or mass under similar conditions; the ratio of the charge to the voltage producing it. The maximum possible charge of a condenser depends upon its insulation and the ability of the dielectric between its plates to resist disruptive discharge. 4. The specific inductive capacity of a material is the ratio between the capacity of a condenser when its plates are separated by a layer of the material, and its capacity when its plates (the same distance apart) have only air between them. capillarity, capillary action, n.

A process, resulting from molecular forces, by which a liquid, when in contact with the bounding walls of minute openings in or between solid bodies, is either raised or depressed. It depends upon the relative strength of the attraction exerted between the molecules of the liquid. for each other (cohesion), the attraction exerted between the molecules of the solid and the liquid (adhesion), and also upon a peculiar manifestation of cohesion which is confined to the surface layer of molecules of the liquid (surface tension). When adhesion between the molecules of the liquid and of the solid is stronger than cohesion between the molecules of the liquid, the liquid wets the solid, and the liquid will rise in a capillary. But when cohesion between the molecules of the liquid is stronger than adhesion between the molecules of the liquid and of the solid, the liquid will not wet the solid, and the liquid will be depressed in a capillary opening. The height to which a liquid will rise in capillary tubes is in inverse ratio to the diameters of the tubes. Capillarity draws ink up into a blotter; removes water from the skin and carries it into the towel; raises kerosene from the bowl of the lamp to where it is vaporized by heat and burned; distributes water throughout the curtains of an iceless refrigerator; aids in the rise of sap in plants; and, most important of all, raises water from below the water-table to a point where the roots of plants can reach it. capillary, adj. [From the Latin word capillus, a hair.] Having a very fine hair-like bore; said of tubes of small diameters, as, a capillary tube or the capillary blood vessels. 2. Pertaining to what occurs in very small openings or tubes; as, capillary attraction, capillary repulsion, or capillary action (q. v.). capillary, n. 

1. A tube of small diameter. 2. In anatomy, one of the network of minute 34

CARAT 

blood vessels which connects the smallest veins with the smallest arteries. Some have a diameter but little greater than that of a red corpuscle of the blood. Their walls consist of a single layer of cells, and it is through these thin walls that, by osmosis, oxygen and nutriment pass into the blood and waste matter passes out of it. carat, n. [From the Greek name of a certain bean which was used as a small weight.] 1. The unit by which precious stones, especially diamonds are weighed. Originally the carat was the equivalent of .205 gram, but by international agreement the equivalent of the carat has been .2 gram since 1913. 2. A measure of the proportionate fineness of gold, being one twenty-fourth part. Thus gold which is 18 carats fine is eighteen twentyfourths or 75% gold. carbide, n.

In chemistry, a compound of carbon and some more metallic element; as, calcium carbide (CaC2), and silicon carbide (SiC). carbohydrate, n.

Any member of a group of neutral compounds, mostly of vegetable origin, which contain carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen, the last two being present in the proportions in which they unite to form water. Typical members of the group are the sugar, starch, and cellulose. Green plants produce carbohydrates by the process of photosynthesis, combining carbon dioxide from the air with water obtained from the soil. Man's food consists of three food principles or nutrients-carbohydrates, fats, and proteins—and carbohydrates constitute two-thirds of the weight of these nutrients. Their chief purpose is to furnish energy to the body. carbon, n. [From the Latin noun carbo, meaning a coal.] Symbol C; atomic weight 

12.005; valence 4. One of the non-metallic elements, which can exist in a number of allotropic forms. The diamond and graphite are crystalline forms of carbon, while charcoal, lampblack, coal and coke are impure amorphous forms. See diamond, graphite, lampblack, coal, charcoal, coke.

Carbon is a constituent of an enormous number of different compounds. 

It is present in nearly all compounds composing the tissues of plants and animals and all those classed as organic; in many minerals, such as marble, limestone, and marl; in coral; in crude petroleum; in natural gas; in the carbon dioxide of the atmosphere; and numerous other substances. At ordinary temperatures carbon is comparatively inactive, but at higher temperatures it combines directly with oxygen, hydrogen, the halogens, sulphur, and other active elements. It also combines with iron, aluminum, calcium and other metals to form carbides. Because of the ease with which carbon unites with oxygen at high temperatures, it is an excellent reducing agent. With an excess of oxygen it forms carbon dioxide (CO2), but in a limited supply of oxygen the monoxide (CO) is formed. The affinity of carbon for oxygen is shown not only toward the free element, but also when the oxygen is combined with other elements. 35

CARBONATE 

Thus, many oxides of the metals are reduced and the metal set free by the reducing action of carbon. Typical of these is copper oxide, which is reduced according to the reaction 2 CuO+C=CO₂+2 Cu. Because of its reducing power, carbon in the form of charcoal and coke is extensively used in metallurgy. When certain metals or their oxides are heated with carbon, their carbides are formed. Among the most important of these are: calcium carbide (CaC2) which reacts with water to form acetylene; silicon carbide (SiC), an extremely hard substance sold under the name of carborundum; and iron carbide (Fe3C), also called cementite, which causes the hardness of steel. Carbon is remarkable for the fact that its atoms readily unite with each other in chains and rings to form large and very complex molecules. There are now more than 240, 000 compounds of carbon known. Organic chemistry deals with these compounds. carbonate, n. [From a Latin noun meaning coal.] In chemistry any salt or ester of carbonic acid (H2CO3); as calcium carbonate (CaCO3), the prinicpal constituent of limestone and marble. carbonate, v.t.

To impregnate with carbon dioxide; to convert to a carbonate. carbon bisulfide

A colorless, highly refractive, heavy liquid (CS2), formed when sulphur vapor is brought into contact with carbon at high temperatures. The pure liquid boils at 46°C, and is very inflammable. Its vapors are poisonous. Pure carbon bisulphide is said to have an agreeable odor. The commercial product usually has a very disagreeable odor which is said to be due to impurities. Liquid carbon bisulphide is an important solvent for resins, waxes, and gums, as well as for some inorganic substances such as iodine and bromine. It is also used in exterminating rats and other rodents. The vapors, being heavy, will readily drain to the very bottom of the rat burrows, where they kill the animals almost instantly.

The compound is also known as carbon disulphide. carbon dioxide. [Carbon + di meaning two + oxide.] A colorless, odorless gas (CO2), found in small quantities in air and formed when carbonates are decomposed by acids. It issues from the ground in some localities, and is a product of combustion and decay. It is also exhaled from the lungs of animals. It is produced during the process of fermentation and when lime is made by heating limestone. Carbon dioxide may be considered the anhidride of carbonic acid (H2CO3), and so is sometimes called carbonic acid anhydride or simply carbonic acid. Carbon dioxide gas was formerly thought to be poisonous, or at least to be a narcotic, but it has no such properties. It is a very stable gas, decomposing only at high temperatures in the presence of reducing agents. It will not burn, nor will it support combustion. The gas unites with alkalis to form carbonates. This reaction takes place when mortar 36

CARBONIZATION 

hardens, Ca(OH)2+CO2 = CaCO3+H2O. Carbon dioxide is used in large quantities in carbonating water which is then known as soda water. All effervescing soft drinks contain carbon dioxide. Certain types of fire extinguishers owe their efficiency to the carbon dioxide released when they are brought into action. The gas is produced by the action of sulphuric acid on sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3). Liquid carbon dioxide, obtained by compressing and cooling the gas, has been successfully used in refrigerating machines, and recently solid carbon dioxide has been introduced as a substitute for ice in cooling refrigerator cars, and in packing other shipping containers. carbonization, n. 

1. Charring, converting to charcoal or carbon. 2. In sugar manufacture the precipitation of lime from the plant juice with carbon dioxide (CO2). carbon monoxide. [Carbon+mon meaning one+oxide.] A colorless, odorless gas (CO), formed during the incomplete combustion of carbon and carbon compounds. The gas is almost insoluble in water and is extremely poisonous when inhaled. It is present in the exhaust gases from automobiles, and frequently excapes from stoves and furnaces which are not supplied with a sufficient amount of air. It has often been the cause of death in such cases. The gas has great affinity for the haemoglobin of the blood, with which it unites to form a very stable compound, so that in a very short time after exposure to the gas oxygenation of the blood in the lungs is greatly reduced. Carbon monoxide also has great affinity for oxygen and so is a powerful reducing agent. carbon tetrachloride. [Carbon+tetra meaning four+chloride.] A colorless, a very volatile liquid (CCl4), made by treating carbon bisulfide with chlorine. boils at 77°C. giving off a vapor which is not inflammable and which will not support combustion. The liquid is extensively used as a solvent for oils and fats, and as a fire extinguisher. It is sold under the trade name of pyrene, and a mixture of carbon tetrachloride and gasolene is known as carbona. carborundum, n. [carbon+corundum.] (Silicon carbide, SiC). A beautiful, iridescent crystalline compound of carbon and silicon, made by heating carbon and silica (SiO2), together in an electric furnace. It is harder than any other known substance except the diamond. After the heat of the electric furnace has caused the silicon and the carbon to unite chemically, the resulting mass is cooled, removed from the furnace, crushed, and graded as to size of crystals. These are used as an abrasive, either loose, or glued onto paper or cloth; or they are mixed with clay, molded into wheels or bars, then baked and burned, thus producing abrading tools. casein, n. [From a Latin noun meaning cheese.] The most important protein of milk and the basis of cheese. It is a white substance, soluble in dilute 37

CATALYSER 

acids and alkalies. It is precipitated from milk by rennin, rennet, and by certain acids. catalyser, n. 

A catalyst or catalytic agent. See catalysis. catalysis, n. [From Greek words meaning down and to loosen.] In chemistry, a phenomenon in which the speed of a chemical reaction is altered by the presence of a substance called a catalyst which itself may be recovered unaltered after the reaction. Frequently, very minute amounts of the substance that causes catalysis (the catalyst) may be quite effective in bringing about the reaction. Catalysis is said to be positive if the speed of the reaction is hastened, and negative if the speed is retarded. In some reactions of this kind the catalytic agent seems to react with one or more of the other substances present and form compounds, which later decompose and release the catalyst in its original form. In other reactions the catalyst does not seem to react with any of the substances present. 

In some cases, at least, condensation of the reacting substances takes place on the surface, or within the pores of the catalyst, thus hastening the reaction by virtue of the concentration of the reacting substances thus effected. The presence of manganese dioxide (MnO2) hastens the decomposition of potassium chlorate (KClO3) by heat, and the presence of platinum black causes sulphur dioxide (SO2) to unite with oxygen to form sulphur trioxide (SO3). These are typical cases of positive catalysis. Among the most important catalytic reactions are those in which organic reactions are brought about by the influence of enzymes. 

The hydrolysis of starch by the action of diastase and the inversion of cane sugar by invertase are examples of reactions which are effected at ordinary temperatures by the catalytic action of enzymes, though they can be effected only at higher temperatures when enzymes are not present. catalyst, n. 

A catalyzer; a catalytic agent (q. v.). catalytic agent. 

A substance which causes catalysis. See catalysis. cathode, n. [From the Greek words meaning downward path.] The negative pole of an electrolytic cell or of a battery; that pole of an electric source through which the current leaves the electrolyte; the opposite of anode. In the electrolysis of electrolytes the metals, such as copper or silver, are deposited on the cathode. In quantitative analysis by electrolysis the cathode usually consists of a platinum dish or cylinder. cathode ray.

A ray consisting of a stream of electrons produced by high potential electric discharge in a vacuum tube similar to the x-ray tube, and usually permitted to escape from the tube into the air through a plate or window of thin metallic nickel. In the x-ray tube a stream of electrons 38

CAUSTIC 

leaves the cathode and moves toward the anode at a velocity approaching that of light. Upon striking the metal plate which terminates the anode the electrons give rise to the extremely short penetrating light waves, for which the tube is named. The tube which produces the cathode rays has a cathode similar to that of the x-ray tube, but in place of an anode terminating in a plate upon which the electrons may strike, the anode in the new tube is hollow and is so arranged as to cause the stream of electrons to pass through its hollow center and strike a thin plate of nickel at the farthermost end, where, owing to the thinness of the nickel, many of them pass through and into the air outside the tube. 

Once out of the tube they behave exactly like the electrons which constitute the Beta rays of radium, with which they are identical physically. In the case of the cathode ray, however, the electrons are many hundred times more numerous and they therefore produce effects much more intensely and rapidly than do the Beta rays. The cathode ray or stream of electrons causes the air with which it comes into contact at the end of the tube to become ionized, a crystal of calcite to glow, certain chemical compounds to change from the liquid to the solid state and others to polymerize (to combine several molecules into one larger one without changing the relative amounts of the elements present). caustic, adj. [From the Greek verb meaning to burn.] Having the power to corrode, to burn, or destroy by chemical action: said especially of strong alkalis. caustic, n.

Any substance which, when brought into contact with animal or vegetable tissues, attacks them chemically, destroying them by cor rosion. Examples: 

Examples: caustic alcohol, sodium ethylate (C2H5ONa); caustic potash, potassium hydroxide (KOH); lunar caustic, or silver nitrate (AgNO3); caustic soda or sodium hydroxide (NaOH); caustic lime or calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2). The last is the cheapest. 

The strongest are caustic soda and caustic potash. cellulose, n.

The carbohydrate substance of which plant cell walls are composed. 

It is a colorless, transparent solid, insoluble in water, alcohol, etc., but soluble in sulphuric acid (H2SO4) and a solution of copper sulphate in dilute ammonia. It is a carbohydrate having the same percentage composition as starch (C6H10O5), and convertible into starches or sugars by the action of heat, acids, or alkalis. Cotton, wood, and paper are composed almost wholly of cellulose. centigrade, adj. [From the Latin centum, meaning hundred, and gradus, meaning step or degree.] Divided into one hundred equal parts. Specifically, pertaining to the thermometer which has the space between the two fixed points the melting point of ice and the boiling point of pure water (at 760 mm. barometric pressure) divided into one hundred degrees; as, a temperature of 21° centigrade or 21°C. 39

CENTIMETER 

centimeter, n.

A unit of length in the metric system, equal to one one-hundredth of a meter. It is equivalent to a little more than three-eighths (0.3937) of an inch. centimeter-gram-second (C.G.S.) System. 

A system of weights and measures based upon three arbitrarily chosen units- the centimeter, the unit of length; the gram, the unit of mass; and the second the unit of time. The centimeter is the 100th part of the meter, the standard unit of length of the metric system, and is equal to .3937 inch; the gram is the 1000th part of the kilogram, the standard unit of mass of the metric system, and is equivalent to .0022046 pound avoirdupois; and the second is the 86,400th part of a mean solar day. 

These three are known as the fundamental units. Units of area, obtained by using the centimeter (or its multiples or submultiples) twice as a factor-e.g., square meters, hectares, etc.- are known as derived units. 

Units based upon two of the fundamental units (or their multiples or sub-multiples) - e.g., the unit of work, the erg, and the dyne,— the unit of force, are also derived units of the C. G. S. system. This system has been adopted by the physicists the world over. centrifugal, adj. 

[From center and the Latin verb meaning to flee from.] 1. Moving or tending to move away from the center. 2. In botany, of inflorescence, same as definite. centrifugal force.

The tendency of the matter in a rotating or revolving body to move away from the center around which it is moving. It is improperly named since it is not a force. It is a manifestation of the inertia of moving matter, and when the moving matter is released from the force which keeps it from departing from its circular path, it moves off in a straight line tangent to the circle in which it moved while under two influences. 

Centrifugal force (so-called) is utilized in the Babcock cream tester, cream separator, centrifugal driers in laundries, centrifugal pumps, governors on engines, etc. It is a source of danger in rapidly rotating flywheels, where it may overcome tenacity and cause them to burst apart. centrifuge, n. [From the Latin words meaning center and to fly from.] A centrifugal machine; any machine making use of centrifugal force, so called, or constructed on the principle that a body moving in a curve tends constantly to depart from the curve at a tangent; a machine used to separate substances of different densities, as cream and milk, by whirling. See centrifugal force. centripetal, adj. [From center and the Latin verb meaning to go toward, to seek.] 1. Moving or tending to move toward the center. 2. In botany, of inflorescence, same as indefinite. 40

CHALYBEATE WATER 

chalybeate water. [From the Greek noun meaning steel.] Water carrying one or more compounds of iron in solution. Iron bicarbonate is the salt usually present. char, v.t. [An old English word meaning to turn to coal.] To convert to charcoal; to convert to a residue of carbon by heating; to carbonize. charcoal, n. [char+coal.] An impure form of carbon prepared by heating wood in the absence of air. After the heating process but 15 to 25% of the weight of the wood remains though the shape is retained. Charcoal is a black, lustrous, and rather soft solid. It is insoluble in acids and other liquids, has great power to absorb gases, and burns readily, giving an almost colorless flame. Animal charcoal is made by heating bones in the absence of air. Like wood charcoal it has great absorptive power and is used in decolorizing sugar solutions and other colored liquids. Wood charcoal is used in the reduction of metals, in filtering, and as a fuel. chemical, adj.

Referring to chemistry or the materials or processes of the science. chemical, n. 

A substance used in chemistry or produced by chemical action. chemical affinity.

The attraction strong or weak, which brings about chemical reactions and which keeps compounds, once they are formed, from decomposing. 

The attraction may be said to exist between the ionic or atomic constituents of molecules and not between the molecules themselves. According to a recent theory, chemical affinity is due to the mutual attraction between the nucleus of one atom and one or more electrons belonging wholly or partially to other atoms. The best measures of chemical affinity between two substances are: the free energy change resulting from their interaction and the rate at which they react. Heat, concentration, state, solution, the presence or absence of catalytic agents, sunlight and electricity, are all factors which may affect chemical affinity. chemical change.

A reaction; a change in which one or more substances having new properties are produced; as (a) the breaking up of a compound into simpler compounds or into elementary substances; (b) the uniting of two elements to form a compound; (c) the interaction of a base and an acid to form a salt; (d) the action of a metal on an acid to release hydrogen and to form a salt. Chemical changes are accompanied by energy changes, heat being absorbed or evolved in most cases. chemist, n.

One who is learned in chemistry or is qualified to practice chemistry. chemistry, n. [From Arabian words meaning to pour plant juices.] That branch of science which deals with the various forms of matter, and their trans- 41

CHEMISTRY 

formation into other forms and combinations. Chemistry is an outgrowth of the pseudo science of alchemy which had its origin hundreds of years before the Christain era. The earliest chemists probably extracted plant juices for medical purposes. The making of dyes, perfumes, essential oils, embalming compounds, paints, and the metallurgy of the common metals represents processes almost as ancient as the extraction of medicinal juices. Theoretical chemistry began when Greek learning was at its height. A theory of an "all pervading primal stuff", resembles the modern theory of the "ether" which is supposed to pervade all space. Another theory which supposed that various kinds of "particles" made up all substances, strongly resembles the Dalton atomic theory. 

The idea of elements was well advanced in Greek times but mistakes were made when water and air were considered elements. The causes of chemical change were not well understood. Four cardinal properties, hot, dry, cold, and wet, were given great prominence. In medieval times, the works of Aristotle and other ancients were accepted as authoritative and the alchemists spent much time and study in their searches for the so-called Philosophers Stone, which the ancients had supposed to have the power to restore youth and assist in the transmutation of baser metals into gold. In the course of their researches, the alchemists discovered many important chemical processes and added much to the store of chemical knowledge. They also helped give the inductive method of laboratory research a place in the respect of the men of science of that day. The beginning of modern chemistry and the emergence of the real science of chemistry out of the mixture of superstition and real knowledge which made up the pseudo science of alchemy, dates from the introduction of actual laboratory investigation, which began in the early part of the 18th century. After this, less and less consideration was given to the works of the ancient writers and more attention was given to the results of laboratory experiment. The real beginning of modern chemistry, dates from the origin of the atomic theory, the discovery of the true nature of oxygen and water, and the introduction of the analytical balance. All these innovations came in the last half of the 18th and the first few years of the 19th century. The middle of the 19th century was marked by the rise of organic chemistry, and the last half of it by the discoveries which gave rise to the electron theory. The early twentieth century has already seen the establishment of the new theory of atomic structures and the rise of a new chemistry based on a new knowledge of atomic energy with a new hope of the atom as a never ending source of power for the use of man. 

The scope of chemistry is very broad and many subdivisions of the science have been made. The chemistry of a century ago was divided into inorganic (the chemistry of substances of the mineral world), and organic (the chemistry of substances produced by organisms.) These two names are still used though the meaning of each word has been somewhat changed. Inorganic chemistry is now the chemistry of all the elements and their compounds except carbon, and 42

CHLOR

organic chemistry is the chemistry of the carbon compounds. Synthetic chemistry is a type of chemistry in which compounds and other chemical substances are built up from simpler or less expensive substances, while analytical chemistry has to do with the breaking down of various substances to determine the constituents of which they are composed (qualitative analysis), and sometimes the amounts of the various constituents (quantitative analysis). Physical chemistry deals with the various theories and hypothesis, by which chemists seek to systematically arrange all the known facts in their best possible order. There are many other subdivisions of chemistry but those given above are the largest and best known. chlor- chloro- Combining form from a Greek adjective meaning greenish yellow. chloride, n. [chlor-+-ide.] A compound which has been, or theoretically could be, formed by the replacement of the hydrogen of hydrochloric acid by another element or radical; a salt or ester of hydrochloric acid; as, sodium chloride (NaCl), or ethyl chloride (C2HCl). chlorine, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning greenish-yellow.] Symbol Cl; atomic weight 35.45; valence 1, 3, 5, and 7; density 2.49 (air = 1), boiling point -33.6°C. A greenish-yellow very active gaseous element of the halogen family. The element never occurs free in nature, but is found in great quantities combined with sodium, potassium and other basic elements. 

The most abundant mineral is common salt (NaCl). The element was discovered in 1774 by the Swedish chemist Scheele who prepared it by causing hydrochloric acid to act on manganese dioxide. Chlorine is still prepared by this method, though commercially, it is usually made by the electrolysis of strong solutions of common salt. The gas has a sharp acrid odor, produces a very disagreeable irritating effect upon the eyes and the lining of the nose and throat, and will cause death if inhaled in any quantity. It unites readily with many metals, attacking sodium, powdered antimony, and finely divided copper with great rapidity and with the evolution of heat and light. The gas is a powerful bleaching agent, its bleaching action being due to its power to release oxygen from water. 

Chlorine was the first poisonous gas used in warfare, and is still used in the manufacture of war gases. Large quantities of chlorine are used in the sterilization of water for drinking purposes and in bleaching. It is also extensively used in the manufacture of bleaching powder, potassium chlorate, carbon tetrachloride, and many other important chemical compounds. The most important chlorine compound manufactured is hydrochloric acid (q.v.). chloroform, n.

A clear, colorless, highly refractive, non-inflammable, volatile liquid (CHCl3). It is used as a solvent, and in medicine as an anaesthetic. It is made by cautiously distilling alcohol or acetone with bleaching powder. 43

CHLOROPHYLL 

chlorophyll, n. [From Greek words meaning greenish yellow and leaf.] The substance which gives the green color to plants. It is a complex and unstable compound containing nitrogen and iron, and the chloroplasts develop it only in cells which are exposed to light. It may be removed from the tissues which contain it by soaking them in alcohol, ether, or certain oils, and the solutions thus obtained are fluorescent. It is seldom the only coloring matter present in the chloroplasts, its presence often being hidden by a greater amount of other pigments, as in the case of the red algae. The carbohydrate plant-foods cannot be formed -i.e., photosynthesis cannot occur- unless chlorophyll be present, and therefore, green plants die of starvation when deprived of light. Plants which are naturally without chlorophyll are of necessity either parasites or saprophytes. chloropicrin, n.

Nitrotrichlormethane or nitro-chloroform (CC13NO2). A heavy colorless liquid whose vapor was much used as a military poison gas in the World War. The liquid boils at 112.4°C and has a specific gravity of 

1.654 at 20°C. It has an extremely bad odor and its vapors are extremely irritating to the eyes and to mucous membranes. The compound has been used successfully as a fumigant and its vapor is known to be highly toxic to insects, bacteria, rodents and to certain types of fungi. chrom- chroma- chromo- Combining form from the Greek noun meaning color. Often refers to chromium. chromate, n. 

A salt of chromic acid. chrome, n. 

See chromium. chromic, adj.

Pertaining to, containing, or obtained from chromium in its higher valences. chromium, n. [From the Greek noun meaning color.] Symbol Cr; atomic weight 52.01; valence 2, 3, and 6; specific gravity 6.92; melting point 1520°C. steel-gray, hard and brittle metallic element. The most important ore is chromite (Cr2O3FeO) or (Fe(CrO2)2), from which the metal is obtained by reduction with metallic aluminum, by the Goldschmidt process. 

The metal can be electroplated on iron or brass, giving a smooth adherent coat which is highly resistant to atmospheric corrosion. Large amounts of chromium are used in the production of alloys. Chromium steels are hard, strong, and very tough. Armor piercing projectiles, safes, stainless steel, auto parts, acid proof steel, stellite (an extremely hard 44

alloy), and many other important ferrous alloys contain chromium. "Nichrome," "chromel," and many other alloys used in the electrical industries for resistance conductors are chromium alloys. Practically all chromium salts are bright colored. Some of them make excellent paint pigments, as lead chromate (PbCrO4), a yellow pigment. Chrome alum (Cr2(SO4)3, K2SO 4,24H2O), is an important salt in the tanning industry. chromous, adj.

Pertaining to, containing, or obtained from chromium in its lower valences. circle, n. [From the Greek noun meaning a circle, a ring.] 1. A uniformily curved line, closed, every point of which is equidistant from a point within, called the center. 2. A plane figure bounded by such a line. A great circle of a sphere is one drawn on its surface in a plane which passes through the center of the sphere; as on the earth, the equator and the meridians. clockwise, adj. and adv.

In the direction taken by the hands of a clock which is observed from in front; used in describing the motion of rotating bodies. co-, col-, com-, con- Prefixes of Latin origin meaning together, with, in association. coagulate, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to make curdle.] To form into a compact or dense mass; to solidify; to curdle; to clot; as, heat coagulates the white of an egg, and acids coagulate the casein of milk. coagulation, n. [From a Latin noun of the same meaning.] The act or process of solidification of a gelatinous mass from solution in a liquid; the change produced by coagulating; the mass of solid or semi-solid matter which results when a liquid is coagulated; a clot. Coagulation is not the result of the removal of a solvent by evaporation, but is a chemical change by which a soluble substance (usually albuminous) becomes insoluble. coal, n. [A name of Anglo-Saxon origin.] A brittle, black or brown, substance, with hardness varying from 2.5 to 5, with density varying from 1.2 to 

1.8, valuable as fuel. Mineral coal is a mixture of hydrocarbons; compounds of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen; moisture; non-combustible earthy solids which remain after burning as ash; together with amorphous carbon, traces of nitrogen and, in some varieties, as much as 3% of sulphur. It had its origin in beds of vegetable matter which hundreds of thousands of years ago, fell into water and were thus preserved from decay. Similar but more recent deposits of undecayed vegetable matter exist today as peat beds. After being buried under strata of sedimentary 45

COAL TAR 

deposits this vegetable matter gradually lost some of its volatile parts, so that the original amount of carbon became an increasing percentage of the whole substance. Peat with about 60% of carbon was changed to lignite with as much as 65% of carbon; then to bituminous coal with about 80% of carbon; and finally to anthracite, with from 85 to 90 or more % of carbon. A still further change would produce graphite (q.v.). See bituminous coal and anthracite. coal tar.

A heavy black viscous mixture obtained during the destructive distillation of bituminous coal. In the manufacture of coal gas, coal tar is obtained from the hydraulic main and from the "tar well" which receives the liquids dropped by the condensers. The tar is washed with water to free it from ammonia and other water soluble compounds and then distilled. The distillate is collected in separate fractions, the temperature being carried up to about 300° C. About half of the original mass remains as a pitch-like residue which is used as a black varnish for metals, in the manufacture of roof paint and as a paving mixture. The distillate yields three classes of substances: compounds soluble in acids, compounds soluble in alkalis and compounds not affected by dilute alkalis or acids. By successive treatment with sodium hydroxide and sulphuric acid the three types of substances are separated. By fractional distillation and other treatments each type is further separated into its components. The most important compounds obtained from coal tar are: naphthalene 6 to 10 percent (of the weight of the tar), mixed cresols 2 to 3 percent, benzene and toluene 1 to 1.5 percent, phenol 4 to 5 percent and anthracene .25 to 45 percent. Smaller amounts of pyridine, xylene and other compounds are also obtained. 

Many of these compounds are the base materials from which many important dyes (the coal tar dyes), and equally important medicinal preparations are made. In addition some of them have very important industrial uses. cobalt, n. [From the German noun Kobold meaning a goblin or evasive spirit, because of the difficulties met by the early chemists in separating the element from its ores.] Symbol Co; atomic weight 58.94; valence 2 and 3; specific gravity 8.96; melting point 1480° C. A malleable, magnetic, silvery metal which takes on a reddish tint on exposure to the air. The metal resists atmospheric corrosion even better than nickel, which it closely resembles in both physical and chemical properties. 

The chief ores of cobalt are found with nickel ores in Canada, New Caledonia, and, more recently, in the United States. The metal is obtained by reducing the oxide with carbon, or with aluminum by the Goldschmidt process. (q.v.). Cobalt is used in the production of special steels, such as cutlery steel; in the manufacture of cobalt salts; in metallurgy; and, more recently, as an electroplated covering metal for expensive signs. Like nickel, cobalt forms two series of salts. These 46

CCCAINE 

in the anhydrous form are usually blue, in solution usually rose red. 

Cobalt oxide is widely used as a blue pigment in coloring glass, glazes and enamels. The salts are used chiefly as chemical reagents. cocaine, n.

A poisonous, habit-forming drug (C17H21NO); a bitter, colorless, crystalline alkaloid obtained from coca leaves and formerly much used in medicine, and in dentistry as a local anaesthetic. cohesion, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to stick together.] The attraction which holds together molecules of the same kind. Cohesion acts only through distances too small to be observed by human senses, or when the attracting minute bodies seem to be in contact. If it is strong enough, the molecules maintain their relative positions, and the substance is a solid. If it is weaker, the molecules slide over one another but resist separation, and the substance is a liquid. If it is absent, the molecules tend to separate themselves indefinitely, and the substance is a gas. 

Heat weakens cohesion and causes solids to become liquids, and enough heat destroys it and changes liquids into the gaseous state of matter. 

It is also the basis of numerous specific properties of substances, such as viscosity, tenacity, etc. cohesive, adj.

Sticking together and resisting separation; possessing cohesion; producing cohesion. coke, n.

The material remaining after the volatile constituents of coal have been driven off by heat; the fixed carbon and ash of coal fused together into an amorphous porous mass. The process of producing coke is carried out in a closed retort so that air is excluded and the carbon has no chance to burn. Coke is the most important by-product of the manufacture of coal gas. coke, v.t. 

To convert (coal) into coke. collodion, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning like glue, viscous.] A pale yellow, sirupy, very inflammable liquid formed by dissolving nitrated cellulose in a mixture of ether and alcohol. This quickly solidifies into a transparent glass-like mass, upon the evaporation of the solvent. It is used in the manufacture of photographic films; in cementing; in coating wounds and abrasions; it is made as one of the intermediate steps in the manufacture of one type of artificial silk. In the manufacture of collodion, cotton is treated with a mixture of concentrated nitric and sulphuric acids. The tri-nitrate of cellulose thus produced is, then dissolved in a mixture of equal parts of alcohol and ether. colloid, n. [From a Greek noun meaning glue.] A jelly-like or glue-like substance permeable by crystalloid substances, but not diffusible through animal 47

COLLOIDAL 

or vegetable membranes. (Some colloids however, seem to be slightly diffusible through certain membranes). Glues, pastes, and jellies are colloids or colloidal solutions. They have no definite solubility and do not crystallize from concentrated solutions. As water is withdrawn the solution simply becomes more and more viscous until it is finally solid. When suspended particles are so fine as to remain suspended in a liquid indefinitely, the mixture is called a colloidal suspension. Finer particles suspended in the liquid might be called a colloidal solution, while a true solution might be conceived of as still finer particles held between the molecules of a liquid. A colloidal solution is thus on the boundary line between a suspension and a true solution. It is supposed that the finely divided particles carry similar electrical charges, and so repel each other. This repulsion prevents them from uniting to form larger particles which would precipitate, and so they remain suspended indefinitely. Such a suspension is sometimes referred to as a sol. If an electrolyte be added to such a solution the presence of numerous charged ions tends to reduce the repulsion. An oppositely charged ion is attracted by and bound to the charged particle, which thus becomes neutral. Many neutral particles unite to form a precipitate. The amorphous precipitate thus formed is sometimes referred to as a gel. 

If a solution of ferric chloride in distilled water be treated with small amounts of ammonium hydroxide, a brown coloration takes place, but no ferric hydroxide separates out. This is a colloidal suspension of extremely finely divided particles of iron hydroxide, which will pass through the filter unchanged. If ammonium chloride be added and the solution boiled, the colloidal suspension is destroyed and the ferric hydroxide precipitates. Numerous colloids and colloidal suspensions play a part in the digestion of foods in plants and animals. colloidal, adj.

Like, pertaining to, or consisting of a colloid; containing a colloid in solution or suspension. combining number. 

See equivalent weight. combining weight. 

See equivalent weight. combustible, adj. [From the Latin verb meaning to burn up.] Capable of being burned; easily set on fire; inflammable. combustible, n. 

Anything which will burn. combustion, n.

Rapid oxidation, producing light and intense heat. Also applied to slower oxidation, such as that which occurs in the bodies of animals. conductivity, n.

The ability of a body or a substance to transmit light, heat, electricity, 48

sound, or any other form of energy;-used to indicate the power or quality of any solid, liquid, or gas which permits the transfer of energy in any form through its mass; as, the electrical conductivity of copper, or the thermal conductivity of aluminum. conductor, n.
