COPPER 

copper, n. [Through the German Kupfer, from the Latin cuprum, a word derived from Cyprium, an old name for the island of Cyprus where copper was mined in ancient times.] Symbol Cu (from the Latin noun cuprum); atomic weight 63.57; valence 1 and 2; specific gravity 8.93; melting point 1083° C; boiling point 2310° C. A reddish brown metallic element occurring both free and combined in nature and known and used by man from earliest times. Large deposits of free copper are found on the south shore of Lake Superior. The ores chalcocite or copper sulphide (Cu₂S), chalcopyrite or copper pyrites the double sulphide of copper and iron (CuFeS2), are found in Montana and Utah and malachite a basic carbonate (Cu)(OH)2CO3) occurs in Arizona, New Mexico and California. Some native copper and considerable quantities of copper ore are also found in Alaska. Where copper occurs uncombined, the ore is usually crushed and the rock particles removed by washing, after which the metal is melted and cast into sheets which are purified by electrolysis. Sulphide ores are roasted to remove much of the sulphur, and to convert the copper and iron compounds to oxides of these metals. The mixture is then reduced in a blast furnace by means of coal or coke. The "copper matte" thus produced (containing 40 to 

50% copper) is then "bessemerized" in a specially designed "converter." 

In this process a suitable flux is added and air is blown through the molten metal until practically all metals other than copper are oxidized and the slag which is formed by combination of their oxides and the flux, is skimmed from the top of the molten copper. The metal, then more than 

97% pure and known as "blister copper," is cast into anode plates for further purification by electrolysis. In this process, plates of pure copper are used as cathode plates, and thick plates of impure copper are made the anode plates. The electrolyte is a dilute sulphuric acid solution. As the current is passed, the pure copper is transferred from the anode to the cathode plates, all impurities falling to the bottom of the cell or going into solution in the electrolyte. Copper ranks next to silver in ability to conduct heat and electricity. It is malleable and ductile. The pure metal is not affected by dilute hydrochloric or sulphuric acids when air is excluded, but readily dissolves in concentrated sulphuric acid and in either dilute or concentrated nitric acid. Copper, when exposed to air, acquires a thin coat of the oxide which protects it from further corrosion. Because of this fact, copper is widely used as a roofing material for public buildings. About onethird of all the copper produced is used in the electrical industries. 

Since the electrical conductivity of the metal is greatly reduced by even traces of other metals, only electrolytic copper can be used. Pure copper is also employed in the manufacture of kettles for cooking purposes, as sheathing for the bottoms of ships, and for boilers and kettles for use in many of the industries. Alloys of copper with other metals are of great importance. Brass, an alloy of copper and zinc, contains from 18 to 40 percent zinc. It is harder but melts at a lower 50

COPRA temperature than copper. Bronze, an alloy of copper and tin, has been. prepared by man for thousands of years. It usually contains some zinc and lead which are added to make the metal softer and more fusible. 

Alloys of copper with many other metals are also used for special purposes. Copper forms two series of salts. The cuprous series, in which copper acts with a valence of one, is the least important. Copper sulphate (CuSO, 5H2O) also called blue vitriol and "blue stone" is the most important salt of the cupric series. It is widely used in the manufacture of batteries (gravity cells), as a mordant in dyeing, in electroplating, as a fungicide (q.v.)., in the manufacture of pigments, and as a chemical reagent. Many other compounds of copper have more limited, but quite important uses. Practically all salts of copper are poisonous. copra, n. [Malayan koppara, or Hindustani khopra]. Coconut meat which has. been dried for shipment to industrial centers where the oil is expressed from it for use in the manufacture of foods, confectionery, soaps, etc. 

It is a large item of import into this country from the South Sea Islands and the East Indies. coral, adj.

Consisting of, made of, or resembling coral; having the pinkish-red color of the ornamental commercial coral. coral, n. [From the Greek name for the same substance.] A mass of calcium carbonate which is secreted by the polyp of several Actinozoa and a few Hydrozoa, and deposited in layers beneath its base, serving the polyp as a place of permanent attachment. The minute polyps, growing in colonies which are often made up of countless individuals, have thus built up calcareous rock deposits of enormous size, such as the Great Barrier Reef which extends for a thousand miles along the coast of Australia. Coral polyps can live only in water whose temperature does not fall below 68° F., therefore coral deposits are now being laid down only in the tropical parts of the ocean, although fossil coral is found as far north as Chicago and Syracuse, N. Y. By food supply requirements coral polyps are limited to the surface layer of the ocean between low tide and 100 feet in depth, but coral has been brought up by dredges from much greater depths. Aside from the reef building corals there are other kinds which are interesting because of structure or color, such as the Mediterranean red coral which is made into beads, jewelry, and other ornaments. By substitution and without loss of its structural characteristics, the calcium carbonate of coral sometimes becomes magnesium carbonate or calcium phosphate. cordite, n.

A British name for a special type of nitrocellulose high explosive. It contains 65% of nitrocellulose, 30% of nitroglycerine, and 5% of vaseline. It is a safer explosive than most nitrocellulose powders, but has a serious corrosive effect on the gun in which it is used. 51

CORRODE 

corrode, v. [From the Latin verb meaning to gnaw.] To destroy by slow deleterious chemical action; to eat away little by little; to disintegrate by slow action of the elements as in the corrosion of copper which is exposed to the air. corrosion, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to gnaw.] The gradual destruction or disintegration effect at the surface of an object by chemical action; as, the corrosion of water pipes, the corrosion of a copper kettle. The corrosion of metals is frequently accelerated by galvanic action. The rusting of iron and the destruction of tin plate are thought to be cases of this kind. Structural iron and tin roofs have to be kept well painted to prevent corrosion. corrosive, adj. 

Said of that which is capable of destroying, as by an acid or an alkali. corrosive, n. 

Any substance which causes corrosion; that which corrodes. corrosive sublimate, n. 

Mercuric chloride (HgCl2), also called bichloride of mercury. A white crystalline, very poisonous solid, used as an antiseptic, in tanning, wood preservation, embalming, and textile printing. In most states this compound must be stored and dispensed in special containers, usually triangular or coffin-shaped bottles. Often tablets of mercuric chloride are colored or otherwise marked to give warning of its poisonous nature. This substance should be clearly distinguished from the monochloride of mercury, or calomel, which is not poisonous. corundum, n. 

1. A mineral form of crystallized aluminum oxide (Al2O3), with small amounts of silica (SiO2), water (H2O), and ferric oxide (Fe2O3) as impurities. The clear or colored varieties of corundum form the gems sapphire, ruby, oriental emerald, and oriental topaz. The granular corundum, containing small amounts of hematite (Fe2O3) and magnetite (Fe3O4), is known as emery. Corundum is the hardest of all minerals, except the diamond. It is rated on the Mohs scale as having a hardness of 9. counter-clockwise, adj.

Opposite to the direction in which the hands of a clock move,-used to indicate the direction of rotation or revolution. critical pressure.

In physics and physical chemistry, the pressure required to liquefy a pure gas at its critical temperature (q.v.). Each gas has its own specific critical pressure. That of oxygen is 50 atmospheres, and its critical temperature is -118° C. Therefore, at 50 atmospheres pressure and at 118° C. oxygen liquefies. At temperatures below 118° C. correspondingly lower pressures liquefy oxygen. The same rule applies to all gases. Theoretically, before the temperature falls to -273° C. 

52

CRITICAL TEMPERATURE 

all gases would be liquefied at atmospheric pressure, and at -273° C. all gases would have changed to the solid state. critical temperature.

In physics and physical chemistry. That temperature specific for each pure gas, above which no amount of pressure will convert the gas to a liquid; the highest temperature at which a given gas may be liquefied by subjecting it to pressure. Every gas may be liquefied if sufficiently cooled and at the same time subjected to sufficient pressure. 

Each gas has its specific critical temperature at which it can be liquefied by a given pressure called its critical pressure (q.v.), and below which it can be liquefied by less pressure, the pressure required decreasing as the temperature is lowered. Thus, nitrogen cannot be liquefied above -146° C., so this temperature is its critical temperature. The critical temperature of oxygen is 118° C., of hydrogen -234° C., and of water 374° C. crucible, n. 

[From the Latin noun meaning a hanging lamp or earthen pot.] 1. A pot of metal, fire clay, graphite, porcelain, or other refractory material for melting or calcining metals, ores, minerals, etc. 2. A hollow space beneath a furnace in which to receive the molten metal, or other product of the furnace. cryolite, n.

A white lustrous mineral (Na,AlF6) found chiefly in Greenland and used in the preparation of aluminum, alum, and some types of glass. This mineral is of great importance in the production of aluminum, since when molten it acts as an excellent solvent for the aluminum oxide from which metallic aluminum is obtained by. electrolysis, by the process invented by C. M. Hall, and now widely used for the production of this important metal. crystal, n. [From the Latin noun meaning ice, crystal.] In chemistry and mineralogy, a symmetrical solid body of uniform composition whose molecules, under the influence of molecular attraction, arranged themselves according to a definite system while solidification was taking place, with the result that the body is bounded by plane surfaces which meet to form angles of very definite dimensions. A crystal may consist of an elementary substance, a compound, or a mixture of substances belonging to these classes if they crystallize in the same form. 

The formation of crystals may be brought about by fusing a crystalline substance, then cooling it; by dissolving a crystalline substance and then evaporating the solvent; or by cooling the vapor of a substance which sublimes. Sulphur, alum, and iodine are substances suitable for treatment in these three ways. Crystals may grow indefinitely by the addition of new layers to the surfaces already formed. crystalline, adj.

Formed by the process of crystallization; having the nature of a crystal, especially the molecular structure; the opposite of amorphous. 53

CRYSTALLIZATION 

crystallization, n.

The act or process of causing the formation of crystals, or the product of such an act or process; the evaporation or removal of the liquid solvent after saturation is reached so that the solute (dissolved substance) settles out in solid form. The process of crystallization is frequently used as a means of purifying chemical substances. The first crystals which form are nearly chemically pure, the impurities. originally present being left in the mother liquor. By redissolving the crystals and recrystallizing from the new solution, a much purer form of the substance is obtained. crystallize, v.i. 

1. To form crystals; to assume crystalline form. 2. v.t. To cause to assume a definite crystalline form. crystalloid, n. 

1. In chemistry, a substance which in solution diffuses readily through animal or vegetable membranes, and is generally capable of being crystallized; the opposite of colloid. 2. In botany, small crystal-like protein bodies found in plant cells. crystalloid, adj. 

Crystal-like; similar to crystals. cupric. 

[From the Latin noun meaning copper.] Of or pertaining to divalent copper; as cupric chloride (CuCl₂), or cupric sulphate (CuSO4). cuprous, adj. 

Of, or pertaining to, univalent copper; as cuprous chloride (CuCl), and cuprous oxide (Cu2O). current density.

The current passing through a circuit as compared to the area of cross section of the conductor, or to some part of the conductor. In electrolysis, the ratio of the current passing (in amperes), to the area of cross section of the cathode (in square decimeters). The symbol N D 100 is used in expressing current density. Thus, N D 100.5 amp. means that the current is to be passed at the rate of .5 ampere for every 100 square centimeters of cathode surface. current strength.

The actual amount of electricity (in amperes), passing through a conductor. See current density. curvature, n. [From the Latin adjective meaning bent.] The degree to which a thing is bent or curved. cyanamide, n. 

A colorless crystalline compound of carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen (CN NH2), made by the action of cyanogen chloride (CNCI), on an etherial solution of ammonia. The hydrogen of the NH₂ group is replaceable by metals. Some of the compounds so formed are of con- 54

CYANIDE 

siderable importance, as calcium cyanamide (CaCN2), white crystals which decompose in water, liberating ammonia. This substance is used as a fertilizer and a source of products of nitrogen. In the process of the manufacture of calcium cyanamide, lime and coke are heated together in an electric furnace so that calcium carbide (CaC2) is produced. While still hot the calcium carbide is subjected to the action of pure nitrogen under pressure. Calcium cyanamide results. cyanide, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to fall apart.] In chemistry, the decomposition of organic compounds, brought about by bacteria. The result ing substances are mainly oxides, and the process is a form of oxidation. 

declination, n. 

[From Latin words meaning that which is bent.] 1. The act of bending aside or downward. 2. In astronomy, the size of the angle, expressed in degrees and subdivisions thereof, formed by two radii of the celestial sphere, one passing through the point or body whose location is indicated, and the other passing through the point of intersection of the celestial equator (see under equator) and the great circle of the celestial sphere which passes through that point or body. Declination is measured either north or south from the celestial equator. 3. In navigation and surveying, (the declination of the magnetic needle) the angular distance (q.v.) between a true north and south line (the meridian of the place) and a line representing the direction taken by the magnetic needle. Since the meridians connect the geographical poles of the earth, and the magnetic needle points toward the magnetic poles, these two lines will coincide on only a small fraction of the earth's surface. decoction, n. To separate (a compound) into simpler compounds or into its constituent elements; to reduce to its component parts by a process of decay; as limestone (CaCO3) is decomposed by heat, forming lime (CaO) and carbon dioxide (CO2), or an organic substance such as protein. is decomposed when it forms nitrogen, ammonia, carbon dioxide, and water by the action of bacteria during the process of decay. decomposition, n. 

1. The process of decay or putrefaction. 2. In chemistry, the act or process of separating a substance or a body into its constituent parts, or the breaking up of a compound into its elements, or parts. In chemmistry, the elements or parts of a compound are spoken of as the constituents of the compound, while substances which make up a mixture are spoken of as components of the mixture. Thus a compound decomposes into its constituents (elements, and sometimes simpler compounds), while a mixture is separated into its components.

[From the Latin verb meaning to deprive of hair.] The removal of hair, either from the human skin, or from hides as in the process of the manufacture of leather. In the process of leather manufacture the hair and the epidermis of which the hair is an outgrowth, are removed from the hide by any one of four or five processes. In the sweating process, now nearly obsolete, the epidermis was permitted to putrefy, thus loosening the hair from the hide. Of the methods now used liming is most common. In this process the hides are soaked in a strong lime solution until the hair is loosened. It is then removed by scraping. depilatory, n.

DESTRUCTIVE DISTILLATION 

destructive distillation.

The decomposition of a solid substance by means of heat in a closed vessel, and the condensation and collection by means of a condenser of all or a part of the volatile substances produced by the recombining of the elements which made up the original substance. This process consists of both chemical and physical changes. The manufacture of methyl alcohol and acetic acid from wood is a typical example of destructive distillation. dew point.

The temperature at which some of the water vapor in the air condenses to the liquid form, becoming dew, rain, etc. The temperature at which this will occur depends upon the amount of water vapor present in the air, i.e., upon its absolute humidity, but, whatever the absolute humidity may be, the dew point is the temperature at which this amount of water vapor saturates the air, i.e., makes its relative humidity 100%. dextrin, n. [From the Latin adjective meaning right and the suffix -in.] A white or yellow amorphous substance (C6H10O5) produced from starch by the action of heat, acids, or ferments. Dextrin is very soluble in water and its solutions have strong dextrorotatory power. The crust on bread, and the mucilage on postage stamps is largely made up of dextrin. 

It is also used in textile printing, in tanning, and in the manufacture of confectionery. dextrose, n. [From the Latin adjective meaning right and -ose, an ending given, in organic chemistry, to sugars.] Glucose, (q.v.). diamond, n. [From a Latin noun meaning hardest iron.] A form of pure crystallized carbon, found free in various parts of the world, and highly prized for its luster and brilliancy, when properly cut and polished. The brilliancy of the cut and polished gem is due to its very high index of refraction, and to the fact that it is cut so as to reflect practically all light which falls upon it. Though brittle, the diamond is the hardest substance known and so will scratch all other substances. Its density is 3.5, which is higher than that of any other form of carbon. The diamond is not always found absolutely pure, but is sometimes yellow, Fink, or blue, owing to the presence of traces of oxides of the metals. 

Black diamonds are also found. These are identical in chemical and physical properties with the transparent form. 

Chemical reagents have but little effect on the diamond but it can be burned in oxygen. 

On combustion it yields only carbon dioxide, leaving no ash. From ffteen to twenty millions of dollars worth of diamonds are now mined yearly, and the output could be easily increased. In addition to its use as a gem, the diamond because of its hardness is utilized in the manufacture of many types of cutting tools. Saws and drills, for cutting 58

DIASTASE 

stone and other hard materials; glass cutters; and other cutting and drilling instruments and tools, are often faced with black diamonds or with faulty diamonds which could not be used as gems. diastase, n. [From the Greek noun meaning separation.] A mixture of starchsplitting enzymes, obtained from an infusion of germinating grains, especially barley and rye. When dry, it is a yellowish-white, colorless, odorless powder. It is soluble in water, but almost insoluble in alcohol. Its actual chemical composition is unknown. The substance has remarkable power to convert starch into dextrin and maltose, and is therefore of great importance in the brewing industry. dielectric, n. [From the Greek prefix meaning through+electric.] In physics, a layer of nonconducting or insulating material which permits induction to take place through its mass. differential, differential gear or gearing. 

A device used on automobiles to transmit the power of the motor to the rear wheels in such a way as to permit movement in a curved path, at which time one rear wheel turns faster and moves farther than the other. diffraction, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to break apart.] In physics. 1. In optics, a change in direction or bending of the paths of light rays which is caused by the passage of the rays very close to the edge of or through openings in an opaque object, or by their reflection from very narrow or minute plane surfaces. As light approaches an opaque object, each ray travels in a straight line. Those rays which pass the object at sensible distances continue on their way without any change in direction, but those rays which pass the opaque object at minute distances change their courses at its edge and move onward in other straight paths which bring them within the boundaries of the shadow of the opaque object. 

Thus the shadow has a border which is partially illuminated. The amount of bending which occurs depends upon and increases with the wave length. Since white light is made up of light of the seven prismatic colors, each with a different wave length, the partly illuminated border of the shadow is made up of parallel bands of the prismatic colors, red outside and violet inside. In addition to these colored bands, light and dark bands are formed, owing to differences in intensity of illumination caused by interference of light. Diffraction is best studied with light reflected from a piece of speculum metal on which have been ruled parallel lines (15,000 or more to the inch)—a diffraction grating. By looking at an electric light through a silk handkerchief two bands of bright light may be seen, crossing each other and each crossed by bands of prismatic colors. A similar example of diffraction and interference may be observed by looking at the moon through a screen door. Diffraction is a phenomenon of all kinds of 59

DIFFUSE 

wave motion, and is, therefore, exhibited by both sound and electricity as well as light. 2. In acoustics, a similar bending of the paths of sound waves. Since sound waves are immensely longer than light waves, they are subject to proportionally greater diffraction. diffuse, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to pour out, to spread.] To subject to or to cause diffusion, (q.v.) diffusion, n.

The gradual spontaneous mixing of substances placed in contact with each other which continues until the molecules of each substance are uniformly distributed throughout the space which the substances occupy jointly. It takes place without the application of external force, and even in opposition to the pull of gravity, since a heavy liquid or gas will diffuse upward into a light liquid or gas. Only miscible liquids will diffuse, i.e., adhesion between their molecules must be stronger than cohesion in either. Diffusion is most rapid and marked between gases, but it is also an important phenomenon of liquids. It is also known to take place between solids, as when lead and gold have been left in contact for a very long time. The molecular theory furnishes an explanation of diffusion. Compare with solution. digest, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to separate, to dissolve.] 1. In physiology, to change food, by both physical and chemical processes, into forms which can be assimilated. See digestion. 2. In chemistry, to heat, more or less intensely, in the presence of a liquid; as, to digest bark in alcohol so as to cause certain ingredients to dissolve. digestible, adj. 

Capable of being digested. direct current.

Electric current flowing in one direction, as distinguished from alternating current which changes its direction many times per second. The electric current supplied by an electrolytic cell or a battery is always direct current, since the electrons move only in one direction. The current supplied by a dynamo, however, may be alternating or direct. 

See dynamo. disinfectant, n.

A substance used to render harmless or safe either objects or substances or places, by destroying in them the germs of contagious or infectious diseases. Common disinfectants are heat (often applied by means of steam), corrosive sublimate (mercuric chloride), carbolic acid, sulphur dioxide (the fumes of burning sulphur), chlorine, so-called chloride of lime (calcium hypochlorite), zinc chloride, and formaldehyde. Since disinfectants are not applied to living bodies, some substances are available as disinfectants which could not be used as antiseptics; and strong solutions of some substances serve as disinfectants, while their weak solutions are used as antiseptics. 60

DISINFECTION 

disinfection, n.

The act or process of disinfecting; the result of such a process. disintegrate, v.t. [From a Latin verb meaning to destroy the unity of.] To break up; to separate into its component parts; to destroy the cohesion of its parts;a term used to indicate the physical destruction of an object, rather than its chemical decomposition. disintegration, n.

The act or process or reducing a body to fragments or separating it into its component parts; as, the disintegration of a rock, meaning its phycial destruction by the action of water and frost, rather than its chemical decomposition. dispersal, n. 

1. The act of scattering widely; the result of such an act. 2. In biology, the spread of plant or animal species over the earth's surface. displacement, n. 

1. The act of putting out of proper place or position. 2. In physics, the quantity of a liquid which is displaced by a solid body immersed in or floated on it. A floating body always displaces a quantity of liquid whose weight is equal to the weight of the body. 3. In mechanics, the difference between the position of a body at any moment, and its original position; the amount of change in its position. dissolve, v.t.

In physics. 1. To liquefy a solid by causing it to break up into molecules and diffuse through a liquid; to make a solution of. 2. To liquefy a substance by bringing it into contact with a liquid whose molecules exert a stronger attraction for its molecules (adhesion) than the attraction which exists between its own molecules (cohesion), thus causing it to disappear into the liquid, where diffusion distributes it uniformly throughout the liquid. Heat weakens cohesion, and therefore hastens the dissolving of a solid in a liquid. Pulverizing a solid causes it to dissolve more rapidly by exposing more surface to the action of the liquid. Cold and pressure retard the motion of the molecules of a gas and are favorable to cohesion. They therefore hasten the dissolving of a gas by a liquid. distil, v.t.

To change a substance to its vapor in a closed vessel by the application of heat, and, by passing the vapor through a cool receiver or worm, convert it back to its original state. The process consists of two distinct steps-vaporization and condensation, both of which are physical changes. Contrast with destructive distillation. distillate, n.

A substance produced by distillation. It is usually a liquid, but if a substance which sublimes were distilled, the distillate would be a solid. 61

DISTILLATION 

distillation, n.

The process of vaporizing all or part of a liquid or a solid substance by heating it in a retort or a still, and then condensing all or a part of the vapor by passing it through a cool receiver or condenser. See fractional distillation and destructive distillation. distributor, n.

A device used on internal combustion engines to transmit the hightension electric current to the spark plugs in such a way as to ignite the mixture of gases in the cylinders at the proper time. divisibility, n.

In physics, that general property of matter by virtue of which any body can be separated into smaller bodies. The limit of separation by physical forces is the molecule. drug, n. [From an old French noun which probably meant a dried vegetable or root.] Any animal, vegetable, or mineral substance which is used as a medicine, whether for external application or to be taken internally, or which is used in the preparation of a medicine. The use made of the substance is the only basis on which to judge whether or not this term applies to it. dry distillation. 

See destructive distillation. ductile, adj. [From a Latin adjective meaning easily led.] Capable of being drawn out into wire. ductility, n.

A specific property of many substances (solids) which makes it possible to draw them out into wires or threads. It is possessed by most metals, and also, when heated, by glass and many gums. Ductility and malleability are usually possessed by the same substances. For example, gold, the most ductile metal, is also the most malleable one. duralumin, n.

A complex alloy of approximately the following composition: aluminum 95 to 97%, copper 3 to 3.5%, manganese .4 to 1%, and magnesium .5 to 2%, with or without traces of silicon. This alloy melts at 640 to 

660°C., has a specific gravity from 2.75 to 2.85 and has the strength of steel though it weighs but one-third as much. It is used in the manufacture of scientific instruments and parts for automobiles and aeroplanes. dynamite, n. [From the Greek noun meaning power+mite.] An explosive prepared by causing nitroglycerine to be absorbed by some porous material. 

The original dynamite prepared by Alfred Nobel in 1866 consisted of a porous infusorial earth called Kieselguhr which he had caused to absorb about three times its weight of nitroglycerine. Dynamite is a 62

DYNAMO 

safer, though less violent explosive than nitroglycerine. It is also more easily handled and transported. There are three types of explosives included under the term dynamite:-(1) In one type an inert base or absorbing material such as infusorial earth is used; (2) In a second type the base, such as wood pulp, is combustible; (3) In the third type the absorbing material is itself explosive. The strength or explosive power of dynamite is usually indicated as 50%, 60%, or 75%, according to the proportion by weight of nitroglycerine present. dynamo, n. [Derived from the Greek word meaning power. It is an abbreviated form of the original name, dynamo-electric machine, in which the significance is lost.] A device by which mechanical energy is transformed into electrical energy. The principle underlying its action-induction, discovered by Faraday-is that when a loop made of a conductor of electricity moves in a magnetic field of force in such a way that the number of lines of force passing through its opening either increases or decreases, there is a difference in potential in the parts of the loop and consequently a current of electricity flows through it. The essential parts of a dynamo are three in number. The first is a magnet, to produce a magnetic field of force. This may be either a permanent magnet or an electromagnet. The second is the armature, in which the induced current of electricity is produced. It is circular in cross section and rotates in the field of force (or, more often now, the field magnets rerevolve around a stationary armature). It consists of a variable number of turns or loops of insulated wire of high electrical conductivity, wound around a soft iron core. While the moving part is making one complete circuit, each loop on the armature is twice in such a relation to the field of force that no lines of force pass through its opening, and twice in such a relation that the maximum number of lines pass through it, once from each side. Thus the direction of the flow of the current of electricity in each loop is reversed once for each such movement, giving an alternating current. The third essential part is a device for leading away the current of electrical energy as it is developed. It consists of a pair of "brushes," strips of copper or of carbon, which are in sliding contact with a collar of conducting material borne on the axle of the armature but insulated from it, to which each coil delivers its electricity. To change such an alternating current dynamo to a direct current dynamo, a fourth part, a commutator, is added. This replaces the collecting collar and is a hollow cylinder composed of separated and insulated segments whose centers are 180° apart, so that one of a pair of brushes (also 180° apart) receives all the electricity flowing in one direction, while its mate receives all which flows in the opposite direction. A separately-excited dynamo has its field of force created by a permanent magnet. Such a dynamo is usually small, and is called at magneto-electrical machine, or magneto. A self-excited dynamo uses. part or all of its own current to magnetize the electromagnets which create its field. If all the current flows through the coils of the electro- 63

DYNE 

magnets-i.e., if the magnets are connected in series with the armature -the dynamo is series-wound. An increased load put upon such a dynamo increases the strength of its magnets and therefore its output of current. In the shunt-wound dynamo a definite quantity of electricity is carried through a shunt wire to the electromagnets, so their strength remains constant, and the output is constant. A third type, called compound-wound, combines the two systems of magnet winding, and is thus enabled to send through the external circuit a current of constant strength in spite of wide variations in the load, fitting it for use in incandescent lighting systems and street car systems. By passing a current of electricity in through one brush, through the armature, and out through the other brush, the dynamo becomes a motor, and transforms electrical energy into mechanical energy. dyne, n. [From a Greek noun meaning power or force.] In physics, the unit of force in the centimeter-gram-second (commonly abreviated to C. G. S.) system; that amount of force which when acting on a mass of one gram for a second will change its velocity by one centimeter per second. The dyne is an absolute unit of force and does not vary from point to point on the earth's surface, as do the gram and the pound, the units of weight. The dyne is equivalent to about 1.02 times the force exerted by gravity upon a mass of one milligram at sea level. 64

ebonite, n. [From the Greek noun meaning ebony, a black wood.] Black hard rubber; black vulcanite. While some authorities make a distinction between ebonite and vulcanite on the basis of the percent of sulphur contained, it is more usual to consider ebonite a variety of vulcanite. 

For methods of manufacture, characteristics, and uses, see vulcanite. ebullition, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to boil out or up.] The act or process of of boiling. See boil. effervesce, v.i.

To agitate a liquid by the formation of many small bubbles which burst at the surface with a hissing sound. effervescence, n. 

The process of effervescing. efflorescence, n. 

[From the Latin verb meaning to blossom.] In chemistry, 1. The process by which a hydrated salt loses part or all of its water of hydration and becomes coated with crystals of the corresponding anhydrous salt or crumbles to a powder. Thus, crystals of hydrated cupric sulphate (bluestone or blue vitriol CuSO4.5H2O), when exposed to dry air, become coated with a layer of the white anhydrous cupric sulphate (CuSO4). Crystals of washing soda (the hydrate of sodium carbonate, Na2CO3 10H2O), unless kept in a tight container lose water and crumble to a powder. Since efflorescence depends upon the absorption of water by the surrounding air, humidity governs the rate at which efflorescence goes on. 2. The crust or body of anhydrous crystals formed when hydrated salts lose water of hydration. Contrast with hygroscopic. elastic, adj.

Of solids, capable of resisting a force which tends to change the shape and size, and of recovering the shape and size when the force ceases acting; of fluids, capable of resisting compression and of recovering from it. elasticity, n.

Springiness; that specific property of certain substances which causes bodies composed of them to resist deformation and after the withdrawal of the deforming force, if solids, to recover their size and shape, if fluids, their size. Elasticity is of four kinds of compression, of extension, of bending, and of torsion or twisting. All matter possesses elasticity of volume to a greater or a less degree, but elasticity of shape belongs to solids only. electro- [From the Greek noun meaning amber, in connection with which substance electric effects were first noted.] Combining form meaning electric, electrical, or pertaining to electricity. 65

ELECTRODE 

electrode, n.

Either terminal of a battery when it is connected in an electric circuit, or either of the conductors when they are inserted in the solution of an electrolytic cell. The terminal or conductor which is connected to the negative pole of the battery is called the negative electrode or the cathode, and the terminal or conductor connected to the positive pole is called the positive electrode, or anode. electrodynamic, adj.

Pertaining to or caused by current electricity. electrodynamics, n.

That branch of physics which deals with electricity in motion, with currents of electricity in their relations to each other, to magnets, etc.; electrokinetics; - contrasted with electrostatics. electrolysis, n.

In chemistry, and physics the act or the process of decomposing a compound by the use of an electric current; as, by electrolysis, water is broken up into hydrogen and oxygen. electrolysis of water,

A process in which a current of electricity is passed through water containing a small percentage of an electrolyte such as sulphuric acid with the result that water is broken up into its constituents oxygen and hydrogen. The acid having ionized (i.e., separated into parts carrying electric charges), the current causes the migration of the charged ions toward the electrodes (conductors from the poles of the battery, inserted in the liquid). The positively charged hydrogen ions migrate to the cathode (negative electrode), where they recover the electrons lost to the sulphate radical on ionizing, and are released as gaseous hydrogen. The negatively charged sulphate ions migrate to the anode (positive electrode), and there cause the decomposition of water. They then unite with the hydrogen resulting from this decomposition, and so sulphuric acid is again formed. The oxygen rises to the surface. Therefore, during the electrolysis of water (in the presence of sulphuric acid), hydrogen is released at the negative electrode, and oxygen at the positive electrode, while the sulphuric acid tends to concentrate at the positive electrode. electrolyte, n. [Electro- the Greek adjective meaning capable of being broken up.] 

In physics and chemistry. 1. Any substance which produces ions upon going into solution. 2. Any substance which, in the molten state or in solution, conducts electricity and decomposes during the passage of the current through it. All electrolytes are acids, bases, or salts, and only those compounds belonging to these classes which are soluble in water are electrolytes. Upon going into solution an electrolyte forms ions, some of which are charged postively and some negatively. 

These do not migrate toward the electrodes until a current is passed through the solution. When the current begins to flow, the positively 66

ELECTROLYTIC 

charged ions (cations) begin to move toward the cathode, and the negatively charged ions, (anions) toward the anode. If copper sulphate is dissolved in water, positively charged copper ions and negatively charged sulphate ions are formed. If a direct current be passed through this solution the copper ions will move to the negative electrode (cathode) and be deposited as metallic copper, while the sulphate ions will move to the positive electrode (anode) and there unite with water to form sulphuric acid and oxygen. Copper sulphate is a typical electrolyte. electrolytic, adj.

Pertaining to electrolysis or to an electrolyte; produced by electrolysis; as, electrolytic copper. electrolytic cell. 

Any cell in which electrolysis takes place; a vessel containing a solution of an electrolyte in which are inserted positive and negative electrodes, so that when the current is passed the electrolyte is decomposed. See electrolysis. electrolytic dissociation. 

See ionization. electromagnet, n. 

A device consisting of a soft iron core around which an insulated wire is wound in such a way as to permit strong induction in the core when a direct current is passed through the wire around it. The strength of the magnet thus produced is proportional to the number of turns which the wire takes around the core and to the current passed. Thus the strength of a magnet may be given in "ampere turns," a name denoting the product of the number of turns of wire about the core and the number of amperes flowing in each turn. The electro-magnet loses almost all its strength the instant the current is broken, and reverses its polarity if the current is reversed. The electromagnet is an essential part of the electric bell, the telegraph and the telephone. Huge electromagnets, weighing several tons and capable of lifting a carload of iron or steel, are in use in industrial plants. electromagnetic, adj.

Pertaining to, derived from, or produced by, magnetism derived from the flow of electric current; as, electromagnetic induction, or induction produced by a current flowing through a conductor. electromotive, adj.

Referring to that which produces an electric current or an electrical effect. 

electromotive series. 

A partial list of the elements, including most of the metallic elements and also hydrogen, in which each one has a greater electrode potential than any which is below it and a less electrode potential than any which is above it, so that any element in the list will replace from its 67

ELECTRON 

solutions any element which stands below it in the list and be replaced by any element which stands above it in the list, -i.e, the elements are in the decreasing order of their electrode potential; a list of elements in the order of the ease with which they give up one or more electrons to form ions. electron, n. 

In physics and chemistry, one of the ultimate particles of matter out of which all atoms are built; the unit or smallest particle of negative electricity. An electron has a mass equal to 1/1845 of the mass of an atom of hydrogen. The cathode rays of the vacuum tube and the beta rays of radium and other radioactive substances are streams of electrons. electrophorus, n. [electrophorous.] In physics, a device invented by Volta for producing small quantities of static electricity by induction and transferring them to other bodies. It consists essentially of a plate of nonconducting material, usually vulcanite (hard rubber or ebonite), which is attached to a plate of metal, a conducting material; and a movable cover plate of metal having a handle of insulating or non-conducting material. By rubbing the non-conducting plate with fur or wool, it becomes charged with negative frictional or static electricity, the corresponding positive charge being taken by the fur or wool. The metallic cover plate, when placed upon the non-contucting plate, does not acquire the positive charge of the under plate by conducting it away from it, but it becomes charged by induction with positive electricity on its lower surface and with negative electricity on it upper surface. While the plates are still in contact a touch of the finger of the operator allows the negative charge to flow through his body to the earth leaving the movable plate positively charged throughout. In this condition it is lifted off and its charge used elsewhere. The mechanical work that is done in separating the two plates against the attraction of two bodies carrying unlike charges is a measure of the amount of the positive charge carried away. Since the non-conducting plate was not discharged during this operation, an indefinite number of positive charges may be thus obtained without re-exciting the vulcanite plate, if favorable atmospheric conditions exist. electroplate, v.t. 

To plate by electrolysis; as, with silver, nickel, or copper. In silver plating, the so called "plating bath" is a concentrated solution of potassium silver cyanide in distilled water. The object to be plated is thoroughly cleaned and suspended in the bath by a wire in such a way as to make it the cathode of an electrolytic cell. The anode consists of a plate of pure silver having approximately the same area of surface as the object to be plated. To produce a smooth crystalline coating of metal, the plating bath must be hot and the current passed at the proper voltage and amperage. The object is usually withdrawn after a time, polished, cleaned and replaced in the plating bath. In this way a 68

ELECTROSCOPE 

double, a triple, or a quadruple plate is obtained. A metal coating obtained in the manner described is said to have a "satin finish." To get a "burnished finish" the object is rubbed with steel burnishing tools and then polished. electroscope, n. 

An instrument for detecting the presence or the nature (positive or negative) of small electric charges, by making use of the law of electrical attraction and repulsion. It commonly consists of a glass flask or jar, through the top of which passes a copper or brass rod or heavy wire, supported in such a way that it is well insulated from the glass and all surrounding objects. The top of the rod or wire terminates in a metallic ball or disk, while the bottom ends in a flat hook, on which is suspended a ribbon of gold leaf caught through its center so that the two halves hang down close together and parallel to each other. If the top of the electroscope be touched with a charged glass rod, the charge will be transferred to the gold leaves at the bottom of the rod and, both becoming positively charged, they will repel each other. 

If now another charged body be brought near the electroscope, the nature and magnitude of its charge can be determined by noting the effect on the charged gold leaves. If the unknown charge be negative, the leaves will collapse; if it be positive, they will diverge still farther. The amount of divergence roughly indicates the magnitude of the charge. electrostatic, adj.

Pertaining to, having to do with, or caused by, stationary charges of electricity. Contrast with electrodynamic, (q.v.) electrostatic capacity. 

See capacity. element, n.

In chemistry. 1. A substance possessing definite characteristic properties and which has never been broken up into two or more simpler substances; a definite species of matter whose molecules are of only one kind. [Note. Very recently some of the forms of matter which have long been regarded as elements have been caused to break up with the formation of simpler substances. This means that the decomposed substances were not elements, as had been supposed, or that elements, after all, may be decomposed when suitable means are found.] 2. 

One of possibly 92 distinct kinds of matter which, either alone or in chemical union, make up every known substance. The number of elements recognized by an international committee of chemists in 1929 was 89. Few characteristics of the elements have proved of such fundamental importance as their relative atomic weights. See atomic weights. 

Mendeleeff, a Russian chemist, in 1869, arranged the then known elements in the order of their atomic weights, and observed their chemical and physical properties. It was apparent to him that a given characteristic repeated itself at fixed intervals, thus dividing the list roughly into series of eight members each. Rearranging the list into a table of eight 69

EMPIRICAL 

columes, it was evident that the left end of the table was made up of elements which were basic, the right acidic, and the middle intermediate in character. When other properties were considered, the same sort of arrangement could be seen. This led Mendeleeff to formulate the periodic law which bears his name - that the properties, physical and chemical, of the elements are periodic functions of their atomic weights. 

Some of the series in this table were incomplete, the gaps indicating the existence of elements as yet unknown. Since these unknown elements must possess characteristics intermediate between those to the right and those to the left in the same series, Mendeleeff was able to describe with considerable accuracy three elements which were later isolated. 

In one or two cases it was even predicted in what mineral the unknown element would be found. The recent discovery of a group of rare gaseous elements in the atmosphere (helium, argon, etc.) called for the addition of a new column to the table. If this does not happen again and if only the filling in of the gaps now recognized is required, the discovery of three new elements will complete the list.

In 1912 Moseley, an English Physicist, made discoveries which led him to assign a number to each of the elements of the periodic system. 

These numbers indicate the magnitude of the electric charge on the nucleus of each of the atoms. The numbers are significant because the fundamental nature of an element is more closely related to the charge on the nucleus than to its atomic weight. See atomic number. empirical, adj. [From a Greek noun meaning experience.] Referring to methods and practices which are founded on experience; depending upon observation alone, without a search for the causes of the observed phenomena; -applied especially to medical practices and "rule of thumb" methods. emulsify, v.t. 

To convert to an emulsion, or to cause to form an emulsion. See emulsion. emulsion, n. [From the Greek verb meaning to milk.] A liquid mixture having the color of milk, consisting of minute globules of a fat or an oil suspended in a liquid which will not dissolve them. Milk is a typical natural emulsion, and mayonnaise salad dressing is a typical artificial one. The separation of the oil from the other liquid is retarded or prevented by coating each globule with a film of some substance which keeps the globules apart and prevents their uniting into larger bodies. The orchardist, in making his kerosene emulsion (kerosene and water) with which to spray his fruit trees, adds whale-oil soap for this purpose. In like manner emulsions of volatile and other oils are made permanent by the use of mucilage. The white color of an emulsion is due to the peculiar manner in which the light is reflected from the surfaces of the oil globules. endothermic reaction.

In chemistry, a reaction which extracts heat from the reacting sub- 70

ENERGY 

stances and their surroundings, and which therefore cannot be started without the application of heat nor carried to completion without its continued application. energy, n.

In physics and chemistry, the ability to cause change, to overcome resistance-i.e, to do work. It manifests itself in various forms, such such as the mechanical energy of a steam engine which raises the ram of a pile driver, the electrical energy which turns the armature of a motor the thermal energy which overcomes cohesion between the molecules and changes water to steam, the chemical energy of exploding gunpowder which drives a bullet, etc. Energy in any form can, by appropriate means, be transformed into energy in any other of its forms, but energy can never be created nor destroyed- a statement which is known as the law of the conservation of energy. There are two kinds of energy -kinetic (called also actual or motive) energy, and potential energy The first is possessed by a moving body only, whether it be a mass or a molecule, as the ability of a swinging hammer to drive a nail or the ability of rapidly vibrating molecules to throw off heat. The second is possessed by a body at rest, viz: (1) by virtue of work having been done on it to place it in a position from which is will move unless obstructed, as the water in a mill-pond held back by a dam; (2) by virtue of work having been done to put its molecules under a strain from which they will escape when not obstructed, as a bent bow before the bowstring is released; or (3) by virtue of its chemical composition which enables it to enter into new combinations and release energy, as the burning of coal - the re-uniting, because or their chemical affinity, of the oxygen of the air and the carbon of the coal to form carbon dioxide gas after the energy of the sun's rays had overcome that affinity and separated them by 

enflammable, adj.

Inflammable; capable of being easily set on fire or ignited; easily combustible. The word is an old form of the word inflammable but it is considered to be a better word for pupils of high school age because of the possibility of accident with easily combustible chemicals should the word inflammable be wrongly understood to mean not flammable (combustible), as insoluble means not soluble, invisible means not visible. On the other hand, the pupil must remember, that there are many words commencing with in that have not a negative meaning. e.g. invaluable, invigorate, etc. enzyme, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning leavened.] A soluble ferment produced in the life processes of various living organisms or cells, which brings about the liquefaction of food so that it can diffuse through the cell wall into the cell, where it may be assimilated. Enzymes have a power to bring about chemical change out of all proportion to the amount present. They resemble proteins in that they are destroyed by heat, 71

EQUILIBRIUM 

precipitated by alcohol and by concentrated salt solutions, and have their action stopped by poisons. Enzymes may be secreted to the outside of the cell (exo-enzymes) or remain in the cell (endo-enzymes). 

They are specific in their action, each acting on only one particular substance. The most important enzymes which have to do with human digestion are ptyalin in the saliva, which acts on starches; pepsin in the gastric juice, which acts on proteins; and amylopsin, tripsin, and steapsin of the pancreatic juice (q.v.), which act on starches, proteins, and fats respectively. They are most effective at temperatures ranging from 

30 to 50° C., and they lose their power at 100° C. in the presence of water. See diastase and rennin. equilibrant, n. [From Latin words meaning equal and balance.] In physics, any force or factor which is capable of balancing another force or factor so as to establish an equilibrium. equilibrium, n.

In physics. 1. A state of balance in which the forces which act on a body are so related that the body is not accelerated. A body at rest is in equilibrium, a body in motion in a straight line at a uniform rate is in equilibrium, and a body moving at a uniform rate around an axis through its center of mass is in equilibrium. 2. The condition of a body in which it has no acceleration either of translation or of rotation. 

3. The condition of a body held in position by gravity, when it is supported by a force equal to its own weight acting upward through its center of gravity. The equilibrium of the body is said to be stable if after only slight displacement it returns to its original position; it is said to be unstable if on slight displacement it moves farther from its original position; and neutral if on displacement it remains in equilibrium in another position. In chemistry. 4. A state reached in a reversible reaction in which two opposing reactions are taking place at the same rate. In the ionization of NaCl which takes place when salt goes into solution in water, the ions produced by the dissociation of the molecules soon become so numerous that oppositely charged ions frequently meet and reunite to form NaCl molecules again. As more of the salt is dissolved and the ions become more and more numerous these meetings take place more and more often until finally the rate of molecular formation through the reuniting of ions is equal to the rate at which molecules of the salt are ionizing. At this point the maximum percent of ionization, under the conditions present, has been established between molecules and ions. It will be seen that equilibrium in this sense is a dynamic, not a static condition. equivalent. 

See equivalent weight. equivalent weight, combining weight, combining number, hydrogen equivalent, equivalent, reacting weight, electro-chemical equivalent, gramequivalent, chemical equivalent. 72

ERG 

1. In chemistry, that number of parts (by weight) of an element which will combine with or replace eight parts (by weight) of oxygen; that number of grams of an element which will combine with or replace eight grams of oxygen; that quantity of metal which will release from an acid one gram of hydrogen (or, more accurately, 1.008 grams or 11.19 liters at 0° C. and 760 mm.). 2. The ratio by weight in which two elements combine; the smallest ratio by weight in which two elements combine. 

In discussing this subject each authority uses one (or more) of the terms listed above. The definitions given are intended to indicate the various shades of meaning put into the various terms. The original idea underlying the phenomena studied, and the idea underlying all of these terms is ratio, regardless of any fixed weight. The atomic weight of an element is always the same as its equivalent weight, or else its equivalent weight multiplied by some small integer. These integers are the same as the valences of the elements. So the atomic weight of an element divided by its valence gives its equivalent weight. Some elements have more than one combining weight. For example, oxygen combines with hydrogen to form both water (H2O) and hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂). 3. In electrochemistry, that weight of an element liberated from an electrolyte by 96,540 coulombs (one faraday) of electricity. When equal quantities of electricity are passed through solutions of electrolytes, the weights of the elements liberated are in the ratios of their equivalent weights. 

Thus, when 96,540 coulombs of electricity are passed through different electrolytes, each of which contains a different metal, it is found that one equivalent weight of each metal has been liberated. A few of these equivalent weights are indicated in the following table:
