Zn.. 32.69 erg, n. [From a Greek noun meaning work.] In physics, an absolute unit of work in the C. G. S. system, being the work done by a force of one dyne when it moves the point on which it acts a distance of one centimeter; a dyne centimeter. The force of gravity gives to a mass of one gram an acceleration of 980 centimeters a second, and gravity is therefore equal to 980 dynes. If a force lifts a mass of one gram against gravity a distance of one centimeter the work done is one gram centimeter or 980 ergs. Ten million ergs equal one joule (q.v.). ester, n.

In chemistry. 1. A compound formed by the substitution of a hydrocarbon radical for the acid-hydrogen of an acid. 2. The product (other 73

ETHER than water) resulting from the action of an alcohol with an acid, the alcohol taking the part of a base. Thus esters resemble salts in their mode of formation and are sometimes called organic salts. They are characteristic constituents of essential oils and are used in the manufacture of synthetic substitutes for these oils in cases where the demand for the oils warrants it. Many esters occur naturally in the bodies of plants and animals, and are often pungent or sweet smelling compounds. 

Oil of wintergreen, a plant product, is largely composed of the methyl ester of salicylic acid. Fats are chiefly the mixed glycerine esters of oleic, palmitic, and stearic acids. Many esters, such as amyl acetate from acetic acid and amyl alcohol, are readily synthesized in the chemical laboratory. ether, n. 

[From the Greek verb meaning upper air, or haze.] 1. A form of matter 

(a substance) which differs from ordinary matter only in being so thin and light that it cannot be detected by any of the human senses. Because no sense of man can detect it, its existence cannot be either proved or disproved. Ether is supposedly the medium for the transference of electromagnetic waves. Because certain experiments have failed to show any effect due to it, its existence is disputed. It is supposed to 

Occupy all space, both the immense spaces between the stars and the minute portions of space which, according to the molecular theory, exist between the molecules of the densest solid bodies (called intermolecular spaces or physical pores). It seems to be perfectly elastic, to show some of the properties of a rigid, very close-grained solid, and some of the properties of a gas. The celestial bodies pass through it without meeting any measurable resistance. Radiant energy, of which light and heat are examples, is carried by vibrations set up in the ether. Ancient writers applied the term to the upper air, to the blue vault of the heavens, and Aristotle supposed it to extend from the stars to the moon. 

In chemistry, 2. A colorless, volatile, and highly inflammable liquid (C2H5)2O, with a characteristic odor and a burning taste, used as a solvent for fats, oils, and many inorganic substances, and as a general anesthetic; sulphuric ether. It is usually prepared by the action of sulphuric acid on grain alcohol. 3. Any of a class of compounds, of which sulphuric ether is a typical member. 4. The distillate from crude petroleum which boils between 120° and 140° F. is called petroleum ether, an unfortunately chosen name, since the substance is not an ether, but a very low-boiling gasolene. ethyl-.

In organic chemistry, a monovalent hydrocarbon radical, C2H5-, found only in combination with other radicals or atoms. In combination with one atom of hydrogen it forms its hydride ethane (C2H6); with one hydroxyl radical, ethyl alcohol (C2H5OH); taken twice and combined with one atom of oxygen, its oxide, ether ((C2H5)2O). 74

ETHYL ALCOHOL 

ethyl alcohol, n.

Grain alcohol (C2H5OH), commonly called simply alcohol; an alcohol which contains the univalent ethyl radicle, (C2H5-). It is a colorless liquid with a specific gravity of .791. It freezes at -130.5° C., boils at 

78° C., is readily ignited, and burns with the production of much heat and little light. It is miscible with water in all proportions. It is not a violent poison, but has injurious effects on the system when taken in excess. It is made by alcoholic fermentation of sugars especially those found in fruits and grains, such as glucose and maltose. Other sugars must be converted to glucose before they can yield alcohol by fermentation. Ethyl alcohol is the alcohol present in all spirituous liquors, such as whiskey, brandy, wine, and beer. ethylene, n.

In chemistry, a colorless, inflammable gas, (C2H4), which burns with a highly luminous flame. It is one of the components of illuminating gas, and is present in typical carburetted water gas. It has recently been discovered that it is of value in the artificial ripening of fruit and that it has remarkable power to produce anaesthesia. It is used in the preparation of mustard gas. Hydrocarbons of the ethelene series are known as olefines, or oil formers. Ethylene is usually made by treating ethyl (grain) alcohol with six times its weight of hot sulphuric acid. eudiometer, n. [From the Greek noun meaning clear (in the sense of pure) air+meter.] 

In physics. 1. An instrument which was intended for use in determining the degree of purity of the air, but found useful in the volumetric analysis of gases and for other purposes. It may consist of a graduated glass tube some 60 cm. long and 2 cm. in diameter, with one end closed and having two platinum wires fused into its walls near the closed end, their ends close together but not touching. These wires permit the passage of a current of electricity through the gas within if it is desired to ignite it. To demonstrate the proportions in which oxygen and hydrogen unite when water is formed, this instrument may be filled with mercury, inverted into a dish of mercury, the mercury in the eudiometer displaced by a known quantity of hydrogen and an approximately equal quantity of oxygen, the mixture exploded by a current of electricity sent through the wires, and the remaining quantity of oxygen determined and subtracted from the amount put in. This excess of gas over what will combine is needed to serve as a cushion and prevent the breaking of the glass tube when the mixture is exploded. 2. A straight 

U-shaped graduated glass tube for use in measuring the volumes of gases. eutectic, adj. [From Greek words meaning easily melted.] In physics, chemistry, and metallurgy, having the minimum melting point;-applied only to a mixture of two or more substances in such proportions that the melting 75

EUTECTIC 

point of the mixture is lower than that of any other mixture composed of the same ingredients but in different proportions. eutectic, n. 

A eutectic mixture-usually an alloy. 

evaporation, n. 

The act or the process of changing a substance from either the solid or the liquid state of matter into the gaseous state. The rate of evaporation depends upon both temperature and pressure. It may be hastened by reducing the pressure, or raising the temperature. Since the boiling point of a liquid is raised by increasing the pressure upon its surface, and since the temperature of the vapor is the same as the temperature of the boiling liquid, a hotter vapor can be obtained by increasing the pressure under which boiling takes place. Since steam produced under pressure has greater expanding power than steam produced under ordinary atmospheric pressure, "super-heated steam" is used in very powerful steam engines. See sublimation. evolve, v.t. [From a Latin verb meaning to roll out.] 1. To unfold; to develop; to work out or elaborate. 2. In chemistry, to throw out, give off, or emit, as gases or odors are evolved during a chemical reaction. exothermic reaction.

In chemistry, a reaction in which heat is evolved, such as the burning of a piece of coal. While in most cases such reactions must be started by the application of heat, some start at ordinary temperatures, and of these a few, under favorable conditions, produce heat enough so that the reaction goes on at increasing speed until the kindling temperature of one of the reacting substances is reached, and spontaneous combustion Occurs. experiment, n.

The operation of putting certain objects or substances under the influence of certain conditions or forces and then observing the results, for the sake of learning the truth or the falseness of some statement or supposition or principle, or for the sake of learning something new. extension, n. 

1. In physics, that general property of matter by virtue of which a body occupies a portion of space or takes up room. Called also magnitude. 

2. In physiology, straightening a limb;-opposite of flexion. extract, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to draw out.] In chemistry and pharmacy, a liquid solution containing in concentrated form the medicinal, nutritive, or other characteristic ingredient of the substance from which it is derived; an essence; as, lemon extract, extract of beef and iron; etc.

For methods of preparing extracts, see extraction. extraction, n.

In chemistry and pharmacy, a physical process often used to remove from 76

EXTRACTION 

a mixture one or more of its ingredients by the use of a solvent which does not affect the other ingredients. The dissolving of a solid ingredient is promoted by maceration (soaking at ordinary temperatures); digestion (soaking in a hot liquid)—by either of which processes an infusion is produced; decoction (soaking in a boiling liquid); or percolation—called displacement by pharmacists (allowing a liquid to trickle slowly through). 

The removal of the solvent and its dissolved matter is then effected by decanting (gently pouring off the liquid portion), straining, filtering, or expressing (squeezing out). 77

Fahrenheit, adj.

Referring to or designating the thermometric scale devised by the 

German physicist Gabriel Daniel Fahrenheit about 1714. On this scale the freezing point of water is at 32 degrees above zero, and the boiling point of water at 212 degrees. The Fahrenheit scale is used for all ordinary purposes in practically all English speaking countries. The scientists of all nations prefer the centegrade scale, and use it in most of their temperature measurements. See thermometer. farad, n.

The unit in which the capacity of condensers is measured, being the capacity of a condenser which will hold a charge of one coulomb when the difference of potential between its terminals is one volt. This is a large unit and condensers are usually rated in microfarads (millionths of a farad). faraday, n. [Named after Michael Faraday (1791-1867), the great English physicist.] In physics, a unit of measurement of quantity of electricity, equivalent to 96,540 coulombs; the quantity of electricity which will deposit from a solution of an electrolyte one hydrogen equivalent of a metal. fatty, adj. 

1. Consisting of, or resembling fat; fat-like; greasy. 2. In chemistry, aliphatic; the aliphatic or fatty hydrocarbons are those whose molecular structures include a chain of two or more carbon atoms. The two great divisions of the hydrocarbons are the aliphatic or chain hydrocarbons, and the aromatic or ring hydrocarbons. feldspar, felspar, n. [From German nouns meaning field and laminated stone.] In mineralogy, any one of a group of widely distributed minerals, all of which consist of aluminum silicate together with one or more of the silicates of potassium, sodium, calcium, and barium. They are component parts of the granites and other igneous rocks, and are the most abundant minerals in the earth's crust. Their crystals are stout prisms and have two distinct forms, but their bounding planes always meet at angles of approximately 60, 90, and 120°. They break readily along two planes nearly at right angles to each other. The feldspars are in color white, yellow, pink, flesh-red, bluish, and dark greenish-brown. Among them are found several semiprecious stones, such as the moonstone and the amazon stone. All the feldspars weather rapidly when exposed to the air or to water containing acids, and by their disintegration kaolin and other clays have been formed. The pottery industry, in addition to its use of clay, uses large quantities of feldspar rock, which is ground fine, made into a paste, and used as a glaze. 78

FERMENT 

ferment, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to boil, to ferment.] A substance which is capable of causing fermentation, such as yeast or zymase. There are two classes of ferments; insoluble organized ferments, which are microscopic vegetable organisms (yeasts and probably some types of bacteria), and soluble unorganized ferments (enzymes), which are not living organisms. These are probably complex chemical compounds which act as catalysts. Acetone, isopropyl alcohol, and many other important compounds may be produced by the action of ferments. It is probable that all the organized ferments produce their effects by means of the unorganized ferments which they produce. ferment, v.i.

To change chemically through the action of yeast or some other ferment. fermentation, n.

The decomposition of organic substances, caused either by the growth in them of certain living organisms—such as yeasts, molds, and bacteria,-called organized ferments; or by the catalytic action of soluble ferments or enzymes. Probably the living organisms which cause fermentation do so by means of enzymes. Different kinds of fermentation are brought about by different organisms. Alcoholic fermentation in fruit juices is caused by various kinds of yeast plant. This is followed by acetous fermentation, caused by certain bacteria, in which the alcohol is oxidized and acetic acid (the acid of vinegar) is produced. 

Lactic fermentation, the souring of milk, is due to bacteria which produce lactic acid by decomposing the lactose or milk sugar present. 

Putrefactive fermentation, due to bacteria, occurs in protein substances of either animal or vegetable origin. ferric, adj.

In chemistry, pertaining to or containing iron which has a valence of three; as iron in ferric chloride, (FeCl3.) ferrous, adj.

In chemistry, pertaining to or containing iron in that state in which it exhibits a valence of two; as iron in ferrous chloride (FeCl2.) filter, n. [From the Latin noun meaning felt, filled wool.] A porous body or substance through which is passed a liquid which carries solid particles in suspension, in order to purify the liquid, to collect the solid matter, or both; a device containing porous matter for use as above; a strainer; a similar device through which gases are passed. Substances used in filtering are wool or cotton fibers, glass wool, unglazed porcelain, unsized paper, cloth, sand, and charcoal. fix, v.t. 

[From the Latin verb with the same meaning.] 1. To make solid or firm; to fasten; to arrange properly, as one's hair; to repair, as a broken chair. 79

FIXATION 

2. In chemistry, to change into a non-volatile solid; as, soil bacteria fix the nitrogen of the air. 3. In dyeing, to cause the dyestuff to become permanently attached to the fabric. 4. In photography, to treat with hypo so as to render the image permanent. 5. In biology, to cause new qualities, traits, or characteristics of either animals or plants, produced by selective breeding, to become permanent. fixation, n. 

1. The process by which something is fixed; as, the fixation of nitrogen by soil bacteria. 2. The process by which colors produced by dyes are rendered permanent; treating with a mordant. flame, n. [From the Latin noun with the same meaning.] A mass of burning gaseous material. Illuminating gas, when burned in the ordinary gas fixture, gives much light chiefly because solid particles of unburned carbon are carried up with the burning gas and heated to incandescence. The yellow flame thus produced is due to delayed oxidation of part of the carbon of the hydrocarbons which make up the gas. The same gas, when burned in a gas-stove or a Bunsen burner, is previously mixed with just enough air to supply the oxygen required for complete combustion, with the result that the pale blue flame produced gives much less light and much more heat. The temperatures produced by flames vary, from 300° C. to 3000° C. according to the substances burning and the types of burner used. flexibility, n.

The quality of being easily bent or pliant. A specific property of a substance which makes it easy to bend a body composed of that substance without breaking it. Flexible bodies may or may not be elastic. A watch-spring is both flexible and elastic; cloth is flexible but not elastic. fluorescence, n. [From fluorspar a mineral in which the property was first discovered.] 

In chemistry and physics, 1. The property, exhibited by certain liquids and solids, of becoming self luminous when subjected to sunlight, light from the X-ray tube, some other source of ultra-violet light, the emanation from radium, or the stream of electrons from the Coolidge tube. 

2. The light given off by fluorescent bodies. Solutions which exhibit fluorescence do so only while the exciting cause is actually affecting them though fluorescent solids usually continue to glow for a short time after the exciting cause ceases to act, and thus become for a time phosphorescent. Fluorescent light is usually, though not always, of greater wave length than the light which gives rise to it. It never consists of light of a single color even though caused by light of one color. 

Alkaline solutions of fluorescein when viewed by daylight or direct sunlight exhibit a greenish-yellow fluorescence. Solutions of quinine sulphate under the same conditions show a pale blue fluorescence.

Many samples of lubricating oils show either blue or green fluorescence. 80

FLUORIDE 

There is no satisfactory theory accounting for all the facts relating to fluorescence. fluoride, n.

A compound formed by the combination of fluorine with a basic element or radical; a salt of hydrofluoric acid; as, sodium fluoride, NaF, and calcium fluoride, CaF2. fluorine, n. [From fluorite, one of the first minerals from which the element was obtained.] Symbol F; atomic weight 19; valence 1; density 38 (hydrogen=1); boiling point -187° C. A pale greenish-yellow gaseous element of the halogen group and so active that it is never found free in nature. It combines with nearly all the elements except oxygen, nitrogen, and the rare gases of the helium group. The chief fluorine containing minerals are fluorite (CaF2), a widely distributed mineral and cryolite (AlF3 3NaF) found chiefly in Greenland and in smaller quantities in various parts of the United States. The element was first isolated by the French chemist Moissan in 1886, by the electrolysis of liquid hydrofluoric acid (HF). The most important compounds of fluorine are hydrofluoric acid (HF) (q.v.) and its salts, the fluorides. flux, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to flow.] 1. A substance having a low melting point and great fluidity when melted which is used when soldering and brazing to promote a better contact between the solder and the surfaces which it is to unite. Rosin is commonly used as the flux when soldering tinware, and salammoniac, niter (saltpeter), borax, tartar (crude acid potassium tartrate), etc. when a more efficient flux is required. 2. Any substance added to metals or minerals when they are placed in a furnace for melting which fuses readily and becomes highly fluid. It increases the average fluidity of the whole furnace charge, and, because it is lighter than the metal with which it is mixed, it floats to the surface and carries with it, in suspension, undesirable foreign matter. 

When it has formed a layer over the surface of the molten metal, it protects the metal against oxidation. The melted flux and the included substances are drawn off as they accumulate, and solidify into slag (q.v.). In smelting iron, limestone is the commonest flux, and the addition of a small amount of fluorspar (fluorite, CaF2) greatly increases the fluidity. While limestone is a basic flux, acid fluxes, such as silica, are also used. 3. A fusible glassy enamel used as a base for less fusible enamels and to furnish a surface suitable for enamel painting. 4. In physics the total number of lines of magnetic field which are included in a circuit. flywheel, n.

A wheel with a heavy rim, attached to a rotating shaft, the purpose of which is to render steady or uniform the motion of a machine. If the force which keeps the machine in motion or if the resistance which it 81

FOCAL 

meets in doing its work is intermittent, a flywheel is useful. Flywheels are employed on stationary steam engines, gasoline engines, sewing machines, etc. focal, adj.

In physics, of or pertaining to the focus of a lens or mirror. focus, n. [From the Latin noun meaning hearth or fireplace.] In physics. 1. The point at which light or heat rays meet after being reflected from a surface or refracted by passing through a lens; the place where an image is formed by light reflected by a mirror or refracted by a lens; the point at which reflected sound waves converge. 2. In optical instruments, focal length; the distance between the center of the object glass, or objective, and the point where parallel light rays are brought to a focus: as, the focus of the Yerkes telescope of the University of Chicago is about 62 feet. 3. The proper adjustment of an optical instrument to secure accurate results; as, the camera was out of focus. foot pound.

The unit of work in the English system; the work done by a force of one pound acting through a distance of one foot. The foot pound is equivalent to 13,560,000 ergs. force, n. 

[A word of Scandinavian origin.] In physics. 1. Anything which is capable of setting a body in motion, increasing or decreasing its rate of motion, changing the direction of its motion, or bringing a moving body to rest. 2. The interaction of two bodies which is capable of causing a change in the condition of either one or of both as to rest or motion; as, the force of gravity. 3. The interaction of two bodies which is capable of changing any kind of relation which exists between them; as, cohesive force the force which holds molecules of the same kind together, or the force of gravitation, which holds the planets in their orbits around the sun. -form. 

A suffix meaning in the shape of; in the form of; as, cuneiform, meaning wedge-shaped (from cuneus meaning wedge+form);-equivalent to -oid. formaldehyde, n. [From formic acid, which is produced when formaldehyde is oxidized.] 

A colorless gas (HCHO) usually obtained by the partial oxidation of wood alcohol (CH3OH). The gas is irritating to the eyes and has a penetrating odor. It is soluble in water and alcohol. A water solution containing 37% of the gas by weight is sold under the name of formalin (q.v.). The gas and its solutions have powerful antiseptic and germicidal properties and are widely used in medicine and sanitation. Formaldehyde is remarkable for the fact that a solution of the gas in water, if allowed to stand exposed to the air or if slowly evaporated over sulphuric acid, readily forms a white insoluble wax-like solid known as 82

FORMALIN 

paraformaldehyde or paraform. This solid is made into candles and sold as a disinfecting agent. When heated it gives off gaseous formaldehyde. Formaldehyde is a member of a series of compounds which react with certain aromatic hydroxyl compounds (the phenols) to form hard rubber-like solids. Large quantities of formaldehyde are now used in the manufacture of these substances. formalin, n. [From formaldehyde.] A trade name for a solution of 37% of formaldehyde by weight in water. The solution also contains some wood alcohol as an impurity. Formalin is used as a disinfectant and germicide; in the synthesis of dyes, synthetic resins and lacquers; in the manufacture of rubber goods, inks, artificial leather; in photography as a reducing agent; as a preservative; and in the hardening of tissues in laboratory technique. When formalin is employed for preserving plants a 3% solution in water is frequently used; for animal tissues a 5% solution is considered best. In using formalin as a fumigating agent, it is generally poured over a small quantity of potassium permanganate, which oxidizes a small part of the aldehyde, thus generating heat and causing the release of the gaseous formaldehyde from the rest of the solution. formula, n. [From the Latin noun, forma, meaning a form.] 1. In chemistry, a combination of symbols indicating the composition of the molecules of a chemical compound or element; as, NaCl, the formula for the molecule of sodium chloride. When the molecules of a compound contain more than one atom of a given element, that fact is indicated by means of a small number at the right of the base of the symbol for the element. 

Thus, H2SO4, the formula for sulphuric acid, indicates that each molecule of sulphuric acid is made up of two atoms of hydrogen, one atom of sulphur (the sub-number one is always understood if no other number is used), and four atoms of oxygen. Groups of atoms (radicals) are enclosed in parenthesis when more than one group is present in the molecule, thus (NH4)2SO4. The formula representing a gaseous element is always written so as to express the actual composition of the gas, thus O2 to indicate gaseous oxygen which contains two atoms to the molecule. 

If the atomic weights of the included elements are known, a formula when completely interpreted indicates, (1) the kinds of atoms making up the molecule; (2) the number of atoms of each element present in one molecule of the compound; (3) the relative weights of the elements present; (4) the molecular weight of the compound, and (5) if gaseous, the weight in grams of 22.4 liters of the compound at 0° C. and 760 mm. pressure. In all formulas for chemical compounds in pharmacy the replaceable hydrogen of acids stands first in the formula. 2. In algebra, a rule expressed in algebraic symbols. 3. In pharmacy, a recipe. formulate, v.t.

To express in terms of a formula; to state definitely and concisely, as though in terms of a formula. 83

FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION 

fractional distillation.

The process of gradually separating the ingredients of a liquid mixture, if they have different boiling points, by vaporizing the mixture in a closed vessel, and condensing the vapors, collecting the various distillates in separate vessels as the temperature rises to the boiling point of each ingredient in succession. 

Fraunhofer lines. [Named after Fraunhofer, who discovered their existence in 1814.] Dark lines in the sun's spectrum, believed to correspond to the bright lines in the spectra of the elements that make up the sun's glowing atmosphere; the dark lines in the absorption spectrum of any of the elements. 

When a solid is heated to incandescence it gives off light which, when passed through the spectroscope, gives a continuous spectrum (i.e. one which has all the colors present). If a body of gas is placed between the incandescent body and the spectroscope, the spectrum is no longer continuous but now marked by black lines which correspond exactly to the bright lines which the gas itself produces when luminous. Thus the solar spectrum is crossed by dark lines which are due to the presence in the sun's atmosphere of gases through which the light from the incandescent center of the sun must pass to reach us. These dark lines are the Fraunhofer lines and the dark line spectra are called absorption spectra (q.v.). free, adj. 

1. In chemistry, not combined with other elements; as, free oxygen. 

2. In botany, not united with other parts; as, the flower is free from the stem. freeze, v.t. and v.i. [Of Anglo-Saxon origin.] To change from the liquid to the solid state of matter because of the loss of latent heat. See freezing point. freezing mixture.

A mixture which produces intense cold because it contains a substance which absorbs heat when it dissolves. The commonest one is a mixture of ice and salt, such as is used in an ice cream freezer. The heat which causes the salt to dissolve is taken from the cream, whose temperature is thus reduced below its freezing point. The lowest temperature which can be obtained by a mixture of one part of common salt and three parts of snow is -22° C.; by calcium chloride (CaCl2 6H2O) and snow (CaCl2·6H₂O) is - 55° C.; by chloroform and solid carbon dioxide -77° C.; and by sulphur dioxide and solid carbon dioxide -82° C. freezing point. 

1. That temperature at which a body of liquid, if it is losing its latent heat, normally begins to pass into the solid state,―i.e., the temperature at which part of it freezes, leaving the rest of it with its temperature unchanged. The insertion of the word normally in this definition excludes cases of undercooling or supercooling (q.v.). As heat is with- 84

FRICTION 

drawn from a liquid the temperature falls and the liquid is being cooled. 

For each degree of fall in temperature a very definite amount of heat must be withdrawn from each gram of the liquid. When a sufficient amount of heat has been extracted from a given body of liquid, the temperature finally ceases to fall and the liquid begins to freeze. No further drop in temperature takes place until all the liquid has changed to the solid state, the amount of liquid solidifying being in proportion to the amount of heat withdrawn. If, after all the liquid has become solid, heat is still further withdrawn, the temperature will be reduced again. 

The temperature at which solidification takes place is called the freezing point of the liquid, and the amount of heat which must be withdrawn to solidify a gram of the liquid, at this temperature, is called the latent heat of fusion. 2. The temperature at which the liquid and the solid forms of a substance will remain in contact without a change in either, at atmospheric pressure. For water this temperature is 0° C. or 32° F. friction, n.. 

[From the Latin verb meaning to rub.] 1. The act of rubbing one surface against another. 2. In mechanics, the resistance to the movement of one body over another with which it is in contact. True friction, called sliding friction or external friction to distinguish it from so-called rolling friction and internal friction, is partly due to cohesion or adhesion, but is for the most part due to the existence of projections on even the smoothest surface which fit into corresponding depressions in the other surface, making it necessary to force the surfaces apart before motion can be produced. Friction is not dependent upon the amount of surface in contact, nor upon the velocity with which the bodies pass over each other. It depends wholly upon the nature of the surfaces and the force with which they are pressed together. It varies directly with this force. 

The resistance encountered when one of the two bodies is round and rolls over the other is much less than when both are flat and they slide over each other. This resistance is called rolling friction, and it is due to the distortion of one or both of the surfaces in contact. This distortion of a solid occurs only when the pressure partially overcomes cohesion and causes the molecules to assume new positions in relation to each other. 

This same resistance to the movement of molecules over one another which causes rolling friction in solids is exhibited by liquids in less degree, and is called internal friction. It is the basis of the specific property viscosity (q.v.). To decrease friction (i.e., real friction or sliding friction), roller bearings or ball bearings are used, and to decrease the distortion in these, very rigid materials are used in their construction. 

A lubricant decreases friction by substituting internal friction of the lubricant for sliding friction of the solids to which it is applied. The friction between solids and fluids is great enough to require important consideration when designing ships, air-planes, and automobile bodies. 

While friction is a disadvantage in many places, it is necessary in others. 

Nails, screws, jackscrews, brakes, etc. would be useless without it. The 85

FRUCTOSE 

energy which is used to overcome friction is transformed into and reappears as heat. fructose, n. [From the Latin noun meaning fruit+-ose.] A sugar (C6H12O6), also called fruit sugar, and levulose (q.v.). fulcrum, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to prop up or support.] 1. In mechanics and in common usage, an object which gives support to a lever or about which a lever turns as it presses against a body in lifting or moving it. 

2. In physics, the point about which a lever turns. fulminate, v.i. [From the Latin noun meaning a thunderbolt.] To explode with a loud report. fulminate, n.

In chemistry, a salt of fulminic acid; mercury fulminate. fulminate of mercury.

A white crystalline powder, Hg(CNO)2, which produces a violent explosion when struck. It is used in making percussion caps, detonating fuses, etc. fumigate, v.t. [From the Latin noun fumus meaning smoke+ago meaning to drive.] 

To destroy disease-producing bacteria or other undesirable organisms by the action of poisonous fumes or vapors. The gas formaldehyde, the fumes of hydrocyanic acid and of burning sulphur (SO2), etc., are used in fumigating. fungicide, n. [Fungicide, from Latin noun meaning a killer.] Any substance which can be used to kill fungi; a germicide. Fungicides are used chiefly in the treatment of seeds to kill the spores of fungi which cause a destructive disease on the growing plant. For this purpose solutions of formaldehyde or mercuric chloride are frequently used. Such solutions are applied to seed potatoes and to practically all grain seeds. Fungicides are also used on growing plants to control fungous diseases or to prevent their attack. Sprays of Bordeaux mixture (copper sulphate, lime and water) are widely used for this purpose. Sulphur and sulphide sprays are also very effective in preventing the growth, or in killing fungi, especially the mildews. Potassium iodide and tincture of iodine have been found effective in destroying a fungus called favus which attacks the human scalp and also the fungus which causes ringworm. See insecticide and antiseptic. fuse, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to pour.] To melt; to heat until liquid; to cause to flow together while molten. 86

FUSE 

fuse, n. 

1. A device for causing the explosion of a blasting charge, a cannon, a shell, a fire-cracker, or other piece of fire-works. The older type of fuse consisted of a tube, a hollow pipe, or cord, of paper, cloth, or metal, and filled with some highly combustible material. In use, one end of the fuse was inserted in the explosive charge and the other end ignited. 

When the fuse had burned throughout its length to the explosive charge, the explosion took place. By varying the length of the fuse, the explosion could be made to take place at any desired time. The newer fuses may be of the percussion type, or of the mechanically timed type. The percussion fuse is a device which causes the explosion of the charge when it strikes a hard object or when an object comes in contact with it. The time fuse is controlled by clockwork. 2. In practical electricity, a wire or strip of metal, which is used as a safety device in wiring systems. The metal is of such a nature that it will conduct a given quantity of electricity, but will heat up and melt, thus opening the circuit in which it is placed, when the current flowing through it exceeds a certain value. fusible, adj.

Capable of being converted to a liquid by heat. fusible alloy, fusible metal. 

See low melting alloy. fusion, n. 

1. In physics, the act or operation of changing a solid to a liquid by the application of heat. 2. The state of being fluid because of heat. 87

galvanic, adj.

G [From Galvani whose observations led to the discovery of current electricity.] Pertaining to galvanism, an old term now rarely used. galvanometer, n.

An instrument for detecting the presence of, or determining the magnitude or direction of an electric current in a curcuit, usually by noting the effect of the current on a magnetic needle. The tangent galvanometer is an instrument by which an electric current can be detected or measured by noting the effect of a current-carrying conductor upon a compass needle suspended in the earth's magnetic field. When this galvanometer is used, the coil is placed in the plane of the earth's magnetic meridian, the current passed, and the direction and the angle noted through which the needle is deflected. The amount of current passed is always in proportion to the tangent of the angle of deflection. 

The D'Arsonval or suspended coil galvanometer consists of a coil of fine wire suspended in a strong magnetic field between the poles of a permanent magnet. The coil is suspended by a fine bronze wire through which it is connected to the source of the current to be measured. The magnetic field between the poles of the magnet is so strong when compared to the earth's field as to make the latter negligible. Likewise other fields near it have little effect, so that the instrument can be used in many places where other galvanometers would be inaccurate. The 

D'Arsonval instrument is, therefore more generally useful than any of the various other types of galvanometers. gamma rays.

In chemistry and physics, the highly penetrating rays given off by certain radioactive substances. They are not material, but, like the Roentgen or X-rays, consist of transverse vibrations set up in the ether. The magnetic or electric field does not deflect them. See radium. gas, n. 

[From a Greek noun meaning a formless space.] 1. Matter in the gaseous state. 2. A substance which has absorbed enough heat to overcome the cohesion between its molecules, so that they no longer attract one another but move away from one another. Under these circumstances the substance will expand indefinitely and the smallest quantity of it will distribute itself uniformly throughout an enclosing space of any size. In recent years it has become possible to liquefy what has been called the "permanent gases," so that there is no longer any real reason for distinguishing between the terms gas and vapor, but it is still convenient to apply the word gas to those substances which at ordinary temperatures and pressures are in the gaseous state, and reserve the word vapor for those which by moderate amounts of heat can be rendered gaseous: as, the gas oxygen, or hydrogen, or nitrogen, but mercury vapor, iodine vapor, water vapor. 88

GASEOUS 

gaseous, adj.

Pertaining to, or having the form or nature of gas. gasolene (gasoline), n.

A mixture of paraffin hydrocarbons usually containing all the members from C6H14 to C10H22, obtained by fractionally distilling crude petroleum and collecting the distillate at temperatures between 60° and 190° C. The name gasolene is now given to all mixtures boiling between the temperatures mentioned. Formerly other names were given to more limited fractions; as ligroin or naphtha, 40° to 120° C; and benzine, 120° to 150° C. The term gasolene thus includes the fractions formerly known as naphtha, ligroin, and benzine. The quality of gasolene was formerly thought to be indicated by its specific gravity. It was assumed that the lighter the gasolene the better suited it was for a motor fuel. In measuring the density the Baumé hydrometer came to be the standard instrument. Since this hydrometer does not indicate the specific gravity directly, but in degrees Baumé, which varies inversely as the specific gravity, a high test gasolene is in reality one of low specific gravity (and also a low boiling fraction), while a low test gasolene is one of high specific gravity (and is a high boiling fraction). 

Normal or straight run gasolene contains all the hydrocarbons just as they are obtained by distillation from the crude petroleum. Blended gasolene is usually a high boiling fraction (often almost a kerosene) to which has been added a small quantity of low boiling gasolene so that it will ignite readily. Some blended gasolenes make very desirable motor fuel. Casing head gasolene is a very low boiling gasolene obtained by extracting low boiling hydrocarbons from natural gas, where they are held in solution in methane and other gases present. Cracked gasolene is made by breaking up the molecules of the heavy hydrocarbons, such as are found in lubricating and other heavy oils, to make lighter molecules, such as are found in gasolene. In this process the heavy oils are subjected to high temperature and pressure. Recently benzole gasolene, which is a mixture of benzole (C6H6) and gasolene, has appeared on the market. It is claimed that mixtures of this type do not cause detonation or knocking in the motor. Tetra ethyl lead (Pb(C2H5) 4), is also dissolved in small amounts in gasolene to prevent detonation. 

Gasolene is used as a motor fuel, as a solvent, as a vehicle in paints, as a fuel in lamps and stoves, and in the manufacture of insecticides, metal polishes, and hundreds of other products. gel, n.

In chemistry, a colloid containing much water, yet firm in consistency; a jelly-like colloidal solid. This term has been introduced by the comparatively new colloidal chemistry. When a one percent solution of gelatine in hot water is allowed to cool, it solidifies into a transparent semi-solid mass. When a concentrated solution of sodium silicate is acidified with acetic acid, it forms a pasty glass like solid. These are gels. 

Many animal tissues which have incorporated much water are also gels. 89

GEL gel, v.t. 

The act or process of being or forming a gel. gelatine, n [From a Latin verb meaning to congeal.] A pale yellow or colorless nitrogenous organic substance obtained by the prolonged boiling in water of horns, hides, hoofs, and tendons of animals. It is in many ways like a very pure glue. If brought into contact with water it absorbs from 40 to 60 times its weight of water, forming a jelly,. With more water, a colloidal solution is obtained. The solid produced by evaporation of gelatine solutions may be either ground or in flakes, in which form it is marketed. It is considered a wholesome food. -gen gen- A prefix and suffix used in forming nouns and adjectives, derived from the Greek verb meaning to produce, to give birth to. generate, v.t. [From the Latin verb meaning to generate, to beget.] 1. To originate; to cause to start. 2. To produce; to create; to bring into existence. 3. In physics, to produce by physical action; as, to generate a current of electricity. 4. In chemistry, to produce by a chemical process; as, to generate oxygen by heating a chlorate. 5. In mathematics, to trace out, as a line is traced out or generated by a moving point; a surface by a moving line; a solid by a moving surface. generation, n. 

1. Reproduction; the act or process of generating. 2. One of the stages or steps by which one descends from a remote ancestor; all the descendents having the same relationship to a common ancestor; all the men or lower animals or plants living at any one time; the average lifetime of man; or the usual or average period which elapses between the successive steps in genealogical descent. generator, n. The apparatus by which some desired substance or form of energy is generated or produced; as, a hydrogen generator; an electric generator; a steam generator. germicide, n. [Germ+the Latin verb caedere meaning to kill.] Anything which kills germs. The term germ here includes all microorganisms which cause disease. Thus, all microscopic forms of plants and animals are included, but especially bacteria. Nearly all germicides are active chemical compounds or mixtures. In a few cases active elements such as chlorine and sulphur are used. Their action usually depends upon their oxidizing power, though some kill by their poisoning action. Chlorine, bromine, iodine, hypochlorites, hydrogen peroxide, and potassium permanganate 90

The material used in producing a glaze as defined above. globule, n. [From a Latin noun meaning a little globe.] A small ball or pellet of matter, particularly of a liquid or of metal which has been molten; a pill; a small shot. glucose, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning sweet+ose.] A sugar (C6H12O6), found in honey and in the juices of many fruits, especially grapes. The crystals seen in raisins (dried grapes) are glucose. For this reason it is sometimes called grape sugar. Glucose is about three-fifths as sweet as cane sugar and is very soluble in water. It can be made commercially by heating starch with a dilute acid. Corn starch so treated produces the corn sirup of commerce, which is a mixture of glucose, dextrin, and maltose, often with a small amount of cane molasses added to give it flavor. Glucose solutions have the power to rotate a plane of polarized light to the right, and for this reason the sugar is often called dextrose. 

In this respect glucose differs from fructose (levulose), a sugar having the same formula but whose solutions rotate polarized light to the left. 

Glucose and levulose are produced in equal quantities when cane sugar is heated with dilute acids. Glucose reduces Fehling's solution, and ferments readily. When wine is made from grapes, the glucose in the grape juice is converted into alcohol by an enzyme which is found in the skin of the grape. Great quantities of glucose are used in the manufacture of candy, in the production of table sirup and for sweetening canned foods. gluten, n. [From the Latin noun meaning glue.] A tough, sticky, gray substance which, when water is added, is formed from two protein ingredients of flour-gliadin and glutenin. It makes dough tenacious and therefore capable of becoming light, and it is more abundant in wheat than in the other bread cereals. Since nearly all of the protein part of bread is in the gluten, it gives to bread an important part of its food value. 

Gluten can be obtained by washing dough under a stream of cold water, 91

GLYCOGEN 

the starch and soluble parts being carried away and the gluten left behind. glycogen, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning sweet+-gen.] A white tasteless carbohydrate, very similar to starch and with the same general formula (C6H10O5). It occurs in the liver, in the leucocytes, cartilage and other tissues. It is an amorphous powder, soluble in water, giving a red coloration with iodine. Its aqueous solutions are strongly dextrorotatory. It is formed and stored in the liver and converted into sugar as the needs of the body require; also called animal starch. gold, n.

Symbol Au. (From the Latin word aurum, meaning gold.) Atomic weight 197.2; specific gravity 19.32; valence 1 and 3; melting point 

1063° C. A heavy yellow metal found native in many parts of the world, and used from most ancient times as a medium of exchange, and as a material for the manufacture of jewelry. The metal electrum used by the Egyptians was an alloy of gold with silver. It is probable that this alloy was thought to be a single metal, as gold and silver frequently are found together. Usually the amount of silver is less than 10% and the alloy is yellow in color. As the proportion of silver increases the color becomes lighter. Gold found in alluvial deposits may be separated by washing away the lighter material with water. Quartz bearing gold ore is crushed and washed over cleats of amalgamated copper. The gold amalgam thus formed is heated in a retort to drive off the mercury and the residue treated with hot sulphuric acid to remove the silver. Some gold ores are washed with a solution of sodium cyanide which dissolves the gold. By electrolysis or treatment with zinc the pure metal is precipitated. Gold is not affected by the gases of the atmosphere, nor by hydrochloric, nitric, or sulphuric acids acting singly. It is soluble in aqua regia, in chlorine water, and in potassium cyanide solution. 

Gold is the most malleable and ductile of all metals, so it can be beaten into extremely thin sheets called leaf gold, which is widely used as a pigment; and can be drawn into extremely fine wire. Gold is too soft a metal for many purposes, and must be alloyed with some other metal, such as copper. Coin gold contains ten percent of its weight of copper. 

Most gold used in the manufacture of jewelry is alloyed with copper. 

Pure gold is referred to as 24 carat gold. See carat. 

Goldschmidt process. 

1. The process of welding by means of thermit (q.v.). This process was developed by the German chemist Goldschimdt. 2. A method of reducing a metallic oxide by means of a powdered metal, the oxide of which has a higher heat of formation; as, the preparation of chromium by means of metallic aluminum, thus: Al₂+Cr2O3 = Al2O3+2Cr. See thermit. gram, n. [From a Greek noun meaning a small weight, a letter.] The fundamental unit of weight in the metric system, being the weight of one cubic 92

centimeter of pure water at 4° C., the temperature of greatest density.

The number of grams of any compound corresponding to its molecular weight; one mole. See molecular weight. granular, adj. [From a Latin noun meaning grain.] Made up or appearing to be made up or composed of granules; granulated. granule, n. [From a Latin word meaning a small grain.] A small grain or particle; a small crystal-like body. graphite, n. [From a Greek verb meaning to write.] A mineral form of carbon which occurs in six-sided prisms, in sheets, in granular form, and in compact masses. It is known also as plumbago and black lead. As mined it frequently runs from 95 to 99% pure carbon. It is from iron-black to steel-gray in color, metallic in luster, greasy to the touch, 1 to 2 in hardness, and somewhat over 2 in specific gravity. It leaves a shining black mark on paper—hence its most widely known use, as the "lead" of lead pencils. For this purpose it is ground to powder, mixed with fine clay, moistened, and compressed into slender cylinders. Different degrees of hardness in lead pencils are obtained by the use of different percentages of clay. Because graphite is unaffected by high temperatures, it is used in making crucibles, where again it is mixed with clay. As stove-polish it protects iron from rusting. It is widely used as a lubricant on machinery. It conducts electricity and is used for anodes, for making a conducting surface on non-conductors when electroplating, for commutator brushes in dynamos, and as an ingrediant in certain battery compounds. It is artificially produced on a commercial scale by passing an alternating current of electricity through anthracite or coke in a furnace from which air is excluded. Nature produced it by starting with growing vegetation and gradually reducing the percentage of volatile matter by pressure and heat, so that the materials remaining in the ground became successively peat, lignite, bituminous coal, anthracite, and finally graphite. gravitation n. [From The Latin adjective meaning heavy.] That attractive force which tends to draw toward each other all bodies, whether molecules or masses, no matter where located. It causes raindrops to fall to the earth, and holds the earth in its orbit around the sun. Sir Isaac Newton (1642-1727), the English philosopher, formulated the law of universal gravitation that every particle of matter in the universe attracts 93

GRAVITY 

every other particle with a force directly proportional to the product of their masses, and inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them. gravity, n.

The attraction which the earth exerts for bodies at or near its surface. 

It is one manifestation of gravitation, and is the cause of weight. 

Compare with gravitation. grid, n. [From an old English verb meaning to scorch.] 1. A perforated or ridged plate of lead which is used in the storage battery. The apertures or holes in the grid are filled with a paste of lead oxide and allowed to dry, thus producing the plate which is the active part of the battery. 2. In radio, the helix of fine wire which is inserted between the plate and filament of the vacuum tube, and which controls the flow of electrons from the filament to the plate, thus causing the tube to act as a valve for the control of the flow of energy. The grid acts as a gate which is controlled by comparatively small amounts of energy, but which permits very large amounts of energy to flow through the tube. See vacuum tube. gum, n. [From a Greek noun with the same meaning.] Any one of a large number of non-crystalline, tasteless substances which are usually formed by the drying out of liquids exuded by plants. True gums are either soluble in cold water, such as gum arabic, used in making mucilage; soluble in hot water, such as cherry gum, which is produced by diseased tissues of the cherry tree; or they merely swell up into a glue-like mass when in contact with water. None of them is soluble in alcohol. The term is inaccurately applied to certain resins, oleoresins, and fossil resins, such as dammar, elemi, copal, etc., which are used in the manufacture of varnishes, and also to chicle, the basis of most "chewing gum." guncotton, n. 

See nitrocellulose. gypsum, n. [From the Greek noun meaning plaster or chalk.] A soft cyrstalline mineral which is transparent (colorless) when pure and splits easily into thin layers. It is a hydrated calcium sulphate (CaSO4 · 2HO) and is found in extensive deposits in many parts of the world. Ground fine and scattered over the surface of the ground it has the effect of a fertilizer, acting on the ammonium carbonate of the soil and converting it into the less volatile sulphate. Gypsum is also used as a paint pigment and as a filler for paper. When it occurs in crystalline masses it is called selenite. When white (or sometimes gray, yellow, or red), it is called alabaster and is sometimes carved into vases, statuettes, etc. When carefully heated, gypsum loses about three fourths of its water of hydration and forms the substance known as plaster of paris,

H halide, n. [From halogen.] In chemistry, a salt or ester of one of the halogens (q.v.). halogen, n. [From Greek words meaning salt and producing.] In chemistry, a name given to any one of four elements, fluorine (F), chlorine (Cl), bromine (Br), and iodine (I), all of which with the exception of fluorine, occur in sea salt. For this reason and also because they combine with practically all other elements they are called halogens, or producers of salt. 

These elements, because of their great activity, their acid character, similarity in valence and other properties, are often spoken of as the halogen family. With hydrogen they all form compounds which are soluble in water, producing a strong acid solution. Salts of the halogen acids, (HF, HCl, HBr, and HI), are called halides. Common salt, (NaCl), hydrochloric acid (HCl), and potassium iodide (KI), are the most common halogen compounds. hardness, n. [Of Anglo-Saxon origin.] 1. The ability to resist scratching or abrasion; a specific property of substances which enables them to resist abrasion (the wearing away of surface particles by friction) or scatching (abrasion by a point along a narrow strip on the surface). The stronger the cohesion between the molecules of a substance, the greater is its degree of hardness. While hardness and brittleness are often possessed by the same substance, there is no necessary connection between these properties. In order that degrees of hardness might be definitely expressed, Mohs selected ten minerals and arranged them in their proper order on the basis of hardness. Talc, which can be scratched with the thumb-nail, represents in this series the lowest degree of hardness, and is given the number 1. Number 10 is crystallized carbon-the diamond, the hardest known substance. The Mohs scale in full is as follows: Talc, 1; rock-salt, 2; calcite, 3; fluor-spar, 4; apatite, 5; feldspar, 6; rockcrystal, 7; topaz, 8; corundum, 9; diamond, 10. The hardness of all substances may now be expressed in terms of this scale. The table which follows shows the hardness of a few substances.

HARDNESS OF WATER 

hardness of water.

A condition or quality of water which is due chiefly to the presence of dissolved calcium, magnesium and iron compounds, usually the sulphates and bicarbonates. These salts render water less desirable for steam purposes as they cause the formation on the interior of the boilers of a hard layer of solid matter which is a poor conductor of heat. 

Hardness also renders water less desirable for laundry purposes becuase the dissolved salts combine with soap to form an insoluble scum (calcium and iron soaps) which sticks to the clothes. The hardness of water is generally recognized as being of two kinds, temporary hardness which can be removed by boiling and permanent hardness, which persists after boiling. Temporary hardness is caused chiefly by the presence of bicarbonates of calcium, magnesium and iron. These soluble bicarbonates on boiling are converted into the insoluble carbonates which settle to the bottom or deposit on the sides of the vessel. Permanent hardness is caused by the presence of sulphates (and rarely the chlorides) of calcium, magnesium and ferric iron. These salts can only be removed by chemical treatment. The process of removing or destroying the hardness of water is spoken of as "softening" or "breaking" and may be carried out by the addition of lime (CaO), and soda ash (Na2CO3). 

These compounds cause the precipitation of calcium, magnesium and iron as carbonates. The soda ash acts chiefly on those dissolved salts which cause permanent hardness and the lime chiefly on those salts which cause temporary hardness. heat, n. [From the Anglo-Saxon noun hoetu having the same meaning.] A form of kinetic energy; the mechanical energy of the moving molecules of a body. The molecules of all bodies on earth are in motion and the energy represented by this motion is heat.. If the molecules of a given body are in more rapid motion than those of another like body, the first body is said to be hotter than the second, or its temperature is said to be higher. Thus the temperature, or intensity of heat of like bodies depends upon the speed of their molecules and is independent of the mass of matter present. The quantity of heat in a body depends upon: a) the temperature of the body, i.e., the speed of its molecules. b) the number of its molecules, or mass of the body and c) the nature of the molecules composing the body (molecules are not alike in their heat capacities). If a body having a higher temperature is brought near a body having a lower temperature heat will flow from the hotter to the colder body until the temperature of the two bodies is the same. 

When heat is transferred from a hotter to a colder body, the molecules of the colder body are forced into rapid motion which may if sufficiently increased cause them to disintegrate with the conversion of their kinetic energy into other form or forms of energy such as light, electricity or chemical energy. When heat is extracted from a body the motion of the molecules is reduced and the temperature falls. In theory, all molecular motion would cease at -273° C., or 0° A. See alsolute zero. 96

HEAT OF COMBUSTION 

heat of combustion.

In chemistry, the heat evolved by the combustion (oxidation) of one gram molecule (the molecular weight in grams) of any pure compound. 

For example, 28 is the molecular weight of carbon monoxide, hence the weight of one gram molecule is 28 grams. When 28 grams of carbon monoxide is burned 68,200 calories of heat are liberated, and so it follows that the heat of combustion of carbon monoxide is 68,200 calories. helium, n. [From the Greek noun meaning the sun.] Symbol He; atomic weight 4; valence O; density 1.98 times that of hydrogen; boiling point — 269° C.

A gaseous element discovered by spectrum analysis of sunlight by, Lockyer in 1868, and first isolated in the laboratory by Ramsay in 1895. Since it combines chemically with no other element it will neither burn nor explode, and has been used as a substitute for hydrogen in the inflation of air ships. The relative buoyancy of He is .92 that of hydrogen. Since the buoyancy of a gas in air is determined by its relative weight as compared with air, the buoyancy of helium as compared to hydrogen is found by the formula (28.955–2)/(28.955 −4) = 

0.92, where 28.955 grams is the weight of 22.4 liters of air, 2 is the weight of 22.4 liters of hydrogen, and 4 is the weight of 22.4 liters of helium. 

Thus helium has 92% of the buoyancy of hydrogen. The United 

States government developed a method of obtaining Helium by fractionating the compressed natural gas of Texas and Oklahoma and just at the close of the World War was prepared to supply the allies with this gas in commercial quantities. helix, n. [From a Greek verb meaning to turn around.] A wire or wire-like object, in the form of a spiral; as the coil of wire around an electromagnet. hematite, n. [From the Greek adjective meaning bloodlike.] The name of an iron ore, an oxide of iron, (Fe2O3), found in nearly all parts of the world, but especially in the Lake Superior region in the United States. It ranges in color from light brown to black. Prepared artificially, it is a bright red powder used as pigment (Venetian red) and as a polishing powder (rouge). Hematite is the most important iron ore mined in the United 

States, 62,000,000 tons having been produced in 1924. Metallic iron is obtained from hematite by the reducing action of carbon, usually introduced in the form of coke, in the blast furnace. hemoglobin, n. [Hemo-meaning related to blood+globin, the name of a blood protein.] The iron compound which gives the color to the red corpuscles of the blood and which serves as a carrier of oxygen from the air which enters the lungs to the tissues within the body. In the presence of air the hemoglobin combines with oxygen, forming an unstable substance 97

HEXANE 

called oxyhemoglobin which is bright red in color. The oxygen thus acquired by the hemoglobin may be carried to the tissues of the body where it is readily given up and reduced hemoglobin again formed. 

This is blue or purle in color, and has great affinity for oxygen. Arterial blood is red because the hemoglobin is carrying a quantity of oxygen. 

Venous blood is blue or purple because most of the oxygen has been lost. When the blue venous blood is exposed to the air, it immediately combines with oxygen and again becomes red in color. Hemoglobin forms a very stable compound with carbon monoxide, thus making this gas very dangerous when breathed. Human blood ordinarily contains about 20% of hemoglobin. hexane, n. [Hex meaning six and referring to the number of carbon atoms in the molecule+ane, an ending given to the names of all members of the methane series.] In chemistry, the sixth member of the methane series of hydrocarbons. It is a colorless, very volatile liquid (C6H14) with a faint, very peculiar odor. The compound is one of the components of ordinary gasolene. It boils at 69° C. and is very inflammable. homo-.

Combining form from the Greek adjective meaning one and the same. homogeneous, adj. [From Greek words meaning same and kind.] 1. Consisting of like parts or components; said of parts of one whole. 2. Having parts of only one kind. hormone, n. [From a Greek verb meaning to rouse, to stir up.] In physiological chemistry, a substance produced within an animal organism which serves as a chemical stimulant or inhibitor and governs the activities of some organ or part. To be effective as a hormone a substance must be (1) incapable of inducing the formation of an antibody; (2) simple in molecular structure, so that it will readily diffuse through cell walls; and (3) easily destroyed or eliminated from the system, so that its effect will not outlast the need for it. Many, but not all hormones occur in internal secretions (see gland). Iodothyrin (formerly called thyroiodin) is a hormone produced by the thyroid body. Secretin, a hormone which stimulates the pancreas is formed in the upper part of the small intestine when the chyle meets the intestinal mucus. Probably many unknown hormones exist. horsepower, n.

The amount of power needed to raise 550 pounds one foot in one second or 33,000 pounds in one minute against the pull of gravity. It is a unit. of power used in rating steam and gasolene engines, or other producers of power. It was originated by James Watt, who manufactured the steam engine which he had invented, and used by him to give prospective customers a definite and understandable idea of what an engine would do. The average draft horse can exert continuously over a 98

HUMID 

stretch of several hours about two-thirds of a horsepower, and Watt is often said to have been inaccurate when he fixed the horsepower at 33,000 foot-pounds of work per minute. On the contrary, he purposely set the figure 50% high so as to aid in building up a demand for a new and poorly known device. humid, adj. [From the Latin verb meaning to be moist.] Moist; damp; containing so much water that its presence is easily perceived (sensible moisture); —applied to air, to climate, to a region, etc. See humidity. humidity, n.

The condition of the atmosphere as to the water vapor which it contains. Humidity varies widely from place to place at the same time, and also from time to time at the same place. Its importance is due to its bearing on human health and comfort, on the cost of heating buildings for human occupancy, on the success of certain manufacturing processes, and on the science of meteorology. It is expressed in two ways, as relative humidity (q.v.) and absolute humidity (q.v.). Since a liter of dry air at 0° C. and 760 mm. weighs 1.293 grams, and water vapor at the same temperature and pressure weighs .806 grams, humid or moist air is lighter than dry air, rather than heavier, as is frequently thought. Consequently, an increase in humidity as well as a rise in temperature may cause a fall in barometric pressure. hydr- hydro-. 

[From the Greek noun meaning water.] In chemistry. 1. A combining form indicating the presence of hydrogen as one of the constituents of the molecule of the compound, or a substance obtained by the reducing action of hydrogen; as, hydrocyanic, a name indicating an acid composed of hydrogen and cyanogen; and hydroquinone, a substance obtained by the reduction of quinone with hydrogen. 2. Indicating the presence of water or the constituents of water; as, hydrate, a substance containing water or having been formed by water. 3. In engineering, a combining from indicating the presence of water or a relationship to water; as, hydroelectric, used to indicate a machine for generating electricity by water power. hydrate, n. [From hydro, meaning water,+ate.] In chemistry, a substance formed by the union of water with some other substance during crystallization, so that a definite amount of water is chemically incorporated; as, CuSO4·5H₂O, formed when copper sulphate crystallizes from its water solutions. The water so incorporated is known as water of hydration or water of crystallization. Hydrates are spoken of as mono, di, tri, etc., according as they contain one, two, or three molecules of water for each molecule of the compound with which the water is combined. Contrast with hydroxide. hydraulic, adj. [From the Latin noun hydraulicus, meaning a water organ.] Referring 99

HYDRIDE to, of, operated by, or caused to function by water or, by analogy, by other fluids. hydride, n. [Hydro+id.] In chemistry, a compound of hydrogen and another element or radical; as silicon hydride SiH4, phosphine or phosphorus hydride (PH3), methane or methyl hydride (CH4 or CH3-H), etc. hydrocarbon, n. [hydro-+carbon.] In chemistry, any of a very large group of compounds whose molecules are made up entirely of hydrogen and carbon atoms. 

All hydrocarbons are combustible, and among them are numerous solid, liquid, and gaseous fuels. Liquid hydrocarbons will not mix with water, and but few of the solid and gaseous ones are even slightly soluble in water. Those hydrocarbons whose molecules contain few atoms are gases (such as methane or marsh gas or fire damp (CH4) the chief component of natural gas and next to hydrogen the most abundant component of the mixture known as illuminating gas); many of those having more atoms to the molecule are liquid (such as hexane C6H14 and benzene C6H6); and those with still more complex molecules are often solids (such as naphthalene (C10Hs), and anthracene (C14H10). Because carbon atoms have a remarkable ability to unite themselves into rings and chains, with hydrogen atoms attached to all other valences, hundreds of hydrocarbon compounds are known and thousands of others can probably be made in the laboratory. By the substitution of other elements and radicals for part of the hydrogen and the carbon of hydrocarbons, many thousands of additional compounds are formed, at least 240,000 hydrocarbon compounds or hydrocarbon derivatives being already known. On the basis of structural relationships, hydrocarbons are classified into two large divisions and these into series each of which is named from its simplest member. The two great divisons of the hydrocarbons are the aliphatic or chain compounds and the aromatic or ring compounds. Each of these great divisions are divided into three subgroups or series. The chain compounds include the methane series, beginning with (CH4); the ethylene series, beginning with (C2H4); and the acetylene series, beginning with (C2H2); and the ring compounds include the benzene series beginning with (C6H6); the naphthalene series beginning with naphthalene (C1oHs), and the anthracene series beginning with (C14H10). hydroelectric, adj. [hydro-+electric.] Pertaining to the production of electricity by using water power to run electric generators or dynamos. hydrogen, n. [From Greek words meaning water and to generate.] Symbol H; atomic weight 1.008; weight .0695 that of air; valence 1; boiling point -252.8°C

A colorless, odorless, highly inflammable gas discovered in 1766 by Cavendish, and one of the constituents of water. It is the lightest element known, and is highly explosive when mixed with air. Hydrogen 100

HYDROGENATION 

is easily obtained by the action of zinc and other metals on acids, or by the electrolysis of water. The gas is used for the inflation of balloons and airships, as a reducing agent in many commercial processes, and in the oxyhydrogen blowpipe. hydrogenation, n.

Causing the addition of hydrogen, usually by means of a catalytic agent. In this process the substance in the vapor state is mixed with hydrogen and passed over hot finely divided nickel. Reduction or combination with hydrogen takes place. When hydrogen is passed through the hot glycerine esters of unsaturated acids, in the presence of finely divided nickel, hydrogenation occurs. By methods similar to this, cotton seed and other oils are converted into lard-like materials, widely used as lard substitutes. hydrogen equivalent. 

See equivalent weight. hydrogen peroxide. [hydrogen+per meaning more than+oxide.] A sirupy liquid compound (H2O2) resembling water in general appearance and made commercially by treating barium peroxide (BaO2) with sulphuric acid. Pure hydrogen peroxide is difficult to prepare and is unstable, decomposing rapidly to form water and oxygen. An aqueous 3% solution of hydrogen peroxide in water is sold commercially as hydrogen peroxide. The commercial reagent is used in the bleaching of wool, hair, and other organic substances; as a mild oxidizing agent in many industrial processes; and as an antiseptic and disinfectant. In all of its reactions hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂) gives up half of its oxygen, leaving water as the decomposition product. hydrogen sulphide. [Hydrogen sulphur.] Also called sulphuretted hydrogen, and hydrosulphuric acid. A colorless, poisonous gas (H₂S), with a strong characteristic odor (of rotten eggs), usually made by treating the sulphide of a metal (such as FeS) with hydrochloric acid. Its water solutions have an acid reaction. Both the gas and its water solutions are important reagents in the chemical laboratory. hydrolysis, n. [hydro+the Greek noun meaning dissolution.] 1. In inorganic chemistry, a chemical reaction in which water causes the decomposition of a compound with the production of an excess of hydrogen (H) ions, or hydroxyl (-OH) ions. Many salts react with water to reform the acid and base from which they were originally produced. This is particularly true of salts formed from weak bases with strong acids, and salts of strong bases with weak acids. (A strong base or a strong acid is one which is highly dissociated in its water solutions.) Thus, copper sulphate (CuSO4) gives an acid reaction to water, due to hydrolysis. When this salt goes into solution in water copper hydroxide (Cu(OH)2 and sulphuric acid (H2SO4) are formed. The copper hydroxide 101

HYDROSTATIC 

is a weak base (not highly ionized), while sulphuric acid is a strong acid (highly ionized). Thus an excess of hydrogen ions are present in solutions of copper sulphate in water, and this solution always has an acid reaction. Salts of strong bases with weak acids yield an excess of hydroxyl ions on hydrolysis. Thus borax (Na2B4O7), potassium cyanide (KCN) and sodium carbonate (Na2CO3), all give water solutions which are distinctly alkaline in reaction; the opposite of neutralization. 2. In organic chemistry, a process of decomposition involving the addition of the elements of water. This process may be brought about by the action of water, by acids, alkalies or by enzymes. Cane sugar (C12H22O11) may be hydrolyzed by the action of enzymes or by boiling its solutions with dilute acids. The cane sugar molecule yields one molecule of dextrose and one molecule of levulose on hydrolysis. Esters may be hydrolyzed by the action of water, yielding an acid and an alcohol. 

Thus fats (glycerine esters of the fatty acids) undergo hydrolysis when treated with superheated steam. Some authorities also include under hydrolysis the action of alkalis on esters, this reaction, however, is usually referred to as saponification. hydrostatic, adj. [From Greek words meaning caused to stand by water.] In physics, produced as a result of the operation of the laws governing the equilibrium and pressure of water and other liquids; pertaining to such laws. hydroxide, n. [hydro+oxide.] In chemistry, a compound formed by the union of an oxide with water, yet not regarded as containing water; a compound containing the (OH) radical; as, Ca(OH)2. Although usually basic, hydroxides may be either neutral or acid, this characteristic being governed by the element the oxide of which enters into combination with the water; as CaO+H2O=Ca(OH)2, a basic hydroxide, and 

CO₂+H2O=H2CO3 an acid hydroxide. Such hydroxides, like hydrates, give up water when heated. Some of them, such as silver hydroxide lose water at ordinary temperatures, changing to the oxide as fast as they are formed. Contrast with hydrate. hydroxyl, n. [hydrogen+oxygen+-yl.] The univalent radical (OH), which, in combination with basic radicals forms hydroxides, and with organic radicals forms alcohols. The presence of an excess of hydroxyl radicals renders a solution alkaline in reaction. hygrometer, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning wet, moist,+meter.] An instrument usually consisting of two thermometers mounted side by side, one having its bulb exposed to the air, the other having its bulb surrounded by some fabric which is kept wet with water. The fabric surrounding the second bulb extends downward into a vessel of water, so that capillary action will carry up a continuous supply of water to evaporate around the bulb. The rate at which this evaporation goes on depends upon both the temperature and the humidity of the air, rapid evapo- 102

HYPER- ration indicating a relatively dry condition of the air. The temperature of the air as shown by the dry bulb and the difference between the temperatures recorded by the two bulbs are then referred to carefully prepared tables which show the relative humidity (q.v.) and the dew point (q.v.). If the air is caused to circulate across the instrument before reading the temperatures, relative humidity may be ascertained in this way with an error of not over 5%. In the recording hygrometer clockwork moves a cylinder upon which a pen is held. The position of the pen is determined by the tension on a dry hair, this tension varying with the moisture in the air. hyper-. [From the Greek prefix meaning over, above.] A prefix meaning over, above, greater, much more than. In chemistry, the same as super- or per, -and now usually shortened to the latter form. hypo-. [From the Greek prefix meaning beneath, lower.] 1. In biology, a prefix meaning below that which is designated by the term to which it is joined, as in hypocotyl. Opposed to hyper-. 2. In chemistry, a prefix used in forming the name of a compound to indicate that it occupies a low or the lowest position in a series whose members differ only in their degrees of oxidation. It is used thus in naming hypochlorous acid (HCIO), and the hypochlorites, to indicate that they contain less oxygen than chlorous acid (HCIO₂) and the chlorites. hyposulphite of soda. 

Sodium thiosulphate (Na2S2O3), a white crystalline salt formed by boiling sodium sulphite (Na2SO3) with sulphur. This salt is called hypo by photographers and is used in dissolving the unused silver bromide from the developed plates and films. Each of the two names for this compound, is intended to show its composition. It is a thio- (from the Greek word for sulphur) sulphate because it .nay be considered a sulphate in which one atom of sulphur has replaced an atom of oxygen. It is a hyposulphite because it contains less oxygen than the corresponding sulphite, the prefix hypo frequently being used to mean less oxygen than. hypothesis, n. [From the Greek noun meaning foundation, supposition.] A generalization tentatively adopted as the probable explanation for a series of related facts; a supposition accepted for the time being or for the purpose of argument, as, explaining a fact or series of related facts; an assumption which seems to be consistent with all known facts but recognized as incompletely established. An hypothesis usually relates to a smaller field than a theory, or at least to phenomena of more limited importance. It is less firmly established than a theory, is supported by less evidence, and is less susceptible of direct proof; as, Proudt's hypothesis, or Avagadro's hypothesis. 103

-ic. 

In Chemistry, (1) a suffix used in forming the name of an acid, generally given to that one of the oxygen containing series, which contains the element that is characteristic of the series in its highest common valence, and with which no prefix is employed; as, sulphuric acid (H2SO4). These acids will, of course, contain more oxygen than other acids of the series. 

In some cases, after a given acid in the series had been named with the -ic suffix, another acid containing more oxygen was discovered. In such a case, the prefix per is used to indicate the still higher state of oxidation. Thus, chloric acid (HClO3), and perchloric acid (HClO4), acids having theic suffix, form salts having ate as a suffix; as, sulphuric acid forms sulphates. (2). A suffix indicating that the chief element in the compound is present in the higher of its common valences; as, ferric chloride (FeCl3) in which iron has a valence of three, and cupric oxide (CuO) in which copper has a valence of two. -id, -ide.
