In chemistry, a suffix used in forming the name of (1) a salt of a hydroacid (one which contains no oxygen) or (2) any binary compound, whether it contains two elements (as hydrogen chloride, HCl), an element and a radical (as ammonium chloride, NH4Cl), or two radicals (as ammonium hydroxide, NH4OH). ignition, n. im- 

[From Latin words meaning to fire.] 1. The act or process of causing a substance or mixture to ignite or explode; the act of starting the process of combustion or of chemical action, in which heat and light are evolved. 2. The state of being exploded or ignited. 3. The apparatus which brings about ignition or explosion. 

Same as in-. image, n. [From a Latin noun meaning an imitation.] 1. A likeness; a picture; something visibly similar. 2. In physics, the likeness or figure produced at the focus of a lens or mirror, as the result of refraction of light through a lens or by reflection from a mirror. The image may fall upon a screen; on the retina of the eye; the film of a camera; or be magnified or reduced by other lenses in a telescope or microscope. impervious, adj. [From Latin words meaning no through road.] Not permitting penetration; not allowing entrance or admission; as, a substance is impervious to the gases of the atmosphere, or to water. impurity, n. [From Latin words meaning not pure]. 1. The state or condition of being mixed with dross, or being adulterated. 2. That which renders 104

IN- in- a substance impure, unwholesome or unclean; an adulterant; undesirable foreign material. (in-). A prefix meaning in, into, toward, as in inhale, induction, inclination; meaning not, as in insoluble, indefinite; or used to strengthen the stem, as in inflammable, invaluable. incandescence, n. [From Latin words meaning to become warm or hot]. The luminosity or glowing whiteness of an object which is very hot. incandescent, adj.

White hot; glowing or luminous because of great heat; as, a piece of lime becomes incandescent when very hot, producing the lime light; or the tungsten filament of the electric light becomes incandescent when the current is passed through the lamp. indestructibility, n.

A general property of matter by virtue of which it cannot be destroyed. indicator, n. [From Latin words which mean that which points out]. 1. A finger or pointer which moves over a dial or a chart so as to show height or amount of water; pressure of steam, air or other gas; elevation, and the like. 2. In chemistry, a substance which shows by a change of color, the condition of acidity or alkalinty of a solution, or indicates in like manner, the completion of a reaction. Indicators are generally used in the solution, some however, are not placed in the solution, but are "spotted out," in drops on a white plate and the solution brought to them by means of a rod or tube. Litmus, is one of the common indicators. It is red in acid solution and blue in alkaline solution. (See litmus). Phenolphthalein and methyl orange are very commonly used as indicators in volumetric analysis. Methyl orange is a weak acid having a bright red color, while its salts are pale straw color. If an acid solution containing a small amount of methyl orange be made. slightly alkaline, a salt of methyl orange will be formed and the color of the solution will change from red to yellow or pale straw color. If an alkaline solution containing methyl orange (really a salt of methyl orange) be made acid, the color change is from straw or yellow to red.

Methyl orange can be used only in the presence of acids strong enough to replace it in combination with a base, as other wise there would be no color change. It may be used with either weak or strong bases, but only with strong acids. Phenolphthalein is also a weak acid. In acid solution, it is colorless, but when neutralized, the salts formed are bright red. This color reaction is sharp only in the presence of strong bases. A great excess of the base destroys the phenolphthalein and renders the solution colorless. There are many other indicators similar to methyl orange and phenolphthalein. 105

INDUCTION 

induction, n. 

[From the Latin verb meaning to lead in]. The act or process by which a body, because of the electric charge upon it or its magnetic properties, produces a charge or magnetism in another body without actually coming into contact with it; as, the positively charged cloud induces a negative charge on the earth under it, or the negatively charged rubber plate of the electrophorus induces an equal positive charge on the plate held over it. inductive, adj. 

1. Working or operating because of induction. 2. Making induction more easily brought about; capable of being acted upon by induction. inert, adj. 

1. Inactive; lifeless; sluggish; passive. 2. In physics, having no power of moving itself or of resisting motion that has been impressed upon it. 

3. In chemistry, not possessed of active properties; having no effect upon other substances with which it is brought into contact; as, nitrogen is an inert gas, but oxygen is an active one. inertia, n.

In physics, that general property of matter by virtue of which a body, if at rest, will remain at rest, and if in motion will continue in motion. forever in a straight line unless acted upon by some external force. 

It is the only unalterable property of matter. inflammable, adj.

Said of that which is easily ignited or set on fire; easily burned; highly combustible. A word sometimes misunderstood since many words of similar construction have a contrary meaning; as, invisible means not visible, and insoluble means not soluble, but inflammable means very conbustible. The word enflammable is suggested as a substitute for inflammable. infusible, adj. [From Latin words meaning not capable of being melted or dissolved.] Not fusible; not capable of being melted or liquefied by heat. ingot, n. [From old English words meaning, to pour into a mold.] A brick or bar of metal which has been poured or cast into a mold, for convenience in handling or shipping. The purity of ingot metal is usually indicated by a stamp on the ingot. inorganic, adj. 

1. Applied to matter which is not and has not been alive; without physical structure which was produced by life processes. 2. In chemistry, that branch of the science which deals with substances not called organic (not containing carbon). insecticide, n. [Insect+cide from a Latin noun meaning a killer.] Any substance which can be used to kill insects. Insecticides may be solids, liquids, gases, or solids suspended in liquids. While insecticides usually kill 106

INSOLUBLE 

by chemical action (poisoning), in a few cases they kill by suffocation. or other means. Insecticides which are poisonous may be divided into two classes: the stomach poisons and the contact poisons. The stomach poisons are sprayed upon the foliage of plants to kill insects which actually eat the foliage. The contact poisons are sprayed or dusted upon plants and animals to kill insects by contact with their bodies. 

Since some insects do not eat the foliage of plants, but puncture the leaf, fruit, or other soft tissues and suck the juice, they can be killed only by poisons of the contact type. The most common stomach poisons used as insecticides are arsenic compounds, copper compounds, lime, phenol, sulphur and sulphites. These are dissolved or suspended in water and sprayed upon the foliage of the infected plants. Of the contact insecticides, pyrethrum powder is one of the oldest and most important. It is composed of the powdered flower heads of a species of chrysanthemum. Nicotine and other tobacco products are successfully used against aphids and against mites on poultry. Soap and oil (petroleum) emulsions are also used to kill many of the soft bodied sucking insects, such as plant lice, ticks, and small Hemiptera of various kinds. It is thought that the insects are killed by suffocation. Lime, sulphur, and water are boiled together and used as a spray to kill lice on animals and also scale insects on orchard trees. Carbondisulphide, hydrocyanic acid gas, and several of the war gases are successfully used in destroying scale insects which attack orchard trees. See fungicide. insoluble, adj. [From Latin words meaning something which cannot be loosed.] Not soluble; not capable of being dissolved in a liquid. When no liquid is mentioned, the inference is that water is meant. insulation, n. 

1. The act of process of separating or isolating a body, by means of a nonconductor; the insulation of a body may be to prevent the passage of heat, electricity, or some other form of energy. 2. The state of a body which is so separated as to prevent passage of heat, electricity, etc. 

3. The non-conducting material used in insulating a body. insulin, n. [From the Latin word for island, because first obtained from certain parts of the pancreas, called the Islands of Langerhans.] A substance obtained from the internal secretion of the pancreas, which has the power to temporarily relieve a victim of the disease known as diabetes mellitus. Insulin relieves but does not cure the disease. Daily injection into the blood stream may give relief for years, but the injections must be continued or the patient suffers a relapse. Insulin was discovered by Dr. F. G. Banting and C. H. Best, in the course of their researches at the University of Toronto in 1924. interference, n. The act or process of affecting a thing so as to impede or accelerate it, or so as to diminish or increase it in some way; as, the interference of one sound wave with another so as to increase or de- 107

IODINE crease its intensity, or the interaction of two wave lengths of light so as to produce increased or decreased brightness, or a change in color. iodine, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning violet colored, so named because of the violet color of its vapor.] Symbol I; atomic weight 126.93; valence 

1, 3, 5, or 7; specific gravity 4.95; melting point 114° C. A brownish black solid element, of the halogen family, formerly obtained by heating the ashes of seaweeds with sulphuric acid and some oxidizing agent, but now produced by the action of sodium sulphite on crude sodium iodate, obtained from the mother liquor after the crystallization of Chili saltpeter (NaNO3). The black lustrous plates obtained when crude iodine is resublimed are almost chemically pure iodine. The element thus obtained has an appreciable vapor tension even at ordinary temperatures, so that it has an acrid rather disagreeable odor. 

At high temperatures, a purple vapor is given off which on cooling yields again the brownish-black solid. Iodine is but slightly soluble in water, but dissolves readily in alcohol, carbon disulphide, chloroform, and water solutions of the alkali iodides. A seven per cent solution of iodine in alcohol is known as tincture of iodine, and is widely used in medicine as an antiseptic and as a counter irritant. Iodine solutions, even when extremely dilute, give cold starch paste an intense blue color. This fact is utilized in testing both for starch and for iodine. 

Iodine compounds are used in medicine, in the manufacture of drugs and dyes, and as chemical reagents. Iodoform (CHI3) is an important antiseptic used in surgery and potassium iodide (KI) is used in the treatment of goiter and certain blood diseases. Potassium iodide and potassium iodate are both used in photography and are important reagents in the chemical laboratory. ion, n. [From a Greek word meaning wanderer.] In chemistry and physics, 1. One of the units formed when a molecule of an electrolyte dissociates (breaks up) on going into solution. An ion may consist of a charged atom or group of atoms. It may be positively charged or negatively charged. The magnitude of the charge carried by a given ion is constant, as is the kind of charge. 2. One of the charged bodies in an ionized gas. See anion, cation, ionization, and ionic hypothesis. ionic hypothesis. 

In chemistry and physics, the electrolytic dissociation hypothesis first advanced by Svante Arrhenius, a Swedish Chemist in 1887. 

This hypothesis in its modern form is based on a number of assumptions as follows: (1) Some of the molecules of an electrolyte (an acid, a base or a salt), are broken up into parts (dissociated) on going into solution.* (2) These parts (called ions) are electrically charged. Some of the ions are positively charged and are called cathions, while some * According to a very recent theory, all electrolytes are wholly dissociated even in concentrated solutions. This idea has not as yet been generally accepted. 108

IONIC HYPOTHESIS 

are negatively charged and are called anions. (3) Any solution of an electrolyte contains, (a) undissociated molecules, (b) atoms or groups of atoms, each of which carry one or more positive charges (cations) and (c) atoms or groups of atoms, each of which carry one or more negative charges (anions). (4) Undissociated molecules are electrically neutral.

As each molecule breaks up (dissociates) one part (composed of one or more atoms of a basic element or of a group of atoms basic in character) gives up one or more electrons, thus becoming positively charged, and the other part of the molecule (composed of an atom or group of atoms, acid in character), acquires one or more electrons thus becoming negatively charged. (5) When an electrolyte ionizes, the total number of charges carried by all the negative ions is exactly equal to the total number of charges carried by all the positive ions. (6) 

Since the properties of ions are often radically different from the properties of the elements of which they are composed, it is assumed that the charges upon the ions render them essentially different from the atoms of which they are composed. (7) Univalent ions all possess equal quantities of electricity, and other ions possess greater quantities than this, in proportion to their valence, the number of charges on an ion always being equal to its valence. (8) In moderately dilute solutions, an equilibrium is established between molecules which are dissociating and ions which are reuniting to form molecules again. 

In such a solution, diluting the solution causes an increase in the number of ions, and concentrating the solution causes an increase in the number of undissociated molecules. (9) As a solution is diluted, the number of dissociated molecules increases and in very dilute solutions dissociation is nearly complete, almost all molecules having been broken up into ions. (10) In electrolysis the positively charged electrode attracts the negatively charged ions and the negatively charged electrode attracts the positively charged ions. Upon contact with the electrodes, the charges upon the ions are neutralized, (the ions lose or gain electrons) whereupon the elements, of which the ions were composed, are released as gases, dissolve in the water, are precipitated upon the electrodes, or else react with the electrodes or the solution which surrounds them. (It should be noted that the ions are present before the current is passed.) Thus, in a solution made by dissolving sodium chloride in water, some of the molecules break up as they go into solution and form positively charged Na ions and negatively charged Cl ions. The molecule of sodium chloride is electrically neutral, due to the fact that the atoms which compose it are neutral. As the molecule dissociates the neutral sodium atom loses an electron becoming a positively charged sodium ion, with properties markedly different from ordinary metallic sodium. As the sodium atom gave up an electron, the chlorine atom gained an electron, thus becoming negatively charged and acquiring new properties. In any solution of NaCl an equilibrium is established between molecules of NaCl which are dissociating and Na and Cl ions which are reuniting to form mole- 109

IONIZATION 

cules. In dilute solutions, the cathions have greater distances to travel before reuniting with anions to form molecules. Thus in dilute solutions equilibrium is established with few molecules and many ions. In very concentrated solutions, ions move very short distances before reuniting to form molecules, and so an equilibrium is established with few ions. and many molecules. If an electric current be passed through a water solution of NaCl, the negatively charged Cl ions are attracted to the positive electrode where they give up their charges (electrons) thus becoming ordinary elementary gaseous chlorine most of which rises to the surface and leaves the solution. (Some of the chlorine dissolves in the water.) At the same time the positively charged Na ions are attracted to the negative electrode where each ion regains an electron thus becoming ordinary metallic sodium, which immediately reacts with the water present to form sodium hydroxide and release hydrogen which rises to the surface and leaves the solution. Thus, the theory of ionization explains why the electrolysis of a solution of sodium chloride causes the release of hydrogen at the cathode, chlorine at the anode and the production of sodium hydroxide within the solution. ionization, n. 

In chemistry and physics. 1. The process by which part of the molecules of an electrolyte, as it goes into solution, are separated into smaller units called ions. See ionic hypothesis. 2. The process by which some of the molecules of a gas become charged with electricity, so that the gas is made capable of conducting a current. When air, nitrogen and other gases are subjected to the action of intense heat, the X-rays, radium rays or especially the stream of electrons from the Coolidge tube, some of the molecules of the gas become positively charged while an equal number become negatively charged. A gas in this condition will conduct an electric current and is said to be ionized because it contains an equal number of positively and negatively charged units, and is thus in a condition similar to that of electrolyte when some of its molecules are broken up into ions which carry equal quantities of positive and negative charges. Thus, ionization of gases is a process quite different from ionization of electrolytes. The units which carry the charges in ionized gases are molecules or electrons, in electrolytes, ions. When dust free air is saturated with water vapor and ionized by the action of radium or by other methods, sudden cooling will cause a fog or cloud to form, each charged molecule of water vapor serving as a nucleus for the condensation of other molecules of water vapor. Some of the molecules of the gases of the atmosphere are always in the ionized state and these together with dust particles serve as the nuclei of the droplets which make up clouds. ionize, v.i.

In chemistry and physics, 1. To break up into ions, said of electrolytes as they go into solution. 2. To yield positively and negatively charged units in equal numbers, and said of gases when they become capable of carrying an electric current. 110

IRIDESCENCE 

iridescence, n. 

[From the Latin noun meaning a rainbow.] 1. The rainbow-tinted display of light which is reflected to the eye from some objects, such as soap bubbles, films of oil floating on water, mother-of-pearl, and the integuments of many insects. This phenomenon is due to the diffraction. of light when it is reflected from surface films or from surfaces which have minute ridges or corrugations. 2. The state or condition of being iridescent. iridescent, adj.

Having rainbow-colors which change with any change in the angle at which the light is reflected from the observed object to the eye; possessing iridescence. iridium, n. [From a Latin noun meaning a rainbow, because of the iridescent colors exhibited by some iridium compounds.] Symbol Ir; atomic weight 193.1; valence 2 or 3; specific gravity 21.15; melting point 2300° C. A rare metallic element of the platinum family, discovered by Tennant, an English chemist, in 1804. The metal is obtained entirely from the residue left after treatment of platinum ores with aqua regia. These ores are obtained from the Ural mountains in Russia, from Brazil, and from California. Metallic iridium is extremely hard and highly resistant to the action of acids, being insoluble even in aqua regia. 

It is very brittle and absorbs hydrogen very readily. Compounds of iridium are comparatively unimportant, but an alloy of nine parts platinum with one of iridium is widely used in the manufacture of points for fountain pens, bars to be used as standards of measure, wires to be used in high temperature pyrometers, and electrodes for the electrolysis of caustic liquids. iron, n. [Through the Anglo Saxon iren, from the Old High German isarn.] 

Symbol Fe (From ferrum the Latin name for iron); atomic weight 55.84; valence 2 and 3; specific gravity 7.86; melting 1530° C. The most common and most useful of the metallic elements and known from ancient times. The metal occurs alloyed with nickel in meteorites but is not found free in nature in commercial quantities. The chief ores are hematite (Fe2O3), siderite (FeCO3), limonite (2Fe2O3·3H2O), and magnetite (Fe3O4). Those ores are mined chiefly in the United 

States, England, and Central Europe. The carbonate ores are roasted to drive off carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide. This roasting converts the iron present to iron oxide which is reduced in a blast furnace with coke. The oxide ores are reduced with coke or charcoal in a blast furnace. The product is cast iron, a commercial form of which is known as pig iron. Cast iron contains phosphorus, sulphur, and manganese as impurities, and is also rich in carbon and silicon. It is hard and brittle but can be readily machined and given a high polish. It melts rather slowly at 1100° to 1250° C. the temperature varying according to 111

IRON 

its composition. It cannot be welded or forged and has little tensile strength. It is used in the manufacture of stoves and small castings of all kinds. Cast iron of high silicon content is especially resistant to the attack of acids and alkalis. It is widely used in the manufacture of sinks and troughs for laboratories and vessels for causticizing alkalis, for concentrating acids, and similar purposes. Cast iron may be converted into wrought iron in the puddlers' furnace, or into steel by the Bessemer, the open hearth, or other processes. Wrought iron is the purest commercial form of iron. In its manufacture, molten cast iron is treated in a reverberatory furnace in such a way as to cause the carbon, silicon, sulphur and phosphorus to combine with the basic materials which line the furnace and so form a slag most of which may be removed by skimming. As the impurities are removed the iron becomes more solid and is finally removed in large lumps and rolled into bars. or sheets. This form of iron contains but .1 to .3% carbon and only traces of sulphur and phosphorus. Wrought iron is tough, and fibrous and has high tensile strength. It melts at about 1530° C. and can be readily forged and welded. It is used in the manufacture of chains, bolts, bars, furnace grates, small parts for farm machinery, wire, and some kinds of nails. Steel is a variety of iron from which phosphorus, sulphur and silicon have been almost completely removed but which contains from .2 to 2% of carbon. A part of which is in the form of cementite (Fe3C), a carbide of iron. Steel may be produced by adding the desired amount of carbon to wrought iron, but is usually made by the Bessemer, the open hearth, or the crucible processes. The various processes for making steel are also classified as basic or acid processes according as the furnaces used are lined with basic or acid materials. 

In the Bessemer process, now usually called the acid Bessemer process, the molten iron is run into large egg shaped steel crucibles called converters. The converter is capable of holding 15 to 20 tons of iron, and is mounted on trunnions which permit it to tip when the molten steel is to be poured. The converter is lined with silica brick and has a perforated bottom through which air can be blown into the molten metal. 

White hot molten cast iron is run from the blast furnace into the converter. Air under great pressure is then blown through the molten metal until all carbon has been removed as carbon dioxide and the sulphur and phosphorus content of the metal greatly reduced. The air blast is then discontinued and iron of high carbon content added in a quantity just sufficient to bring the total carbon content of the charge to the desired amount. After the charge has been thoroughly mixed the converter is tipped on the trunnions and the molten steel poured into smaller containers, for removal and further treatment. The entire process may require only twenty to thirty minutes. In the basic open hearth process, a large reverberatory furnace lined with limestone or other basic materials receives the charge of molten iron from the blast furnace. 

The metal is kept molten for several hours. The fuel oxidizes the carbon of the iron and thus gradually removes it, together with some phosphorus 112

ISOLATE 

and sulphur. The lining of the furnace also removes some of the sulphur and phosphorus. A portion of the iron is removed at regular intervals and an analysis made. When the carbon content has been reduced to the desired proportion the molten metal (now steel) is withdrawn for further treatment. The Bessemer process is much more rapid than the open hearth process, but can only utilize cast iron which is practically free from sulphur and phosphorus. The open hearth process does not require iron of high degree of purity and though requiring more time, can be easily controlled and so made to produce a more uniform product. Most of the steel now produced in the United 

States is made by the open hearth process. Iron and most iron alloys corrode readily and form a reddish brown non-adherent mixture (of oxides) called rust. Iron is sometimes given a protective coat of some metal which will protect it from atmospheric corrosion. Galvanized iron is iron which has been coated with zinc. Tin plate is iron which has 

I been coated with tin. There are two series of iron salts. In ferrous compounds iron has a valence of two, in ferric compounds a valence of three. Ferrous sulphate (FeSO4 7H2O) is the most important ferrous salt. It is also called copperas and green vitriol, and is used in the manufacture of ink, in the purification of water, and in making other iron compounds. Ferric chloride (FeCl3) is the most common ferric salt. It is a valuable laboratory reagent, is used in medicine, and in the dyeing industry. Compounds of iron, an alkali metal and cyanogen are of considerable importance in a number of commercial processes. 

Potassium ferrocyanide (K4Fe(CN) 6) is used in the manufacture of laundry bluing, blue pigments, and in the case hardening of steel. 

Potassium ferri-cyanide (K3Fe(CN) 6) is an important laboratory reagent and is the most important compound used in blue-printing. isolate, v. t. [From the Latin verb meaning to isolate, which in turn was derived from the noun meaning an island.] 1. To separate from others like itself; to set apart; to put by itself. 2. In electricity, to insulate. 3. In chemistry, to obtain in a pure state, not combined with any other substance; as, to isolate a compound of radium from the other constituents of pitchblend. joule, n.

[Named for the English physicist James Prescott Joule (1818-1889).] 

In physics, a C. G. S. unit of work, equivalent to 10,000,000 (107) ergs, 0.737,324 foot-pound, or 0.24 calorie. As an electrical unit it is. the amount of work done in one second in maintaining a current of one ampere against a resistance of one ohm; the energy expended when a coulomb of electricity passes through a conductor in which the difference in potential is one volt; a watt-second; a volt-coulomb. 113

kaolin, n.

[From Chinese words meaning High Ridge, the name of an early source of the mineral.] A very pure, fine, whitish clay which resulted from the disintegration of rocks containing feldspar. It is an impure hydrated aluminum silicate, (Al2Si₂O7) · 2H2O, and is used in the production of high grade chinaware or porcelain. Any porcelain clay which does not discolor during the firing is commonly known as kaolin. kelp, n. 

1. In botany, a type of seaweed of the Fucus (brown algae) family. 

2. Sometimes means ashes of seaweeds. These ashes contain many valuable salts of sodium and potassium, as well as small quantities of iodine. The ashes were formerly used in the manufacture of soap and glass, but are now chiefly treated for iodine and potassium hydroxide. ketone, n.

An organic compound containing two hydrocarbon radicals joined together by a carbonyl group (CO); as, di-methyl-ketone (CH3-CO-CH3), commonly called acetone. The ketones are usually produced by the oxidation of secondary alcohols. They are closely related to the aldehydes, which are produced by the oxidation of primary alcohols. kilo-, [From a Greek word meaning one thousand.] A prefix meaning one thousand. kilo, n. 

An abbreviation for kilogram. kilogram, n. [Kilo+gram.] The basic unit of weight in the metric system; one thousand grams. The kilogram is equivalent to 2.2046 pounds. kilogram meter, n. [ Kilo+gram+meter] A unit of measurement of work; the work done in raising a kilogram through a vertical distance of one meter. kiloliter, n. [Kilo+liter] One thousand liters; a cubic meter. The kiloliter is equivalent to 35.31 cubic feet, or 264.2 gallons. kilowatt, n. [Kilo+watt] One thousand watts. A unit of power in the C.G.S. system, and equal to 1.341 horse power, 1,000 joules per second, or 238.7 calories per second. 760 kilowatts are equivalent to 1000 horse power. 

The kilowatt is the unit ordinarily used in the purchase or sale of electric power, though the actual commercial unit is the kilowatt hour. 

Thus, electricity may be sold at a rated price of 12 cents a kilowatt, when the actual sale price is 12 cents per kilowatt hour. If one ampere of electricity is passing under a pressure of one volt, the power involved 114

KILOWATT HOUR 

is said to be one watt. If one ampere passes under a pressure of one volt for one hour, the power involved is one watt hour. Thus, an incandescent light might allow .454 amperes of current to pass under a pressure of 110 volts. Such a lamp would be rated as a 50 watt lamp, and it would consume in one hour, 50 watt hours of power. At 12 cents a kilowatt hour, it would cost 12×50/1000=.6 cents per hour for the power used to light this lamp. kilowatt hour. [Kilo+watt+hour] A commercial unit of power equal to one thousand watt hours or 1.341 horse-power hours. See kilowatt. kindling point, kindling temperature.

The temperature at which a substance just begins to burn; the lowest temperature at which combustion starts. This temperature depends upon the nature of the substance, (whether finely divided or coarse), its physical condition, and the condition of the air in contact with the substance. kinetic, adj. [From a Greek word meaning moving.] Relating to, caused by, or resulting from motion. Kinetic energy is the energy which a body possesses by virtue of its motion; as the energy exhibited by gases in enclosed vessels. The kinetic theory of gases, assumes that gases are composed of molecules moving at a high rate of speed. The pressure exerted by an enclosed gas is due to the blows struck by all the molecules against the walls of the vessel. As an increase in temperature would increase the velocity of the molecules the blow struck by the impact of each molecule against the wall of the vessel would be increased, thus accounting for the effect of temperature on the pressure of an enclosed gas. krypton, n. 

[From a Greek adjective meaning hidden.] Symbol K; atomic weight 

82.92; valence O; specific gravity 41.506 (0=16); boiling point — 151.7°

C. One of the rare gaseous elements of the atmosphere. An extremely inert element with a very definite boiling point and characteristic spectrum. There are no known uses of krypton. 115

lac, n.

L [The Sancrit noun meaning 100,000 or an indefinitely large number -given as a name to the scale-insect which clusters in vast numbers upon the plants which it infests.] A resin-like solid excretion of the female scale-insect chardia lacca. It attaches the eggs to the surface of the twigs of the host plant and hardens into a thin coating over their bark. When removed and purified, it serves as the foundation of lacquer and other varnishes, sealing wax and dyes. Lac in thin plates, the commonest commercial form, is known as shell-lac or shellac. lacquer, n. [Derived through the French from lac, q.v.] 1. An opaque or translucent varnish made by dissolving lac in alcohol (usually with coloring matter added), used widely as a protective coating on metals (especially brass), wood, etc. 2. Any varnish based on a resin. 3. Specifically, a Japanese varnish made from the resin obtained from the lacquer-tree, Rhus vernicifera, by making incisions in its bark. Japanese lacquer is noted for its beauty and durability. 4. Wooden articles coated with lacquer as defined above. Japan excels all other countries in the production of such goods. lactose, n. 

Milk sugar (C12H22O11 H2O), a sweet white crystalline substance found in milk. It is soluble in water, and is used mainly as a vehicle for medicines. It does not ferment readily and so is a desirable sugar to be used in infant foods. It is not as sweet as cane sugar. Lactose is obtained as a white crystalline solid from the whey after the cream has been separated and the casein or cheese of the milk has been precipitated by rennin (rennet). latent heat of fusion. 

In physics. 1. The amount of heat, expressed in calories, which is required to change one gram of substance at its melting temperature from the solid state to the liquid state of matter without raising its temperature. This same amount of heat is given off when the substance changes from the liquid to the solid state. This term originated before the real nature of heat was understood, when heat was supposed to be a weightless fluid which could be absorbed by a substance and driven out of it again. Modern writers omit the inappropriate word latent. 

To change one gram of ice at 0° C. to water at 0° C. requires 80 calories of heat, so the heat of fusion of ice is 80 calories. 2. The heat equivalent of the energy required to overcome the cohesion between the molecules of a solid sufficiently so that the molecules will move freely over one another-i.e., to liquefy the substance. latent heat of vaporization.

In physics. 1. The number of calories of heat required to change one 116

LATEX 

gram of substance from the liquid state to the gaseous state of matter without changing its temperature. 2. The number of calories of heat energy required to vaporize one gram of substance. The heat of vaporization of water is 536 calories. Compare with latent heat of fusion. latex, n. [The Latin noun meaning a fluid.] A kind of plant juice, usually of milky color and consistency, which is produced by special cells and found in a network of tubes in the tissues of ten families of Angiospermae. The commonest example is the juice of the milkweed. It oozes out of a wound in considerable quantities because it is under pressure in the laticiferous vessels, and it coagulates upon reaching the outer air. Opium and morphine are made from poppy latex, and caoutchouc or India rubber is commercially the most important substance yielded by a latex. The long list of substances dissolved and suspended in latexes includes gums, resins, sugars and other carbohydrates; oils; proteids; salts, especially of calcium and magnesium; tannins; many alkaloids, some of them poisonous; enzymes; etc. laughing gas.

A gaseous compound known in chemistry as nitrous oxide (NO) and used in dentistry as an anaesthetic. The gas is prepared by heating ammonium nitrate (NH4NO3). It is a colorless, neutral gas, which when inhaled in quantity quickly produces total, though usually brief, insensibility. It is one of the safest of the anaesthetics. When inhaled in small amounts it frequently causes brief fits of uncontrollable laughter. 

Hence the name, laughing gas. law, n. [From an early English word lawe, meaning law.] 

1. A rule of conduct established by custom or laid down by competent. authority. 2. In science, a concise statement of a fact or series of facts relating to natural phenomena which has so far held true in every experimental test. As Boyle's Law, Graham's Law, etc. law of definite composition. law of definite proportions. 

In chemistry, a general statement, in accordance with all observed facts, of the way in which elements combine in the formation of compoundsviz., that every compound always consists of the same elements, united in fixed and invariable proportions by weight. While certain elements unite to form more than one compound (e.g., H₂O and H2O2), each of these compounds is formed in accordance with the above law. lead, n. 

[From the Anglo Saxon noun of the same meaning.] Symbol Pb (From the Latin noun plumbum meaning lead; atomic weight 207.22; valence 

2 and 4; specific gravity 11.4; melting point 327.4° C. A soft, bluishgray, very malleable, metallic element, known from most ancient times, and obtained chiefly from galena or galenite (PbS). The ore is roasted in air until a portion of it is converted into a mixture of lead oxide (PbO) 117

LENS 

and lead sulphate (PbSO4) after which the air is cut off and the temperature raised until the sulphur has passed off as sulphur dioxide (SO2), leaving the molten lead which is then cast into bars. Lead obtained in this way sometimes contains silver which can be almost completely removed by the Parks process. (See silver). Pure lead may be obtained by electrolysis. The metal is comparatively active and quickly tarnishes in air, due to the formation of a thin layer of lead suboxide (Pb₂O). This oxide is very stable and protects the metal from further corrosion. Lead is easily attacked by nitric and acetic acids and slightly by hot concentrated hydrochloric and sulphuric acids. Soft water has a slight action on lead, often producing enough lead hydroxide (Pb(OH)2) to make it distinctly poisonous. Lead is used in the manufacture of acid resisting utensils, as a covering for roofs, and in the manufacture of water pipes and other objects which have to be exposed to the action of air or water. 

Large quantities are used in the making of lead alloys and lead salts. 

The chief alloys are type metal (lead, tin and antimony); antifriction metals (lead, antimony, tin, and sometimes arsenic and zinc); solder (lead and tin); shot metal (lead and arsenic) and Wood's Metal (lead, tin, cadmium and bismuth). The most important lead compounds are litharge, the yellow oxide (PbO) which is used in the manufacture of paints and glass; and white lead, the basic carbonate (Pb(OH)2.2PbCO3) one of the most important white pigments. All lead compounds are poisonous. lens, n.

A transparent body bounded by two curved surfaces (usually spherical), or by one curved and one plane surface. Lenses fall into two classesthose which cause rays of light to converge and those which cause them to diverge. Converging lenses are named according to the nature of their bounding surfaces double-convex, plano-convex, and concaveconvex. Similarly, diverging lenses are called double-concave, planoconcave, and convexo-concave. 2. In anatomy, the crystalline lens; a double-convex body, a part of the eye, which lies back of the iris, with its center inline with the center of the pupil, between the aqueous and the vitreous humors. Its function is to bring the rays of light which enter through the pupil to a focus on the retina. lever, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to raise.] A rigid bar which can be made to rotate about a fixed point or axis at which it is supported and thus to change the direction or magnitude of a force, or to exert pressure. Perhaps the first of the six simple machines (lever, inclined plane, wedge, screw, wheel and axle, and pulley) to be used by primative man, it is found in many places in the human body and is a part of nearly every machine which man constructs. The use of the lever involves four things: (1) The force exerted, (2) the resistance to be overcome, (3) a rigid bar or elongated object to turn (the lever), and (4) a supporting fixed point or axis around which the lever turns (the fulcrum). Levers 118

LEVULOSE 

are classified as belonging to the first, second, or third class on the basis of the relative positions of the fulcrum and the points of application of the moving force (called the effort) and the resistance. A lever of the first class has the fulcrum between the other two; in a second class lever the resistance, weight, or load is between the other two; and in a third class lever the moving force, effort, or power is applied between the resistance and the fulcrum. The claw of a hammer when used to pull a nail and a pair of shears illustrate the first class lever; the wheelbarrow, the oar of a boat, and the nut-cracker are examples of second class levers; and sugar-tongs and the human jaw are third class levers. levulose, n. [From a Greek word meaning left+ose, an ending used to indicate a carbohydrate]. A sugar (C6H12O6) also called fruit sugar and fructose, occurring in company with glucose in many fruits and other natural products. It is called levulose because it has the power to rotate a plane of polarized light to the left. Levulose can be made artifically by heating a solution of cane sugar (C12H22O11) with a small amount of hydrochloric or other acid. This produces a mixture of equal amounts of glucose, (dextrose) and fructose (levulose), which is known as invert sugar because the mixture is levulo rotary, while the cane sugar from which it was produced is dextro rotary. Fructose resembles glucose in most of its properties, except in that it is sweeter and less soluble in water than glucose. 

Leyden jar.

A type of electrostatic condenser devised in 1746 by Musschenbroek at Leyden in Holland. The condenser is made by coating a jar with tinfoil both inside and out to about two-thirds its height. A rod or chain connects the inside layer of tin-foil with a knob at the top of the jar. To charge this condenser, one pole of an electric machine is connected to the knob or inside coating of the jar, while the other or outside coating is connected to the other pole of the machine or to the earth. Thus the two coatings receive equal and opposite charges. The Leyden jar may be discharged by connecting its two coats by means of a conductor. light, n. 

1. The opposite of darkness; that without which vision is impossible; a sensation which is produced when the optic nerve is stimulated. 2. In physics, radiant energy which is transmitted in waves whose lengths lie between certain limits and is capable of affecting the retina, thus enabling the organs of sight to perform their function of vision. It differs in one respect only from radiant energy which will not affect the retina. thus-in wave-length. According to the undulatory theory or wave theory, the vibrating molecules of any substance whose temperature is at any point above absolute zero set up vibrations in the ether. These vibrations travel outward from their source in all directions, in straight lines, at a speed of about 186,300 miles per second. They are all transmitted by the ether in waves, and the vibrations of the ether in a wave 119

LIGHT 

are at right angles to the direction in which the wave is traveling-in much the same way that heads of wheat swing up and down when a wind sweeps waves across a wheatfield, or that the particles of water in the surface layer rise and fall without moving forward when waves sweep over the surface of a pond. The number of waves sent out each second and the length of each wave depend upon the temperature of the substance which causes the waves—that is, upon the speed with which the molecules of the substance are moving. As the temperature rises, the number of waves per second increases and the length of each wave decreases correspondingly, since radiant energy of all wave-lengths is transmitted at the same rate of speed. As long as the wave-lengths are greater than 7.6 ten-thousandths of a millimeter, no effect is produced upon the retina of the human eye, but heat effects are produced which may be detected by the use of the thermometer. When the body which is the source of the radiations reaches a temperature of 525° C. the ether vibrations affect the retina, the heated substance becomes luminous, the light which it throws off is red, and the body is said to be red-hot. The wave-length of red light is 7.6 ten-thousandths of a millimeter. With a still further rise in temperature the body radiates more heat and more light, an increasing proportion of the molecules move faster than is necessary to produce red light, and light of other colors is added to the red lightfirst orange, then yellow, green, blue, indigo, and finally violet, whose wave-length is 3.9 ten-thousandths of a millimeter. When violet rays have been added to rays of all the other colors, the combined effect of the mixture of these colored lights is white, and the body is said to be white-hot. As the body becomes still hotter ether vibrations are set up whose wave-lengths are less than 3.9 ten-thousandths of a millimeter, but they do not affect the retina. Their effects are chemical or actinic, such as turning silver chloride black, as in photography, or producing a coat of tan on the human skin. So, light is radiant energy transmitted by ether vibrations in waves whose lengths lie between 3.9 and 7.6 tenthousandths of a millimeter. The human eye recognizes a body as luminous not when a few of its molecules have reached the temperature of 525° C., but when the average temperature of all its molecules is 525° C. By that time some of its molecules may be sending out light rays and others sending out actinic rays. As heating progresses, heat rays predominate first, then light rays, then actinic rays, but except at the start all three kinds are given off at the same time. Radiant energy exactly similar to light except that it is transmitted in waves somewhat longer than those of red light-the infra-red rays-is heat. Still longer waves are now familiar as radio waves. At the other end of the scale, beyond the violet, come the ultra-violet, extra-ultra-violet, x-rays, and others, each kind increasingly actinic. The phenomena of light are classified under the heads (1) absorption, (2) reflection, (3) refraction, (4) dispersion, (5) interference, and (6) polarization. (q.v.) 120

LIGHTNING ARRESTER 

lightning arrester.

A device used to prevent the high potential surges of atmospheric electrical disturbances from damaging electrical apparatus;-especially common as a protective device on radio sets and transmission lines. lignite, n.

A brownish-black coal in which the alteration of vegetable material has progressed farther than in peat, but not so far as in sub-bituminous coal. 

The woody texture can still be seen. The volatile matter and ash runs to a higher percentage and the coal is lighter, softer, and more powdery than sub-bituminous coal. It burns with a smoky flame. lime, n. 

1. The oxide of the metal calcium (CaO) known as quicklime, to distinguish it from slaked lime (Ca(OH)2). It is the most important ingredient of the plaster which covers the inside walls of houses and the mortar which holds bricks and stones together in walls; it is used in medicine; in the manufacture of soda, glass, commercial fertilizers, and chemicals; in purifying illuminating gas; in removing hair from hides in the manufacture of leather; and in many other ways. Not to be confused with lye (q.v.). Calcium oxide does not exist in nature except in combination with other substances, but in such combinations it exists in immense deposits. In limestone (q.v.), which is impure calcium carbonate (CaCO3), man finds an inexhaustible supply of material from which lime is very readily made. Lime (quicklime) is a white, amorphous, caustic solid with density from 3 to 3.3, requiring the heat of the electric furnace to melt it. It is obtained by calcining (subjecting to dry heat) limestone, marble, shells or other forms of calcium carbonate in a limekiln, so as to drive off the carbon dioxide. When exposed to moist air, lime absorbs water and carbon dioxide from the air, and gradually crumbles to a powdery form of calcium carbonate known as air-slaked lime. 

When water is supplied more rapidly, much heat is evolved and the lumps of lime crumble to a pasty mass of slaked lime or calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2). Into this mass, sand is mixed to make mortar or plaster. 

As this gradually dries it absorbs carbon dioxide from the air. The CaO and the CO₂ combine to form again the rocklike CaCO3 from which the lime was originally made. Water in which slaked lime is dissolved (lime water) is a common household remedy for correcting acidity of the stomach. It also furnishes a positive test for the presence of carbon dioxide, since no other gas, when passed through it, turns limewater milky. limestone, n.

A kind of sedimentary rock; usually dull gray in color but of various shades, of which the bluish and the yellowish are commonest; hardness about 3 (easily scratched with the point of a knife-blade); density nearly 

3; sometimes semi-crystalline. It is composed of calcium carbonate (Ca- 

CO3), together with various impurities which account for the variations in color. Much limestone has a large admixture of magnesium carbonate (MgCO3), in which case it is called dolomite or dolomitic limestone. 121

LIMEWATER 

This frequently has a bluish tint, but the two varieties cannot be distinguished by the eye with any certainty. Some limestone deposits originated by chemical precipitation, but more of them by the accumulation of the remains of aquatic animals-mollusks, corals, crinoids, etc. whose fossils abound in beds of limestone. It is very widely distributed and very abundant, whole mountain ranges being composed of it. 

Marble is the metamorphic form of limestone. Both non-magnesian and dolomitic limestone, as well as their metamorphic form, marble, are valuable and widely used building materials. A compact, finegrained, yellow or light gray variety is used in the printing trade as lithographer's stone. Pearls, coral, oyster-shells and egg-shells are, like limestone, composed largely of calcium carbonate. While limestone is practically insoluble in pure water, it is somewhat soluble in water which has carbon dioxide dissolved in it. Underground streams of such water, flowing through crevices in beds of limestone, have dissolved and carried away enough of the rock to form many thousands of caves, of which the Mammoth Cave in Kentucky is the best known. Water which has dissolved limestone is known as hard water. limewater, n.

A solution of calcium hydroxide Ca(OH)2 in water. Ordinarily a saturated solution is meant. Such a solution contains about .14 percent of calcium hydroxide by weight. liquid, adj. [From the Latin verb meaning to be liquid.] In physics, having the ability to flow freely but made up of molecules which cling together and do not repel one another as do the molecules of a gaseous substance; fluid but not gaseous. This term is used to designate that one of the three states of matter in which a body of substance retains its size but readily changes its shape under the influence of gravity or any other force which acts upon it. Cohesion between the molecules of a substance in the liquid state is too weak to produce the rigidity which characterizes the solid state, not strong enough to keep the molecules from sliding freely over one another, but strong enough to resist their separation. liquid, n.

A substance which is liquid; one which is not rigid. A liquid readily takes the shape of the containing vessel, with a horizontal plane for its upper surface, induced by gravity. While the molecules of a gas repel one another, causing indefinite expansion, those of a liquid attract one another, and so a body of liquid has a definite size. Liquids are very slightly compressible. All degrees of viscosity exist in liquids, and as a result there is no sharp line separating liquids from solids. later, n.

The unit of capacity in the metric system, being one cubic decimeter, or the volume of one kilogram of pure water at the temperature of greatest density. It is equal to 61.022 cubic inches, 0.9081 U. S. dry quart, 1.05668 U. S. liquid quarts or 0.26417 U. S. liquid gallons. 122

LITHARGE 

litharge, n. [From the Greek words meaning silver-stone, probably because litharge frequently is found in silver bearing lead ores.] The yellow oxide of lead (PbO), obtained by roasting lead nitrate or lead carbonate or strongly heating metallic lead in the presence of air. Litharge is used in the manufacture of rubber, as a paint pigment in the making of glass and pottery, and as a constituent of acid proof cements for use in plumbing, setting glass in aquaria, etc. lithium, n. [From a Greek noun meaning stone.] Symbol Li; atomic weight 6.94; valence 1; specific gravity .534; melting point, 180° C. A soft gray, extremely light metal obtained by the electrolysis of fused lithium chloride.

Like sodium and potassium, the metal quickly tarnishes when exposed to the air. It decomposes water at ordinary temperatures, releasing hydrogen and forming a solution of lithium hydroxide. Lithium salts are found widely distributed in nature but only in small quantities, and so lithium must be classed as a rare metal. All lithium compounds have the power to impart a carmine color to a flame, and all produce a brilliant crimson line when examined with the spectroscope. Lithium salts are used in medicine and in the manufacture of ceramic materials. litmus, n. [From Persian words meaning a lacquer obtained from fruit.] A violetblue coloring substance obtained from certain species of lichens by allowing them to ferment in contact with ammonium carbonate and wood ashes. The blue color of litmus is due to the presence of ammonium and potassium salts of an organic acid. In the presence of all except the very weakest acids, these blue salts are decomposed and the free acid in litmus is released. This acid is red, but its salts are blue. Litmus solutions and litmus paper are used to determine the alkaline or acid nature of liquids. When brought into contact with an acid solution, the litmus turns red, but in alkaline solutions, the blue color is restored. loadstone, lodestone, n.

A piece of magnetite, the magnetic oxide of iron (Fe3O4), which has magnetic poles and the power of attracting bits of iron or steel; a natural magnet. low melting alloy,

Any alloy which is easily melted, as Wood's metal, an alloy usually consisting of bismuth 50%, lead 25%, tin 12.5% and cadmium 12.5%. This alloy melts at 60.5° C which is a lower temperature than any of the metals which compose it, and lower than any other alloy which can be made from them. It is used in making fusible plugs for automatic fire extinguishers and low melting parts for similar saftey devices. Other low melting alloys are used in making safety plugs for the prevention of steam explosions in boilers. (Also called fusible metal.) 123

LUBRICANT 

lubricant, n. [From a Latin verb meaning to make slippery.] A substance-such as oil, graphite, and the like—which reduces the resistance to the motion of one part or object over another. lubricate, v.t.

To cause to slip easily by applying grease, oil, or some other lubricant to the parts which move over each other, so as to change the true or sliding friction between the solid bodies to internal friction of the lubricant. See friction. lye, n. 

1. A solution in water of potassium carbonate (together with some other salts) which is obtained by leaching wood ashes. It is strong enough to be caustic and is used in making soap. When diluted it is used in washing clothes and in other rough cleaning processes. See potash. Not to be confused with lime. 2. Any strongly alkaline solution, such as sodium hydroxide or potassium hydroxide. 124

magnalium, n.

M [Magn from magnesium + al and ium from aluminum.] A hard, light, very stable alloy, of aluminum and magnesium. An alloy having a very low coefficient of expansion. The proportion of magnesium depends upon the use to which the alloy is to be put and ranges from 4 to 25 percent. Magnalium is used in the manufacture of structural parts for airships, scale beams for analytical balances, parts for optical instruments, and many other objects where lightness, rigidity, strength, and durability are desired. magnesium, n. [From the Greek name Magnesia, a country in Asia Minor.] Symbol 

Mg; atomic weight 24.32; valence 2; specific gravity 1.69 to 1.72; melting point 632.6° C. A silver white metal slightly acted upon by the atmosphere at ordinary temperatures and easily attacked by air and water at high temperatures. The metal is obtained by electrolysis of the fused chloride. It burns readily in air giving off a brilliant bluish-white light. The powdered metal is an important ingredient of photographic flash powders. Magnesium is widely used as a constituent of the alloy duralumin. (q.v.). Magnesium compounds are widely distributed in nature, often in great quantities. Dolomite magnesite, talc, and asbestos are among the most important minerals containing magnesium as an important constituent. Magnesium sulphate (epsom salts), magnesium oxide, (magnesia), magnesium hydroxide (in suspension as milk of magnesia), and other magnesium salts are used in medicine and in the industries. magnet, n. 

1. Loadstone (q. v.). 2. Any body which has the power of attracting bits of steel and iron, and which, when suspended so that it can turn easily, tends to come to rest in a position approximately north and south. This it does because the earth is a magnet and governs the position of any freely suspended magnet. Horseshoe magnets, bar magnets, etc. are so called because of their shapes. A few substances beside steel and iron are capable of becoming weakly magnetic, among them nickel and cobalt. Every magnet has two poles, called the north pole and the south pole. Like poles repel each other, unlike poles attract. This statement is known as the law of magnets. 

The earth's north magnetic pole, instead of coinciding with its geographic north pole, is almost 10° distant from it. It is located in northern Canada, in Boothia Felix, at 70°5' west longitude. The south magnetic pole is a little east of Victoria Land. The compass needle, or any other freely suspended magnet, places itself parallel to a meridian joining these poles. A piece of steel can be made into a permanent 125

MAGNETIC 

magnet in various ways—by contact with a magnet, by passing a current of electricity through it, by placing it parallel to the earth's magnetic lines and striking it sharply with a hammer, etc. A coil of wire through which a current of electricity is flowing becomes a magnet, and when such a coil or helix surrounds (and is insulated from) a piece of soft iron or core, this also becomes a magnet by induction, and the attractive power of the two is very great. Such a combination constitutes an electromagnet. Small ones are essential parts of telephone and telegraph instruments, and large ones are used to lift great weights, such as steel rails, masses of scrap iron, etc. magnetic, adj. 

1. Having the properties of a magnet; as, a magnetic needle. 2. Capable of becoming a magnet; as, nickel is slightly magnetic. 

magnetism, n. 

[From magnet.] 1. That branch of physics which deals with magnets and magnetic phenomena. 2. That quality the possession of which makes a body a magnet; that state by virtue of which a body has the properties of a magnet. See magnet and electromagnet. magnetite, n. [From Magnesia, a country from which the ore was first obtained.] 

The magnetic oxide of iron (Fe3O4), which has the natural property of attracting iron. Some samples exhibit polarity and are called lodestone. magnetize, v. t.

To cause a thing (as a body of iron or coil of wire) to acquire the properties of a magnet. When a current of electricity is passed through an insulated coil of wire surrounding a soft iron rod, the rod acquires the properties of a magnet and is said to be magnetized. The rod loses its magnetic properties (is demagnetized) when the current is interrupted. A steel rod or bar may be permanently magnetized by repeated stroking in one direction with a strong magnet and by many other means. See magnet. magnification, n. [From the Latin verb magnificare, meaning to make great, to enlarge.] 

1. The state of being magnified or the act of magnifying. 2. The ex tent or degree to which a thing is magnified or enlarged, usually indicated in terms of the ratio of the apparent diameter to the real diameter of the object. magnify, v. t. and v. i. 

1. To increase the size of a thing. 2. To make a thing appear larger; to exaggerate. malleability, n.

That specific property of certain substances (solids) by virtue of which bodies composed of them are capable of being hammered into new shapes or rolled into sheets. Malleability and ductility are usually possessed by the same substances. For example, gold, the most mal- 126

It is less sweet than cane sugar, but resembles it in most other properties. It is strongly dextro-rotatory, very soluble in water and has but feeble reducing power.
