mass, n.

In physics. 1. A group of molecules, usually of considerable size, held together by cohesion; a body; as, a mass of gold, a mass of water. 2. 

The quantity of matter in a body. matter, n.

Anything which occupies space or takes up room; anything which 127

MEAN SOLAR DAY 

possesses the property of extension or magnitude. Having this property, it must have the property of impenetrability. All human experience supports the belief that anything which has extension and impenetrability, also has weight, inertia, porosity, divisibility, and indestructibility. These seven are therefore known as the general properties of matter, and the possession of any one of them is sufficient grounds for inferring the possession of the other six. Matter exists in three states or forms-the solid, the liquid, and the gaseous (q. v.). mean solar day.

The average length of all the solar days in the year; 86,400 seconds. 

It is about four minutes longer than the siderial day. medium, n. [From the Latin noun medium meaning middle.] 1. In physics, the substance through which any form of energy acts or by which it is transmitted; the instrument by means of which an effect is produced; any substance or body intervening between a cause and an effect. 

2. In biology, the nutritive substance upon which, or in which, microscopic forms of life are grown for examination or study. 3. One who acts as an intermediate agent. melt, v, i. and v.i.

To fuse; to liquefy; to change from the solid state of matter to the liquid state by the application of heat, which reduces cohesion between the molecules and allows them to slide freely over each other. melting point. 

1. That temperature at which a solid as it is gaining heat normally begins to pass into the liquid state, i. e., the temperature at which a part of the solid melts leaving the rest of it with its temperature unchanged. As a solid is gaining heat the temperature rises. For each degree of rise in temperature a very definite amount of heat must be gained by each gram of the substance. When a sufficient amount of heat has been added to a given body of the solid the temperature finally ceases to rise and the substance begins to melt. No further rise in temperature takes place until all of the solid has changed to the liquid state, the amount of solid melting being in proportion to the amount of heat added. The temperature at which liquefication takes place is called the melting point of the solid. 2. The temperature at which the solid and the liquid forms of a substance would remain in contact at atmospheric pressure without change in either. For water this temperature is 0° C. or 32° F. mercury, n. [From the Latin Mercurius, a god with whom the metal was associated.] Symbol Hg (from the Latin noun hydrargyrum meaning water metal); atomic weight 200.61; valence 1 and 2; density 13.596 at 0° C.; melting point -39.4° C.; boiling point 357° C. A silvery liquid metallic element known from ancient times and formerly called quicksilver. 128

METAL 

It is obtained chiefly from Spain, Mexico, and the United States. 

The metal is sometimes found free in nature, but the most important source is the mineral cinnabar (HgS) from which the metal is obtained by roasting. Mercury has an appreciable vapor tension at ordinary temperatures, and a piece of gold or copper held near the liquid metal. will be whitened in a few hours. Mercury forms alloys, called amalgams, with all the familiar metals except platinum and iron. The amalgams are of great importance in dentistry, and in many chemical. processes. Metallic mercury is used in the manufacture of barometers, manometers, thermometers, and many other instruments.

Large quantities are used in the manufacture of compounds of mercury, several of which are important paint pigments. The metal is also used in the extraction of gold and silver from their ores. The most important compounds of mercury are: corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride (HgCl) a very poisonous substance used as an antiseptic, calomel or mercurous chloride (HgCl) used in medicine; and mercuric fulminate Hg (ONC), an important explosive. All compounds of mercury are poisonous. The vapor from the highly heated metal is extremely dangerous. metal, n. 

1. In chemistry, an elementary substance which is fusible, opaque, usually malleable, conducts heat and electricity, and possess a bright shining surface-commonly called a metallic luster. Gold, silver, and copper were known in the days of remote antiquity because they occur in native form, and lead and tin because they are so easily reduced from their ores. The classical Greeks and Romans knew mercury, which sometimes occurs native, but it was not distinctly classed as a metal until it was learned that it could be frozen at a temperature only moderately low and was then malleable, for malleability was then considered a necessary characteristic of all metals. Antimony, bismuth, and zinc were added to the list of metals in the 15th and 16th centuries. Their ores had been used for centuries before this time and all of them being brittle, they were at first classed as semimetals. More than three-fourths of the elements are now classed as metals, and all of the metals but one (mercury) are solids. 2. The term is less technically extended to include alloys, such as bronze, brass, type-metal, etc. meter, n. 

[From the Greek noun metron meaning a measure.] 1. The primary unit of length in the metric system (q. v.) and equivalent to 39.37 inches. 

2. An instrument or device with which the quantity of something may be measured, or recorded, or both; as, a water meter, an electric meter, etc. -meter. 

A suffix often used in naming measuring instruments.

129

METHYL 

methyl, n. [So named because it may be considered as having been derived from its hydride methane (CH4).] In chemistry, a monovalent organic radical (CH3-), which exists only in combination with other elements or radicals. Combined with the hydroxyl radical (-OH) it forms methyl alcohol (CH3OH), and with chlorine, methyl chloride (CH,Cl).

It is the first of the series of organic radicals known as alkyls. methyl alcohol. [Formerly called wood alcohol, methylated spirit and in the crude or impure form, wood spirit. Known in chemistry, pharmacy and medicine only as methanol.] A colorless liquid compound (CH3OH), one of the substances produced when wood is destructively distilled. 

Large quantities are also produced by synthesis from coke and water. 

It is the first member of a series of alcohols of which grain or ethyl alcohol is the second member. Methyl alcohol (methanol) has an agreeable etherial odor, boils at 66° C. and has a specific gravity of .796 at 20° C. It is much more poisonous than ethyl alcohol, is a sedative, and a narcotic. Methyl alcohol is a very important solvent in the paint and varnish industries, is one of the most widely used chemicals for denaturing grain alcohol, and is the substance from which formaldehyde and a large number of other important chemical compounds are made. metric, adj. 

[From the Greek noun metron, a measure.] 1. Of or pertaining to meas ure or measurement. 2. Of or pertaining to a system of weights and measures in which the meter is the main primary unit. metric system.

A decimal system of weights and measures used in the sciences in all countries, and the legal system in Germany, France and most of the other important countries of the world except the U. S. and the countries of the British Empire. The basic units of this system are for length the meter, and for mass the kilogram. The meter is the distance between two parallel lines at 0° C. on a platinum-iridium bar called the International Prototype meter and kept in the vault of the 

International Bureau of Weights and Measures at Sevres, France. 

The meter is equal to 39.37 inches or 1,093611 United States yards.

The meter was originally defined as the 10,000,000th part of a quadrant, the distance measured on a meridian, from a pole to the equator. 

Recent studies have shown, however, that the ten-millionth part of a meridional quadrant is not exactly equal to the standard meter. 

The most important metric units derived from the meter are: the centimeter, the hundreth part of the meter; the millimeter, the thousandth part of a meter; and the kilometer which is 1000 meters. The kilogram, the primary standard of mass, is the mass of the International Prototype Kilogram of platinum-iridium kept in the vault of the International Bureau of Weights and Measures at Sevres, France. 130

MICROPHONE 

It is equal to the mass of 1000.027 cubic centimeters of pure water at 

4°C. and 760 mm. pressure. The kilogram was originally defined as the mass of a cubic decimeter (1000 cubic centimeters) of pure water at 4° C. and 760 mm. pressure, but was later found to be slightly greater than this. The standard unit of volume is the liter which is the volume of a kilogram of pure water measured at 4° C. and 760 mm. pressure and from this derived the milliliter, the 1000th part of the liter. The liter is equivalent to 1.000027 cubic decimeter or 1000.027 cubic centimeters. (For practical purposes, the liter is frequently considered to be equal to a cubic decimeter or 1000 cubic centimeters, and the milliliter to one cubic centimeter). Multiples of the primary units of the metric system are indicated by the prefixes, the most common of which are: deka, 10-; hecto-, 100; kilo-, 1000; and myria-, 10,000. Thus a dekaliter is equal to 10 liters and a kilogram to 1000 grams. Submultiples of the primary units are indicated by prefixes, the most common of which are: deci-, one-tenth; centi-, one-hundredth; milli-, one-thousandth; and micro-, onemillionth. Thus, a decigram is one tenth of a gram, a millimeter, one thousandth of a meter, etc. microphone, n. 

[From Greek words meaning small voice or sound.] An instrument or device for increasing the intensity of feeble sounds. The earliest form of the microphone was based on the fact that variations in pressure upon loosely packed carbon granules, in an electric circuit, produce variations in resistance and this causes corresponding variations in the intensity of the current passing through the circuit. Thus, in the telephone, the circuit consists of a battery, a receiving phone, the connecting wires, and the microphone or transmitter. Sound waves entering the transmitter (microphone) cause pulsations in the pressure upon the carbon granules and corresponding variations in resistance to the passage of the current from the battery. These variaations in resistance result in variations in intensity of the current passed and like variations in the strength of the electromagnet in the receiving phones. Thus the microphone acts like a valve upon which a small amount of energy in the form of sound waves may so act as to open the way for a current of much greater intensity through an electric circuit. The telephone transmitter is the most common form of microphone. microphotograph, n. [From Greek words meaning a small photograph.] A photograph of an object made so small as to require a microscope for its examination. microscope, n. [From Greek words meaning small and to see.] An instrument which enables the eye to see objects too small to be seen without it or to see details in the structure of minute objects which cannot be detected by the unaided eye. The simple microscope consists of a single convex lens (with or without a frame to hold it), a platform for the object 131

MICROSCOPIC 

viewed, and other accessories which render it handier to use, by which the rays of light from an object are bent out of their courses in such a way as to form an enlarged image, apparently nearer to the eye than the object. The compound microscope has two essential parts, (1), an object glass or objective, placed close to what is being examined, which forms a real image, enlarged and inverted; and (2), an eyepiece, by which this image is still further enlarged. Usually both eyepiece and objective are made up of several lenses, so constructed and combined as to avoid certain disadvantages which go with the use of two simple lenses, but these combinations still perform the functions of the two simple lenses described above. microscopic, adj. 

1. So called when visible only by the aid of the microscope. 2. Having the power to see very minute objects; as, a microscopic eye. milli-. [From the Latin word for thousand.] A prefix used to indicate the thousandth part of some unit of measure or value. milligram, n. [milligram.] The one thousandth part of one gram. One milligram is equivalent to .01543 grain. milliliter, n. [milliliter.] The one thousandth part of a liter. The liter has recently been made the standard unit for the measure of fluids, and volumetric measures are now graduated in terms of milliliters, or 1/1000 of a liter. The milliliter is almost, but not exactly, the equivalent of the cubic centimeter. millimeter, n. [millimeter.] The one thousandth part of a meter. One millimeter is equivalent to .03937 of an inch. mineral, n. 

1. In general, anything produced by a mine, or dug out of the ground; anything which enters as a constituent into the earth's crust. 2. Under the old classification of the universe into three kingdoms-the animal, the vegetable, and the mineral,—anything which is neither an animal nor a vegetable. 3. An inorganic substance which occurs in nature, whose molecules are of a fixed and uniform chemical composition which can be expressed by a chemical formula, and whose physical characteristics, such as color, density, crystallization, etc., are definite and uniform. Only two minerals-water and mercury-occur in the liquid state. Some rocks are simple minerals-as quartzite and limestone,but most of them are mixtures of two or more minerals. For example, granite may consist of quartz and feldspar only, but usually contains mica and hornblend, together with small quantities of other minerals. miscible, adj. [From the Latin verb meaning to mix.] Said of that which will mix with another substance. 132

MIXTURE 

mixture, n.

A substance which consists of two or more thoroughly intermingled and uniformly distributed ingredients whose molecules are not chemically united. Each ingredient retains its own specific properties, and if suitable means are employed, the ingredients can be separated by purely physical processes. Mixtures may be composed of elementary substances, of mixtures, of compounds, or of any two or of all three of these kinds of substance. For instance, air, a mixture of gases, has four chief ingredients, of which two (nitrogen and oxygen) are elementary substances, while two (carbon dioxide and water vapor) are compounds. Petroleum is a mixture of hydrocarbons (compounds), of which some are solids, some liquids, and some gases dissolved in the liquids. Some substances may form either mixtures or compounds. 

Thus, oxygen and hydrogen will retain their identities indefinitely in a mixture if the conditions necessary for their chemical union are absent, but will unite with explosive violence when those conditions are met. While the proportions of the ingredients of a mixture may be varied at will and indefinitely, the constituents of a compound unite chemically in fixed proportions in accordance with the law of definite proportions (q. v.). Alloys, emulsions, and solutions are all mixtures. molal, adj.

Of or pertaining to a solution which contains one mole of solute to one liter of solvent. molar, adj. [From the Latin noun meaning a mass.] In chemistry, referring to a mole (q. v.) or containing one mole; as molar volume, molar solution, or molar weight. molar volume. See gram-molecular-volume. mole, n. [From molecule.] In chemistry, one gram-molecular weight (q. v.) that number of grams of a compound which is numerically equal to its molecular weight. molecular, adj.

Pertaining to, made of, or produced by molecules. molecular theory.

In physics, the theory that any mass of any substance is composed of minute particles called molecules, which are far too small to be seen singly even by the aid of the most powerful microscope; that the molecules are separated by spaces; that in these spaces the molecules vibrate continually under the influence of heat, never in contact except momentarily when they collide; that each molecule retains the physical characteristics (specific properties) of the substance; and is incapable of further subdivision by physical forces (i. e., without bringing about a chemical change). The molecules of a gas are much 133

MOLECULAR WEIGHT 

more widely separated than those of a liquid or a solid, but the number of molecules in a cubic centimeter of air is estimated to be about 27 billion billion (27 x 1018). molecular weight.

The weight of the molecule of any element or compound; the sum of the weights of the atoms which compose the molecule of any element or compound. The unit of measurement of molecular weight is the same as that used to measure atomic weights, and is the sixteenth part of the weight of the atom of oxygen. Thus, the molecular weight of the compound sulphuric acid (H2SO4) is the sum of the weights of the two atoms of hydrogen (2), the atom of sulphur (32) and the four atoms of oxygen (64), or 98, and sulphuric acid is said to have a molecular weight of 98 because a molecule of sulphuric acid weights 98 times as much as the atom of hydrogen. molecule, n.

In physics and chemistry, the smallest mass of a substance which can exist and still retain the specific properties of the substance; the smallest portion into which a mass of matter can be divided without producing a chemical change and thus destroying the identity of the substance; the smallest subdivision of matter which can be made by physical forces only. Usually, but not always, the molecule consists of two or more atoms,-in the case of some organic compounds the number being very high. E. g., a molecule of stearin, the hardening constituent of mutton tallow, is made up of 173 atoms. It is estimated that one cubic centimeter of any gas contains from 25 to 36 million million millions of molecules. molybdenum, n. [From a Latin word for galena, an important lead ore, because of the resemblance of certain ores of molybdenum to metallic lead.] Symbol 

Mo; atomic weight 96; valence 2, 3 and 6; specific gravity 8.6; melting point about 2520° C. A silvery white brittle metal obtained by reduction of the trioxide with aluminum by the Goldschmidt process, and used chiefly as a constituent of high speed tool steel. Molybdenum steel is also used in the manufacture of crank shafts, parts for airplane motors, and other objects where tensile strength or toughness is desired. Molybdenum compounds are not widely used. The sulphide MoS2 (molybdenite) occurs in nature as a black lustrous mineral resembling graphite. It has some commercial uses, in addition to being the chief source of molybdenum. The trioxide of molybdenum is used as a blue pigment in the manufacture of pottery and and in the preparation of ammonium molybdate which is an important reagent used in the determination of phosphoric acid. momentum, n.

The potential energy of a moving body, calculated by taking the product of the mass and the velocity. Momentum is a manifestation of the inertia of a moving body. 134

MORDAN'T 

mordant, n. [From a French word meaning to bite.] In dyeing and in the printing of fabrics and rugs, a substance used to fix or make the dyestuff permanently fast to the fabric or rug. Mordants are of two general types; basic mordants (used to fix acid dyes), and acid mordants (used to fix basic dyes). The most common mordants used to fix basic dyes are tannic acid and its antimony compounds. The most common mordants for acid dyes are acetates of aluminum, chromium and iron. The mordant frequently modifies the color produced by the dye and it is possible by properly treating the fabric with the mordant to produce two different colors from the same dye solution. 2. Any acid or other corrosive liquid used in etching. 135

naphtha, n. N 

[From a Greek noun meaning a pitch or bitumen.] 1. An old name for any low boiling liquid obtained by the distillation of coal, shale, or crude petroleum. 2. A term formerly used to indicate a low boiling gasolene, having a specific gravity of about .7. Now rarely used. naphthalene, n. 

1. A white crystalline compound, (C10Hs) which is obtained from coal tar. It is soluble in alcohol, benzol and ether, and has antiseptic properties. It is usually sold in the form of flakes or balls. The commercial naphthalene has a peculiar odor, very penetrating and somewhat repulsive. The "moth balls" of commerce are nearly pure naphthalene. 

They are used in storing wool and furs to repel a type of moth, the larvae of which are very destructive to these materials. Naphthalene is fairly volatile and will gradually disappear if left in the open air. 

A solution of naphthalene in gasolene is used as a spray for the destruction of bed bugs and other vermin. Naphthalene is an important compound in the manufacture of coal tar dyes. 2. A name given to any member of a series of aromatic hydrocarbons, of which, naphthalene (C10Hs) is the simplest member. The general formula for members of the series is CnH2n-12. narcotic, n. [From the Greek verb meaning to benumb, to stupefy.] In medicine, a substance which, in moderate doses, quiets over-wrought nerves, deadens the senses, relieves pain, and soon produces deep sleep. In excessive doses it brings on stupor, coma, convulsions, and finally death. Opium (from which morphine is extracted) is a typical narcotic. narcotic, adj.

In medicine, having the properties of or producing the effects of a narcotic; as, a narcotic poison. nascent, adj. [From a Latin word meaning to be born.] In chemistry. A term used to describe a transient atomic condition of gases during which they are exceptionally active. Some gases exhibit greatly increased activity at the instant of their formation or release from combination with other atoms. At this instant they are said to be in a nascent state or nascent condition. There are two theories as to the cause of the nascent state. One holds that in ordinary hydrogen, the molecule consists of two hydrogen atoms already tightly bound to each other. 

In this condition the hydrogen molecule must be first decomposed into atoms before any reaction involving the atoms can take place. 

In the nascent condition the atoms at the moment of their release 136

NEON from combination, are already in the atomic condition and so are ready to take part in any reaction. The second theory holds that the increased activity of hydrogen and other gases just at the instant of their release from compounds, is due to the catalytic effect of the metals which are always present when the gases exhibit the properties which accompany the nascent condition. neon, n. 

[From a Greek adjective meaning new.] Symbol Ne; atomic weight 

20.2; valence 0; specific gravity 9.9 (HI); boiling point -243° C.

One of the rare gases of the atmosphere and present to the extent of one part in 65,000. The gas is absolutely inert and so it forms on compounds. Considerable quantities of neon are now being used in the manufacture of tubes for electrically lighted signs. These are glass tubes shaped like the various letters of the alphabet and filled with neon under very low pressure. They have no filaments, the light being produced by a high potential discharge through the rarefied gas. When the current is passed a beautiful reddish purple light is produced which seems to stand out in all directions from the glass walls of the tube. Electric signs lighted with neon tubes are very attractive and at the same time very cheaply operated. Very large neon tubes are coming into use in the lighting of aviation fields and in signalling between ships. neutral, adj. 

In chemistry, showing neither acid nor basic characteristics. neutralization, n.

In chemistry, the act of the process of chemically uniting equivalent amounts of an acid and a base, thus producing a salt and water; destroying the acidity of a solution by means of a base, or destroying the basicity of solution by means of an acid. nickel, n. [From the German noun kupfernikel meaning false copper, given to an ore which was thought to contain copper but from which no copper could be obtained.] Symbol Ni; atomic weight 58.69; valence 2 and 3; specific gravity 8.8; melting point 1466° C. A silvery white, hard, malleable and ductile metallic element discovered by Cronstedt in 1751. The metal is feebly magnetic and is a fair conductor of electricity. It is not attacked by hydrochloric or sulphuric acids, but dissolves readily in nitric acid. It is not easily corroded, keeping a high polish for a long period in air. The chief commercial sources are pentlandite, a mixture of the sulphides, of nickel, copper and iron, which occurs at Sudbury, Ontario, and which is roasted, reduced and finally subjected to treatment in a Bessemer converter (q. v.) and garnierite, a hydrated silicate of nickel and iron found in New Caledonia, and which is roasted to the oxide and then reduced with carbon. Nickel is used chiefly in nickel-plating, the preparation of alloys, and the manufacture of nickel compounds. Iron, steel and brass objects are 137

NICOTINE 

plated by attaching them to the cathode in a plating bath of nickelammonium-sulphate. The nickel plate is then highly polished. The chief alloys of nickel are coin nickel (copper 3 parts, nickel 1 part); monel metal (nickel 67 parts, copper 28 parts, iron 5 parts) a hard alloy not easily corroded; German silver (nickel 1 part, zinc 1 part, copper 2 parts) a white metal not easily corroded and widely used in the manufacture of small objects; nickel steel (1 to 4% nickel) used in making projectiles, armor plate, and auto parts; and invar (a steel alloy containing 36 percent nickel) an alloy which does not expand when heated. Nickel salts are poisonous and have decided antiseptic properties. They are widely used in the chemical industries. nicotine, n. [From the name of the Frenchman Nicot who first introduced tobacco into France.] An oily colorless liquid alkaloid (C10H14N2), found in tobacco in amounts varying from 1/2 to 9 percent. Nicotine has a burning acid taste, a characteristic odor and is very poisonous. It is soluble in water and is used as an insecticide in the destruction of ticks on cattle and lice on plants. About one half of the nicotine in tobacco escapes with its smoke, the rest being destroyed by the burning. nitrocellulose, n.

A mixture of the nitrates of cellulose; a product formed by the action. of a mixture of nitric and sulphuric acids on cotton, wood pulp, or other forms of cellulose. The number of acid radicals introduced depends upon the concentration of the acid solution and the temperature. A mixture of the lower nitrates (compounds in which two to five nitrate radicals have replaced hydroxyl radicals in cellulose) is known as pyroxalin (q. v.). The hexanitrate is usually made by the action of hot concentrated acids on cotton and is known as guncotton. 

It explodes violently when denoted, and is the principal ingredient in smokeless powder. nitrogen, n. [From the Latin noun nitrum meaning niter + -gen from the Latin verb generare meaning to generate.] Symbol N; atomic weight 14; valence 3 or 5; density .967; boiling point-195.7° C. A colorless, oderless, tasteless, gaseous element making up 78% of the atmosphere by volume, occuring combined with other elements, especially as sodium nitrate (NaNO3), and in compounds making up the bodies of animals and plants. Nitrogen is a very inactive element uniting with oxygen and a few of the metals only at very high temperatures. Compounds of nitrogen were at one time made from saltpeter (KNO3), and later from Chili saltpeter (NaNO3) over 60,000,000 tons of which have been taken from one great deposit in Chili since its discovery in 1809. 

Prior to 1914 nitrogen compounds were made chiefly from ammonium sulphate ((NH4)2SO4), obtained from coal tar; from Chili saltpeter; or from nitric acid made by the Birkelund and Eyde process, which 138

NITROGENOUS 

utilizes the nitrogen of the air. During and after the great war, several new proceses of extracting nitrogen from the air were developed. 

In the Haber process, nitrogen obtained by evaporating liquid air is heated with hydrogen under 200 atmospheres of pressure in the presence of metallic osmium or uranium. This produces ammonia which can easily be converted to nitric acid by the Ostwald process, in which the ammonia is heated with oxygen under pressure and in the presence of a suitable catalyst. Another method of converting atmospheric nitrogen into ammonia, was invented by Caro and Frank, and made a commercial success as early as 1906. In this process calcium carbide (made by heating coke and lime together) is heated to about 1100° C. in the presence of nitrogen (obtained by evaporating liquid air). The resulting compound is calcium cyanamide (CaCN2), a substance which when treated with an alkali and steam under pressure produces ammonia. The world's annual consumption of compounds of nitrogen is enormous and the demand for products made from atmospheric nitrogen is growing steadily. Approximately 720,000 tons of nitrogen were taken from the air and converted into useful compounds and in 1927. Nitric acid and ammonia are the most important compounds of nitrogen and from them most other nitrogenous compounds, including the high explosives, are made. Nitrogen is absolutely essential to plant and animal life. The element is a constituent of the proteins which make up the flesh of animals, and unless it is present in the soil in available form no plants can grow. Some plants obtain at least a portion of their nitrogen directly from the air, the assimilation being accomplished through the agency of certain micro-organisms which are gathered in little tubercles on the roots of the plants. 

While most of the nitrogen thus obtained is built into the body of the plant a considerable portion is converted into nitrogenous compounds and left in the soil, which is thus enriched. Free nitrogen is used, as described above, to prepare compounds of nitrogen. It is also used in the manufacture of electric light bulbs and thermometers. nitrogenous, adj.

Referring to nitrogen, or containing nitrogen. When applied to organic compounds, it often marks them as proteins. nitroglycerine, n.

A colorless or yellowish, oily liquid, (C3H5(NO3)3) formed by the action of a mixture of nitric and sulphuric acids on glycerine. It is poisonous and highly explosive. Dynamite is made by impregnating infusorial earth or sawdust with nitroglycerine. Physiologically, it acts as a heat stimulant and in medicine is used in treating angina pectoris and asthma. nucleus, n. [From the Latin noun meaning a little nut, a kernel.] 1. A central part, kernel, or core; the central and most important part around which other parts are grouped and to which they are subordinate; a center 139

NUCLEUS 

for growth from without; as, the nucleus of a new political party. 2. 

In biology, a spherical or elliptical mass of specialized protoplasm lying within the general protoplasmic mass (cytoplasm) of most animal and vegetable cells. The constructive metabolic functions of the cell-nutrition, growth, and reproduction are carried on chiefly by the nucleus. Within its delicate enclosing membrane (nuclear membrane) is a network of thread-like structures (reticulum), in which granules of chromatin are embedded. Between these threads is the more fluid nuclear sap. 3. In anatomy, a group of nerve cells within the brain or the spinal cord which constitute a ganglion and from which nerve fibers run out. 4. In organic chemistry, a group of atoms within the molecule of a compound whose members are firmly held together by one kind of chemical affinity while by another kind of chemical affinity they are less firmly combined with the other atoms of the molecule, so that one or more of the atoms outside the group can be replaced by another sort of atom without destroying the structure of the central group. The nucleus of the molecule of any benzene derivative-i. e., of any member of the "aromatic series" is the benzene ring composed of six carbon atoms. Also, a single atom within the molecule of a compound to which the other atoms, similarly replaceable, are losely united. The carbon atom around which four hydrogen atoms are grouped in the methane molecule is the nucleus. 

5. In atomic physics, according to the Bohr theory, a part of an atom consisting of a proton, of a group of protons, or of a group made up of protons and electrons around which other electrons (planetary electrons) revolve. 6. In geology, the central unexplored and unknown portion of the earth upon which the crust rests. 7. In astronomy, a small region within the head of a comet which is denser and brighter than the rest usually but not always present; a similar region often present in a nebula. 140

octave, n.

[From a Latin noun meaning an eighth.] In music. 1. A group of eight consecutive tones in the modern musical scale, the highest of which is produced by twice as many vibrations as the lowest. Two of the intervals between these tones (between 3 and 4 and between 7 and 8) are only half as great as the intervals separating the other five-i.e., the octave consists of two half-tones and five whole tones. The highest and lowest, which differ only in pitch, are given the same name and indicated by the same letter, and each is the starting-point of a similar group or octave above or below the given one. The human ear is capable of recognizing tones which comprise about eleven octaves. Since some of the lower animals produce sounds outside this range, it is believed that they perceive such tones. 2. The eighth tone above or below a given tone, both being counted; any one of a series of tones at similar intervals and indicated as the second, third, etc. octave above or below the given tone; a tone and its first octave sounded together; that part of a musical instrument which produces the octave as defined in (1) such as eight consecutive piano keys; the stop on the pipe organ which causes the first octave above each note played to be sounded with it, the stop usually called principal. 3. In physics, by analogy, the term octave is applied in other vibration series, such as those of heat or light, to rays whose vibration numbers are in the ratio of one to two. The range of the human eye is less than one light-octave. ohm, n. [Named after the German scientist G. S. Ohm.] In electricity, the standard unit of measurement of electrical resistance, being the resistance offered to a constant current by a column of mercury 106.3 cm. in length and 14.4521 gm. in mass, at 0° C.; the resistance of a circuit in which a pressure of one volt will cause a current of one ampere to pass. 

Ohm's law. -ol.

A statement of the relation of the potential and the resistance of an electric circuit to the current passed. The law is usually stated as follows: The current passing along a conductor is directly proportional to the electromotive force driving it and inversely proportional to the resistance offered by the conductor. Ohm's law may be expressed as follows: 

C=e, or Current (in amperes) 

E. M. F. (in volts) 

R. (in ohms) 

[From alcohol.] In chemistry. 1. A suffix used in naming chemical compounds to indicate that they contain the hydroxyl (-OH) radical 141

OLEIN 

which is typical of alcohols; as glycerol (glycerine), C3H5(OH)3. 2. -ol [From the Latin noun meaning oil.] A suffix frequently used in naming oils or substances which are oily in appearance, as, benzol (benzene, C6H6) and pyrole (CHN). olein, n. [From the Latin noun oleum meaning oil.] An oily fat found in both plant and animal tissues; the gylceride of oleic acid ((C17H33COO) 3C3H5) the trioleate of glycerine; triolein. Most common fats and vegetable oils are mixtures of olein, palmitin, and stearin. Fats consisting mainly of olein are more liquid than fats in which stearin or palmitin predominate. When subjected to saponification with sodium hydroxide (NaOH), olein yields glycerine and sodium oleate, the latter being a hard soap. oleo-, n. [From the Latin word oleum, meaning oil.] In chemistry, a combining form for olein, or oleic. oleomargarin, n.

A substitute for butter, made largely from animal fat; margarine. In the production of margarine (oleomargarine), cotton seed oil, cocoanut oil, palm oil, or even lard or tallow, may be used. A quantity of milk is cooled to 10° C. and is churned with a melted mixture of the fats and oils, the temperature being raised to about 30° C. When the mixture. has been thoroughly emulsified, it is cooled and allowed to stand for several hours, so that the ferment present in the milk may work and give the mixture a butter-like flavor. oleoresin, n. [Oleo+resin.] 1. A natural product, a balsam, consisting of an essential oil with a resin dissolved in it. Oleoresins usually have the consistency of honey, but most of them become solid or more nearly so with age and exposure to the air. 2. In pharmacy, an aromatic liquid obtained from certain plants or some of their parts by digesting them in ether and then evaporating the ether. The resulting preparation consists of an essential oil with a resin dissolved in it. open hearth.

The name of a process for making steel from cast or pig iron and of the furnace used in the process. (See iron). More than 75% of all the steel now made in the United States is made by the open hearth process. 

In the process scrap iron, iron oxide, and pig iron are heated together in a furnace lined with basic material (CaO) or (MgO), or with sand. 

The furnace is of the reverberatory regenerative type, i.e., one in which the heat is reflected downward from the roof of the furnace onto the metal (reverberatory); and in which the highly heated products of combustion are caused to heat the gases and air which are to be burned in the furnace, thus effecting a large saving in fuel. The open hearth process is easily controlled, produces a uniform product and is very efficient where large quantities of steel are being manufactured. 142

ORE 

ore, n. [Origin unknown.] Any native mineral material, which contains one or more metals in sufficient quantity to make their extraction profitable. 

Thus, gold-bearing quartz is an ore if the gold is sufficiently abundant to justify its extraction. Ores are usually named from the metal which is present in largest paying quantity. Thus an ore may be called a lead ore, though it also contains silver and gold. organic, adj.

In biology. 1. Made up of or possessed of organs; as, organic structure. 

2. Affecting an organ as an organic disease. 3. Pertaining to, or produced by living things; as, an organic substance. 4. In chemistry, applied to that branch of chemistry which deals with the compounds of carbon. This use of the word grew up when all known compounds of carbon were to be obtained only as the result of life processes, (having been produced by either plants or animals). After such compounds had been produced artificially, the name was not changed, but the drift is now toward the meaning chemistry of the carbon compounds, or chemistry of the hydrocarbons and their derivatives. oscillate, v.i. [From a Latin word meaning to swing.] 1. To vibrate or swing backward and forward like a pendulum; to fluctuate in intensity or volume between two limits. 2. In physics and mathematics, to vary or fluctuate above and below a mean value or standard. oscillation, n.

The act or process of swinging back and forth, or of periodically vibrating up and down, or above and below a mean or standard; the act or process of fluctuating in intensity or volume, with reference to a standard or average. 2. In physics, one single swing or vibration, of that which is oscillating, from one extreme to the other. oscillatory, adj.

Oscillating or vibrating; of or pertaining to oscillation or vibration. -ose. 

In chemistry. A suffix used in naming many carbohydrates. osmosis, n. [From a Greek word meaning pushing.] In physics and chemistry. A type of diffusion in which certain components of a solution pass through a semipermeable membrane in such a way as to tend to equalize the concentration of the solvent on the two sides of the membrane; the selective passage of some of the components of a solution through a porus wall which separates the solution into two or more parts. If a solution of cane sugar in water be placed in a cell which is closed on one side by a porus membrane (such as a hog's bladder) and the cell be immersed in pure water, the molecules of water will at once begin to pass through the membrane in both directions. The rate at which the molecules of water pass into the cell is greater than the rate at which they pass out of the cell-the molecules of sugar seeming to retard the natural 143

-OUS rate of passage outward or to increase the rate of passage inward. As the amount of water increases inside the cell the level of the solution will rise and a pressure will be produced (called osmotic pressure). 

With this increase in pressure the rate at which the molecules of water pass out of the cell increases and it finally equals the rate at which the molecules enter the cell. This equilibrium is maintained by the higher pressure inside the cell and this pressure (osmotic pressure) is directly proportional to the concentration of the sugar molecules and to the absolute temperature. Osmosis explains how water enters the root hairs of plants and how sap rises in trees. It also explains why celery crisps when placed in water, how many important physiological process of the body are possible, and many other phenomena of every-day life. osmotic pressure. 

See osmosis. -ous. 

In chemistry. 1. Suffix used in forming the name of an acid to indicate that it differs from another acid only by containing less oxygen; as sulphurous acid (H2SO3), to distinguish it from sulphuric acid (H2SO4). 

The name of a salt resulting from the neutralization of an -ous acid is given a name ending in -ite, as, sodium sulphite (Na2SO3), the sodium salt of sulphurous acid (H2SO3). 2. A suffix used to indicate that the principal element present is acting in its lowest valence; as, ferrous chloride (FeCl2) in which iron has a valence of two. overtone, n. [From the German word oberton, having the same meaning.] In physics and music, one of the upper partial tones of a musical note; a harmonic. 

When a string (as of a musical instrument) vibrates, it emits a series of tones all of which are heard together, and the whole sound is called a note. The ear is able to assign to this note a definite pitch which is that of the lowest tone of the series, called the fundamental. All the other tones which make up the complex sound of that note are called overtones or upper partial tones. Some of these overtones have frequencies which are exact multiples of the fundamental tone and are called harmonics. oxidation, n. [From oxygen.] In chemistry, the process by which oxygen unites chemically with some other substance; the condition or state of a substance which has been oxidized. Removing hydrogen; increasing the proportion of the negative element or reducing the proportion of the positive. element in a compound; increasing the active valence of a positive element or decreasing the active valence of a negative element; removing electrons as opposed to reduction which is adding electrons; the opposite of reduction. Oxidation originally meant simply the direct addition of oxygen to a substance but as it became clear that the action of the oxygen was not always a direct one and sometimes was not an additive process, the term was extended to include cases where the oxygen acted so as to remove hydrogen as in the oxidation of an 144

OXIDE alcohol to an aldehyde (CH3OH+0=HCHO+H2O), and less direct cases such as the oxidation of ferrous chloride to ferric chloride (2FeCl2+ O+2HCl=2FeCl3+H2O). In the last named process the ferrous ion gains one electron and increases its valence from 2 to 3, and the term oxidation now includes all similar reactions in which the positive element increases its valence or gains one or more electrons. Since in such a process as the oxidation of ferrous chloride, the proportion of the negative element is always increased and the proportion of the positive element is always decreased, the term has now come to include all cases where such increase in the negative element and decrease in the positive element takes place. oxide, n. 

In chemistry, a compound produced by the chemical union of oxygen and some other element or radical; a compound of oxygen and some more electro-positive element. Some oxides are gases (as carbon dioxide), some are liquids (as water), but most of them are solids (as the iron oxides). oxidize, v.t. and v.i. 

In chemistry. 1. To subject to oxidation; to cause a substance to combine with oxygen, or to remove hydrogen from it. 2. To increase the valence of a positive element or remove electrons from it. See oxidation. oxyacetylene, adj.

Referring to or made with oxygen and acetylene; usually used in reference to the blowpipe in which oxygen is used to burn acetylene gas. See blowpipe. oxygen, n. [From a Greek work meaning sharp or sour, +gen meaning to generate.] 

So named by Lavoisier because he thought oxygen a necessary constituent of all acids.] Symbol O; atomic weight 16; valence 2; specific gravity 1.195 (air = 1); boiling point - 182.5° C; melting point - 227° C.

A colorless, odorless, tasteless, very active elementary gas, occurring uncombined in the atmosphere and in great abundance in the earth's crust in combination with other elements. Oxygen is one of the most abundant, as well as one of the most important of the elements. Its nature was first understood by Scheele, a Swedish chemist who prepared it in 1771, but the discovery of the true nature of oxygen is usually attributed to Priestley, who was first to publish an account of the element. Water contains 88.8% of oxygen by weight; the air about 

20.8% of oxygen by volume; the common rocks and minerals such as clay, limestone, and sand about 50% by weight; the bodies of plants about 40% by weight; and of animals about 60% by weight. Oxygen is the necessary element in combustion and is absolutely necessary in the maintenance of both plant and animal life. Animals breathe oxygen into the lungs from which it passes into the blood and is carried to the various tissues of the body where it enters into chemical combination especially with compounds of carbon which are present and thus releases heat and other forms of energy, necessary for the continuance of the 145

OXYHYDROGEN 

life processes of the animal. Thus animals are continually using up oxygen. They convert this to carbon dioxide and breathe it out into the air. Oxygen may be prepared by heating certain compounds which are rich in oxygen, such as potassium chlorate (KClO3), or mercuric oxide (HgO), and by several other methods. It is usually prepared commercially either by passing direct current electricity through water containing a small amount of sulphuric acid (electrolysis of water), or by the evaporation of liquid air. In the latter process the liquid air is allowed to evaporate and the liquid nitrogen (boiling point 194° C.) vaporizes most rapidly, leaving the liquid oxygen (boiling point-182.5° 

C.) behind. Finally the oxygen is allowed to pass into steel tanks where it is stored under great pressure. The most important chemical property of oxygen is its chemical activity, since it forms compounds with all elements except fluorine and the inert gaseous elements of the atmosphere. Oxygen also reacts readily with many common compounds with the evolution of heat and often also of light; especially compounds such as cellulose (paper, wood, straw, etc.), and, the hydrocarbons (gasolene, fuel, oil, etc.). Oxygen also unites with many of the metals; as, with iron to form rust, and with copper and silver to form oxides (tarnish). Some substances unite slowly with oxygen at ordinary temperatures and much more rapidly at higher temperatures sometimes gradually heating and finally bursting into flame. Thus the spontaneous combustion (q.v.) of coal and oily rags is to be explained. In the process of oxidation each substance evolves a very definite amount of heat, thus a gram of pure carbon on oxidation always evolves 8080 calories of heat, whether the oxidation be rapid or slow. oxyhydrogen, adj.

Pertaining to, or designating a mixture of oxygen and hydrogen; as, the oxyhydrogen flame, a flame produced by burning hydrogen in undiluted oxygen; and the oxyhydrogen blowpipe, the blowpipe with which the oxyhydrogen flame is produced. When hydrogen burns, an intensely hot flame is produced. The amount of available heat is greatly increased when the hydrogen is burned in pure oxygen, as no heat is lost in raising the temperature of the inert nitrogen which is present in ordinary air. Thus with the oxyhydrogen blowpipe, a temperature of 2000° to 2500° C. may be reached. ozone, n. [From a Greek word meaning to smell.] A condensed gaseous form of oxygen in which three atoms make up the molecule. The gas is produced during the oxidation of yellow phosphorus, when moist potassium permanganate acts on concentrated sulphuric acid, by high potential electric discharges in air, and by other methods. The odor of the gas recalls that of burnt feathers. Ozone is a stronger oxidizer than oxygen, having the power to kill many kinds of bacteria, to instantly tarnish moist silver, to ignite ether, and to cause the rapid hardening of various drying oils. It is used commercially for sterilizing water and for bleaching various plant and animal products. 146

palmitin, n.

P [From palmatic acid which is so named because abundant in palm oil.] A solid almost crystalline compound (C15H31COO) 3C3H5, occurring in nearly all animal and vegetable fats; the tri-palmitate of glycerine; the glyceryl ester of palmitic acid. The consistence of fats and oils depends upon the relative proportions of olein, palmitin, and stearin which they contain. As these substances are all glyceryl esters of the corresponding acids (oleic, palmitic and stearic), they all are capable of saponification yielding a soap and glycerine as products. 

paraffin, n. 

[From Latin words meaning little affinity.] A name given to members of the methane series of hydrocarbons, because they are chemically very inactive. paraffine, n.

A white wax-like solid obtained commercially during the process of refining crude petroleum. It is a mixture of hydrocarbons consisting chiefly of some of the higher members of the methane series. 

The refined parafine is colorless, tasteless, odorless, and very resistant to practically all chemical reagents. It melts, according to its composition from 50° to 70° C. Parafine is used chiefly in water proofing fabrics and textile materials, making candles, and preserving foods. pendulum, n. [From the Latin word pendulus, meaning that which hangs or swings.] 

A hanging body so supported as to permit it to swing backwards and forwards, under the influence of gravity, with a minimum of frictional resistance. The period of a pendulum of given length is independent of the amplitude of oscillation. peptone, n. per. [From a Greek adjective meaning cooked, digested.] Any of a class of substances, usually described as secondary derived proteins, formed by the hydrolytic action of acids or pepsin on albumins in the stomach. 

Peptones are completely soluble in water, diffuse readily through animal or vegetable membranes, give the biuret reaction, are not precipitated from solution by saturation with ammonium sulphate solution, and are not coagulated by heat. 

In chemistry, a prefix frequently used to indicate that the substance contains more oxygen than another compound in which the same elements are present; as, in sodium perchlorate (NaClO4), so named because it contains more oxygen than sodium chlorate (NaClO3). periodic system.

A scheme of classification of the chemical elements into natural groups and families, on basis of similarity in properties. The scheme was 147

PERIPHERY 

developed by Mendeleeff, a Russian chemist in 1869. Doebereiner, Lothar Meyer, and others had already pointed out the fact that the elements show differences in properties which have constant relations to differences in atomic weights, but it remained for Mendeleeff to show that if the elements are arranged in the order of their increasing atomic weights, a periodic repetition of properties is obtained. He prepared the table or scheme of classification which bears his name and was able to predict, because of vacancies in the table, the discovery of elements then unknown. These elements were later discovered and found to have the properties predicted for them, thus establishing the value of the hypothesis which was the basis of the scheme of classification. See element. periphery, n. [From the Latin noun peripheria meaning that which extends around.] 

1. The outside edge or superficial surface of a body. 2. The circumference of a circle, ellipse or other curved figure. periscope, n. [From a Greek word meaning around+scope from a Greek word meaning to look around.] An instrument, apparatus, or device for viewing things not in the direct line of vision. The most common type of periscope consists of a tube or shaft supporting a system of mirrors so arranged as to allow light to enter through the side near one end and to pass through the other side near the opposite end. This apparatus enabled soldiers in the trenches, during the World War, to view the enemy's lines without exposing themselves, and the crew of a submarine war vessel to view the surrounding waters before the vessel actually came to the surface. phenol, n. [From a Greek verb meaning purple +-ol, oil-like.] 1. The first monohydric substitution product of benzol (C&H,OH) and also called carbolic acid. It is a white crystalline solid melting at 41° C., has strong acid properties, and is very poisonous. Phenol is obtained by distillation of the heavy oil from coal tar, and from benzene by fusing potassium hydroxide with potassium benzene sulphonate. It is used in the manufacture of salicylic acid, picric acid, bakelite, and condensite, and in the preparation of various antiseptics. The carbolic acid used in medicine is an 87% solution of phenol in water. 2. A member of the series of aromatic hydroxyl compounds of which phenol is the first member, any hydroxyl substitution product of benzene or a member of the benzene series in which the hydroxyl is directly united to the ring or nucleus. -phorous. 

Combining form from the Greek verb meaning to bear, used to signify bearing or producing. phosgene, n. [From Greek words meaning light and to generate or to produce.

] A 148

PHOSPHORUS 

poisonous gas (COCl2) which can be condensed to a liquid boiling at 8° C. It is prepared by exposing a mixture of carbon monoxide and chlorine to the sunlight, or passing it through a tube containing hot charcoal. 

The two gases combine to form carbonyl chloride or phosgene.. It is used in preparing certain dyes, as a poisonous gas in warfare, and in the manufacture of certain other very poisonous war gases. phosphorus, n. [From Greek words meaning light bearer.] Symbol P; atomic weight 

31; valence 5 and 3; specific gravity 1.83; melting point 44.3° C. A very active nonmetallic element of the nitrogen group never found free in nature but occurring combined in small quantities in nearly all soils, in the bodies of plants and animals, and in large quantities as a phosphate in apatite and several other minerals. Phosphorus is prepared from bone ash by treatment with sulphuric acid and subsequent heating of the phosphoric acid thus obtained with sand and carbon. The yellow or white phosphorous thus produced distills away and is condensed under water. From this yellow phosphorus other allotropic forms can be obtained. When heated to 240° to 250° C, out of contact with the air, the red form of the element is produced. White (yellow) phosphorus is a colorless or pale yellow wax-like solid which is soft and easily cut with a knife. It has a specific gravity of 1.83, melts at 44° C., and takes fire quickly when exposed to the air. It is extremely poisonous and persons exposed to its fumes or handling it continually soon succumb to a disease, (Phosphorus Necrosis) the chief characteristic of which is destruction of the jaw-bone. Phosphorus gives a faint light when exposed to air in a dark room and oxidizes readily to the pentoxide (P2O5), when ignited. It is used in the manufacture of chemicals, rat poison, and in warfare in the production of incendiary bombs and smoke screens. Red phosphorus is a powdery crystalline form, odorless and insoluble in water and carbon disulphide. It does not take fire in air, igniting only when heated above 240° C., and is not poisonous. 

Its specific gravity is 2.3. Red phosphorus is used in the manufacture of matches, phosphor bronze, and many chemicals. Phosphorus is a necessary constituent of all fertile soils, and great quantities of phosphate rock (Ca3(PO4)2) are mined annually and converted into phosphate fertilizer or "super phosphate," a mixture of compounds of nitrogen, potassium and phosphorus. Compounds of phosphorus are very numerous, and some of them of great commercial importance. Few of them, however, are used in very great quantities. photo- 

Combining form from the Greek noun meaning light. photometric, adj. PHOTOMICROGRAPH 

photomicrograph. [From Greek words meaning to write small by light.] A picture or print made from the enlarged photograph of a microscopic object. 

Compare with microphotograph. photosynthesis, n. [From Greek words meaning putting together by light.] The process by which carbohydrates are formed from water and carbon dioxide in the chlorophyll containing tissues of plants exposed to sunlight. 

The exact steps in this process are still unknown. physical change.

Any change in place, in shape, in temperature, in state of matter, and the like, which does not alter the nature of the substance-as distinguished from a chemical change (q. v.). Examples: the falling of an apple to the ground, the beating of gold into gold leaf, the heating of a piece of iron, the changing of water into its vapor or into ice, the grinding of wheat into flour, the mixing of white lead and linseed oil to make paint, etc. A physical change affects masses and even molecules, but not atoms. It belongs to the field of the science of physics, while changes affecting the arrangement of atoms in molecules belongs to the science. of chemistry. pitchblende, n.

A heavy brown or black mineral having a pitch-like luster and usually considered to be a mixture of oxides of uranium with small amounts of compounds of radium, and lead, and traces of a number of other metals. 

It is the chief source of radium and uranium and is mined in Bohemia, England, and in the United States, in Colorado, Texas, and South 

Dakota. 

plaster of Paris. 

A fine white powder (CaSO4)2 H2O, made by carefully heating gypsum (CaSO4 2H2O) at about 125° C until three-fourths of the water of hydration has been driven off. When water is added to the powder and mixed into a thick paste, the whole mass quickly begins to harden, and in a short time "sets" to a rigid solid, expanding slightly in the process. 

The substance is used in making molds for casting metals, for making casts to hold broken bones in place, in the manufacture of fireproof wall board, in the preparation of interior finish for buildings, in dentistry, and hundreds of other important processes. platinum, n. [From the Spanish noun platina a dimunitive from of plata meaning silver, and applied to platinum because of its resemblance to silver.] 

Symbol Pt; atomic weight 195.2; valence 2 and 4; specific gravity 

21.5; melting point 1755°C. A grayish white metal first introduced into 

Europe from Mexico about 1550. The metal rarely occurs combined with other elements, but is found alloyed with iron, copper, gold, or with some of the metals of the palladium group. Russia produces about 90% of the world's platinum, the remainder coming from Colombia, Brazil, 150

POLARISCOPE 

the United States, and Canada. The platinum alloys found in nature are dissolved in aqua regia and the platinum precipitated as ammonium chlorplatinate by means of ammonium chloride; upon ignition this compound yields the metal as a spongy black mass. Metallic platinum is very malleable and ductile and is a good conductor of electricity. It does not tarnish in air and is not attacked by hydrochloric or nitric acids, but dissolves readily in aqua regia. It is also attacked by fused nitrates and by potassium cynide, and disssolves readily in molten lead and other molten metals. When in a finely divided form, platinum readily absorbs hydrogen, oxygen, and other gases, at the same time, increasing their reactivity. It is one of the best catalysts known, and in the finely divided form, (platinum black) is known as "spongy platinum" which is widely used in industrial processes. Because of its high melting point, and its resistance to the action of acids, platinum is used for making crucibles, dishes, wire, electrodes, and other apparatus for the chemical laboratory. Metallic platinum is used in the manufacture of jewelry, in dentistry, in photography, and in the electrical industries. With iridium, an alloy is produced which is harder and more resistant to corrosion than pure platinum. This alloy is used in making pen points, and contact points for induction coils, magnetic vibrators and similar devices. Because of the wide usefulness of platinum made possible by its unusual properties, and the comparatively small amount of it available, the price of the metal is very high, (usually above $3.00 per gram), and many substitute alloys have been devised. Many of the compounds of platinum have unusual properties, but only limited usefulness, due to their high cost. Chlorplatinic acid (H₂PtCl) is probably the most important reagent containing platinum. It is used in the qualitative and quantitative analysis of solutions containing potassium, since potassium chlorplatinate (K₂PtCl), is practically insoluble in water. polariscope, n. [Polar+scope, also called polarimeter.] An instrument consisting essentially of two Nicol prisms so placed that one, the polarizer, produces a ray of polarized light (light vibrating in one plane only), while the other the analyzer, is free to rotate to the right or left. The instrument is used in the analysis of light and in the examination of liquids which have the power to rotate the plane of polarized light. When the ray from the polarizer is passed through a tube containing a solution of dextrose, the plane of the ray is rotated to the right, the amount of rotation being in proportion to the amount of pure dextrose in the solution. By rotating the analyzer until the plane of the rotated ray is found (to the point of maximum intensity) the amount of rotation in angular degrees can be read, and when the weight of dextrose and the volume of the solution is known, the percent of purity can be calculated. 

Some liquids and solutions of certain compounds are dextrorotatory (rotate a plane of polarized light to the right), while others are levorotatory (rotate the plane of the ray to the left). By means of the polariscope optically active substances may be analyzed both qualitatively 151

POLARIZATION 

and quantitatively. The instrument is extensively used in controlling the manufacture and refining of sugar. In this work, specially constructed instruments reading in both angular degrees and percentage of sucrose are used. These are called saccharimeters. See polarization. polarization, n. [From polarize.] 1. The act or process by which a body is caused to exhibit polarity or brought into a state of having poles; the degree of polarity exhibited by a body. 2. In electricity. The condition produced in an electrolytic cell, by the accumulation of hydrogen around its electrodes so that the internal resistance of the cell is greatly increased thus reducing the output. 3. In optics. A condition of rays of light as the result of which they exhibit different properties in different directions with maximum differences shown by vibrations at right angles to each other. According to the undulatory theory light is due to vibrations which produce waves transverse to the direction of propagation of the ray, but in all planes which cut its axis. By reflection from any plane surface such as a mirror or piece of polished metal, these waves can be given a definite direction and the light is then said to be polarized. 

By refraction of rays passing through crystals of Iceland-spar (crystallized calcium carbonate), other crystals, and through certain solutions polarization can also be brought about. In rotary polarization, as shown by the Nicol prism (Iceland-spar), solutions of dextrose, etc., the plane of the ray of polarized light is twisted or displaced an amount depending upon the nature of the optically active substance through which it has passed and the distance through it. Media which are capable of causing the angular displacement of a polarized ray of light are said to be optically active. Some rotate the ray to the left and are described as levorotatory while others which rotate the ray to the right are said to be dextrorotatory. All organic compounds which are optically active are found to contain an asymmetric carbon atom—(i.e. One which has its four valences satisfied by four different atoms or radicals). 

See polariscope. porosity, n.

In physics, that general property of matter by virtue of which every substance has minute irregularly shaped spaces, called pores, between the molecules, in which the molecules move back and forth. These pores are not to be confused with or thought of as resembling the openings in the human skin through which sweat issues. potash, n. 

1. Potassium carbonate obtained by leaching wood ashes to obtain lye and then evaporating the water from the lye and calcining the solid which results. This impure or crude potash, when most of its impurities have been removed, becomes pearlash. 2. Caustic potash is potassium hydroxide, KOH. potassium, n. [From the French word potossa meaning potash, because one of its im- 152

portant salts (K2CO3) was originally obtained from wood ashes. 

Symbol K (from the Latin noun kalium meaning potassium); atomic weight 39.1; valence 1; specific gravity .87; melting point 62.5° C.

A silver-white, soft, metallic element obtained by the electrolysis of fused potassium hydroxide. The metal readily attacks water liberating hydrogen and it oxidizes quickly in air so that it is never found free in nature. The chief ores are carnallite, (KCl. MgCl2.6H2O) which occurs. abundantly in nature, especially at Stassfurt in Germany, and as sylvinite (KCl) which occurs at Stassfurt and in Alsace and Galacia in smaller amounts. Potassium nitrate (KNO3) or saltpeter has been known and used from most ancient times. It is found in small amounts in many parts of the world especially in arid regions. Small amounts of potassium salts have been obtained from the ashes of sea weed. Potassium is the most electro positive of the common metals and its hydroxide is one of the strongest bases known. This substance (KOH) can be made by the electrolysis of a concentrated solution of potassium chloride, and by treating a concentrated solution of K2CO3 with a solution of Ca(OH)2. 

It is extensively used in the manufacture of soap. Potassium carbonate (K2CO3), the original potash of commerce, has been made from earliest times by evaporating the solution obtained when wood ashes are leached with water. This salt is used in the manufacture of soap and glass and many important chemicals. Potassium chlorate (KClO3), made by bubbling chlorine through a hot KOH solution, or by electrolysis of a hot KCl solution is a white crystalline solid, which yields all of its oxygen. on heating. It is a largely used in the preparation of explosives and fires works and as a laboratory reagent. [From a Latin word meaning strength or power.] In electricity, that electrical condition of a conductor or body which determines the flow of electricity from it or to it; electrical pressure. precipitate, n. [From the Latin verb meaning to throw or hurl down.] In chemistry, an insoluble solid substance formed within a body of liquid by some chemical or physical change. This change may be a rise or fall in temperature which renders insoluble part or all of a substance present in solution (examples: heating a solution of calcium sulphate, CaSO4 or cooling a solution of copper sulphate, CuSO4); or it may be the evaporation of part of the solvent, so that more of the solute is present than the remaining solvent can hold in solution (example: evaporating sugar cane juice to get sugar); or it may be the introduction into the liquid of a substance- either a solid, another liquid, or a gas-so that a chemical change or reaction takes place. (Example: the formation of insoluble barium sulphate, BaSO4, by mixing solutions of barium chloride, BaCl2, and sulphuric acid, H2SO4.) A precipitate may be crystalline, flocculent, or gelatinous; heavy enough to sink to the bot- 153

PRECIPITATE 

tom, light enough to float to the top, or just heavy enough to remain distributed throughout the liquid. Contrast with sediment. precipitate, v.i. and v.t. 

To separate out in solid form or deposit from solution; as, barium chloride added to a sulphuric acid solution causes the precipitation of barium sulphate BaSO4, as a white crystalline solid. In this case the solution contains (Ba) ions, (H) ions, and (SO4) ions, all of which travel freely about the liquid and come into contact with the other ions present. When (Ba) ions meet (SO4) ions, BaSO, molecules are formed. and, since this compound is not soluble in water, it immediately precipitates as a white crystalline solid. prism, n. 

1. In physics, a solid transparent body whose ends or bases are parallel regular polygons, usually equilateral triangles, and whose sides are rectangles,- for use in experiments in refraction and dispersion of light. 2. In geometry, a solid having similar, equal, and parallel polygons for its bases and parallelograms for its sides, distinguished as triangular prisms, pentagonal prisms, etc., from the shape of the base. process, n. 

1. A series of connected or related acts, operations, or treatments, all leading to one common result; as, the process of growth or of decay; a manufacturing process. 2. In biology, a projection; as, the spinous process of a vertebra. property, n.

A quality or attribute which is characteristic of, peculiar to, and always present in a substance, and individual, or a class; as, sweetness is at property of sugar; neutrality is a property of salts; irritablity is a property of living protoplasm. protein, n. [From a Greek adjective meaning first+in.] Any of a class of complex organic substances, found along with fats and carbohydrates in all living cells, and which always contain carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, and nitrogen, usually sulphur, and sometimes phosphorus, iron or other element. They are believed to be formed in the body by combination of various amino acids with the elimination of water. All proteins contain approximately 16% nitrogen, and all have very great molecular weights, though their exact composition is unknown. Proteins. seem to be used in the body, chiefly for the construction and repair of tissues. Proteins are divided according to the apparent complexity of the molecule, into three groups; the simple proteins, the conjugated proteins, and the derived proteins. I. The Simple Proteins are those which on hydrolysis yield only amino acids or their derivatives. II. The 

Conjugated Proteins are those composed of simple proteins combined with other groups which are not proteins in nature (i.e., acid, carbohydrate or inorganic groups.) III. Derived Proteins are those formed. by synthesis or by the decomposition of natural proteins. These are 154

PROTON 

divided into two groups, primary and secondary on basis of the complexity of the reaction by which they are formed. proton, n.

In physics and chemistry, one of the ultimate particles of matter out of which all atoms are built: the unit or smallest particle of positive electricity. A proton has approximately the mass of the hydrogen atom, but its volume appears to be extremely small. The individual atoms of all the elements contain an equal number of protons and electrons. 

In nearly all cases all the protons and about one half of the electrons make up the nucleus of the atom, while the remaining electrons revolve about the nucleus. The nucleus is thus always positively charged. pyrite, n. 

A brass-yellow substance (FeS2) occurring in cubical crystals and in compact masses. Sometimes called fool's gold, because of its superficial resemblance to gold. It is used as a source of sulphur dioxide in the manufacture of sulphuric acid. pyroxylin, n. [From Greek nouns meaning fire and wood] A mixture of the lower nitrates of cellulose made by treating cotton or wood pulp with a cold mixture of concentrated nitric and concentrated sulphuric acids. The compound is very inflammable, burning quitely in air but with explosive violence if ignited in an enclosed space. When dissolved in amyl acetate, it makes a valuable lacquer. When dissolved in a mixture of equal parts of alcohol and ether, a substance known as collodin is formed. 

This is used in the waterproofing of fabrics and in the manufacture of artificial silk, photographic films and transparent paper. It is also mixed with small amounts of turpentine and Canada balsam and sold as liquid courtplaster. Pyroxylin and camphor are the basic materials used in the preparation of celluloid. See nitrocellulose. 155

qualitative, adj.

Q [From the Latin qualis meaning of what kind.] Referring or pertaining to the kind of constituents or materials present; as, qualitative analysis, analysis to determine the kind or to identify the constituents present. quantitative, adj. 

[From the Latin word quantus meaning how much.] 1. Of or pertaining to quantity; the estimation or determination of quantity either of a body or substance, or of the constituents of a body or substance; as quantitative analysis, or analysis in which the quantity of a substance or a constituent is determined. 2. Referring to or designating something which may be measured in quantity. quartz, n. [From the German word of the same meaning.] A natural form of crystallized silicon dioxide (SiO2), (an abundant mineral in the earth's crust) and known under many names. Rock crystal (a very pure form), silica (common white sand), amethyst (violet colored), agate, onyx, flint, and jasper are all forms of quartz. The mineral is insoluble in acids, is very resistant to chemical reagents and so resists atmospheric weathering to a greater degree than other minerals with which it is. found. It also has a very low coefficient of thermal expansion. When haeted to 1600° C. quartz melts to a plastic liquid which can be cast into sheets or molded into utensils of various forms. The high resistance to the action of chemical reagents and the low coefficient of thermal expansion, are properties which make silica ware made from quartz very valuable for the various operations of the chemical laboratory. Such ware may be highly heated, then thrust into cold water without breaking. Silica sheets made from quartz allow the ultraviolet rays of sunlight to pass through them more readily then ordinary glass and so windows of this material have been recommended for hospitals and for homes where the beneficial effects of the ultraviolet rays are desired. quasi, adj., adv., or prefix. [From a Latin adverb meaning as if, as it were, just as.] In a certain sense; to a certain degree; apparently; implying doubt, unsoundness, or falsity; as, quasi-historical, quasi-organic. quicklime, n 

Unslaked lime, or calcium oxide (CaO). See lime. quicksilver, n. 

Another name for the metal mercury (q.v.). 156

R radiation, n.

In physics. 1. The throwing out of rays, such as light rays, heat rays, radium rays, etc. 2. The process by which radiant energy travels away from its source equally in all directions, in straight lines, at the speed of light, through transparent media, without affecting the medium through which it passes. According to the undulatory or, wave theory, the kinetic energy of the molecules of any substances which possesses any heat—i.e., any substance whose temperature is higher than -273° C.-is transformed into, or sets up, vibrations in the ether. 

These vibrations are transmitted in waves of various lengths. The effects produced by them depend upon the length of the waves, and also upon the nature of the opaque object upon which they fall. If the waves are less frequent than 395 trillion per second and longer than ten-thousandths of a millimeter, they are known as infra-red rays and produce thermal (heat) effects only. Waves with frequencies from 395 to 760 trillion per second and lengths from 7.6 down to 3.9 ten-thousandths of a millimeter affect the human retina, and are called light (q.v.) rays. Those with greater frequencies and shorter wave-lengths, known as ultra-violet rays, produce no effect upon the human eye, but do produce actinic (chemical) effects. 3. That which is radiated, as light or heat. 4. Radiation of heat, a mode of heat transference in which the heat (the kinetic energy of the rapidly vibrating molecules of a hot body) is first transformed into ether-waves, and these, upon striking an opaque substance, set up in it molecular vibrations which again are known as heat. Strictly speaking, heat is not transferred by radiation. 

Radiant energy is thrown off, and this is transformed into heat. This mode of heat transference differs from convection and conduction of heat in being practically instantaneous, carried in straight lines, only through transparent media, independent of ordinary matter, and without effect upon the medium through which the waves pass. radio-. 

A combining form indicating relation to or connection with a ray or radius, or to anything which is radiating from a central point or focus. radio, n. 

1. A system or process whereby sound energy or light energy may be reproduced at distant places by means of electrical energy which follows a path through certain apparatus and the ether. 2. The collection of apparatus by which the above result if achieved. The process has two main divisions-first, the transmission or sending or broadcasting; and, second, the receiving. The first of these consists of five steps, the second of four. In the reproduction of sounds the first step is to 157

RADIO 

cause a metallic diaphragm to vibrate, by directing sound waves against it. Second, the vibrations of the diaphragm are caused to produce variations in strength, or surges, or pulsations in a continuous, direct current of electricity. Third, these pulsations in the direct current are used to vary the intensity of the oscillations in an alternating current of electricity. Fourth, this alternating current is made to set up electrical vibrations in a wire which is exposed to the air.
