Fifth, this wire. sends out in all directions waves of electrical energy (radiations, hence the name radio), which travel through the air. These steps constitute broadcasting. The second stage of the process, the receiving of what is broadcast, consists of four more steps, reversing those which constitute broadcasting. The sixth step is the setting up in a second wire (similar to that used in four), of electrical impulses identical in nature to those set up in the wire used in the fourth step. Seventh, these impulses of alternating current cause a surging flow of direct current. Eighth, this vibrates a diaphragm similar to the one used in step one. Ninth, this diaphragm causes air vibrations which the ears of the listeners perceive as sounds. A radio outfit consists of two parts-sending apparatus and receiving apparatus. The essential parts of the sending equipment are: (1) a means of producing a uniform, continuous direct current of electricity a direct-current dynamo or a storage battery; (2) a means of using this to produce an alternating current of high voltage and high frequency an oscillating electric arc or a vacuum tube oscillator; (3) a means of varying the intensity of the oscillations-a modified telephone transmitter or microphone; (4) a means of setting up electrical disturbances in the air—an antenna. The essential parts of the receiving equipment are: (1) an antenna to receive the electrical impulses sent out by the sending antenna; (2) a detector, to change the alternating current of varying intensity to a direct current which flows in pulsations; (3) a telephone receiver to set up air waves similar to those which affected the telephone transmitter at the sending station. By a variation of the above process light energy is used instead of sound energy, and pictures, drawings, etc. are reproduced at the receiving station, (television). The average human ear recognizes as sound only those vibrations which vary in number between 16 per second and 50,000 per second. Sound is transmitted through air in waves which travel about 1/5 of a mile per second. The waves of electrical energy commonly used in radio transmission vary in number between 10,000 and 30,000,000 per second, and in length between about 18 2/3 miles (30,000 meters) and 1/600 of a mile (10 meters), and they travel at the speed of light- 186,300 miles per second. Radio telephone (in common usage the word radio is applied to this only, improperly excluding radio telegraphy) is an evolution, based upon the results of the work of Faraday (induction), Henry (the condenser), Hertz (electromagnetic waves), Marconi ("wireless" or radio telegraphy), Fleming (the two-electrode vacuum tube, or rectifier), DeForest (the three electrode vacuum tube rectifiermodulator, or audion), and scores of others. 158

RADIOACTIVE 

radioactive, adj.

Referring to or designating that which is spontaneously emitting radiant energy, has radioactivity or that which will produce the effect of radioactivity. It is supposed that all the elements are radioactive and that they are emitting energy as a result of, or else coincident with, the decomposition of their atoms. The term is usually used in describing those elements which are radiating energy at a very rapid rate. Uranium, uranium X, thorium, actinium, polonium, and radium are the elements commonly described as radioactive. radioactivity, n.

That property of certain elements by virtue of which they spontaneously emit radiant energy and produce electrical and other effects in the atmosphere around them. The common manifestations of radioactivity are: 1. The emanation of rays capable of affecting the photographic plate and that pass through opaque or thin metal objects; as, x-rays. 2. 

The ability to discharge an electroscope, placed near them, by making the air become an electrical conductor. 3. The emanation of rays which cause certain substances, such as crystalline zinc sulphide, to become fluorescent. radium, n. [From the Latin noun radium meaning ray, because of its property of emitting rays.] Symbol Ra; atomic weight 225.95; valence 2; melting point 700° C. A silvery white intensely radioactive metallic element belonging to family A or Group II, of the periodic classification. It decomposes water at ordinary temperatures and tarnishes quickly in air. The existence of such an element was first made known in 1910 by 

Madame Curie, who treated several tons of ore in order to isolate a few milligrams of a salt of the metal. The chief ores are pitchblende (occurring in Europe) and carnotite (found in Colorado and in greater quantities in Belgium Congo) in which salts of the metal occur to the extent of a few milligrams to the ton of ore. Radium is remarkable for the fact that it is disintegrating at a rapid rate, and at the same time giving off considerable energy in the form of heat, a radium salt being always 3° to 5° C. warmer than the surrounding air. It is estimated that one-half of a given quantity of the element will have disappeared in about 1700 years. As the radium decomposes, it gives rise to a number of disintegration products, including niton, helium, and lead. The radiations from radium compounds affect a photographic plate just as light does. They ionize the air around them causing it to conduct electricity. Emanations from radium compounds also cause certain other substances to become luminous so that watch dials, door knobs, push buttons, and the like may be treated so as to be made visible in the dark. All these phenomena result from the decomposition of the atoms of the element. It has been definitely proved that two kinds of electrically charged particles are being spontaneously shot out from the element, (Alpha and Beta rays) and that at the same time pulsations 159

RE- re- in the ether are produced (Gamma rays) either by the emitted particles or by the force which emits them. The Alpha rays are composed of positively charged helium atoms, and these cause most of the electrical effects. The Beta rays are composed of electrons (extremely small charges of negative electricity) which cause most of the photographic effects of radium. The Gamma rays are not material in nature but are similar in their penetrating power to X-rays. Radium produces important physiological effects and is widely used in medicine, especially in the treatment of malignant growths. 

A preface meaning back, again. reaction, n.

In chemistry, a chemical change; the production of a new substance, either by the uniting of two or more substances to form a new one, or the decomposing of one into two or more different substances. A reaction always results in a change in the chemical energy of the substances involved, and this is usually indicated by a rise or a fall in temperature. 

On this basis reactions are classified as exothermic (heat-producing), as the dissolving of iron in hydrochloric acid; or endothermic (heatabsorbing), as the breaking up of sugar into carbon, water vapor, etc., by the application of heat. On the basis of the number of molecules involved, reactions are classified as first order or monomolecular, second order or bimolecular, etc. A third classification is as reversible or nonreversible. reagent, n.

In chemistry. 1. A compound, element, mixture or solution used to bring about some definite chemical change. Most reagents are liquids and are usually solutions of acids, bases, or salts in water. Reagents are largely used in making chemical analyses, both qualitative and quantitative, and in experimental work. 2. Any substance or mixture of at chemical nature used to make known the condition or nature of the substance under investigation. reduce, v. [From Latin re back+ducere to lead.] In chemistry, to add hydrogen or to take away oxygen; to bring back to the metallic form; as, to reduce ferric oxide (Fe2O3) to iron. reduction, n. 

[From a Latin verb meaning to lead back.] 1. In metallurgy, the act or process by which a metal is extracted from its ores; and, since this usually involves the removal or oxygen, the opposite of oxidation. 2. 

In chemistry, the removal of oxygen; the addition of hydrogen; introducing or increasing the proportion of the positive element or removing or reducing the proportion of the negative element; increasing the active valence of the negative element or decreasing the active valence of the positive element; adding electrons; the opposite of oxidation. 

Reduction originally meant the removal of oxygen or another negative 160

REFRACT 

element often with the production of the free metallic constituent, but as it became clear that the action of reducing agents was not always a direct one, the meaning of the term was extended so as to include such cases as the reaction between hydrogen and ferric chloride (2FeCl3+H2=2FeCl2+2 HCl), and other reactions in which the hydrogen causes a reduction in the proportion of the negative element. 

Since in such processes the valence of the positive element was reduced from 3 to 2, and the atom gained an electron, the term reduction has now come to include all cases where there is a gain of electrons or where the proportion of the positive element is increased. refract, v.t. and v.i. 

1. To bend up or down: to turn aside. As, the walls of a furnace refract heat rays and thus keep the heat within the furnace. 2. In physics, to deflect from its path a ray of light or radiant energy, by causing it to pass obliquely from a rarer into a denser medium by which the velocity is reduced, or from a denser into a rarer medium by which the velocity is increased. refraction, n. 

1. In physics, the change in direction or the bending from a straight course which occurs when a ray of light or heat or sound passes obliquely from one medium into another in which it travels with a different velocity. When passing into a denser medium the velocity of the ray is decreased and it is bent toward the perpendicular to the surface which separates the media. When passing into a rarer medium, the bending is away from the perpendicular. Refraction of light occurs in all optical lenses, it causes a fish in water to seem to be at a higher level than it really is, the sun rises slightly earlier than it would if its rays were not refracted in passing through the atmosphere, and refraction accounts for the long twilights of high latitudes. 2. In astronomy, the bending of a ray of light as it passes from space, where it travels in a straight line, into the rare upper layer of the earth's atmosphere, and the continuously increased bending that occurs as it travels on into still denser layers as it approaches the earth's surface. The effect of this is that the source of the ray seems to be in a position higher than it really is. 

The refraction for a star at the zenith is 0, but for one at the horizon it is as much as 35' when conditions of temperature and moisture are such as to make it greatest. This is called atmospheric refraction or astronomical refraction, and the term is also applied to the amount of correction which must be made in calculating the positions of celestial bodies. refractory, adj. [From the same root as refraction.] 1. Referring to that which is unyielding, that which does not permit heat and other forms of energy to pass through. 2. In chemistry, referring to anything which resists ordinary treatment by means of chemical reagents or heat; indicating that which is difficult to fuse; referring to an ore that is difficult to reduce. 161

REFRIGERATION refrigeration, n. 

[re+the Latin verb meaning to make cool.] The act or process by which substances are made cool or cold, or are frozen, usually for the purpose of retarding decay. Refrigeration causes heat to be taken from. the substances by cold water or brine, cold air, or ice, and uses the cooling effect of dissolving certain salts (see freezing mixture), the evaporation of volatile liquids (such as liquid ammonia), and the expansion of compressed gases. regenerate, v.t.

To revive or give new vigor to; to strengthen or reinforce; to renew. regeneration, n. [re the Latin verb meaning to beget.] In physiology. 1. The production of new tissues or cells to replace those which have been worn out in the regular performance of their functions. 2. The growing together of parts which have been cut or broken, as in the healing of a severed nerve. 3. In biology, the reproduction or replacing of organs or parts lost by injury or disease. The ability to do this is shown by some vertebrates, as when a lizard grows a new tail to replace one which has been destroyed. It is more common among invertebrates and is most strikingly exhibited by the hydra. If this animal is cut into numerous pieces, each piece is capable, under favorable circumstances, of replacing all the other parts and becoming a complete animal. 4. 

In radio, the effect produced by reimpressing the energy from the detector tube back on to the grid, thus increasing the intensity of the incoming signals. As a result the vacuum tube becomes both a detector and an amplifier and its sensitiveness is enormously increased. regenerative, adj.

Of, pertaining to, using, or caused by regeneration; as a regenerative radio receiver-one which uses the principle of amplification of signals by regeneration, or a regenerative furnace-one in which heat is returned again to the furnace, so that its efficiency is greatly increased. relative humidity.

The amount of water vapor present in a body of air as compared with the amount required to saturate the same body of air. Relative humidity is expressed as the percentage which the first amount is of the second. 

If air is saturated, its relative humidity is 100%; if it contains no water vapor, its relative humidity is 0%. A rise in temperature causes an increase in the capacity of air to hold moisture, but the rate of increase grows less as the temperature rises. Thus, between 20° C. and 30°C., a rise in temperature of 10° C. nearly doubles this capacity, while between 

190° C. and 200° C. the capacity increases less than one-fourth. Warming a body of air without changing the amount of water which it contains lowers its relative humidity. Cooling it raises its relative humidity until, at the dew point, it is 100%. Further cooling lessens the amount of water vapor in the air by causing condensation, but leaves the relative humidity at 100%. Human comfort is greatest in artificially heated 162

RENNET 

rooms when the relative humidity is between 40 and 65%. See humidity, absolute humidity, and dew point. rennet, n. 

[From an Anglo-Saxon verb meaning to curdle.] 1. A preparation used in the manufacture of cheese to curdle or coagulate the milk. It consists of the dried lining of the stomach (especially the fourth stomach) of an unweaned calf; any preparation or infusion obtained from the above source. 2. Any preparation used to curdle milk, especially of plants of the madder family. 3. Rennin (q. v.). rennin, n.

In physiological chemistry, the active milk curdling enzyme of rennent. 

This enzyme is found in the gastric juice of higher animals including man. Some lower animals and some plants produce either this or a similar enzyme which will produce the same effect. Rennin diffuses readily through animal membranes and is not precipitated by heat. resin, n. 

[From the Latin noun resina meaning rosin.] Any of a class of uncrys tallizable solid or semisolid usually transparent or transluscent organic substances which are insoluble in water, but soluble in ether or turpentine. Resins may be exudated from living trees or the fossil remains of exudates of trees of other ages. They are mostly brown or yellow in color, soften on heating, are nonconductors of electricity and burn with a smoky flame. Resins are chiefly used in the manufacture of lacs and varnishes, some of the fossil resins such as copal and dammar forming the chief ingredients in our finest furniture and automobile varnishes. Rosin, the residue left after the distillation of crude turpentine is our commonest resin. It is used in the preparation of cheap varnishes, in the siezing of paper, in the manufacture of linoleum, and as an important ingredient in laundry soaps. resinous, adj. 

Like or similar to resin; containing or made from resin; gum-like. resonance, n. 

[From the Latin noun resonantia meaning an echo.] 1. The degree to which a body will resound; the condition of a body which is capable of resounding. In physics. 2. Increased intensity or duration of sound due to the sympathetic vibration of some body capable of vibrating at the proper rate. 3. Increased intensity or duration in the case of other phenomena due to vibrations; sympathetic vibration. resonator, n. [From the Latin verb resonare meaning to sound.] 1. That which resonates or resounds; anything that reinforces as a result of sympathetic vibration. In physics. 2. A device for reinforcing or intensifying sound or similar phenomenon, by sympathetic vibration. A device of this type depends upon the fact that when a train of waves is passing through 163

RESPIRATOR 

a medium in which there is a body which when it vibrates, has the same frequency as the wave, then that body will itself be caused to vibratethus reinforcing the effect of the train of waves. 3. A device used in the analysis of complex sounds. This device depends upon the fact that an air column in a tube of given dimensions will easily be set into vibration and thus reinforce sound of a certain pitch, but is not affected in the least by a tone of another pitch. 4. That part of a musical instrument which intensifies or modifies the sound; as, the sounding board of a piano, or the belly of the violin. respirator, n. 

[From the Latin verb meaning to breathe.] 1. A device consisting of a wire framework covered with gauze, with which to cover the nose or the nose and mouth so as to prevent the inhalation of harmful substances, such as acid fumes, dust, etc. Respirators are worn by grinders and firemen for this purpose, and by people with weak lungs to warm the air before it is inhaled. Medicated vapors may be administered by means of respirators. 2. In chemistry, a device used to hasten the process of filtration. It partially exhausts the air below the filter, and thus allows the air pressure above to push the filtrate through the filter more rapidly. resultant, n. [From the Latin verb resultare meaning to spring back.] 1. That which results or issues. 2. The force, velocity, angle, or direction resulting from two or more component forces (velocities, angles, or directions.) reverberatory, adj. [From a Latin verb meaning to strike back, to repel.] Of, or pertaining to that which is driven back or reflected; as wind, light, sound, or heat. reverberatory furnace.

In chemistry and metallurgy, a type of furnace in which the fuel is burned in a fire box at one side of the furnace, and the flame led over the metal or ore being treated, thus reflecting the heat downward from the arched roof of the furnace upon the metal or ore. reversible reaction, See reaction. revolution, n. 

1. The act of a solid body in following a circular or an elliptical path around an axis which is inside the path, but outside the body; as, the sun. Revolution is independent of rotation (q.v.), which may take place at the same time; as, the revolution of the earth around the sun occupies the same time that is occupied by its rotation 365 times. 2. A completed trip of a revolving body, or the time occupied by such a trip; as during a revolution of the earth, 365 rotations are completed. The confusion. of revolution with rotation is so imbedded in usage that there is little hope of its eradication. For instance, a wheel in a machine which is in running order may make "so many rotations per minute," but never "so many revolutions per minute"-the expression always used. 164

RHEOSTAT 

rheostat, n. [From Greek words meaning a stream and standing still.] An electrical device for controlling resistance in a circuit, thus bringing the current to a fixed value; an adjustable resistance. rhombic, adj. [From the Latin noun rhombus.] 1. Of the shape of a rhombus, i.e. a figure that has equal sides but unequal angles. rosin, n. [From resin.] A hard yellowish-red resin obtained as a residue from the distillation of crude turpentine. See resin. rotation, n.

The act of a solid body, such as a wheel or a sphere, in turning about an axis which is within it, so that each part of the body traces a circular path. 2. A completed trip of a rotating body, or the time occupied by such a trip. Compare with revolution. rotor, n. [From the Latin noun rota meaning a wheel.] 1. In mechanics, that part of a machine or other device which revolves; a rotator; as the rotating armature of a dynamo, or the shaft of a turbine. 2. In mathematics a quantity having magnitude, direction, and position. rubber, n. [The name is said to have come from the early use of the substance in rubbing out or erasing pencil marks.] The coagulated juice (latex) of certain tropical plants, especially of the genus Hevea; chemically a hydrocarbon (C5Hs), also known as caoutchouc. It is related to guttapercha and to turpentine, and can be synthesized from isoprene (C5H8), a liquid hydrocarbon obtained from turpentine. The plant juice has the consistency and appearance of thick cow's milk and contains approximately one-third of its weight of rubber. The juice is caught, strained, and coagulated with acetic acid. The "crepe" rubber thus obtained is the "raw" or "crude" rubber from which all other kinds are made. 

Practically all rubber on the market has been vulcanized. In the proc-< ess of vulcanization, sulphur is incorporated with the crude rubber, often with the addition of "fillers" which add weight and color to the finished product. In the vulcanization process, by varying the amount of sulphur added before the mixture is heated, products of varying degrees of hardness and elasticity are obtained. When 7% of sulphur is added, a soft pliable rubber is obtained, with 30% the hard or "ebonite" form results. Zinc oxide is usually used as a "filler" in the making of auto tires, white rubber tubing, and similar articles; lamp black is added for black rubber; and antimony sulphide and mercuric sulphide are used to give weight and color to red rubber. Pure gum is a very pure "latex" or raw rubber which has been vulcanized with but little sulphur and no filler. Rubber is used in the industries in ever increasing quantities and many rubber substitutes have been made. Chemists are trying to find a commercially successful process for synthesizing rubber from some cheap and plentiful material. 165

RUST 

rust, n. [From the Anglo Saxon word rust meaning red.] The reddish-brown coating formed on iron when it is exposed to the action of moisture and the oxygen of the air. This material is essentially ferric oxide (Fe2O3), but it usually contains some ferric hydroxide. The formula for rust is sometimes given as Fe2O3-3H2O, but this cannot be considered as exactly correct, as the amount of hydration of the oxide varies, and then some ferrous oxide (FeO), is usually present. Iron rust weights about 1.9 times as much as the iron which produces it. In botany. 2. A type of fungus which exists at some part of its life cycle as a parasite on grain or other plants, and produces visible red or black spots, thus giving the appearance of iron rust; as, wheat rust. 3. The disease of plants caused by the parasitic fungus mentioned above. 166

S saccharin, n. [From a Latin word meaning sugar.] A white crystalline synthetic product (C7H5NSO3) which is said to be 550 times as sweet as cane sugar. It melts at 220° C. It is manufactured on a large scale from toluene obtained from coal tar. It is used in many ways as a sugar substitute, particularly for sweetening the foods of diabetics. not a food in the sense that sugar is a food, but its use has not been proved harmful. Saccharin has antiseptic properties. saccharose, n. [From the Latin word for sugar.] Cane sugar, or sucrose (C12H22O11). 

A hard white crystalline compound very sweet and very soluble in water, obtained from the juices of many plants such as the sugar maple, the sugar beet, the sugar cane, and many others. The various varieties of sugar cane are the chief source of the manufactured product. The cane is crushed and the juices expelled and collected in tanks. Milk of lime is added to neutralize vegetable acids which are present. The clear juice is then boiled to coagulate albumenous matter, and carbon dioxide added to precipitate the excess of lime. The purified juice is then evaporated in vacuum pans. Upon cooling, the sugar crystallizes and is separated from the molasses by means of a centrifuge, which throws the molasses out through fine. holes but retains the crystals of sugar. Raw sugar obtained as above described is refined by dissolving in water, boiling with animal charcoal to decolorize, and recrystallizing by evaporation. When heated, pure sugar melts at 160 °C. and at 

170° begins to decompose forming a dark colored substance called caramel. On further heating, water is driven off and pure carbon alone remains. Concentrated sulphuric acid chars cane sugar and dilute acids convert it to invert sugar by hydrolysis. In this process one molecule of cane sugar is caused to unite with a molecule of water forming a larger molecule which decomposes producing one molecule of levulose and one of dextrose. Since a cane sugar solution will rotate the plane of a ray of polarized light to the right, and since a mixture of equal parts of levulose and dextrose rotates the plane of a ray of polarized light to the left, the process is spoken of as inversion. Sugar is a valuable food and is widely used as a sweetening agent and as a preservative of fruits and fruit products. sal ammoniac, n. [A name said to have been given to the salt because it was first obtained by treatment of camel's dung at the Temple of Ammon in Egypt.] 

In chemistry and pharmacy, ammonium chloride (NH4Cl), a white very soluble salt, obtained from the wash liquor from the scrubbers during the destructive distillation of coal. The salt is the cheapest compound of ammonium and so is frequently used as a source material in the preparation of ammonia gas. 167

SALINE 

saline, adj. [From the Latin word sal meaning salt.] Consisting of or containing salt; salty. salt, n. [From the Latin word sal meaning salt.] One of the products formed by the neutralization of an acid by a base or a base by an acid, the other product being water; a substance formed by the combination of a basic radical other than H, and a negative radical other than O or OH; any substance which yields ions other than hydrogen or hydroxyl ions; as, NaCl or common salt and KHCO3 or potassium bicarbonate. 

Salts are classified on basis of their structural composition as acid, basic, or normal. An acid salt is one which contains replaceable hydrogen; as NaHSO4. (Acid salts are sometimes called bi-salts; as, NaHCO3 sodium bicarbonate.) A basic salt is one which contains replaceable hydroxyl; as, Ca(OH) Cl. A normal salt is one which contains neither replaceable hydrogen nor replaceable hydroxyl. Salts containing only two elements or groups are described as binary salts and are named by replacing the suffix -ic of the name of the acid by -id; thus NaCl, the sodium salt of hydrochloric acid, is known as sodium chloride - the hydro of the name of the acid being dropped. Salts containing oxygen (i.e. salts derived from oxygen acids) are given names derived from the acids from which the salts are actually or theoretically obtained, the -ic suffix of the acid being changed to -ate in the salt (as, Na2SO4, sodium sulphate), and the -ous of the acid being changed to -ite in the salt; (as, Na₂SO, sodium sulphite). The prefixes hypo and per of the acids are retained in naming the salts (as, NaCIO, sodium hypochlorite and NaClO4, sodium perchlorate). The properties of salts vary greatly depending upon their composition. In taste they may be salty, bitter, sour or tasteless; in form crystalline, powdery or viscous solids. 

The color will usually be determined by the color of the acid and the base represented. All soluble salts produce both acid and basic ions on going into solution and so solutions of salts always conduct a current of electricty. Solutions of normal salts are not always neutral in reaction. 

A solution of a salt formed from a strong base and a weak acid will give a basic reaction; as, Na2CO3 (a solution of which is strongly basic), and a solution of a weak base and a strong acid will give an acid reaction; as, CuSO4 (a solution of which is distinctly acid in reaction). A salt may contain replaceable hydrogen and so be called an acid salt, and actually give a basic reaction in solution; as, NaHCO3, (sodium acid carbonate) solutions of which are distinctly basic in reaction. This is due to the strongly basic character of the sodium ion and the weakly acid character of the bicarbonate - (HCO3) ion. saltpeter, n. [From the Latin words sal meaning salt+petrae meaning stone.] 

1. Potassium nitrate (KNO3), a very soluble, colorless salt also known as "nitre." The salt was known to the ancients who obtained it by leaching certain soils and soft stones with water. It is believed to be 168

SAPONIFY 

formed by the oxidation of nitrogenous organic matter in the presence of alkali and some nitrifying organism. It is now made by the action of sodium nitrate (chile saltpeter) on potassium chloride. It is used in making gunpowder, fireworks, and matches; as a food preservative; as a flux in soldering; and in medicine as a diuretic and diophoretic. 

2. Chile saltpeter (Sodium Nitrate). 3. Wall saltpeter (Calcium Nitrate). saponify, v.t. [From the Latin noun meaning soap.] To undergo or subject to saponification. The process of saponification is in reality the decomposition of an ester by means of an alkali. In this process an alcohol and a salt are produced. The manufacture of soap depends upon the process of saponification. In this process animal fat, which is a mixture of the glycerine esters of oleic, palmitic and stearic acids, is boiled with caustic soda (NaOH) or caustic potash (KOH), soap and glycerine (an alcohol) resulting from the reaction. saturate, v.t. 

1. To fill completely with a liquid all the openings of a porous body; to soak; to cause complete penetration by a liquid: as, to saturate a sponge with water. In chemistry. 2. To satisfy the chemical affinity of some substance; to cause a reaction between two substances which are capable of chemical combination until no more of either substance remains uncombined; as, when an acid is brought into contact with an alkali until all of the alkali has been neutralized, the alkali is said to be saturated with the acid, or the acid is said to be saturated with the alkali. 3. To cause one substance to dissolve in another until the solvent will take up or dissolve no more of the solute; as, water is saturated with salt when any added salt will sink to the bottom unchanged instead of disappearing into the liquid. 4. To cause an atom or group of atoms to unite with the maximum number of atoms of some other kind; as, to saturate ethylene (C2H4) with hydrogen and thus produce ethane (C2H6). 5. In physics, to cause a portion of space, whether empty (a vacuum) or filled with some gas or vapor, to take up all that it will hold of some gas or vapor. saturated solution.

A body of liquid in which is dissolved the greatest amount of a certain solute than can be retained when an excess of the solid solute is present. 

Dissolved and undissolved solute can remain in a solution without change in their relative amounts only if an equilibrium between them has been established, and the establishment of this equilibrium is proof that the solution is saturated. Thereafter the number of molecules going into solution equals the number going out of solution.

A solution which is saturated with one solute may still dissolve some other substance. For example, a saturated solution of salt in water will dissolve sugar. In some cases the presence of one solute in a solvent increases its capacity to dissolve another. Thus, a very little iodine satu- 169

SATURATION 

rates water, but the addition of potassium iodide to this saturated solution renders it capable of dissolving large additional quantities of iodine. saturation, n.

The degree to which a body is saturated; the condition of being saturated; the act of saturating. second, n. 

[From the Latin adjective meaning second in order or following.] 

1. A unit of time, — the second subdivision of the hour,- equal to 1/60 of a minute, 1/3600 of an hour, or 1/86,400 of the mean solar day;- by symbol ("). It is the time unit in the C. G. S. system. 2. A unit of angular measurement, the second subdivision of the degree, equal to 1/60 of a minute of arc or 1/3600 of a degree of arc. 3. A unit of length, equal to 1/12 of an inch; a prime; a line. selenite, n. 

1. In chemistry, a salt of selenous acid. 2. In mineralogy, a form of crystallized calcium sulphate. See gypsum. selenium, n. [From the Greek word for moon, because of the similarity in properties between this element and tellurium, which derives its name from the 

Greek word for earth.] Symbol Se; atomic weight 79.2; valence 2 and 

4; melting point 217° C; specific gravity of the common black metal-like form 48. One of the nonmetallic elements, occurring in three allotropic forms, and resembling sulphur in many of its properties. It is obtained commercially from the mud which settles in the cells of the electrolytic copper refinery. Black selenium has a metallic appearance and is a good conductor of heat and electricty. Its conductivity is increased by light. Gray selenium and amorphous selenium are not good conductors. Selenium is used in the manufacture of the selenium cell, in the coloring of glass (gives red color), and in the preparation of selenium compounds. selenium cell.

A device which acts as a valve by which the flow of electricity through a circuit is controlled by light. The black crystalline form of selenium may be molded into wires or bars, the electrical conductivity of which varies directly with the amount of light falling upon it. Thus light rays may be translated into electromagnetic waves. By connecting this cell into a circuit with a suitable device for converting electromagnetic waves back into light waves, photographs may be sent over wires, or even by radio. The selenium cell is also utilized in other devices, such as the optophone by means of which the blind are enabled to read the printed page through the agency of the telephone. short circuit, v.t. 

1. In electricity, to introduce a shunt circuit of low resistance; to produce a short and easy path by accident or design, so that the current flows more directly. 2. To send or direct by an easier or shorter way or path. 170

SHUNT shunt, n.

In electricity, a side path or track; a way of connecting into an electric circuit so as to cause a part of the current to flow through a by-path or parallel circuit. shunt, v.t. [From an old English word meaning to push aside, or to avoid.] In electricity. 1. To turn an electric current to one side or in another direction. 2. To connect a conductor or an electrical instrument in such a way as to cause a part of the current to flow through it. When a conductor or instrument is connected in shunt, a divided circuit is produced and the amount of current which flows through each arm or branch of the circuit will be inversely proportional to the resistance of that arm or branch. silica, n. [Silic from Silicon+a an ending given to oxides of certain metals.] 

The oxide of silicon. Silica occurs in abundance as sand and as a constituent of clays and sandstones. It is also found in the crystalline form as agate, quartz, amethyst, rock crystal, flint, onyx, and opal. 

Enormous quantities of silica are used in the manufacture of glass, paints, pottery, bricks, and chemical products, such as sodium silicate and carborundum. silcon, n. [From the Latin word silex, a flint.] Symbol Si; atomic weight 28; valence 4; melting point above 1200 °C; specific gravity 2.37. A brittle grayish black or brown nonmetallic element, having two allotropic forms. The grayish black metal-like form is prepared by reducing the oxide with carbon in an electric furnace, and is a very hard brittle crystalline solid. The other form a soft brown powder is obtained by heating potassium silico fluoride with metallic potassium. Silicon is insoluble in water, hydrochloric and nitric acids, but soluble in hydrofluoric acid and some alkalis. Silicon imparts hardness and acid and alkali resisting properties to iron and steel. silver, n. 

[From the Anglo-Saxon noun seolfer, meaning silver.] Symbol Ag 

(From the Latin argentum meaning silver); atomic weight 107.88; specific gravity 10.5; valence 1; melting point 962 °C. in the absence of air or 

955 °C. in air or where oxygen can dissolve in it. Silver is a white lustrous metallic element occurring in nature both in the metallic state and in combination with many other elements. The chief ores of silver are argentite or silver glance (Ag₂S) and horn silver (AgCl). Considerable quantities of silver are also obtained in the process of refining lead and copper ores. The United States, Canada, and Mexico are the chief silver producing countries, giving the world three fourths of the total amount produced. The method of obtaining silver from its ores, depends upon the type of ore being treated. Sulphide ores are roasted with sodium chloride and then treated with metallic mercury. Ores containing 171

SLAG 

finely divided free silver are crushed and treated with mercury. In both cases an amalgam of silver-mercury is obtained. When heated in a retort the mercury boils away and is condensed for further use while the silver remains in the retort. Silver is recovered during the refining of lead, by the Park process in which about 1 1/2% of zinc is melted with the silver bearing lead, and the whole thoroughly mixed. Since the silver is much more soluble in molten zinc than in molten lead, nearly all the silver is dissolved and carried to the top by the zinc which is then skimmed off and subjected to distillation upon which the zinc boils away leaving the silver behind. Silver is usually refined by electrolysis or by melting and heating the metal in a blast of air to oxidize and blow away the lead, zinc, and other metallic impurities. Silver is the best conductor of heat and electricity known. It is also very ductile and malleable. 

It is not attacked by the oxygen of the air but is tarnished when hydrogen sulphide in moist air, comes into contact with it. Foods containing sulphur readily cause a coating of black silver sulphide to form on silver tableware. Silver is soluble in concentrated nitric acid and in hot concentrated sulphuric acid, but insoluble in hydrochloric acid. Large quantities of silver are used in coinage, in the production of jewelry and tableware, and in electroplating. Sterling silver or British coin silver was until recently an alloy of 92 1/2% silver and 7 1/2% copper. United

States coins contain 90% silver. The copper is added to harden the silver, which is a comparatively soft metal. Large quantities of silver are used in the production of mirrors. In this process an ammoniacal solution of silver nitrate is run over the surface of the glass and is then treated with a reducing agent such as formaldehyde or glucose. In this way a thin film of pure metallic silver is deposited upon the glass. 

Great quantities of silver salts, especially the bromide, chloride, and iodide, are used in photography. Silver nitrate or lunar caustic (AgNO3), is the most important salt of silver. It is used in medicine, in photography, and in silver plating. Many other silver salts are used in medicine and in the industries. slag, n. [The Swedish word slagg, meaning refuse.] In metallurgy, a product of the smelting process made up of refuse materials included in the furnace charge; dross;-also called cinder. During the smelting these materials fuse and because they are comparatively light, float to the top of the molten mass. Slag consists chiefly of refractory oxides present in the charge, oxides formed during the smelting, and silicates of calcium, magnesium, and aluminum derived from the flux. After removal from the furnace it solidifies into a mass which is glassy or crystalline, solid or porous, stonelike or earth, according to the substances composing it, its initial temperature, the rate of cooling, etc. Blast furnace slag is used in making Portland cement. The basic steel process yields a slag rich in phosphorus, and it is used in the manufacture of fertilizers. 

Some slags are made into paving and building blocks, while others are fit only for road ballast and filling. See flux. 172

SLAKE 

slake, v.t. [From an old English word meaning to grow slack.] 1. To cause to combine with water producing a hydroxide; as, the slaking of lime. 

2. To quench; to allay; as to slake ones thirst. 3. To retard; to relax. soda, n. [Through the Italian soda, from the Latin noun solida, (feminine of solidus, a solid), a name probably given to glasswort a species of marine plant whose ashes yielded sodium carbonate used in making glass.] 

1. In chemistry, sodium oxide (Na2O). Less correctly, sodium hydroxide (NaOH). 2. In common usage, sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) or saleratus and commonly called baking or cooking soda. 3. Less frequently, and especially in the industries, sodium carbonate (Na2CO3), also called soda ash (when impure), washing soda, and sal soda. The word soda has come to be used in reference to any strongly basic sodium compound to indicate its soda-like character. Thus, caustic soda (NaOH), and soda lime (a mixture of sodium and calcium oxides) get their names. Soda water (water charged with carbon dioxide) is so named because it was developed from an effervescing beverage in which the carbon dioxide was produced by the action of vinegar or lemon juice on baking soda. sodium, n. [From the same root as soda.] Symbol Na (From the Latin natrium meaning sodium); atomic weight 23; valence 1; specific gravity .9735; melting point 97.6° C. A soft silvery-white metallic element, which decomposes water with considerable violence. The chief source of sodium and sodium compounds is sodium chloride or common salt, which is mined in various parts of the world and also obtained by the evaporation of sea water. The metal is obtained by the electrolysis of the chloride or hydroxide. Sodium is a strongly basic element forming stable compounds with practically all acids. Sodium chloride (NaCl) or common salt, sodium carbonate (Na2CO3) or soda ash (washing soda), sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) or baking soda, sodium hydroxide (NaOH) or caustic soda, and sodium nitrate (NaNO3) or Chili saltpeter, are all of great commerical importance. sol, n.

In colloidal chemistry, a colloid dissolved in just enough liquid so that it remains unsolidified indefinitely. When water is withdrawn from solution containing a sol, the liquid becomes more viscous until it finally forms a firm, semi-solid, jelly-like mass called a gel. (q.v.) solid, n.

In physics, a mass of matter which preserves its definite shape and size when no force is applied to it; one which shows no tendency to flow; one with enough rigidity so that when a force sufficient to move it is applied to it, the whole body moves without its parts changing their relative positions; a portion of matter in the solid state. There is no sharp line of distinction between solids and liquids. Ice is commonly thought of as a brittle solid, yet in large masses, as in a glacier lying in a 173

SOLUBILITY 

valley, ice flows just as water does in a river-bed-most rapidly in the center at the top, most slowly at the sides and bottom-although everywhere very slowly. Masses of rosin, pitch, etc. slowly lose their sharp outlines and flatten out. Knowledge of these facts has given rise to the use of the terms semi-solid, viscous solid, and viscous liquid. The states of matter depend upon the strength of cohesion, and this is influenced. by both pressure and heat. Many solids become liquids upon the application of a moderate amount of heat, without any change in pressure. Others change to the liquid state only in response to a change in both temperature and pressure. All solids can be liquefied by supplying the proper conditions of pressure and temperature unless before the required temperature is reached the heat brings about some chemical change. For example, wood under ordinary pressure cannot be liquefied because oxidation occurs at a lower temperature than liquefaction. Solid carbonic acid sublimes (q.v.) under ordinary pressure, but liquefies under a sufficiently high pressure. 2. In geometry, anything having the three dimensions, length, breadth, and thickness; a portion of space enclosed by bounding surfaces. solubility, n. [See solution.] 1. That property of certain substances which makes them capable of being dissolved. A rise in temperature increases the solubility of most solids and decreases the solubility of all gases. 2. In chemistry, a numerical expression of the amount of a solute required to saturate its solvent. With solid solutes this usually takes the form of the ratio between the weights of the solute and the solvent; as, the solubility of silver chloride (AgCl) in water is 1.53: 1,000,000, or, the solubility of sodium hydroxide (NaOH) in water is 313:100. gaseous solutes the ratio between the volumes of the solute and the solvent is usually given; as, the solubility of ammonia gas in water (at 

0° C. and 760 mm. pressure) is 1298.9:1. The solubilities of liquids in liquids are expressed in both the above ways. soluble, adj. 

Capable of being dissolved. solute, n. 

A dissolved substance; a soluble substance. solution, n. [From a Latin word meaning to dissolve.] A body of homogeneous character consisting of one or more substances distributed throughout another and whose composition may be varied within wide limits. The most common solutions are those of salts or other compounds dissolved in water. In such solutions it usually is possible to vary the quantity of the salt or other compound dissolved within wide limits and still have a solution of this salt in water. There are many other kinds of solutions. Solutions of gases in gases, gases in liquids, gases in solids, liquids in liquids, solids in liquids, and solids in solids are all common. 174

SOLVENT 

solvent, n.

A substance which dissolves another substance, called the solute. The solvent is usually a liquid, but is sometimes a solid. The solute may be either a solid, a liquid, or a gas. The commonest solvent is water, particularly for salts. Alcohol is a solvent for resins, but not for gums. 

Ether is a solvent for fats and resins, etc. specific, adj.

Special or peculiar; characteristic of one thing only, as of a species. specific gravity.

In physics. 1. A number expressing the ratio of the mass of a given volume of some particular substance to the mass of the same volume of some other substance taken as a standard. Pure water at 4° C. is used as a standard in calculating the specific gravity of liquids, and hydrogen at 0° C. and 760 mm. pressure is the standard for gases. Since any volume of gold weighs 19.3 times as much as the same volume of pure water at 4° C., the specific gravity of gold is 19.3. 

2. Relative density. This term is now less frequently used than formerly, its place having been taken by density (q.v.). specific heat.

In physics. 1. The number of calories of heat required to raise the temperature of one gram of a substance 1° C., or the amount of heat given off by one gram of a substance when its temperature falls 1° C. 2. The ratio between the quantity of heat required to raise the temperature of any mass of a given substance any definite amount, and the amount of heat required to raise the temperature of the same mass of pure water that same amount. Under the first definition specific heat is a concrete number; as, the specific heat of copper is 0.0952 calories. Under the second definition specific heat is an abstract number; as, the specific heat of lead is 0.0315. The first definition is to be preferred. specific property.

In physics, any property which is peculiar to a substance and helps to distinguish it from other substances. To describe a substance is to give a list of its specific properties, and substances are distinguished from each other by comparing their specific properties. All matter possesses seven general properties-weight, extension, inertia, divisibility, porosity, indestructibility, and impenetrability-but different kinds of matter, or substances, differ in their specific properties (or in the degree to which they possess the same properties)-such as color, taste, odor, hardness, brittleness, ductility, malleability, tenacity, viscosity, melting point, temperature of vaporization, density, specific heat, etc. spectroscope, n. [spectram+scope.] An instrument used for producing and examining spectra, and for the analysis of light. A simple form of this instrument consists of a prism through which the rays of light are to be passed, a tube through which the light passes to the prism (the collimator), and 175

SPECTRUM 

a tube carrying a lens through which the light passes after leaving the prism. By means of this instrument the chemical composition of various substances can be determined, and light may be analyzed into its component rays. When a ray of white light is passed through a glass prism, it does not emerge as white light but as a number of individual colors, each component of the white ray emerging from the prism at a point and in a direction peculiar to it and depending upon the extent to which it has been refracted. Various chemical substances when heated to incandescence give rise to a series of rays, the wave length and color of which is peculiar to them, and always the same for the particular substance. These colors are not ordinarily visible as they blend into one ray of mixed colors, but the spectroscope separates the ray of mixed colors into its component parts thus making it possible to identify any substance or mixture by identification of the colors produced or by measurement of the wave lengths of the component rays. This method of analysis is very reliable and gives good results even with traces of many of the chemical elements. Light from very distant sources, such as the sun or stars, when passed through the spectroscope, may be made to reveal the elements present at its source, or even those present in a glowing gas, such as the sun's atmosphere, through which it has passed. (See Fraunhofer lines.) The spectroscope has become an indispensible instrument in the chemical laboratory where it has been the means of the discovery of a number of new elements and in the astronomical observatory where it reveals the nature of many of the heavenly bodies as well as their speed and direction as they are traveling through space. spectrum, n.

In physics, (the visible, ocular, or chromatic spectrum) a continuous band of colored light which is obtained when a beam of sunlight is passed through a prism. It is made up of seven regions, in each of which one of the colors of the rainbow, or prismatic colors, is most prominent These regions are not clearly marked off, the neighboring colors gradually shading into each other. Radiant energy which affects the human retina-i.e., that which is transmitted in waves whose lengths lie between 3.9 and 7.6 ten-thousandths of a millimeter (see light)is all refracted or bent out of a straight path by passing it through a prism, the red rays being refracted least, the violet rays most. Thus when the white light of the sun is passed through a prism, it is dispersed, and the colors are arranged in the order of their wave lengthsviolet, indigo, blue, green, yellow, orange, red. Vibgyor, a meaningless word composed of the initial letters of the names of these colors in their proper order, will serve as an aid in remembering their sequence. The spectrum formed by the dispersion of the light from an incandescent solid or liquid is continuous, while the light from a burning gas, unless under great pressure, gives a spectrum which is crossed by bright lines or bands (the bright-line spectrum). Since each element produces a spectrum with the lines spaced in a characteristic way, it is possible to 176

SPHERE 

learn the nature of the source of a beam of light by observing its spectrum. Thus, by the use of the spectroscope, much has been learned of the composition of the stars, and by 1891 Rowland had shown that 36 of the known elements exist in the sun. Helium (named for the Greek god Helios) was discovered by spectrum analysis in the sun before its presence in the earth's atmosphere was suspected. Spectrum analysis affords so delicate a test that the presence of one-fourteen billionth of a gram of sodium can be shown. It is used in detecting adulterations in foods, and in other practical ways. For absorption spectra, normal spectra, Fraunhofer lines, etc., consult a treatise on physics. sphere, n. 

1. In geometry, a solid body or a portion of space bounded by a uniformly curved surface every point of which is equally distant from a point within called its center; or the bounding surface of such a body. The formula for the surface of a sphere is 4πR²; for its volume 3/4πR³, R being the radius of the sphere. 2. In astronomy, (the celestial sphere) an imaginary spherical surface of which the earth is the center, one-half of which is visible from any point on the earth and constitutes the sky for that place. In this surface all the celestial bodies seem to be located, and on it all the astronomical circles-such as the equator, the tropics, the ecliptic, etc.—are thought of as being drawn. static, n. [From a Greek word meaning causing to stand.] In radio, the frying, crackling or roaring noise sometimes produced during the operation of receiving apparatus, and thought to be due to discharges of static electricity either from particles of water vapor in the air, or from charged bodies on the surface of the earth. steapsin, n.

One of the digestive ferments found in the pancreatic juice which is poured into the small intestine. Steapsin acts on oils and fats converting them to glycerine and fatty acids. The fatty acids are further attacked in the small intestine and converted into soaps and emulsions. 

See tripsin, and amylopsin. stearic, adj. 

Relating to stearin or tallow. stearic acid, n.

A white fatty compound (C17H35COOH) whose glycerine ester is a very important constituent of the more solid fats of animals and plants; as, beef and mutton suet. The ester is also found in the fat of cows' milk, in human fat, and in many vegetable fats. It is used for making candles, and then called commercially and improperly stearin. stearin, n. [From a Greek word meaning tallow or grease.] Glycerine tri stearate (C17H35COO) 3C3H5); the glycerine ester of stearic acid; one of the more solid constituents of nearly all animal and vegetable fats and oils. Stearin is a white oily solid melting at 71° C., to a yellowish white 177

STEEL 

oil. It is soluble in alcohol and ether, but insoluble in water. This is one of the main constituents of tallow, and is used as a base material for the manufacture of candles, soap, and the like. steel, n. [From an old Teutonic root, meaning rigid or firm.] A malleable alloy of carbon and iron which has been cast, free from slag, from the molten. condition or which is capable of being greatly hardened by sudden cooling. See iron. In such a carbon-iron alloy, silicon must not be present in amounts greater than a few hundredths of one per cent, sulphur and phosphorus must be present only in traces and the carbon content must not exceed two per cent. The properties of finished steel will depend upon the kind and amounts of the elements present in addition to iron, and also upon the treatment of the metal in the course of its preparation. In the manufacture of steel cast iron is treated so as to remove sulphur, silicon, phosphorus, and carbon, or to reduce these elements to the desired amounts. Steel is produced commercially almost entirely by either the Bessemer or the open hearth processes. In the 

Bessemer process, now usually called the acid Bessemer process, the molten iron is run into a large egg shaped steel crucible called the "converter." The "converter" is capable of holding 15 to 20 tons of iron, and is mounted in such a way as to permit it to tip when the molten steel is to be poured. The "converter" is lined with silica brick and has a perforated bottom through which air can be blown into the molten metal. White hot molten cast iron is run from the blast furnace into the "converter." Air under great pressure is then blown through the molten metal until all carbon has been removed as carbon dioxide and the sulphur and phosphorus content of the metal greatly reduced. 

The air blast is then discontinued and iron of high carbon content added in a quantity just sufficient to bring the total carbon content of the charge to the desired amount. After the charge has been thoroughly mixed the "converter" is tipped on its side and the molten steel poured into smaller containers for removal and further treatment. The entire process may require only twenty to thirty minutes. In the basic open hearth process, a large reverberatory furnace lined with limestone or other basic materials receives the charge of molten iron from the blast furnace. The metal is kept molten for several hours. The mixture of air and fuel gas oxidizes the carbon of the iron and thus gradually removes it, together with some phosphorus and sulphur. The lining of the furnace also combines with and thus removes some of the sulphur and phosphorus. In the course of operation samples of the iron are removed from the furnace at regular intervals and analysis made. 

When the carbon content has been reduced to the desired proportion, the molten metal (now steel) is withdrawn for further treatment. The 

Bessemer process is much more rapid than the open hearth process, but can only utilize cast iron which contains a very small amount of sulphur and phosphorus. The open hearth process does not require iron of high degree of purity and though requiring more time, can be easily 178

STEEL 

controlled and so made to produce a more uniform product. Most of the steel now produced in the United States is made by the open hearth process. Small amounts of steel, of a type requiring an exact carbon content, are made by the crucible process. In this process a quantity of open hearth steel is usually heated with charcoal and very high grade pig iron in a clay or graphite crucible. Only about one hundred pounds of steel is prepared at one time and the product is very uniform in composition. Steel for making razor blades, needles, watch parts, surgical instruments and fine tools is prepared by this method. High grade steel for automobile axles and gears, and for other expensive machines, is now prepared in considerable amounts in a special type of electric furnace. While only ten to twenty tons of steel can be prepared at one time, the operation is easily controlled and a very uniform and high grade product can be easily made. The properties of steel vary with its composition and with the method of preparation but it can be said that steel usually has greater tensile strength than wrought iron, is harder, can be welded and tempered, may be permanently magnetized and is more uniform in texture than any other kind of iron. When steel is heated to temperatures above 900° C. and then suddenly cooled, it becomes extremely hard, and brittle. If it is then cautiously reheated and allowed to cool slowly, the hardness is partially removed. By carefully controlling the temperature during the reheating process, steel of any desired degree of hardness may be obtained. This treatment is called tempering. By heating objects made of mild or semi-hard steel in the presence of powdered charcoal, potassium ferrocyanide, or other nitrogenous material containing carbon, a layer of steel on the outersurface may be caused to increase its carbon content considerably and its hardness thus greatly increased. This treatment is called case hardening. When it is applied to armor plate for battleships, with certain modifications of the process, Harveyized steel is produced and the process is known as the Harvey process after its inventor. Small amounts of such metals as manganese, aluminum and silicon are sometimes added to molten steel because they combine with and remove various impurities. Metals added for purposes of purification are called scavengers. Larger amounts of other metals may be added to molten steel to make alloy steel. The addition of 7 to 20% of manganese produces a very tough hard steel which is used in the manufacture of burglar-proof safes and the jaws of rock-crushing machinery. Chromium causes steel to become hard and when added. with nickel produces a steel of great tensile strength. Stainless steel contains about 15% of chromium and 12 to 15% of nickel. When a small amount of vanadium is added to chromium steel an alloy of great tensile strength is produced. This alloy is also very rigid, yet can be bent double without breaking. It is used in the manufacture of automobile axles and frames, and for parts for other expensive machines. 

When ordinary steel is heated to redness it loses its temper and becomes soft. Steel which contains 8 to 20% of tungsten is very hard and does 179

SUBLIMATE 

not lose its temper when heated. It is used in the manufacture of high speed tool steel. Its cutting efficiency is approximately 400 times that of good carbon steel. Steel containing 2 to 4% of nickel is very resistant to corrosion and is very hard and elastic. It is used in making springs, propeller shafts, armor plate, wire cables, and other articles of a similar type. Steel containing 36% nickel, expands less than any other alloy or metal when heated and is therefore used in the manufacture of pendulum rods, measuring instruments, and watch parts. This alloy is known under the trade name of invar. Titanium and molybdenum are also used in the manufacture of steel for special purposes. Silicon renders iron and steel resistant to acids and alkalis. Many other metals are added to steel in order to produce alloys with special properties. sublimate, n.

A substance formed by sublimation (q.v.); as, corrosive sublimate, HgCl2. sublimate, v.t. and v.i. 

1. To produce by sublimation (q.v.). To cause (a substance) to sublime. 

2. To change from the solid to the gaseous state and back to the solid state again, as the effect of heating and cooling. sublimation, n.

The act or process of changing a substance from the solid state of matter into its vapor and then back into the solid state, apparently without passing through the liquid state. Like distillation, this process consists of two steps-vaporization and condensation, and also like distillation, it is frequently used as a means of separating a substance from its impurities. Iodine (not the tincture of iodine, which is an alcoholic solution of iodine, but the dark gray, scale-like crystals of the elementary substance iodine), when gently heated, produces clouds of purple vapor which on cooling condense into the crystalline form again. Some other substances which sublime readily and are usually purified by sublimation are mercuric chloride (corrosive sublimate), naphthalene (of which moth balls are made), camphor, and arsenic trioxide. 2. Inaccurately defined by some writers as vaporization from the solid state, without reference to the second step, solidification from the vapor state. These same writers inconsistently refer to the result of sublimation as a sublimate, which is always a solid. substance, n.

A particular kind of matter. It may be composed of molecules all of which are alike an elementary substance; or of molecules each of which is composed of two or more unlike atoms in chemical union—a compound substance; or of unlike molecules, either elementary or compound -a mixture. Substances are distinguished from each other by their specific properties, and to describe a substance is to give a list of its specific properties. sucrose, n. [From the French word sucre, meaning sugar.] The white crystalline 180

SUGAR 

compound known variously as cane-sugar, beet sugar, maple-sugar, etc. according to its origin, all of these forms being identical chemically, and having the composition (C12H22O11). See saccharose. sugar, n. [From the French noun sucre meaning sugar.] 1. A crystalline sweet tasting solid (C12H22O11), white when pure, also called cane sugar (q.v.), sucrose, and saccharose, and occurring in the juices of many. plants but obtained commercially from the juice of the sugar cane and the sugar beet. 2. In chemistry, any of a class of crystalline carbohydrates, soluble in water, having a sweet taste, and directly or indirectly fermentable; as, maltose, (C12H22O11 H2O), dextrose (C6H12O6), and lactose (C12H22O11 · H2O). sulphate, n.

A salt or ester of sulphuric acid; as sodium sulphate (Na2SO4). The normal suphates of the metals are all soluble in water except the sulphates of lead and barium which are insoluble and the sulphates of strontium and calcium which are sparingly soluble. Sulphates of some of the metals are known as vitrols; as, blue vitrol or copper sulphate, and green vitrol or iron sulphate. sulphide, n.

A compound formed by the chemical combination of sulphur and some other element or radical; a salt of hydrosulphuric acid; as ferrous sulphide (FeS) and carbon bi-sulphide (CS2). Sulphides were formerly known as sulphurets, and sulphuretted hydrogen (H2S) and sulphuret of antimony (Sb2S3) are names still quite commonly used. Sulphides of some of the metals occur in abundance in nature. Lead sulphide (PbS) and zinc sulphide (ZuS) are typical sulphide ores. sulphite, n.

A salt or ester of sulphurous acid; as, sodium sulphite (Na2SO3). sulphur, n. 

Symbol S; atomic weight 32.064; valence 2, 4, 6; density (rhombic sulphur) 2.06; melting point (rhombic sulphur) 114.5° C. A nonmetallic element occurring free in nature in great quantities and also combined in sulphides of lead, iron, copper, zinc and other metals, and in sulphates of calcium, strontium, barium and magnesium. The element was known to the ancients as brimstone (burnstone) and to the alchemists who considered it one of the fundamental elements. The most important deposits of free sulphur are in the United States (Louisiana and Texas), Sicily, and Japan. The Sicilian deposits have been longest known and up to the year 1900 furnished practically all the sulphur used commercially. The Sicilian method of extraction is unusual in that the sulphur rock is ignited and heat is thus generated to melt the sulphur in the surrounding rock. This method causes the loss of nearly one fifth of the sulphur in the rock, but it is used because of the lack of coal or other fuel. In Louisiana and Texas, sulphur is obtained by the Frash method, in which superheated steam is pumped down to the sulphur 181

SUPERCOOL 

deposits (which lie about 900 feet below the surface) and after a quantity of sulphur has been melted, compressed air forces the frothy mixture of air, hot water and molten sulphur to the surface. In this way some wells are made to yield as much as 500 tons of sulphur per day, and a total of more than 1,500,000 tons are now produced yearly. The 

Louisiana and Texas sulphur is very pure and now yields at least 80% of the world's supply. Iron pyrites (FeS), also called fool's gold, is a mineral occurring in all parts of the world; but great quantities of a very pure form of the mineral are imported into the United States from Spain, for the manufacture of sulphuric acid (q.v.). Sulphur exists in a number of allotropic forms. A crystalline (rhombic) form having a melting point of 114.5° C. and a density of 2.06 can be obtained by dissolving sulphur in carbon bisulphide (CS2), and allowing the liquid to evaporate. The crystals thus obtained are stable below 96.5° C., but at temperatures above this point, they change to needle like (monoclinic) crystals which have a density of 1.96 and melt at 119° C. 

In addition to the two crystalline forms of sulphur there are three amorphous (non crystalline) forms; plastic sulphur, a rubber-like form, obtained by pouring molten sulphur into cold water; roll brimstone, a yellow waxlike solid made by pouring molten sulphur into molds; and precipitated sulphur (also called milk sulphur and lac sulphur) occurring naturally in sulphur springs and made commercially by decomposing a sulphide with a strong oxidizing agent. Sulphur is a very active element uniting with nearly all the metals and many of the nonmetals. When sulphur burns in air, the dioxide (SO2) is produced. 

This is easily converted into the trioxide (SO3) which is the anhydride of sulphuric acid (q.v.). More than 1,000,000 tons of sulphur are used annually by the industries of the United States. The largest quantities of sulphur are consumed in the vulcanization of rubber, in the manufacture of chemicals, in the bleaching of fruits, and in the preparation of insecticides and fungicides. Sulphur is marketed in the forms of roll brimstone, precipitated sulphur, and "flowers of sulphur" (sublimed sulphur). The most important compounds of sulphur are sulphuric acid, the vitriols (q.v.), the alums (q.v.), and the various sulphides many of which are brilliantly colored and comparatively stable, thus being well adapted for use as paint pigments. supercool, v.t. 

In physics and chemistry, to reduce the temperature of a liquid (as water) below its normal freezing point without having it solidify. 

Though the freezing point of water is 0 degrees C. water may be slowly cooled to a temperature much below this point without its freezing. It is then said to be supercooled. If a crystal of ice be dropped into it, or if it be shaken or otherwise agitated, it will immediately solidify and the temperature will rise to the freezing point. Many other liquids, like water, may be supercooled. supercooling, n.

In physics and chemistry, the phenomenon which occurs when a body 182

SUPERHEAT 

of liquid is cooled below its freezing point without any part of it passing into the solid state. Any jarring which facilitates the rearrangement of the molecules in crystalline form, or the presence of even a tiny crystal to serve as a nucleus around which more of the substance can crystallize, will prevent supercooling. superheat, v.t. 

To heat to excess; to overheat; specifically to raise the temperature of a vapor (as steam) to a point above the usual boiling temperature of the liquid from which it is formed; to raise the temperature of a liquid higher than its boiling point. supersaturated solution, n. 

A solution containing more solute than is present when the solution is saturated under normal conditions. If a hot saturated solution of sodium acetate be slowly cooled, no solid separates out even after a drop in temperature of several degrees. This solution is then said to be supersaturated. If the solution be shaken or if a crystal of sodium acetate be added, all excess salt crystallizes at once. supersaturation, n.

That condition of a solution in which more of the solute is dissolved than the maximum amount possible in the presence of a crystal of the solute. surface tension. 

A characteristic of the surface film of a liquid which causes it to behave like a stretched, elastic membrane. Being both stretched and elastic, the surface film tends to contract and to bring the body of liquid which it bounds to the form which has the smallest bounding surface for its volume the sphere. This result can be attained only when surface tension is not interfered with by any other force, as when a raindrop falls freely through the air. When the liquid is in a small tube and wets its walls, the upper surface is curved upward along the walls because adhesion is stronger than surface tension, but the tendency of surface tension is to straighten this curved surface, and so to reduce its area. 

The molecules below the surface, being surrounded by other molecules and so attracted in all directions (cohesion), are in a state of equilibrium, while the top layer of molecules, being without such attraction on one side, are under strain, which induces movement-the contraction of the film. This manifestation of cohesion affects the molecules only to a depth equal to the radius of the sphere within which the cohesive attraction is effective-less than one-thousandth of a millimeter. 

Water-spiders are able to walk on the surface of the water because the elastic film pushes upward strongly enough to support their weight, and a needle (greased to avoid adhesion) floats on water because of surface tension. See capillarity. suspension, n. [From a Latin word meaning to hang up.] In chemistry. 1. A mixture of a liquid and a solid in which, though finely divided, the solid particles are still visible and will settle out on standing. 2. The state of the solid in a mixture as described above. 183

SYNTHESIS 

synthesis, n. [From a Greek word meaning to put together.] 1. The composition, combination, or putting together of a substance; the building up process; the opposite of analysis. 2. In chemistry, the process by which a compound is built up from its elements or constituent parts. synthetic, adj. 

1. In chemistry, pertaining to or designating that which has been made by a process of building it up from its units, or from simpler substances. 

2. In biology, pertaining to or designating an organism or group of organisms in which characteristics of several other groups are present. 184

T technical, adj. [From the Greek noun meaning an art.] Pertaining or belonging chiefly or wholly to some one science, art, or skilled occupation; as, a technical course in engineering, the technical vocabulary of chemistry. temperature, n. 

[From the Latin verb temperature meaning to temper.] 1. The degree or intensity of heat of a body. 2. In physiology and medicine, (colloquially) the degree of heat of a person or animal above the normal; as, a person has a temperature of two degrees, and meaning a temperature of two degrees above the normal temperature of 98 3/5° F. 3. In physics, degree of heat or cold; intensity of heat; the condition of a body which indicates the amount of kinetic energy represented by the motion of its molecules. According to the kinetic theory, a body the molecules of which contains no heat has a temperature of - 273° C. (0° Absolute) and its molecules are at rest. At any temperature above 0° absolute, the molecules contain heat and are in motion, and the heat contained or carried by the molecules is in proportion to the absolute temperature. 

The absolute temperature of different bodies when they have acquired heat varies according to the capacity of the molecules to carry heat. 

Thus, temperature is directly proportional to the amount of heat contained in a body and inversely proportional to the capacity of its molecules to carry heat. Temperatures cannot be measured directly but they can be compared by means of various easily measured effects. 

See thermometry. Thermometers, pyrometers, and other thermometric instruments may be used in the determination of temperatures. See thermometer. tension, n. 

[From the Latin verb tendere meaning to stretch.] 1. The act of stretch ing; the state or condition of being stretched or strained. 2. In mechanics, the degree or amount of stretch or strain; as, a wire is under a tension of one hundred pounds; the pressure exerted by a substance or body under strain or in a state of unstable equilibrium. 3. In physics, the strain set up in a solid, which causes it to tend to be torn apart and balanced by cohesive forces which hold it together; the force which gives a body elasticity. 4. In electricity, a condition or state by virtue of which a charged body tends to lose its charge through leakage into the air or by passing it to another body of different potential. This tendency is greatest when the charge upon a body is nearest the maximum charge which the body can carry; stress due to difference of potential or voltage. See surface tension and vapor tension. tervalent, adj. 

Having a valence of three: trivalent. 185

TEST 

test, n. 

1. An investigation to determine (1) the identity of, (2) the nature of, or (3) the composition of some unknown substance. The test may consist of a procedure something done-or of a reaction—a chemical change brought about. A single test will seldom determine the identity of a substance, but a properly chosen series of tests may do so. Qualitative analysis of a substance consists of such a series of tests. Quantitative analysis goes farther and determines the amount of each ingredient present. Tests are spoken of as positive when they show the presence of a looked-for substance, and negative when they show its absence; as, the leaves picked at noon gave a positive iodine test for starch, while those picked at midnight from the same plant gave a negative starch test, showing that after sunset the starch had been removed from the leaves for storage in another part of the plant. 

2. In botany, same as testa. The outer coating or skin which covers a seed. It is usually more or less hard and brittle. 3. In zoology, the outside layer of many invertebrate animals made of a more or less hard material. In mollusks it is made of calcium carbonate; in infusorians of chitin or silica; in some tunicates it is like cartilage, in others leathery, in others soft and gelatinous. test, v.t.

In chemistry, to examine by means of some reagent or some special mode of treatment. theory, n. [From a Greek noun meaning a speculation or theory.] A general statement as a natural law, based on observed facts, but not yet proved universally true; an explanation of a series of related facts, supported by a large number of experimentally determined truths, but not yet shown to cover all possible applications; as molecular theory, electron theory, theory of relativity, etc. therm-, thermo-.

Combining form from the Greek noun meaning heat. thermal, adj. [Thermal.] Relating to heat; hot; warm; as, thermal springs, thermal conductivity. thermit, n. [Therm+it.] A mixture of finely divided aluminum and powdered iron oxide. Sometimes other metallic oxides are used. Its use in the thermite process, sometimes called the Goldschmidt process after its inventor, is as follows: Thermit is placed in a crucible, and a primer, such as a piece of magnesium ribbon, is inserted into its surface. The burning of the primer raises the temperature of some of the thermit to a point where the oxide is decomposed-i.e. reduced. The oxygen thus set free immediately and violently unites with the aluminum, producing intense heat-probably 3000° C. This reaction spreads rapidly downward; a fluid slag floats to the top; and below it collects a mass of 186

THERMOMETER 

intensely hot and very fluid metal derived from the original oxide. 

Metal thus obtained has been used to melt holes through sheets of steel; to fuse together broken parts of heavy machinery without removing them from their places, as in cases of breakdown in midocean; and to weld together the ends of conductor-rails of electric street-car systems.
