The thermit process has also been employed to obtain chromium free from carbon, an impurity which makes that metal useless in the manufacture of chrome steel and one which is always present when chromium oxide is reduced by the ordinary methods. Similarly pure manganese and other metals for special uses have been obtained. thermometer, n. [From the Greek therma, heat, and metron, measure.] An instrument by which the temperatures of substances are determined. All types of thermometer are based on the fact that heat expands all substances (with a very few exceptions), and the further fact that the amount of expansion is in proportion to the increase in temperature. Galileo is credited with the invention of the thermometer because he was the first to recognize these facts and to devise an instrument by which temperature changes were measured by measuring changes in volume. 

This he did some time prior to 1611. His pupils developed his crude thermometer, the Florentine philosophers by 1641 using one containing alcohol and having the tube graduated into divisions representing about 1/50 of the capacity of the bulb. Segrede divided the scale into 360 parts, to correspond to the divisions of the circle, hence the term degree, still in use. Fahrenheit added the detail of "fixed points," making it possible to compare the readings of different thermometers, but he chose his fixed points unwisely. For the lower one, or 0°, he took what he considered to be absolute cold—the temperature obtained by a freezing mixture (q.v.) of sal ammoniac and ice. For the upper one, 100°, he took the temperature of the healthy human body. 

De l'Isle, about 1730, first used as the fixed points the melting point of ice and the boiling point of water. Celsius put 0° at the upper fixed point and 100° at the lower one. Linnaeus reversed this, thus completing the evolution of the Centigrade thermometer, the most logical of the mercury-in-glass thermometers in use today. For very accurate work the expansion of a gas instead of a liquid must be measured, for gases expand with nearly perfect uniformity throughout the scale, while mercury and alcohol do not. The so-called metallic thermometer measures temperature changes by means of the unequal expansion and contraction which they produce in two metals. In its commonest form the essential part of this thermometer consists of two strips of metal, one of brass and one of steel, brazed together and made into a coil or helix. A change in temperature causes this helix to become more or less tightly coiled. One of its ends is fixed, the other free to move. The movements of the free end cause a pointer to move over a dial graduated in degrees of temperature. For measuring high temperatures, resort to solid metals is necessary because the ordinary mercury- 187

THERMOMETRY 

in-glass themometer is useless at temperatures above the boiling point of mercury (357° C.). (When the space above the mercury is filled with nitrogen, the pressure of the gas raises this boiling point and correspondingly increases the usable range of the instrument.) thermometry, n.

The measurement or estimation of temperature by means of suitable apparatus; calculations involved in the measurement of temperature by means of thermometric apparatus; the science or method of temperature measurement. Thermometric instruments may be based on the expansive effect of heat on a metal or a gas, on changes in the electrical properties of metals, or on any other property of a substance or body which varies regularly with temperature changes, and which may be easily measured. thermos bottle. 

[The Greek adjective meaning hot.] The trade name of a container designed to prevent the loss or gain of heat by its contents, which are usually liquid. Aside from its protective coverings, it consists essentially of a modified Dewar bulb. Two glass bottles, one within the other, have their side walls parallel and separated by a narrow space. Their necks are molded into a continuous wall. Through an opening in the outer wall the air is exhausted from the space between the bottles and the opening is sealed up. The vacuum thus created prevents the passage of heat into or out of the inner bottle by conduction or convection, and by silvering both walls the passage of heat in either direction by radiation is reduced to a minimum. thermostat, n. [From the Greek words meaning heat and to make stand.] An automatic device for controlling temperature. Most thermostats have movable parts which are operated by the expansion of mercury, or some other metal by heat. In some devices the expansion of the mercury closes an electric circuit which operates the heat control apparatus. In other devices, an electric circuit is opened and closed, or a damper is operated, by the unequal expansion of two different metals by heat. In these devices a strip of copper is tightly riveted to a strip of iron of exactly equal size. One end of this strip is securely clamped while the other end is free to move. Since the coefficient of expansion of copper is not the same as that of iron, the free end of the riveted strip of metals will move aside as the temperature changes. Thus the strip may be made to open or close an electric circuit or to operate moveable parts of the heat control apparatus. in, n. [From the Anglo-Saxon word for the metal.] Symbol Sn (from the Latin word stannum for the metal); atomic weight 118.7; valence 2 and 4; density 7.3; melting point 232° C. A white lustrous metal highly resistant to the atmosphere and not readily attacked by ordinary acids. 

At temperatures below 18° C. the metal tends to change to a gray 188

TINCTURE 

powder and at very low temperatures articles made of tin may crumble. 

At 100° C. the metal is malleable and at 200° C. so brittle that it can be ground to a powder. When a bar of tin is bent, it emits a peculiar cracking sound similar to that produced by the breaking fibers of a piece of soft wood. The most important ore of tin is cassiterite (SnO2) which is obtained chiefly from the East Indies, Bolivia, and China. 

The ore is roasted in air and then heated in a furnace with carbon. The metal thus obtained is further purified by just remelting and pouring away the molten tin. Tin may be plated upon iron by simply dipping very clean iron sheets into molten tin. Tin plate thus produced has a variety of uses, chief of which is the manufacture of tin cans for preserving and storing fruit, vegetables and other food. Since tin is less. active than iron, the tin serves as a protective coat as long as the surface is unbroken. But if the underlying sheet of iron is at any time. exposed by a break in the tin-a galvanic couple is produced and corrosion of the iron takes place at a very rapid rate. Tin is a constituent of many important alloys. Copper and tin are the chief constituents of bronze; lead and tin, (usually in equal amounts) of solder; and tin, lead and antimony of bearing metal. A number of tin salts are of considerable importance in the industries. tincture, n.

A liquid in which is dissolved some substance or its characteristic or essential part. The solvent may be alcohol (either dilute or pure)' spirits of ammonia, sulphuric ether, or wine. So according to the solvent used, tinctures are called alcoholic, ammoniated, or etherial tinctures, or medicated wines. The solute may be a vegetable substance, as in tincture of nux vomica; an animal substance, as in tincture of musk; or a mineral substance, as in tincture of iron (ferric chloride (FeCl3) dissolved in alcohol). Lemon extract and essence of peppermint are tinctures. titanium, n. [From the Latin name Titanes meaning son of the earth.] Symbol Ti; atomic weight 47.9; specific gravity 4.5; valence 3 or 4; melting point above 1800° C. One of the rare metallic elements occurring as a constitutent of several minerals but never free. The chief ores are rutile (TiO2) and ilmenite (FeTiO3), which are found chiefly in the United States and in Norway. (Compounds of titanium are not made from the metal directly but from its ores.) Rutile is used as a source material for the manufacture of titanium compounds, while ilmenite is used for the manufacture of ferro-titanium compounds, arc lamp electrodes, and titanium pigments. Metallic titanium can be obtained as a dark grey metallic powder by heating titanous chloride with metallic sodium.

The metal decomposes water at 100° C., combines with nitrogen at high temperatures, and is converted to the dioxide when burned. In its reactivity with acids, and in other respects the metal resembles tin. 

The metal is used in metallurgy chiefly because of its power to remove oxygen and nitrogen, thus improving the quality of steel and other alloys even when present in only very small amounts. The dioxide 189

TITRATE 

of titanium (TiO2) is a white powder very inert chemically and with remarkable power to resist chemical action. It is used as a paint pigment, as a coloring agent in porcelain glazes and for many other purposes. Titanium tetrachloride, (TiCl4), is a heavy colorless liquid which is decomposed by moisture with the formation of TiO2 and HCl. This liquid, along with SiCl4, was largely used during the World War in producing smoke-screens. Several titanium compounds are used as mordants in the dyeing industry. titrate, v.t. 

[From a French word meaning to standardize or establish title to.] To evaluate or measure by means of a liquid of known concentration; to standardize by a volumetric process. 

titration, n. 

In chemistry, a quantitative chemical process in which the volume of a liquid chemical reagent required to bring about a definite chemical reaction is measured. The liquid reagent is usually added by means of a burette to another solution or sample of substance of known weight or volume until a definite chemical reaction has been completed, as indicated by some color change, or other effect. Thus, the concentration of an alkaline solution may be determined by neutralizing a sample portion of t with an acid solution of known concentration. From the volume of the acid required to neutralize the alkaline solution (the neutral point being shown by a change in color of an indicator which has been added) the amount of alkali present or the concentration of the sample of alkaline solution can be calculated. torque, n. 

[From the Latin verb torquere meaning to twist.] In physics. 1. The tendency to twist or turn about an axis, or to cause to twist or turn about an axis. 2. The time rate of change in angular momentum. 3. 

That which tends to produce change in angular momentum; that which tends to cause a body to turn about an axis. toxic, adj. [From the Greek word meaning the poison used on arrows.] Referring to, caused by, or having the nature of poison; poisonous. toxin, n.

In biology. 1. Any poisonous substance (usually an albumen) produced by action of an organism. 2. A poisonous base produced by bacterial action; a ptomaine. transmission, n. [From Latin words meaning to send across.] 1. The act of sending or passing of a thing; the state or condition of that which is being sent or passed; as, the transmission of property from one generation to the next, or the transmission of signals or music by radio. 2. The apparatus connecting the engine to the driving axle of an automobile. transmit, v.t. 

1. To send or cause to pass over or through; to hand down from one 190

TRANSMITTER 

generation to another; to convey by sending; as, to transmit a message.

One who sends or transmits; that which sends or transmits, especially that portion of a telephone or radio apparatus which converts sounds into the corresponding pulsations in the electromagnetic waves which carry the signals. transverse, adj. [From Latin words meaning to turn across.] Extending or lying across or crosswise. tripod, n. [From Greek words meaning three and foot.] 1. Any stand, stool, or supporting object, which has three feet or legs. 2. A three legged device, often folding or telescoping, used to support a camera, machine gun, surveyor's transit, telescope, or similar instrument. triturate, v.t. [From a Latin word meaning to rub to pieces.] 1. To crush by rubbing; to bruise or mash by rubbing. 2. To reduce to an extremely fine powder, by grinding. trivalent, adj. 

Having a valence of three. tungsten, n. [From the Swedish words tung meaning heavy and sten meaning stone.] 

Symbol W. (From the German noun wolframium the name of an ore of tungsten); atomic weight 184; specific gravity 18.64; valence 2,4,5, & 

6; melting point about 3370° C. (the highest of all the metals). A hard, steel gray metal obtained chiefly from the ore wolframite (FeMnWO4), by fusion with sodium carbonate and treating with acids to get tungstic acid (H₂WO4 H2O), which on ignition yields the oxide (WO3). By reduction with hydrogen at 1000° C. the oxide yields the powered metal, which can be molded into bars and by heat and compression drawn down into rods, or wire. China and the United States are the greatest producers of tungsten, about 6000 tons of the metal being produced in the latter country in 1922. Tungsten does not rust and is not attacked by ordinary concentrated acids. It imparts hardness and toughness to steel and gives it the ability to hold its temper at a red heat, this latter property making possible the high speed tool steel so important in the automobile and other industries where steel parts must be exactly shaped and fitted. Large quantities of tungsten are used in the manufacture of electric light filaments. These are nearly three times as efficient as the carbon filament which they replaced. Considerable quantities are also used in making hard tough steel for auto parts, phonograph needles, electric apparatus and many other machine parts where steel of unusual properties is desired. Very few tungsten compounds are of any importance. 191

TURBINE 

turbine, n. [From the Latin noun turbo meaning that which spins or whirls.] A wheel, shaft, or drum carrying a large number of small blades or paddles and which may be caused to whirl rapidly upon its axis, by a jet of stream of water, steam, or some other fluid directed against the blade or paddles. Some of the largest electrical generators are driven by turbines in which water under great pressure is forced against thousands of small blades set at an angle in the turbine shaft, thus causing the shaft to turn rapidly. Some of the most powerful modern steam engines are of the turbine type, the shaft being caused to move by jets of steam acting against many thousands of blades inserted in it. type-metal, n.

An alloy whose most abundant ingredient is lead, from which types for printing are cast. Types must have (1) sharp outlines, so that they will leave clear impressions on the paper; and (2) enough hardness to retain their sharp outlines under rough usage. Types made of lead alone would lack this first characteristic, because lead shrinks when changing from the liquid to the solid state of matter. Lead alone is too malleable to meet the second requirement. By adding antimony and tin-and sometimes copper and iron also-to the lead, an alloy is secured which expands when solidifying and also resists wear. The specific properties which are required in the material vary with the uses and nature of the types, so the proportions of the ingredients are varied to meet the requirements. A typical mixture for casting small types (from brilliant to brevier) consists of 100 pounds of lead, 40 pounds of antimony, and 20 pounds of tin; while for larger types (from bourgeois to pica) a satisfactory mixture is 100 pounds of lead, 30 pounds of antimony, and 15 pounds of tin. Extra hardness is obtained by adding 6 or 

8 percent of copper and increasing the proportions of antimony and tin. 

Type-metal, cast iron, and water are three substances which do not follow the rule that liquids contract when they pass into the solid state of matter. Of the three exceptions the last is by far the most important. 192

U ultra. [From a Latin adverb meaning beyond, on the far side of.] A prefix usually joined to place words to give the meaning beyond or on the other side of: as ultramarine, and ultraviolet; and to other words to indicate excess or extreme: as ultrafashionable, ultrafastidious. Ultraviolet in reference to light refers to all waves shorter than the very short waves which produce violet light; ultramodern, means extremely modern or very modern; the ultramicroscope is an instrument which magnifies beyond the power of any ordinary instrument; ultramarine is an old name for a blue pigment which was obtained in ancient times from foreign lands, hence the name which means beyond the sea. ultraviolet. 

A term usually used to indicate all those waves in the ether which are shorter than those producing violet light. See ultra, and light. undercooling, n. 

See supercooling. unit, n. 

1. The smallest whole number; single thing. 2. Any fixed quantity of anything—as of length, mass, heat, energy, or time—which is used as a standard when measuring other quantities of the same thing; any quantity which is indicated by the number 1. univalent, adj. [Uni+the Latin word valens meaning to have power or strength.] Having or indicating a valence of one. urea, n.

A white crystalline compound (CO(NH2)2) formed in the human body when protein is used up in the life-processes, particularly when muscle fibers wear out. It is carried away from the worn out cells by the blood, taken out of the blood by the kidneys, and passed out of the body in the urine, of which it is the chief solid constituent. A man of average weight excretes 8 to 30 grams of it daily and it must be gotten out of the blood promptly if a healthy condition of the body is to be maintained. It is very soluble in water, and forms crystalline compounds by uniting with both acids and bases. It is found in the urine of both mammals and birds. Another name for it is carbamide. 193

V vacuum, n. 

Space which contains no matter. The complete absence of matter can be conceived by the mind, but in practice it cannot be attained. The term is commonly applied to an enclosed space from which nearly all the air has been removed by means of an air pump or other appropriate means; as, the space between the walls of a thermos bottle, or the space inside a barometer tube above the mercury column. The latter is very nearly a complete vacuum, containing nothing but a small amount of mercury vapor. vacuum pan.

A device of which the purpose is the removal of surplus water from a liquid so as to concentrate the residue,-consisting essentially of a covered metallic vessel which can be made air-tight after being partly filled with the dilute liquid; and air-pump, by which a partial vacuum can be created and maintained within the vessel; some means of heating the liquid, either by passing heat through the walls or by sending steam through a coil of pipes immersed in the liquid; and some means of removing the surplus water from the interior after it has been evaporated. 

Lowering the atmospheric pressure within the vessel lowers the boiling point of the contained liquid, thus lessening the danger of damage by over-heating and saving fuel. It also hastens the evaporation of the water, thus shortening the time consumed in the process. Vacuum pans are extensively used in boiling down the dilute solutions from which sugar is obtained, in the manufacture of condensed or evaporated milk, etc. vacuum tube. 

1. A tube from which much of the air has been removed. 2. In radio, a term applied to a glass tube or bulb exhausted almost completely of air and containing especially: (1) a filament which is heated by a direct current and thus caused to emit electrons, (2) a plate which is kept positively charged so as to attract the electrons emitted from the hot filament, and (3) a grid which consists of a helix of fine wire inserted between the filament and the plate and which serves to control the flow of electrons from the hot filament to the plate. The vacuum tube can be used as a detector, as an amplifier, or for the generation of high frequency electro-magnetic waves. valence, n. [From a Latin noun meaning strength.] In chemistry, the capacity of an atom or radical to combine with other atoms, as compared with that of hydrogen, and determined by its capacity to give up or receive electrons. 

Atoms are said to be univalent, divalent, trivalent, tetravalent, pentavalent, and the like according as they combine with or replace one, two, three, four, or five hydrogen atoms or their equivalents, (i. e. according 194

VAPOR as the atom will give up or acquire one, two, three electrons, etc.). Atoms are said to have a positive valence when they give up electrons and negative when they acquire electrons. The combining power of all the atoms was originally measured in terms of their ability to combine with or replace the atom of hydrogen. Thus chlorine is monovalent because one atom of chlorine unites with one atom of hydrogen to form the molecule HCl, and carbon is tetravalent because one atom combines with four atoms of hydrogen to form the molecule CH4 or methane. 

Aluminum is trivalent because one atom of aluminum will combine with three atoms of chlorine which is known to be monovalent because it unites atom for atom with hydrogen. Many elements exhibit two or more valences, but usually a very definite valence under given conditions. 

Thus in the presence of reducing agents, elements almost always have the lowest valence, while oxidizing agents will in general cause an increase in valence, in proportion to the vigor of the reaction and the completeness of the oxidizing process. Thus, in the presence of reducing agents iron exhibits a valence of two, forming a chloride with the formula FeCl2, while in the presence of oxidizing agents the trichloride 

FeCl3 is formed. See oxidation. vapor, n. [From the Latin noun vapor meaning smoke or haze.] 1. As commonly used, any substance in the gaseous state. 2. In physics, any substance in the gaseous state, the condition of which is ordinarily that of a liquid or solid; as, water vapor, or iodine vapor; a gaseous substance which has a critical temperature higher than ordinary temperature and can, therefore, be liquefied by pressure alone. For the distinction between a gas and a vapor, see gas. vapor pressure.

In physics and chemistry, the pressure per unit of area exerted by a saturated vapor while it is still in contact with the liquid or solid from which it was generated. At any temperature between the melting point and the boiling point of a liquid, a very definite force within the liquid tends to send the molecules out into the space above it, so that the space above a liquid in a closed vessel will always contain a very definite quantity of the gas which has escaped from the liquid. This quantity is constant for any given temperature for a given liquid. As molecules are continually leaving the surface of the liquid and escaping into the space above it, this constant concentration of molecules above the liquid is maintained by the return of large numbers of molecules to the liquid. 

Thus, on standing at any given temperature, an equilibrium is established in which the number of molecules leaving the surface of the liquid is just equal to the number of molecules returning to it, and vapor tension (the upward pressure exerted by the molecules leaving the liquid), is just equal to vapor pressure (the pressure exerted by the molecules returning to the liquid). Vapor pressure is always a measure of vapor tension and both increase with rise in temperature. See aqueous pressure. 195

VAPOR TENSION 

vapor tension.

In physics and chemistry, the upward force per unit of area exerted by molecules escaping from the surface of a liquid; the force per unit of area of molecules escaping from the surface of a solid; a measure of the tendency of a substance to enter the vapor state (to change to a gas). The tendency of liquids to change to a vapor, and consequently their vapor tension increases with rise in temperature until the boiling point is reached when it is just equal to atmospheric pressure. Some solids, such as iodine, have a tendency to change directly to a vapor without passing through the liquid state. These solids have, therefore, vapor tension. Ice or water at 0° C. has a vapor tension equal to 4.5 mm of mercury. See vapor pressure. velocity, n. [From the Latin word velocitat meaning swiftness or quickness.] Speed; swiftness; quickness of motion; rate of movement in a straight or curved line; rate of change in direction or position of a body moving around in a fixed center. A body has uniform velocity when its rate of travel is constant. Velocity is described as accelerated when the rate of travel constantly increases, and as retarded when it constantly decreases. The time rate of motion of a body in moving direction is called linear velocity. The time rate of motion of a body moving about a fixed center is called angular velocity. vertical, adj. 

1. Having the same direction as a line joining the zenith (the point directly overhead) and the nadir (the point directly underneath). 

2. Having a position at right angles to the plane of the horizon; upright; plumb. 3. Situated at the highest point or vertex. This word should not be confused with perpendicular, which means at right angles to any other line or plane, while vertical means at right angles to a particular plane the plane of the horizon. vibration, n. [From the Latin vibrare meaning to vibrate.] 1. The act of moving to and fro or forward and backward; as, the vibration of the reed of a clarinet, or of a violin string; the state of being vibrated, or rapidly moved forward and backward or up and down with reference to a fixed point or position; as, the vibration of a floor by the movement of a heavy machine. 

Vibration usually refers to the movement made possible by the elasticity of a body, so that when disturbed, it alternately moves in opposite directions from a mid point or normal position. The amplitude of vibration is the amount of maximum displacement from a mid point or position of rest. The period of vibration is the time between two successive passages in the same direction through any given point. 2. A single to and fro or up and down movement of a body which is vibrating. 

Compare with oscillation. viscose, n. [From the Latin word viscosus meaning sticky.] A solution made by 196

VISCOSITY 

treating finely divided cellulose with caustic soda, carbon disulphide, and water. When heat and certain acids act upon it cellulose is regenerated. Viscose is used as a cement, in the preparation of artificial silk, and in the sizing and glazing of textiles. In the preparation of artificial silk the viscose is squirted through orifices into an acid solution. The extremely fine fibers of cellulose thus produced are dryed, dyed and spun into thread or woven into fabrics. viscosity, n. [From the Latin word viscosus meaning sticky.] 1. The state of being viscous, or the degree to which a substance is viscous. 2. In physics, the degree to which a fluid or semi-fluid resists a force tending to make it flow. A specific property of fluids, due to the resistance of their molecules to motion over each other. This resistance is thought to be due both to cohesion and adhesion. Fluids differ greatly in mobility. Some fluids flow rapidly, when an attempt is made to pour them while others can hardly be poured at all. Water when disturbed gives rise to waves which surge back and forth but many other liquids such as molasses, glycerine, and tar, flow slowly or hardly at all. In all cases the viscosity is affected by change in the temperature, usually decreasing with rise in temperature. This difference in mobility is due to internal friction of the molecules of the substance. Fluids which flow slowly are said to be very viscous, or to have a high viscosity. Fluids like ether, alcohol, and water which flow rapidly are not very viscous. All known fluids, even gases, have some viscosity. In the physical examination of oils the viscosity is usually determined, since this property is directly related to the power of the oil to lubricate properly. viscous, adj. 

1. Sticky; adhesive; not flowing freely; as, molasses is viscous. 2. In physics, possessing the specific property viscosity. visual, adj. [From the Latin word visus meaning sight.] 1. Of, referring to or indicating sight or the sense of sight. 2. Visible, capable of being seen. 

3. Caused or resulting from sight or the organ of sight. vitamine, n. [Fom the Latin noun meaning life+amin.] One of a number of highly complex nitrogenous compounds which are present in a wide range of organic foods consumed by man. Their presence in minute quantities is necessary to and effective in promoting the assimilation of the food, which process they govern by serving as catalytic agents. Certain diseases of man have been clearly demonstrated to be due to the absence from the diet of certain vitamines. The absence of the fat-soluble vitamine A causes a peculiar disease of the eyes, a similar connection exists between the water-soluble vitamine B and beri-beri, between vitamine C and scurvy, and between vitamine D and rickets. While the chemical composition of the vitamines and the number of kinds necessary to human well-being are still open to question, there is no doubt of the importance of the investigations in this new field of human knowledge. 197

VITREOUS 

vitreous, adj. [From a Latin word meaning glass.] Having the appearance of, or resembling glass; glassy. vitriol, n. 

[From the Latin word vitreolus meaning glass-like.] 1. The crystalline sulphates of certain metals; as, copper sulphate (blue vitriol) zinc sulphate (white vitriol). Sulphuric acid was formerly called Oleum

Vitrolis or oil of vitriol. Hence many of the sulphates are called vitriols because derived from this acid. 2. Sulphuric acid. volatile, adj. [From the Latin verb volare, to fly.] Readily vaporizable; easily converted into the gaseous state; having a tendency to change into the gaseous state at ordinary temperatures. volatilize, v.t.

To render or cause to become volatile by means of heat or reduced pressure; to convert to a vapor; to exhale or evaporate; to drive off as a vapor. volt, n. 

[Named after the Italian scientist Allesandro Volta (1745-1827).] 

The practical unit of measurement of electromotive force or difference in potential, being the electromotive force needed to drive a current of one ampere through a circuit having a resistance of one ohm. The volt is one hundred million times the size of the C. G. S. or absolute unit of electromotive force. See Ohm's law. voltage, n.

In electricity, an electromotive force or difference in potential, expressed in volts. voltaic, adj. [From Allesandro Volta, who first produced apparatus for developing electric current by chemical action.] Referring to, or produced by current electricity obtained by chemical action. voltameter, n. [From voltaic referring to current electricity+meter meaning to measure.] 

A device or apparatus for measuring the quantity of electricity passing through a circuit; a coulometer. There are two common types of voltameter. Both depend upon the principle that one Faraday or 96,540 coulombs of electricity passing through a solution will deposit or release one gram equivalent of any element. In the copper voltameter, the quantity of electricity is measured by ascertaining the quantity of copper deposited on passing the current for a given time. In another type the quantity of hydrogen or oxygen evolved is used as a measure of the current passed. volt ammeter, n. [volt+ammeter.] An instrument for measuring the amount of power in volt-amperes (watts) passed through a circuit; a wattmeter. 198

VOLTMETER 

voltmeter, n.

An instrument for measuring or recording the difference in potential in volts between two points in an electric circuit. A voltmeter is commonly a galvanometer with a coil of very high resistance. It is always connected in parallel with the circuit in which the voltage is being measured. Contrast with ammeter which has a coil of low resistance and which is always connected in series with the circuit in which the amperage is being measured. vulcanite, n. [Vulcan (god of fire)+-ite).] A substance which is made by thoroughly mixing with caoutchouc 1/4 to 1/2 as much sulphur, and then treating the resulting mass in a closed vessel with steam at a temperature of about 150° C. This treatment results in the chemical union of the caoutchouc and part of the sulphur (vulcanization). The same result can be obtained by other processes, which dispense with the heat and cause the sulphur to be taken up from liquid solutions or from gases. Vulcanite is readily molded when warm, and when cold can be machined and polished. It is widely used in making buttons, combs, fountain pens, etc. Because it is a good electrical insulator, it is used in electrical apparatus, and because it resists the attack of chemicals, it is used in chemical apparatus. vulcanization, n.

A process of treating crude caoutchouc (rubber) to produce greater elasticity, hardness or other desirable property by heating with sulphur, sulphides and other chemicals or soaking in a solution of sulphur chloride. vulcanize, v.t. 

1. To subject to vulcanization. 2. To harden a fibrous or gum-like substance by heating it or compressing it with chemicals. 3. To toughen, harden, or otherwise improve the quality of caoutchouc or India rubber, by heating it with sulphur or other chemicals. See rubber. 199

water of crystallization.

W An older and less used term for water of hydration, (q.v). water of hydration.

The water held by hydrates; water of crystallization. Not to be confused with the (-OH) held by hydroxides. A number of different compounds (especially sulphates of the metals) crystallize from water solution with a very definite amount of water of hydration. Copper sulphate, for example, forms crystals which contain 36.14% of water by weight. This is equivalent to exactly five molecules of water for each molecule of anhydrous (CuSO4). watt, n. [Named after James Watt, (1732–1819), the English inventor.] A unit of power in the C. G. S. System, one ampere passing under a pressure of one volt; the watt is equal to 1/746 horsepower. watthour, n. [watt+hour.] A unit of work in the C. G. S. system, one watt passing for one hour; the watthour is equivalent to 3600 joules. The watthour is sometimes known as the Kelvin. wattmeter, n. [Watt+meter.] An instrument or device used to indicate or record in watts the amount of electric power passing through a circuit or which has passed in a given time. Most devices or instruments of this kind ought to be called kilowatt hour meters, since they actually record the kilowatthours of power used or passed. A common form of this type of meter consists of a recording device driven by a shunt motor which turns at a speed proportional to the rate at which electrical energy is passing through the meter. weight, n.

In physics, 1. The measure of the pull of gravity. 2. One of the general properties of matter, due to the earth's gravitative attraction. It is expressed in grams, pounds, etc. While weight is in proportion to mass, the weight of a body may vary, while its mass does not. Moving a body to a higher altitude (increasing the distance between the centers of the mutually attracting bodies) decreases the weight of the body. 

At the same altitude but at different latitudes, the weight would also vary because of different surface velocities of the earth, and consequent differences in centrifugal force. Thus at the poles, at sea level, a body's weight would be 1/289 less than at the equator at sea level. wet bulb hygrometer.

An instrument used to determine the relative humidity of the air by means of two thermometers, one of which records the temperature of the air, and the other the temperature of a moist cloth which has been cooled by evaportion of water from it. Since the amount of cooling will be in proportion to the rate of evaporation, and since the rate of evapora- 200

WORK 

tion is greater in dry air than in moist air, it is possible by reference to the difference in temperature between the two thermometers, to determine the relative humidity of the air. work, n. 

1. Putting forth effort either physical or intellectual, for the sake of accomplishing something. 2. In physics, producing motion against resistance. 201

xenon, n. [From a Greek word meaning a guest, because it always accompanies the other rare gases of the atmosphere.] Symbol Xe; atomic weight 

130.2; valence 0; specific gravity 63.5 (H=1); boiling point - 109° C.

One of the rare gases of the atmosphere which, like the other gases of this group, is inert and so forms no compounds. The gas is present in the atmosphere to the extent of only one part in 170,000,000 and so must be considered one of the rarest of elements. x-rays, n. [So called because the nature of the rays for a long time were unknown.] 

Very penetrating rays of short wave length sent out from an oblique platinum plate when the cathode rays of a Crooks tube are converged upon it; Roentgen rays. When a glass tube is fitted with platinum cathode, an anode terminating in a plate set at a 45 degree angle, and the air almost completely exhausted from it, all the elements of a Crooks tube are present. When a tube of this type is connected with a large induction coil and the current passed in proper manner, the cathode gives rise to a stream of electrons or negatively charged particles, which when they strike the anode give rise to x-rays which leave the oblique plate and pass through the walls of the tube as light passes through ordinary glass. The rays thus produced are x-rays, or Roentgen rays. 

These rays may be detected by a photographic plate and by their power to cause a screen of barium platinocyanide to glow. They are not deflected by the magnet and are not reflected or refracted by prisms or lenses. They have remarkable power to penetrate substances of low density such as wood, paper, cloth, the light metals, and even human flesh, though they are stopped by the denser metals, especially by lead. 

The x-rays are thought to have the power to arrest the development of certain forms of malignant growth and are known to be deadly to many forms of disease producing bacteria. X-rays have become indispensible to modern surgery in the examination of internal parts of the body, and to modern industry in testing materials and standardizing products. 202

zeppelin, n.

Ꮓ 

[From the name of the inventor, Count Zeppelin of Germany.] A name given to an airship of the lighter than air type, invented and first used in Germany by Count Zeppelin. The zeppelin, as used during the World 

War, consists of a cigar-shaped balloon about 300 feet long and 50 to 70 feet in diameter built over a structional framework of magnalium, under which were suspended two gondolas which housed powerful gasoline engines. The Graf Zeppelin, which circumnavigated the globe in August 1929, was 776 feet in length and 114 feet in width and was capable of carrying a personnel, crew and group of passengers of sixty people. zinc, n. [From the German name zink for the metal.] Symbol Zn; atomic weight 65.38; specific gravity 7.1; valence 2; melting point 419° C. A grayish crystalline metallic element, not found free in nature, but occurring combined with many other elements. Smithsonite or zinc spar (ZnCO3) and sphalerite or zinc blende (ZuS) are the chief zinc ores of the United States which mines about half of the world's yearly output of more than one million tons. Sulphide ores of zinc are usually concentrated and then roasted to convert the zinc sulphide (ZnS) to zinc oxide (ZnO), which is then heated with carbon thereby being reduced to metallic zinc. Zinc is a hard crystalline metal which becomes readily malleable when heated to 100° to 150° C., so that it can be rolled into thin sheets which remain soft and malleable after cooling to ordinary temperatures. Zinc is attacked slightly by the oxygen and carbon dioxide of the atmosphere, forming a protecting film of basic zinc carbonate. Large quantities of zinc are used in galvanizing iron to prevent rusting. In this process the iron is treated with a cleaning solution, dried and dipped into molten zinc. A thin coat of zinc is left on the surface of the iron. Zinc when melted with copper forms the alloy brass, which has a multitude of uses. Since zinc is one of the cheapest of the strongly electro positive metals, it is widely used in the manufacture of dry batteries. Zinc salts are all poisonous and cooking utensils and other food containers should never be made of the metal. 

Zinc oxide, zinc sulphide, and zinc carbonate are used in the manufacture of white paint, and as a filler for white rubber. Many other zinc. salts have important industrial uses. 203
